# Interactive English – Condensed Study Notes

Condensed from 749 auto-caption transcripts. Each lesson keeps only the new vocabulary, rules or tips; scripts, greetings and promo are removed.

## 1. 1-HOUR LESSON - C2 Vocab Every Serious Learner Must Know

**Phrases**
- **let's face it** – introduces something true and undeniable (or true but unpleasant) – "Let's face it, you laugh at anything I say." (links: "face it")
- **few and far between** – not happening/existing often – "Good workers are few and far between." (and → 'n')
- **fall into place** – things happen satisfactorily without problems – "Everything was falling into place."
- **for the best** – action that is unpleasant now but improves things – "Breaking up is for the best."
- **conflict of interest** – can't judge fairly because you're affected by the result – "I'm judging a contest my daughter is in."
- **dawn on someone** (something dawns on you) – realize for the first time – "It dawned on me I forgot my wallet." (suddenly/finally dawn on)
- **crack down on** – take strong action to stop something – "crack down on password sharing"
- **in the blink of an eye** – extremely quickly – "Six years have gone by in the blink of an eye."
- **butt in** – interrupt a conversation / get involved in others' business – "Don't butt in."
- **by all means** – "yes, certainly" (giving permission) – "Could I borrow this book? By all means."
- **behind closed doors** – in secret, away from the public – "The decision was made behind closed doors." (d's elide)
- **come to light** – facts become publicly known – "New evidence came to light."

**Verbs**
- **linger** (v) – continue to exist / take longer than usual to leave – "My cold is still lingering." /ˈlɪŋɡər/ rhymes with finger, not singer
- **dole out** (phrasal v, separable) – give something (usually money) to several people – "dole out freebies"
- **pinpoint** (v) – find the exact position/cause – "Doctors couldn't pinpoint the cause."
- **plague** (v) – cause worry/pain/difficulty over time (plagued by) – "plagued by back injuries" /pleɪɡ/
- **prescribe** (v) – doctor says what treatment to have – "prescribe rest/painkillers" (stress 2nd syllable)
- **utter** (v) – say/make a sound (formal) – "without uttering a word" (flap t)
- **curb** (v) – control/limit something unwanted – "curb screen addiction"
- **lure** (v) – persuade by offering something exciting (into/to) – "lured into working the weekend"
- **indulge** (v) – allow yourself something enjoyable, more than good for you (in) – "indulge in sweets" (stress 2nd, g = j)
- **delegate** (v) /ˈdelɪɡeɪt/ – give a job/duty to someone else – "good at delegating" (noun ends /-ɡət/)
- **reconcile** (v) – make two opposed things agree/coexist – "reconcile our differences"; "reconcile growth with sustainability"
- **loathe** (v) – hate (strong) – "I loathe anchovies." (ends with voiced th)

**Idioms**
- **at the end of your rope** – no strength/patience left – "I'm at the end of my rope."
- **to the bitter end** – until finished, despite a bad outcome – "fight to the bitter end"
- **get off on the right/wrong foot** – make a good/bad start – "The project got off on the wrong foot."
- **over the top** – too extreme/exaggerated – "Her gift was way over the top."
- **at the crack of dawn** – very early in the morning – "We'll get up at the crack of dawn."
- **a change of heart** – change of feelings/opinion (use with "have", usually past; emotional) – "She had a sudden change of heart."

**Nouns**
- **perseverance** (n) – continued effort despite difficulty (positive) – "Learning a language takes perseverance." (stress 3rd syllable)
- **workaholic** (n) – person addicted to work; suffix -holic = addicted (shopaholic); words ending -ic stress the 2nd-to-last syllable
- **hierarchy** (n) – system ranking people/things by importance – "company hierarchy" (stress 1st, schwa 2nd)
- **bias** (n) – unfair support/opposition from personal opinion – "unconscious bias", "confirmation bias"
- **scrutiny** (n) – careful, detailed examination – "come under intense scrutiny" (glottal t)
- **concession** (n) – something given up to end a disagreement – "make a concession" (-sion words stress syllable before)
- **remorse** (n) – sadness for something you've done – "feel no remorse", "filled with remorse" (stress 2nd)
- **snob** (n) – person who respects only high social class; or has very high standards – "coffee snob / wine snob"
- **exile** (n) – forced to leave your country, often political – "go into exile / live in exile"
- **narrative** (n) – the story/version of events someone wants others to believe – "change the narrative" (flap t)
- **xenophobia** (n) – extreme dislike/fear of foreigners; adj xenophobic; suffix -phobia = fear (claustrophobia, arachnophobia, glossophobia) – /zen-/ or /zee-/, stress 3rd syllable
- **influx** (n) – arrival of many people/things at once (influx of) – "an influx of tourists"

## 2. 1-HOUR LESSON - Improve Your Writing ✍️ (Academic, Professional, & Descriptive)

**13 tips for professional writing**
- Know your purpose: narrative, descriptive, expository, or persuasive.
- Know your audience; choose formal (no idioms/slang/colloquialisms, complex structures) vs informal style.
- Organize ideas with a comprehensive outline (full thoughts) for cohesion (logical order) and unity (one idea per paragraph).
- Find your voice (your own words/sentences, be authentic); keep a consistent tone (aggressive, humorous, nostalgic, cheerful...) - no roller coaster.
- Paragraphs: topic sentence, supporting sentences, concluding sentence.
- Use transitions: in addition (add), however (contrast), first and foremost (start).
- Vary sentence structures (simple, compound, complex, compound-complex) for rhythm; all-simple sounds choppy.
- Keep it simple in the draft; fine-tune words when proofreading.
- Proofread for one error type per pass (punctuation, spelling, word choice); use spell/grammar tools.
- Get a second pair of eyes; you can reject content feedback (it's your voice).
- Habits: read regularly and write regularly (journal).
- Idioms taught: **the big picture** (whole view), **in a nutshell** (in the fewest words).

**Reporting verbs (instead of "said")**
- Persuade: **pleaded** (urgent/emotional request), **begged** (stronger), **entreated** (try hard to persuade), **implored** (sincere, emotional ask), **beseeched** (ask showing you need it badly), **cajoled** (persuade by pleasant talk), **insisted** (say firmly against opposition), **demanded** (ask forcefully, expecting no refusal)
- Happy: **gushed** (excessive, maybe insincere praise), **cheered** (loud shout of approval), **touted** (repeatedly praise/advertise to sell), **laughed**, **chortled** (laugh with satisfaction), **giggled** (laugh in an uncontrolled way), **proclaimed** (announce officially, positive), **announced**, **declared** (clearly, firmly, publicly)
- Sad/upset: **pouted** (push out lower lip), **complained**, **sobbed** (cry noisily), **cried**, **lamented** (express sadness/regret), **sneered** (unkind, disrespectful), **whined** (long high sad sound), **bemoaned** (complain/express sadness)
- Loud: **shouted**, **exclaimed** (suddenly, from surprise/fear/pleasure), **bellowed** (very loud voice), **yelled** (anger/pain/excitement), **screamed**, **hollered** (to get attention)
- Quiet: **murmured** (very quietly), **muttered** (low voice so others can't hear; "under one's breath"), **mumbled** (quiet and unclear), **whispered**

**Academic writing tips**
- Be concise: fewest words, use action verbs not -tion nouns ("The report explained the results", not "provided an explanation for"); use vocabulary you know.
- Organize the paper: make a quick outline first (even 5 minutes).
- Avoid contractions, idioms, colloquial expressions, phrasal verbs: put up with -> endure/tolerate; check out -> view/consult; piece of cake -> straightforward; no biggie -> avoid.
- Vary sentence structures: simple = 1 independent clause; compound = 2+ independent; complex = independent + dependent; compound-complex = both.
- Prefer active voice (shorter, shows the doer): "Archaeologists conducted a study" (9 words) vs passive with "by archaeologists" (11).
- Avoid repetition and "very"; vary transitions: also -> in addition, furthermore, moreover, besides.
- Proofread: forward (flow, order, repetition); backward, sentence by sentence (spelling, grammar, punctuation).

**Common mistakes**
- Subject-verb agreement: "Neither John nor Mary is at school"; "who always asks"; "he doesn't" (not "he don't").
- Contraction vs possessive: you're/your, it's (it is/it has)/its.
- Articles: "check the weather forecast"; "an umbrella"; no article before "Yosemite National Park" (original: "a national park" context).
- Wrong preposition: interested in, good/great at, depends on (good at math more common than good in math; Ngram Viewer to check).
- Tense shifts: habits = present simple; "yesterday" = past; "tomorrow" = future.
- Comma misuse: after introductory phrase ("After going to the dentist,"); before a quote ("The dentist said, "..."); before and/but joining two independent clauses.
- Wrong word/collocation: **buy time** (delay), not purchase time; make/give/do a presentation (make = create, give = deliver); **effect** (noun) vs **affect** (verb).
- Formal email ("Dear Sir or Madam"): drop idioms (in the nick of time, piece of cake) and reductions (gonna -> going to); "in a timely manner" is fine.

## 3. 1-HOUR LESSON - Learn Over 100 English Phrasal Verbs

**Listening**
- **listen up** – listen carefully (to get attention) – "Everybody, listen up."
- **listen in (on)** – secretly listen to a conversation – "They're listening in on our call."
- **hear about** – receive information about something/someone – "Did you hear about the comments?"
- **hear from** – (1) get a call/message from someone; (2) listen to someone speak on a topic; (3) angry warning – "You'll hear from my lawyer."
- **hear someone out** – listen to them finish without interrupting – "Hear me out."
- **hear of** – receive news of / know of – "I haven't heard of anyone."; "I won't hear of it" (refuse)
- **pick up** (listening) – detect a sound – "Are you picking anything up?"
- **tune in** – listen to/watch a show or broadcast – "Tune in next week."
- **tune out** – stop paying attention to – "I just tune her out."
- (expression) **hang on to someone's every word** – listen very carefully because it's captivating

**Fall**
- **fall apart** – break into pieces / stop working well – "This table is falling apart."
- **fall behind** – fail to do something fast enough/on time; fall below a level – "I'm falling behind with my work."
- **fall through** – plans don't happen – "My weekend plans fell through."
- **fall back on** – use as support (esp. money) when all else fails – "I can fall back on my savings."
- **fall down** – fall to the ground/floor – "I tripped and fell down." (**fall over** = tip onto its side)
- **fall off** – (1) detach – "The painting fell off the wall."; (2) rate/quality drops – "Sales are falling off."
- **fall out** – drop out of; come loose – "His hair is falling out."
- **fall for** – (1) develop romantic feelings; (2) be tricked – "I fell for the oldest trick in the book."
- **fall into** – (1) go into (pool, coma); idioms: **fall into place** (things work out), **fall into your lap** (unexpected good opportunity)

**Positive**
- **believe in** – feel confident about someone – "I believe in you."
- **brush up on** – improve a skill/knowledge you've forgotten – "brush up on your history"
- **get along** – be friendly with each other
- **get over** – return to normal after illness/bad experience – "get over a breakup"
- **get through** – survive/endure until the end – "You're going to get through this."
- **go beyond** – do more than expected (go above and beyond)
- **goof off / goof around** – avoid work, waste time
- **help (someone) out** – do work/give money for someone
- **keep at (it)** – continue working, encouragement – "Keep at it!"
- **keep up** – maintain effort – "Keep it up!"
- **light up** – look happy (face lights up; light up the room)
- **look forward to** – be excited about the future – "I look forward to your call."
- **look up** – become better – "Things are looking up."
- **pay off** – result in success – "Hard work pays off."
- **pull through** – recover from serious illness
- **settle down** – become established in a place, have a family
- **sort out** – deal with a problem successfully
- **straighten up** – behave well after behaving poorly
- **work through** – manage a complicated problem with several parts
- **work up** – make progress / work your way up the corporate ladder

**Shopping**
- **sell out (of)** – no longer available – "They sold out of eggs."
- **try out** – test (clothes/shoes) before buying
- **pick up** – buy/grab quickly from the store – "pick up some stuff on the way home"
- **pick out** – choose – "help me pick out what to wear"
- **mark down** – reduce the price – "marked down 50%"
- **look around** – browse without a plan – "I'm just looking around."
- **shop around** – compare prices in several stores
- **shell out** – pay a lot of money at once – "shelled out $5,000"
- **blow (money) on** – spend wastefully – "blow $30 on a gym membership"
- **take back** – return to the store

**Study**
- **think through** – carefully consider outcomes – "Think it through."
- **pick up** – learn (language, instrument) – "pick up Romanian"
- **cheat on** – act unfairly in a test/exam – "cheat on a test"
- **read up on** – read to learn more about a topic
- **go through** – examine carefully – "go through a report for mistakes"
- **hand in / turn in** – submit homework/essay
- **fall behind** – not up to date compared with others
- **drop out (of)** – leave school/program without finishing
- **hand out** – distribute – "hand out a quiz"
- **catch on** – understand/learn how to do something

**Sickness**
- **come down with** – start to become ill, usually not serious – "coming down with a cold"
- **burn up** – be very hot from fever – "You're burning up."
- **throw up** – vomit
- **cough up** – cough out liquid/mucus/blood
- **puff up** – swell (eyes, cheeks, allergies)
- **flare up** – a condition returns/worsens – "My symptoms are flaring up."
- **break out** – (skin) start suddenly with a rash/spots
- **fight off** – resist an illness – "fight off a cold"
- **get over** – recover from illness
- **pass out** – lose consciousness
- **come to / come around / come round** – regain consciousness
- **put (someone) on** – prescribe treatment – "The doctor put me on a new medication."

**Speaking**
- **talk to / talk with** – speak directly to someone (with implies another person involved)
- **talk someone up** – speak enthusiastically about them – "I was talking you up."
- **talk back (to)** – reply rudely – "Don't talk back to your dad."
- **speak up** – speak louder – "Could you speak up?"
- **speak up for** – defend someone
- **speak for** – represent someone's opinion – "I speak for everyone when I say..."
- **shut up** – (1) tell someone angrily to be quiet; (2) express disbelief – "Shut up! Really?"

**Computer**
- **log in / sign in (to)** – access a computer/site with name and password
- **click on** – select with the mouse
- **scroll up / scroll down** – move to the top/bottom; **scroll through** – go up and down looking for info
- **back up** – keep an extra copy of data
- **hack into** – illegally access a computer/network
- **run out of** – supply decreases – "running out of space"
- **free up** – make space/time available
- **pop up** – suddenly appear (noun: popup)
- **opt in / opt out** – choose to receive / stop receiving messages
- **filter out** – remove unwanted emails
- **turn off / shut down / power off** – end a session (turn off most common)
- **plug in** – connect a power cable to a socket
- **print out** – make a hard copy
- **set up** – install a program / get a computer ready
- **keep up with** – stay at the same level as changes – "hard to keep up with technology"

## 4. 1-HOUR PRONUNCIATION LESSON | 100+ Words to Fine-tune Your Pronunciation

**Commonly mispronounced (American)**
- **subtle** /ˈsʌtl/ – silent b; flap t
- **segue** /ˈseɡweɪ/ – smooth transition; 2nd syllable "way"
- **epitome** /ɪˈpɪtəmi/ – perfect example; stress 2nd; ends "-me" (not "-tome")
- **candidate** /ˈkændɪdət/ – stress 1st; keep the d; last syllable "-dit"
- **literally** /ˈlɪtərəli/ – 4 syllables, stress 1st; flap t
- **meme** /miːm/ – long e, one syllable (not "Mimi")
- **debut** /deɪˈbjuː/ – stress 2nd; t silent
- **albeit** /ɔːlˈbiːɪt/ – "all-BEE-it"; means although
- **resume** (noun, CV) /ˈrezəmeɪ/ stress 1st vs **resume** (verb) /rɪˈzuːm/ stress 2nd
- **kibosh** /ˈkaɪbɑːʃ/ – stress 1st; "put the kibosh on" = put an end to (informal)

**Food words**
- **zucchini** /zuˈkiːni/ – stress 2nd; cch = k
- **salmon** /ˈsæmən/ – silent l
- **caramel** – "CAR-uh-mel", "CAR-uh-mul", or two-syllable "CAR-mul"
- **quinoa** /ˈkiːnwɑː/ (or /kiˈnoʊə/)
- **daiquiri** /ˈdækəri/ – stress 1st; qui = k
- **guacamole** /ˌɡwɑːkəˈmoʊli/ – stress 3rd; final "e" pronounced
- **parmesan** /ˈpɑːrməzɑːn/ – s = z
- **artichoke** /ˈɑːrtɪtʃoʊk/ – stress 1st; soft r
- **cinnamon** /ˈsɪnəmən/ – c = s
- **filet mignon** /fɪˈleɪ miːnˈjɑːn/ – French; stress 2nd of each word

**Common words**
- **exhausted** /ɪɡˈzɔːstɪd/ – x = gz; silent h
- **brewery** /ˈbruːəri/ – soft r's, schwa
- **Antarctica** /ænˈtɑːrktɪkə/ – stress 2nd (ant-ARC-tica)
- **phenomenon** /fəˈnɑːmənɑːn/ – ph = f; stress 2nd
- **specific** /spəˈsɪfɪk/ – c = s; short i
- **temperature** /ˈtemprətʃər/ – 3 syllables; tu = ch
- **rural** /ˈrʊrəl/ – two r's, quick schwa
- **choir** /ˈkwaɪər/ – ch = k; "kwire"
- **literature** /ˈlɪtərətʃər/ – tu = ch
- **squirrel** /ˈskwɜːrəl/ – two syllables, schwa

**Brand names (American pronunciation)**
- **IKEA** – "eye-KEE-uh" (Swedish: ih-KAY-uh)
- **Nutella** – "nuh-TEL-uh" (Americans)
- **Coke** – long o (short o is slang for cocaine)
- **Tommy Hilfiger** – "HIL-fig-er"
- **Hyundai** – "HUN-day" (Korean), Americans say "HY-un-day"
- **Nike** – "NY-kee" (Greek goddess), not "Nike" as one syllable
- **Adidas** – "ah-DEE-das" (Adi + Das; Adolf Dassler)
- **Sephora** – ph = f; "seh-FOR-uh"
- **Porsche** – "POR-shuh" (two syllables)
- **Louis Vuitton** – "LOO-ee vwee-TAWN" (many say "LOO-ee vuh-TAHN")
- **Samsung** – "SAHM-sung" (Americans: "SAM-sung")
- **Versace** – "ver-SAH-chay" (Italian); English speakers say "ver-SAH-chee"
- **Chevrolet** – "shev-ruh-LAY", t silent; Chevy
- **Stella Artois** – "STEL-uh ar-TWAH"
- **Adobe** – "uh-DOH-bee"
- **Abercrombie & Fitch** – "AB-er-crom-bee and FITCH", not "Finch"
- **Volkswagen** – "VOLKS-wah-gen" (English speakers use /v/)
- **Anthropologie** – "an-thruh-puh-LOH-jee" (not "anthropology")
- **Huawei** – "HWAH-way"
- **Levi's** – "LEE-vyz"
- **Budweiser** – "BUD-wy-zer"
- **H&M** – "aitch and em"
- **Jack Daniel's** – "Jack DAN-yulz"; "on the rocks" = served over ice
- **Nestlé** – "NES-ul" (Americans), silent t
- **Corona** – "kuh-ROH-nuh"

**US states** (stressed syllable in caps)
- Alabama a-la-BAM-a; Alaska a-LAS-ka; Arizona ar-i-ZO-na; Arkansas AR-kan-saw; California cal-i-FOR-nia; Colorado col-o-RAD-o; Connecticut con-NET-i-cut; Delaware DEL-a-ware; Florida FLOR-i-da; Georgia GEOR-gia; Hawaii ha-WAI-i; Idaho I-da-ho; Illinois il-i-NOI; Indiana in-di-AN-a; Iowa I-o-wa; Kansas KAN-zas; Kentucky ken-TUCK-y; Louisiana lou-ee-zee-AN-a (5 syll.); Maine (1 syll.); Maryland MARE-i-land; Massachusetts mass-a-CHU-sets; Michigan MICH-i-gan; Minnesota min-ne-SO-ta; Mississippi miss-i-SIP-pi; Missouri mi-ZUR-i; Montana mon-TAN-a; Nebraska ne-BRAS-ka; Nevada ne-VAD-a; New HAMP-shur (not "-shire"); New JER-sey; New MEX-i-co; New YORK; North Car-o-LI-na; North Da-KO-ta; Ohio o-HI-o; Oklahoma o-kla-HO-ma; Oregon OR-e-gun; Pennsylvania penn-syl-VANE-ia; Rhode ISL-and (Rode); South Car-o-LI-na; South Da-KO-ta; Tennessee ten-ne-SEE; Texas TEX-as; Utah YOU-tah; Vermont ver-MONT; Virginia vir-GIN-ia; Washington WASH-ing-ton; West Vir-GIN-ia; Wisconsin wis-CON-sin; Wyoming wy-O-ming

## 5. 1-HOUR VOCABULARY LESSON - Descriptive Words & Phrases to be More Accurate & Precise

Adjectives for places (landscape)
- **serene** (adj) – calm, peaceful, undisturbed – "the landscape appeared serene"
- **stunning** (adj) – extremely beautiful – "stunning views" (collocates with view)
- **picturesque** (adj) – visually attractive, like a picture (-esque = resembling) – "the view was picturesque"
- **desolate** (adj) – empty and unattractive (negative) – "the forest looked desolate after the fire"
- **unspoiled** (adj) – beautiful because not damaged by people/development – "the rainforest is unspoiled"
- **pristine** (adj) – in very good condition, well-kept – "pristine condition"

Atmosphere
- **tranquil** (adj) – peaceful and calm, no noise (serene = looks; tranquil = feeling)
- **vibrant** (adj) – exciting, energetic – "a vibrant festival"
- **homely** (adj) – comfortable and cozy (used for a hotel/room, not your own home)
- **bustling** (adj) – very active and busy – "bustling with tourists"

Cities / towns
- **renowned** (adj) – famous (renowned for) – "Italy is renowned for pizza"
- **cosmopolitan** (adj) – including people from many countries – "Hong Kong is cosmopolitan"
- **walkable** (adj) – easy/safe to walk in – "easily walkable"
- **overcrowded** (adj) – too many people/things (stronger than crowded)
- **spacious** (adj) – large with lots of open space – "Central Park is spacious"
- **charming** (adj) – pleasant, attractive – "a charming little street"
- **quaint** (adj) – attractive because old-fashioned – "quaint little cafes"

Buildings / landmarks
- **iconic** (adj) – very famous, a symbol of something – "an iconic landmark"
- **contemporary** (adj) – belonging to the present (architecture) – "contemporary architecture"
- **majestic** (adj) – beautiful, powerful, admired – "the Golden Gate Bridge is majestic"
- **gaudy** (adj) – unpleasantly bright/showy – "gaudy casinos"
- **dilapidated** (adj) – old and in poor condition, falling apart

Location
- **out of the way** (adj phr) – far from where people usually go – "an out of the way restaurant"
- **off the beaten path / track** (idiom) – not known or popular (positive) – "hiking off the beaten path"
- **godforsaken** (adj) – unattractive, nothing pleasant – "a godforsaken place"

Verbs of movement
- Walking/running: **strut** – walk confidently/arrogantly; **stagger** – walk with great difficulty, nearly falling (e.g., drunk); **bolt** – move very fast, almost run – "I bolted to the airplane"; **slip** (by/past/inside/outside) – move quietly so as not to be noticed; **drag one's feet** (idiom) – move/act reluctantly and slowly, procrastinate – "stop dragging your feet"
- Driving/flying: **hitchhike** – get a free ride; **carpool** – travel by car in a small group to save money/environment; **leapfrog** – take turns passing each other; **swerve** – suddenly change direction; **soar** – fly high
- Animals: **glide** – smooth continuous motion (bird, swan); **slither** – slide and twist (snake); **wobble** – sway side to side with poor balance (penguin); **gallop** – run with all four feet off the ground (horse); **wiggle** – small quick movements side to side (puppy)
- Situations: **approach** – get nearer; deal with a problem – "How should we approach this issue?"; **circumvent** – avoid, usually cleverly – "circumvent the tax law"; **smuggle** – move secretly, often illegally; **lag (behind)** – make slow progress, behind others; **spiral (out of control)** – get worse and harder to control

Nouns for people
- Profession: **amateur** – does it for fun, not a professional; **tutor** – helps a student outside school; **scholar** – studies a subject in great detail; **big fish / big wig** – powerful, important person at a company; **one's right hand (man)** – person who supports you at work; **valedictorian** – student with the highest grades, gives the graduation speech
- Personality: **dilettante** – interested in a subject but no deep understanding; **aficionado** – very enthusiastic fan/expert – "soccer aficionado"; **confidant** – person you trust with secrets; **proponent** (of) – publicly supports a plan/cause; **guru** – wise spiritual leader/teacher; **gourmet** – knows a lot about (fancy) food; **humanitarian** – helps people who are suffering
- Characteristics: **coward** – easily frightened, not brave; **introvert** – quiet, reserved, likes being alone; **extrovert** – very social, talkative; **perfectionist** – thinks everything must be perfect; **purist** – believes in doing things the traditional way; **whiz** – highly intelligent in an area – "math whiz"

Adjectives for people
- **overzealous** – too eager/enthusiastic – "overzealous fans ran onto the stage"
- **tenacious** – refuses to give up (usually positive) – "a tenacious effort"
- **candid** – truthful, honest – "a candid response"
- **facetious** (/fəˈsiːʃəs/) – joking when perhaps not appropriate – "I'm being facetious"
- **obtuse** – slow to understand; refuses to accept the obvious
- **boorish** – rude, insensitive – "boorish behavior" (unrelated to boring)
- **jittery** – nervous, unable to relax – "too jittery to stay seated"
- **capricious** (/kəˈprɪʃəs/) – impulsive, unpredictable, changes mind – "capricious weather"
- **gullible** – easily persuaded to believe anything – "I fell for the scam, I was gullible"
- **adamant** (about) – refuses to change mind – "adamant about protecting the environment"
- **feckless** – weak, ineffective, lacking character – "feckless leadership"
- **charismatic** – influential, easily gets attention (not necessarily good)
- **chatty** – likes to talk a lot, friendly/informal
- **surly** – unfriendly, bad mood
- **level-headed** – calm, handles difficult situations
- **easygoing** – relaxed, not easily upset
- **absent-minded** – forgetful, not paying attention
- **above average / below average** – higher/lower than usual level – "a below average tennis player"
- **grumpy** – easily annoyed, complains
- Note: attributive (before noun) vs predicate adjective (after linking verb)

Nouns/phrases for people
- **grump** – unfriendly person who complains ("grumpy grump")
- **jack of all trades** – capable of doing many different jobs well
- **dark horse** – unexpected winner/candidate
- **black sheep** – embarrassing/different member of a family or group
- **know-it-all** – thinks they know everything (negative)
- **wise guy** – annoyingly clever person
- **stick-in-the-mud** – old-fashioned, avoids fun
- **yes-man** – always agrees to please a superior
- **sitter** – babysitter/house sitter/pet sitter/dog sitter/cat sitter

## 6. 1-HOUR VOCABULARY LESSON | 100 Phrases for Casual Conversation

15 collocations
- **break a promise** – fail to do what you promised
- **a lasting impression** – effect/memory that stays a long time – "left a lasting impression on me"
- **a heavy workload** (not "big") – a large amount of work
- **a light workload** – a small amount of work
- **take initiative** – be first to act to reach a goal/solve a problem
- **gain momentum** – progress at an increasing pace
- **a profound impact** – very significant, deep effect
- **meet standards** – reach the expected quality/requirements
- **an overwhelming majority** – very large proportion (about 75%+)
- **a frank discussion** – open, honest, straightforward
- **take responsibility** – accept responsibility – "I take full responsibility for the delay"
- **grant approval** – officially agree/allow
- **a mutual agreement** – one all people involved accept
- **a positive influence** – influence that leads to improvements
- **a competitive advantage** – something that lets you outperform others

15 make / do / have phrases
- **make the most of** – take full advantage of an opportunity that won't last
- **make do with** – use what is available though not enough/not what you wanted
- **make amends** – do something to show you're sorry for past bad behavior
- **make a point of** (+ -ing) – emphasize/ensure you do it – "I make a point of calling my parents weekly"
- **make peace with** – someone: stop fighting; something: accept what bothered you
- **do a number on** – harm someone/something (physically or psychologically)
- **do one's part** – do what you're responsible for/able to do
- **do your own thing** – do what you want regardless of others' opinions
- **do your best** – make the greatest effort possible
- **do your worst** – do the most harmful thing you can (challenge)
- **have a one-track mind** – think about one thing only
- **have an in with** – have a connection with someone in authority – "I have an in with the manager"
- **have mixed feelings** – pleased and not pleased at once
- **have the presence of mind (to)** – react quickly and calmly in an emergency
- **have your hands tied** – unable to act freely because of rules/law – "sorry, my hands are tied"

15 phrasal verbs
- **pull off** – succeed in something difficult – "I know you'll pull it off"
- **take off** – become successful/popular – "my channel took off"
- **put off** – delay/postpone – "put off the meeting"
- **turn around** – make a failing business/skill successful
- **call off** – cancel (vs put off = delay)
- **catch up on** – do what you haven't had time to do – "catch up on emails"
- **blurt out** – say suddenly without thinking (have a big mouth)
- **comb through** (b silent) – review thoroughly and completely
- **skim through** – review quickly without detail
- **gloss over** – mention briefly, avoid discussing in detail
- **pan out** – have a successful outcome – "the strategy didn't pan out"
- **drift off** – gradually lose focus or fall asleep unintentionally
- **dip into** – spend part of savings/reserve – "dip into our emergency fund"
- **pick up** – learn informally through exposure – "picked up coding over the years"
- **shake off** – get rid of a problem/mistake and move on

50 adjective + noun collocations
- **high**: high school, high ground, high-tech, high standards, a high degree of certainty
- **big**: big mistake, big surprise, big deal, big decision, big money
- **little**: little boy/girl, little thought, a little bit, little time
- **heavy**: heavy drinker/smoker, heavy traffic, heavy rain, heavy sleeper
- **light**: light drinker/smoker, light sleeper, light rain, light breeze, light shade (of a color)
- **strong**: strong argument, strong feeling, strong desire, strong wind/breeze, strong evidence, strong hand (position of power), strong relationship
- **deep**: deep thought, deep voice, deep sleep, deep breath, deep depression, deep down (inside)
- **fast**: fast start, fast talker, fast asleep, fast pace, fast food, fast learner
- **quick**: quick glance, quick bite (to eat), quick shower, quick recap, quick decision
- **slow**: slow growth, slow progress, slow motion, slow day (not busy), slow learner

## 7. 100 Collocations Native Speakers Use in Academic Discussions

- **range** (n) – variety of things – a wide / broad / diverse / limited / full range of
- **vary** (v) – differ in size/amount/degree – vary considerably / significantly / widely; vary depending on; vary among
- **implement** (v) – put a plan into action – implement a plan / strategy / policy; implement effectively / quickly
- **subsequent** (adj) – happening after – subsequent research / analysis / event / discussion / stage
- **rely** (v) – depend on with trust – rely heavily / primarily / increasingly / excessively / solely on
- **undertake** (v) – begin a long or difficult task – undertake research / a project / a study / a task / responsibility
- **research** (n) – detailed study to discover info – conduct research; groundbreaking / extensive / empirical (based on verifiable experience) / qualitative (interviews, observations, no numbers) research
- **benefit** (n) – helpful or good effect – mutual / long-term / potential / economic / health benefit
- **approach** (n) – way of dealing with a problem – adopt an approach; innovative / theoretical / alternative / systematic approach
- **outcome** (n) – how something turns out – desired / positive / negative / eventual / adverse (harmful) / binary (two-valued) outcome
- **assess** (v) – evaluate nature/quality – assess performance / effectiveness / impact / progress / needs
- **source** (n) – where something originates – primary / secondary / original / reliable / anonymous source
- **significant** (adj) – large enough to matter – significant difference / impact / factor / change; statistically significant
- **justify** (v) – show to be reasonable – justify a decision / action / behavior / conclusion / expenditure
- **indicate** (v) – show, point out – indicate a trend / relationship / need; studies (clearly) indicate
- **policy** (n) – agreed plan of action – public / economic / foreign policy; implement / enforce a policy
- **volume** (n) – amount of something / space it occupies – total / sales / high volume; increase / decrease the volume
- **assume** (v) – take on responsibility/control – assume responsibility / control / leadership / a role / a position
- **require** (v) – need – require approval / attention / clarification / analysis / resources
- **evidence** (n) – facts showing something is true – provide / evaluate evidence; strong / ample (= sufficient) / empirical evidence

## 8. 100 Most Common Words in English | YOU NEED TO KNOW THESE WORDS

Gist: Top 100 words from the Oxford English Corpus (OEC = large collection of real written/spoken English). Check the ones you cannot use confidently, e.g., then vs than, their vs there vs they're, its vs it's. Top 10 lemmas (word families) cover about 25% of all words used.
- Top 10: the, be, to, of, and, a, in, that, have, I
- 11-20: it, for, not, on, with, he, as, you, do, at
- 21-30: this, but, his, by, from, they, we, say, her, she
- 31-40: or, an, will, my, one, would, there, their, what, so
- 41-50: up, out, if, about, who, get, which, go, me, when
- 51-60: make, can, like, time, no, just, him, know, take, people
- 61-70: into, year, your, good, some, could, them, see, other, than
- 71-80: then, now, look, only, come, its, over, think, also, back
- 81-100 (as heard): after, use, two, how, our, work, first, well, way, even, new, want, because, any, these, give, day, most, us
- **lemma** – root word plus its family/conjugations (be = am, is, are, was, were, been)
- **crutch / filler words** – words that add no value, used to buy thinking time (um, like, actually); avoid them to improve speaking
- Facts: most common letter = e; longest word = pneumonoultramicroscopicsilicovolcanoconiosis (lung disease from inhaling fine particles); about 4,000 words added to the dictionary yearly; **pangram** = sentence with every letter ("The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"); shortest complete sentence: "I am" / "I do"; about 1.5 billion English speakers (under 400 million native); English is official in 67 countries; word with most meanings = **set** (464 in OED)

## 9. 10 Adjectives to Help You Sound Like a SUPER GENIUS 🤓 | Advanced Vocabulary

(Adjectives for how people speak/write)
- **hyperbolic** (adj) – exaggerated, making something sound better than it is (noun: hyperbole) – "a hyperbolic sales pitch"
- **flippant** (adj) – not serious about a topic, trying to be funny/clever – "a flippant remark"
- **articulate** (adj, /-lət/) – able to express ideas clearly and effectively; verb "to articulate" ends /-leɪt/
- **prescient** (adj) – knowing/suggesting what will happen in future – "you were prescient, it came true"
- **emphatic** (adj) – making something very clear because of strong feelings – "an emphatic victory"; "emphatic about..."
- **glib** (adj) – speaking confidently but carelessly, maybe not honest – "a glib speaker" (negative)
- **unintelligible** (adj) – cannot be understood – "unintelligible handwriting/announcement"
- **rhetorical** (adj) – meant to influence/seem important – "rhetorical question" (asked for effect, not answered)
- **defamatory** (adj) – false statements that damage a reputation
- **verbose** (adj) – using more words than needed

## 19. 10 Difficult Words to Pronounce in English

- **applicable** – both /ˈæplɪkəbl/ and /əˈplɪkəbl/ accepted
- **pronunciation** – 5 syllables, stress before -tion (nun-ci-A-tion); -tion = "shun"; not "pro-NOUNce"
- **lettuce** /ˈletɪs/ – c = s; US flap t
- **suggestion** /səˈdʒestʃən/ – stress 2nd syllable; g+e = "j" sound
- **asterisk** /ˈæstərɪsk/ – 3 syllables (don't drop the schwa)
- **usually** /ˈjuːʒuəli/ – "zh" sound (voiced), 3 or 4 syllables
- **encyclopedia** – 6 syllables, ends with schwa
- **poignant** /ˈpɔɪnjənt/ – silent g, "-yunt"; strong, emotionally moving (French origin)
- **lingerie** /ˌlɑːndʒəˈreɪ/ – French-style pronunciation; stress last syllable
- **literature** – 3 or 4 syllables (4 more accepted); -ture = "chur"
- Rule: in words ending -tion, stress the syllable before it

## 20. 10 Food Words that Are Difficult to Pronounce in English

- **zucchini** /zuˈkiːni/ – 3 syllables, stress on 2nd; "cchi" = "kee"
- **salmon** /ˈsæmən/ – 2 syllables, stress on 1st; silent "l"
- **caramel** /ˈkærəməl/ or /ˈkærəmel/ or /ˈkɑːrməl/ – 3 syllables, stress on 1st; common 2-syllable US variant "car-mel"
- **quinoa** /ˈkiːnwɑː/ or /kiˈnoʊə/ – stress on 1st (or on 2nd in 3-syllable version); Spanish/Andean word
- **daiquiri** /ˈdaɪkəri/ – 3 syllables, stress on 1st; "qui" = "k"; rum cocktail
- **guacamole** /ˌɡwɑːkəˈmoʊli/ – 4 syllables, stress on 3rd (Spanish: gwa-ka-MO-le)
- **Parmesan** /ˈpɑːrməʒɑːn/ (or stress on 3rd) – 3 syllables; "s" sounds like "z"
- **artichoke** /ˈɑːrtɪtʃoʊk/ – 3 syllables, stress on 1st; soft "r"
- **cinnamon** /ˈsɪnəmən/ – 3 syllables, stress on 1st; "c" = "s", then schwa
- **filet mignon** /fɪˈleɪ mɪnˈjɒn/ – French; stress on 2nd syllable of each word

## 22. 10 Useful Tips to Help You Become an Excellent Writer ✍️

- Writing is hard because it's learned last, is rule-based, and complements speaking (write out answers before speaking to prepare).
- 1. Avoid reductions in writing (gonna, wanna) – unprofessional in formal/business writing.
- 2. Be mindful of punctuation – it controls rhythm/meaning: "Let's eat grandma" vs "Let's eat, grandma."
- 3. Use transition words (furthermore, in addition, also; first, next, then) to connect/separate ideas.
- 4. Cut nonspecific words (thing, something, "very"); use a precise word instead – "very fast" -> rapid / unbelievably fast.
- 5. Numbers: spell out numbers written in one or two words (MLA); use numerals for big awkward ones (151); always spell out a number starting a sentence; stay consistent.
- 6. Avoid redundancy – don't repeat the same words/information; use synonyms.
- 7. Read a lot, for longer sessions (new words, sentence structures, punctuation in context).
- 8. Vary sentence structures: simple -> compound -> complex as you advance; keeps reader engaged.
- 9. Big words don't make writing better; don't swap in unfamiliar thesaurus words (unhappy != despondent). Input hypothesis "i+1": read/write one step above your level.
- 10. Proofread – re-read for mistakes; reveals your recurring errors.

- **aficionado** (n, Spanish) – devoted, knowledgeable fan – "a wine aficionado"

## 27. 12 English Words that People Say WRONG 😳  | Pronunciation Lesson

- **answer** /ˈænsər/ – silent "w"
- **debt** /det/ – silent "b"
- **receipt** /rɪˈsiːt/ – silent "p"
- **recipe** /ˈresəpi/ – stress on 1st syllable (not "ree-seep")
- **comfortable** /ˈkʌmftərbəl/ – "kumf-tuh-buhl"
- **hierarchy** /ˈhaɪərɑːrki/ – "hy-uh-rar-kee"
- **suite** /swiːt/ – a room (not "suit")
- **colonel** /ˈkɜːrnəl/ – sounds like "kernel"
- **Wednesday** /ˈwenzdeɪ/ – silent "d"
- **salmon** /ˈsæmən/ – silent "l"
- **glamour** /ˈɡlæmər/ – "glam-er" (not "gluh-more")
- **chocolate** /ˈtʃɔːklɪt/ – "chawk-lit"; middle "o" silent

## 28. 12 GOOD Words to IMPROVE YOUR VOCABULARY

- **valuable** (adj) – of great worth/importance
- **excellent** (adj) – extremely good, superior quality
- **exceptional** (adj) – unusually good
- **outstanding** (adj) – remarkably good
- **superb** (adj) – impressively good/beautiful
- **divine** (adj) – godly; out-of-this-world good
- **brilliant** (adj) – shining; an idea/performance that outshines others (more British)
- **impressive** (adj) – blows your mind, makes a strong impression
- **admirable** (adj) – worthy of respect/admiration
- **flawless** (adj) – no defects or faults
- **wonderful** (adj) – much better than good

## 29. 12 Powerful Phrases to Talk About Personality Without Sounding Boring!

- **a stickler for the rules** – person who follows rules strictly – "My boss is a real stickler for the rules."
- **go out of one's way (to do)** – try very hard to do something helpful – "She went out of her way to help me."
- **talk out of both sides of one's mouth** – give opposite opinions in different situations (negative; politicians) – "He was talking out of both sides of his mouth."
- **have a good head on one's shoulders** – make good decisions, good judgment (compliment) – "She has a good head on her shoulders."
- **exude an air of (confidence/mystery/superiority)** – show a quality to a great extent
- **know-it-all** (n) – person who thinks they know more than others (negative) – "Don't be such a know-it-all."
- **neat freak** (n) – person who likes everything very tidy/clean – "I'm kind of a neat freak."
- **the life of the party** – lively, fun person at social events
- **have an inquisitive mind** – eager for knowledge, intellectually curious (inquisitive: 4 syllables, stress on 2nd)
- **... to a fault** – excessively so (too much of a good thing) – "honest/generous/loyal to a fault"
- **hear oneself talk/speak** – be self-absorbed in one's own speech, ignoring others (negative) – "He likes hearing himself talk."
- **run in the family** – trait/skill/illness shared by family members – "Talent runs in the family."
- Pronunciation: linking (have a, head on, runs in), flap T (out of, party), "for" weak form /fər/, W-intrusion in "to a fault".

## 30. 13 Scary Words  👻   | Useful Vocabulary Lesson

- **fearful** (adj) – showing/feeling fear or anxiety
- **panicked** (adj) – overwhelmed by sudden terror/anxiety; "mass panic"
- **startled** (adj) – shocked/alarmed, usually mildly – "You startled me."
- **petrified** (adj) – so scared you can't move or react (also: turned to stone)
- **terrified** (adj) – extremely afraid; "terrified of clowns"
- **aghast** (adj) – filled with horror and shock
- **anxious** (adj) – worried/uneasy about unknown outcome (not exactly "scared")
- **rattled** (adj) – nervous and unable to focus after an event
- **intimidated** (adj) – frightened/nervous, losing confidence
- **frightened** (adj) – feeling fear or worry; good synonym of scared
- **spooked** (adj, informal) – frightened as if by a ghost
- **unnerving** (adj) – makes you feel uncomfortable/weird; deprives of courage
- **chilling** (adj) – frightening so much you feel a physical shiver

- **draw a blank** – can't think of an answer/remember

## 36. 17 Super Useful Ways to Become FLUENT FAST 🚀

Idea: build an "English bubble" – surround yourself with English.
- 1. Read every day (even short articles). 2. Take online classes. 3. Listen to podcasts (train listening, no images).
- 4. Write down new words/phrases and review later. 5. Focus on language suited to your job/interests.
- 6. Study other subjects in English (content-integrated learning). 7. Watch more lessons. 8. Find a study partner (accountability, motivation).
- 9. Think in English during idle moments. 10. Identify and work on your weaknesses.
- 11. Develop a routine/schedule. 12. Use subtitles to match words with pronunciation. 13. Practice writing daily, even a little.
- 14. Stay at (or slightly above) your level for listening/reading. 15. Be realistic about timelines; don't stress.
- 16. Start conversations online (groups on your interests, in English). 17. Give your brain rest sometimes.

## 37. 17 Writing Tips That Will Help You Write Like a Pro!

- 1. Read your work aloud – catches awkward phrasing (e.g., "inquired for", "gentle enough" -> "kind enough to").
- 2. Proofread multiple times. 3. Read your writing backwards (sentence by sentence) to catch typos/grammar.
- 4. Read widely and consistently (different styles, grammar, vocab, creativity). 5. Write every day.
- 6. Use active voice ("My grandfather built the house") – direct, engaging.
- 7. Vary sentence structure (simple, compound, complex, compound-complex).
- 8. Use strong verbs (trudge, scamper, limp instead of walk).
- 9. Punctuate correctly (period, comma, question mark, capitalization) – "Let's eat, Grandma."
- 10. Eliminate wordiness – "the brother of my friend" -> "my friend's brother".
- 11. Avoid repetition. 12. Avoid jargon/overcomplicated words; don't misuse thesaurus synonyms.
- 13. Avoid clichés (better safe than sorry, think outside the box). 14. Outline before writing (essays/reports).
- 15. Use transitions. 16. Revise (structure, clarity), don't just edit (spelling/grammar). 17. Get feedback from others.

## 38. 1 HOUR LESSON - Advanced Grammar In Use

- **Prepositions of place** – IN: enclosed/within a larger area (in the car/taxi, in a town, in water, in class, in a dictionary/book, in human resources). ON: surface/attached (on the table, on a floor, on the road/river/coast, on a bus/plane/bike, on the left/right, on Netflix/YouTube, on a page/slide). AT: a point/specific location (at the front door, at 44 Main Street, at the mall, at a party, at school/university, at work/home, at the beginning/end). "in/on/at the lake" = in the water / on a boat / at that location.
- **Modals of lost opportunity** (+ past participle; reduced: "should've/coulda/woulda"): **should have** = didn't happen but you wish it did (regret); **could have** = was possible but didn't happen ("could not have" = impossible); **would have** = imagined different result, often 3rd conditional ("would have won if he hadn't been injured"); could = possibility, would = certainty. Idiom "should have, could have, would have" = dismissing someone's regret.
- **used to (verb)** – past habit/state no longer true; no change for person; "did you use to...?", "didn't use to" (written without d in questions/negatives; pronunciation same). **be/get used to + -ing (adj)** – accustomed to ("I'm used to hot weather", "get used to it").
- **Present perfect** (have + past participle) – finished action at unspecified time; focus on result ("I've been to China", "Have you hit the like button?").
- **Present perfect continuous** (have been + -ing) – unfinished action from past continuing now/future; focus on action; "recently/lately". Don't use with non-action verbs (have = possession, know, like).
- "I have studied English" ~ "I have been studying English" (for/since); interchangeable mostly with teach, play, learn, study, work/live; present perfect = more permanent, continuous = more temporary ("I've been working at home since the pandemic").
- **-ed vs -ing adjectives** – -ed = how you feel (bored); -ing = the thing/person's characteristic (boring).
- **Mixed conditional** – If I had taken my medicine this morning, I wouldn't feel sick right now (past condition, present result).
- **Noun clause word order** – "I don't know where I should go" (not "where should I go").
- **Future for scheduled events** – "The movie starts / is starting / will start at 8"; present simple (schedule) most common; present continuous emphasizes arrangement.
- **Subjunctive** – base verb after recommend/suggest: "I recommend that everyone hit the like button"; "I wish I were taller" (informal: was).
- **in spite of + noun/-ing** vs **although + subject + verb** – "In spite of making progress..." / "Although we made progress...".
- **leave for + destination** – "We left for New York City" (not to/from).
- **who vs whom** – who = subject, whom = object ("Whom did you invite?"; spoken English uses "who").
- **Rule of first mention** – first mention a/an, then the ("I bought a gift... I hope she enjoys the gift").

## 39. 1 HOUR LESSON - Useful Idioms & Expressions to Build Your Vocabulary

Fall/autumn idioms
- **turn over a new leaf** – start fresh, change attitude/circumstances – "Starting tomorrow I'm turning over a new leaf."
- **the autumn years** – later years of life
- **drive someone nuts** – make someone crazy – "He's driving me nuts."
- **under the weather** – slightly sick – "I'm a little under the weather."
- **neck of the woods** – a particular area/neighborhood – "What brings you to my neck of the woods?"
- **not out of the woods** – still in danger/not yet safe
- **shake like a leaf** – tremble, usually from fear
- **get wind of something** – hear a rumor/info about it – "Once the press gets wind of this..."
- **the apple of someone's eye** – the person loved most
- **cold turkey** – quit a bad habit suddenly – "I quit smoking cold turkey."

Sports idioms
- **the ball is in your court** (tennis) – it's your decision/responsibility now
- **get off the hook** (fishing) – escape blame/punishment – "You're not getting off the hook that easy."
- **go to bat for someone** (baseball) – defend/support someone
- **give it your best shot** (hunting) – try your hardest
- **the home stretch** (horse racing) – final part, nearly finished
- **not up to par** (golf) – not good enough
- **throw in the towel** (boxing) – give up
- **blindsided** (football) – hit by something unexpected – "blindsided by the news"
- **slam dunk** (basketball) – a sure thing, easy success
- **call the shots** (billiards) – make the decisions

Outdoor idioms
- **beat around the bush** – speak indirectly instead of getting to the point
- **bark up the wrong tree** – make a mistake/pursue the wrong thing
- **go with the flow** – accept the situation, relax
- **nip something in the bud** – stop it early before it grows
- **knock on wood** – hope for good luck
- **living under a rock** – ignorant of well-known news – "Have you been living under a rock?"
- **grassroots** (phrase) – at the basic, ordinary-people level – "a grassroots campaign"

Love idioms
- **fancy someone** (mostly British) – like someone and enjoy their company
- **fall for someone** – develop romantic feelings
- **head over heels (in love)** – completely, deeply in love
- **lovey-dovey** – very affectionate (sometimes too much)
- **be smitten (with)** – strongly attracted
- **get hitched / tie the knot** – get married
- **go through a (bit of a) rough patch** – temporary relationship trouble
- **double date** – two couples go out together
- **on the rocks** – relationship in trouble, likely to fail

Weird idioms
- **pardon my French** – apology for swearing
- **weasel out of something** – dishonestly avoid it
- **hold your horses** – be patient, wait
- **the pot calling the kettle black** – hypocrisy
- **the whole kit and caboodle** (heard "kitten Kaboodle") – everything, the whole lot
- **tongue-in-cheek** – not serious, ironic/joking
- **wet behind the ears** – inexperienced
- **gung-ho** – very eager/enthusiastic
- **mind your p's and q's** – mind your manners
- **a bull in a china shop** – clumsy/careless person
- **bite the dust** – die, fail, come to an end
- **Bob's your uncle** (UK/Ireland) – "and there you have it", it's done

Shopping idioms
- **window shopping** – looking without buying
- **rip-off** (noun/verb) – overpriced; to cheat – "$4 for an avocado is a rip-off."
- **shop till you drop** – shop until exhausted
- **retail therapy** – shopping to feel better
- **bargain hunting** – looking for good deals
- **shoplift** – steal from a store
- **fit like a glove** – fit perfectly
- **shopping spree** – short period of buying a lot
- **pay through the nose** – pay far too much

American idioms/expressions
- **a piece of cake** – very easy
- **run late / run behind** – be late – "I'm running a little late."
- **get the hang of it** – learn how to do something
- **all set / good to go** – ready
- **sound like a broken record** – repeat the same thing over and over
- **pitch in** – help as a group
- **blow off some steam** – release pent-up energy/anger
- **it's not rocket science** – not difficult
- **table something** – postpone discussion
- **jump on the bandwagon** – join a trend because it's popular
- **nosebleed section** – seats very high up in a stadium
- **fall through the cracks** – get overlooked

## 40. 20+ ANIMAL GROUP NAMES | Useful Vocabulary Lesson

Collective nouns (called "terms of venery", from medieval English hunting tradition / Book of St Albans, 1486)
- **flock** – birds (generic: sheep, children, tourists)
- **parade** – elephants
- **murder** – crows
- **flutter** – butterflies
- **pride** – lions
- **colony** – ants, bees, rats, penguins (also **swarm** of bees)
- **raft** – penguins on land/out of water; **waddle** – penguins in water
- **school** – fish
- **parliament** – owls
- **smack** – jellyfish
- **bloat** – hippos
- **pack** – wolves, dogs, mules
- **tower** – giraffes
- **prickle** – porcupines
- **herd** – cows
- **embarrassment** – pandas
- Most useful in daily life: flock, school, pack, pride, herd.

## 43. 225 Useful Collocations You MUST Know | 1-Hour Vocabulary Masterclass

Collocation = words frequently used together (e.g., not "raise your vocabulary").

B2
- **considerable** (adj) – a considerable amount of time / difference / progress / influence over
- **feedback** (n) – give/provide/offer feedback; get/receive feedback (not "tell feedback"); positive/negative feedback
- **deeply** (adv) – deeply worried/concerned about; deeply flawed; deeply sorry; deeply moved by; deeply in love
- **controversial** (adj) – a controversial issue/decision/figure; highly/extremely controversial
- **wisdom** (n) – conventional wisdom (long-held common opinion); wisdom tooth/teeth

C1
- **call** (v) – call a meeting / an emergency meeting; call for a strike/election/halt
- **tedious** (adj) – boring: tedious job/work/task
- **bubble over with** (phr v) – bubble over with excitement/enthusiasm/emotion
- **burning** (adj) – very strong: burning desire/ambition; urgent: burning issue/question
- **capture** (v) – capture the imagination/attention/interest (of someone)

C2
- **underlying** (adj) – real but not obvious: underlying cause/condition/principle/motivation
- **framework** (n) – legal/regulatory/theoretical framework
- **expenditure** (n) – government/annual/personal expenditure; cut/reduce expenditure
- **literal** (adj) – literal meaning/interpretation; in a literal sense
- **finite** (adj) – limited: finite resources/number/amount

Alternatives to "very" – adverb + adjective
- **pretty** good/bad/easy/difficult/fast/slow/smart/dumb
- **extremely** important/dangerous/grateful/difficult
- **highly** successful/unlikely/competitive/motivated
- **awfully** tired / awfully nice of you / awfully sorry / awfully crowded
- **truly** thankful/passionate/happy/content/remarkable
- **immensely** talented/popular/useful/profitable

Precise adjectives instead of "very + adj"
- very good → **incredible, amazing, spectacular, phenomenal, breathtaking**
- very bad → **terrible, horrible, lousy, dreadful, atrocious**
- very happy → **cheerful, delighted, elated, ecstatic, jubilant**
- very sad → **down** (informal), **heartbroken** (love), **dejected** (hopeless), **bitter** (sad+angry), **wistful** (thinking of the past)
- very beautiful → **lovely, marvelous, gorgeous, elegant, stunning**
- very tired → **exhausted, drained, beat** (informal), **exasperated** (tired + annoyed), **burnt out** (from work)

Adverb collocations
- **strongly** – condemn, disagree, suggest, recommend, support
- **fully** – aware, awake, operational, upright
- **absolutely / utterly** – alone (utterly more common), amazed, appalled, beautiful (absolutely), convinced, devastated, furious, miserable, ridiculous, stupid
- **deeply** (mostly negative) – concerned, regret, divided, ashamed
- **highly** – unlikely, recommend (= it's the best; stronger-pushing is "strongly recommend"), unusual, controversial, sophisticated, educated, intelligent, paid
- **grossly** (negative) – exaggerated, negligent, inaccurate, mistreated, unfair, reckless, misled, underestimated
- **mildly** – upset, concerned, surprised
- **widely** – available, known, accepted
- **perfectly** – normal, acceptable, willing

Everyday collocations
- **rough** – rough day / draft (unfinished) / estimate (approximation) / neighborhood (dangerous)
- **touch** – a touch of paint/flu (slight amount); finishing touch; a nice touch; have a nice touch (do something well, esp. sports); soft touch (delicate)
- **break** – break a record / a leg (good luck) / a promise / the ice (start conversation) / the law
- **do** – do someone a favor / housework / laundry / the dishes / your best / harm
- **time** – spend time; waste time; kill time (do something unimportant while waiting); pass the time; right on time
- **run out of** – none left (ran out of gas); running out of = almost none left; run out of patience/time/money/coffee
- **money** – waste / invest / donate / save money; set aside money (for a rainy day)

Make vs. Do
- **make** (create/build/produce): coffee, breakfast/lunch/dinner, a cake, soup; made of (material) / made from (ingredient) / made in (place); a plan, a decision, make up your mind; money, a profit, a living; a mistake, a phone call, a sound, a promise, an excuse; "make me smile" (reaction)
- **do** (activity/work): the dishes, exercise, laundry, homework, your job, chores, hair, nails, the assignment, nothing/anything/everything

## 44. 25 Academic English Words You MUST Know | Great for University, IELTS, and TOEFL

(Academic Word List; stress in CAPS)
- **achieve** /əˈtʃiːv/ (v) – accomplish
- **acquisition** /ˌækwɪˈzɪʃən/ (n) – act of gaining possession
- **administration** /ədˌmɪnɪˈstreɪʃən/ (n) – management of a business/office/organization
- **affect** /əˈfekt/ (v) – influence/change someone or something
- **appropriate** (adj, /əˈproʊpriət/) – suitable; (v, /əˈproʊprieɪt/) – set apart/authorize for a purpose
- **authority** /əˈθɔːrəti/ (n) – legal/moral right to control; an accepted reliable source
- **compensation** (n) – money paid for services/loss/damage – "receive compensation"
- **consequences** (n, usually plural) – results, usually negative
- **data** (n) – facts, statistics, information
- **derived** (v) – obtained from a source – "derived from"
- **distinction** (n) – noticing a difference – "a clear distinction"
- **focus** (n) – central point of attention
- **framework** (n) – structure of joined parts; system of rules/ideas – "legal framework"
- **journal** (n) – record of experiences; periodical for a profession
- **legislation** (n) – making laws – "pass legislation"
- **obtained** (v) – got, acquired
- **participation** (n) – taking part in something
- **perceived** (v) – became aware of through the senses
- **perspective** (n) – a way of looking at something – "a unique perspective"
- **previous** (adj) – coming before
- **relevant** (adj) – connected with what's being discussed
- **sought** (v) – past of seek; tried to find/get
- **subsequent** (adj) – coming later – "subsequent to"
- **survey** (n, stress 1st) – examination of opinions by asking people; (v, stress 2nd) – examine carefully
- **text** (n) – main body of a book/manuscript
- Tip: learn the whole word family (cooperate / cooperative / cooperation).

## 45. 25 Academic English Words You Should Know | Great for University, IELTS, or TOEFL

- **arbitrary** (adj) – based on chance/desire rather than reason – "an arbitrary decision/choice/date"
- **inherent** (adj) – existing as a natural, basic part – "an inherent risk/weakness"
- **notwithstanding** (adv/prep) – despite – "Injuries notwithstanding, the team won."
- **norm** (n) – accepted standard of behavior – "social norms"
- **straightforward** (adj) – easy to understand, simple
- **furthermore** (adv) – in addition, more importantly (starts a sentence)
- **enormous** (adj) – extremely large – "enormous impact/difference/amount"
- **precise** (adj) – exact and accurate
- **deviate** (v) – depart from the usual way – "deviate from"
- **explicit** (adj) – clear and exact – "explicit directions/instructions/warning"
- **phenomenon** /fɪˈnɑːmɪnɑːn/ (n; pl. phenomena; stress 2nd syllable) – something observable, esp. unusual – "natural phenomenon"
- **mediate** (v) – help two sides reach agreement – "mediate between"
- **undergo** (v; underwent, undergone) – experience something unpleasant/involving change – "undergo surgery / a transformation"
- **likewise** (adv) – in the same way (informal: "me too")
- **via** (prep) – through/by way of; by means of – "via Route 66", "via satellite"
- **finite** (adj) – having a limit – "finite resources/amount/number"
- **ideology** (n) – set of beliefs, esp. political
- **innovate** (v) – introduce new ideas/changes
- **confer** (v) – exchange ideas to decide – "confer with someone"
- **widespread** (adj) – existing in many places/among many people – "widespread support/corruption/hunger"
- **rigid** (adj) – not permitting change; stiff – "a rigid schedule"
- **reluctant** (adj) – unwilling (reluctant to do; reluctantly; reluctance)
- **justify** (v) – give good reason for – "the ends justify the means"
- **radical** (adj) – extreme change/views – "radical change"
- **whereas** (conj) – contrasts two things – "She works slowly, whereas I rush."

## 46. 25 Academic Words You Need to Know | Perfect for University, IELTS, or TOEFL

- **adequate** (adj) – enough/satisfactory – "an adequate amount of food"
- **analysis** (n) – careful study – "detailed/close analysis"
- **constraints** (n) – limits on what you can do – "time constraints"
- **corresponding** (adj) – similar/connected – "the corresponding period last year"
- **cycle** (n) – events repeating in order – "life cycle"
- **dominant** (adj) – stronger/more noticeable than others
- **exclude** (v) – prevent from joining
- **hence** (adv) – for this reason
- **implication** (n) – future effect of an action – "broad implications"
- **indicate** (v) – show/point out
- **interpretation** (n) – explanation of meaning – "open to interpretation"
- **link** (n) – connection – "a link between exercise and less stress"
- **maintenance** (n) – work to keep something in good condition
- **maximum** (n/adj) – largest allowed/possible
- **minimum** (n/adj) – smallest allowed/possible – "keep spending to a minimum"
- **normal** (adj) – usual, as expected
- **obvious** (adj) – easy to see/understand
- **parameters** (n) – limits that define how something must be done
- **period** (n) – length of time
- **proportion** (n) – part compared to the whole – "a large proportion"
- **reliance** (n) – depending on – "reliance on oil"
- **role** (n) – position/purpose in a situation – "play an important role"
- **sequence** (n) – series of related things/events in order – "a sequence of events"
- **shift** (v/n) – slight change of position/direction
- **variables** (n) – factors that can change

## 47. 25 Academic Words You Should Know for University, IELTS, and TOEFL

- **alternative** (n/adj) – additional/other possible choice
- **assessment** (n) – evaluation
- **benefit** (n) – something advantageous
- **complex** (adj) – many connected parts, hard to understand – "complex problem/issue"
- **concept** (n) – an idea formed in the mind
- **conclusion** (n) – the end/final result – "In conclusion..."
- **conduct** (v, stress 2nd) – organize/lead – "conduct research/business/a meeting" (noun conduct = behavior)
- **consistent** (adj) – not contradictory; applying the same rules to everyone
- **context** (n) – surrounding words/situation that clarify meaning
- **coordination** (n) – parts/people working together
- **environment** (n) – condition of your surroundings
- **estimate** (v) – make an approximate guess
- **factor** (n) – something contributing to a result
- **function** (n) – purpose something is designed for
- **identified** (v) – recognized
- **impact** (n) – strong effect/influence
- **indicate** (v) – point out/show
- **investment** (n) – use of money to gain profit
- **major** (adj) – greater in size/importance
- **method** (n) – planned technique/way of doing something
- **occur** (v) – happen/take place
- **potential** (n/adj) – could possibly happen/develop – "He has potential."
- **primary** (adj) – first in importance (chief, principal)
- **range** (n) – upper and lower limits – "a range of $2000-$4000"
- **significant** (adj) – important, of consequence
- Tip: learn word families (decide / decision / decisive).

## 48. 25 Advanced Adjectives to Describe Places

Landscape
- **serene** – calm, peaceful, undisturbed (look)
- **stunning** – extremely beautiful – "stunning view"
- **picturesque** – visually attractive, like a picture (-esque = resembling)
- **desolate** – empty, unattractive, lifeless
- **unspoiled** – beautiful because not damaged by people/development
- **pristine** – in very good, clean condition

Atmosphere
- **tranquil** – peaceful, calm, quiet (the feeling)
- **vibrant** – exciting, energetic – "vibrant festival"
- **homey** – comfortable, cozy (hotel/room)
- **bustling** – very busy and active – "bustling with tourists"

Cities/towns
- **renowned** (for) – famous for – "Paris is renowned for the Eiffel Tower."
- **cosmopolitan** – with people from many countries
- **walkable** – easy/safe to walk in
- **overcrowded** – too many people
- **spacious** – large with lots of open space
- **charming** – pleasant, attractive
- **quaint** – attractive because old-fashioned

Buildings/landmarks
- **iconic** – very famous, a symbol – "iconic landmark"
- **contemporary** – present-day (architecture)
- **majestic** – beautiful, powerful, admired
- **gaudy** – unpleasantly bright/showy
- **dilapidated** – old and in poor condition

Location
- **out of the way** – far from where people usually go
- **off the beaten path / track** – not well known or popular (positive)
- **godforsaken** – unattractive, nothing pleasant (often venting)

## 49. 25 Grammar Rules on When to Use THE

Use "the" for:
- Something already mentioned (first mention: a/an; second: the) – "a man with a bucket... the bucket"
- Only one of something assumed – "Where's the toilet?"; the park; the mall downtown
- Specific count nouns – "Have a seat on the couch."; "hit the like button"
- Specific non-count nouns – "Pass the salt."; "Is the tap water safe?"
- Unique objects – the sun, the moon, the sky
- Unique people/roles – the boss, the president, the pope
- Superlatives – the smartest, the best, the most beautiful
- Ordinal numbers – "the first time", "the second time"
- Clauses with "only" – "the only day", "the only person"
- Decades – the 1960s, the 80s
- Groups of people (collective) – the French, the British, the elderly, the police, the rich, the poor
- Rivers – the Amazon, the Nile
- Mountain ranges – the Rockies, the Andes, the Alps
- Oceans – the Atlantic, the Pacific
- Deserts – the Sahara, the Mojave
- Points on the globe – the North Pole, the South Pole, the equator
- Island groups – the Bahamas, the Hawaiian Islands, the Galapagos Islands
- Plural-name countries – the Philippines, the Netherlands
- Countries named by state type – the United States, the United Kingdom, the Republic of China
- Newspapers – the New York Times, the Washington Post
- Buildings/structures – the Empire State Building, the Great Wall, the pyramids
- Monuments – the Statue of Liberty, the Statue of David, the Taj Mahal
- Museums – the Louvre, the Metropolitan Museum of Art
- Hotels – the Marriott, the Hilton (some exceptions)
- Family names (plural) – the Potters, the Kardashians
- Tip: don't memorize; exposure through reading/listening builds the instinct.
