- **lifeguard** – keeps swimmers safe at the beach
- **translator / interpreter** – converts one language to another
- **plumber** – fixes pipes, toilets, sinks
- **accountant** – works with money and math for a business
- **veterinarian (vet)** – animal doctor
- **judge** – decides guilty or not guilty in court
- **butcher** – sells/prepares meat
- **janitor / custodian** – cleans a building
- **real estate agent** – helps you buy a home
- **surgeon** – doctor who operates
- **architect** – designs buildings/homes
- **umpire** – enforces rules in baseball (other sports: **referee**)
- **chauffeur** – drives a car for a living
- **florist** – sells flowers
- **dean** – head of a university (or president)
- **paramedic** – works in an ambulance
- **courier** – delivers valuable/important items
- **bellhop / bellboy** – carries luggage at a hotel
- **archaeologist** – digs up ancient artifacts/ruins
- **conductor** – leads an orchestra (**maestro** = famous conductor)
- **foreman** – boss at a construction site
- **mortician / undertaker** – prepares bodies for funerals (undertaker is informal/euphemism)
- **headhunter** – recruits people for high-level positions
- **bar back** – bartender's assistant (clears glasses, restocks, changes kegs)
- **podiatrist** – foot doctor

## 51. 25 Jobs & Occupations | Vocabulary Lesson (Live-Stream)
- Job vs occupation: near-synonyms; "occupation" is more formal (forms/applications).
- **lifeguard** (n) – watches over swimmers' safety at the beach
- **translator / interpreter** (n) – converts one language into another (translator slightly more official)
- **plumber** (n) – fixes pipes, toilets, sinks, showers
- **accountant** (n) – works with money/finances, makes sure the numbers add up
- **veterinarian / vet** (n) – animal doctor; "t" is a flap t, sounds like a d
- **judge** (n) – leader of the courtroom, decides guilty / not guilty (lawyer = argues/represents someone)
- **butcher** (n) – sells/prepares meat
- **janitor / custodian** (n) – cleans a building (office, school, airport); housekeeper = works in a home
- **real estate agent / realtor** (n) – helps people buy/sell homes
- **surgeon** (n) – doctor who operates; surgery = the procedure (noun)
- **architect** (n) – designs homes/buildings before construction
- **umpire** (n) – baseball's referee (other sports: referee)
- **chauffeur** (n) – drives people for a living (for a person, family, or business)
- **florist** (n) – sells flowers
- **dean** (n) – head of a university's administration (school: principal/headmaster)
- **paramedic** (n) – trained medical worker who rides in/drives an ambulance
- **courier** (n) – professional who delivers valuable/important packages (more formal than mailman)
- **bellhop / bellboy** (n) – hotel worker who carries luggage to rooms (bellhop is gender-neutral)
- **archaeologist** (n) – digs up ancient artifacts/ruins to study history
- **conductor / maestro** (n) – leads an orchestra (maestro = famous conductor)
- **foreman** (n) – boss at a construction site
- **mortician / undertaker** (n) – prepares the body for a funeral (undertaker = more informal euphemism)
- **headhunter** (n) – searches for candidates for top-level jobs (CEO, CFO, COO); recruiter fills many levels
- **bar back** (n) – bartender's assistant (restocks, changes kegs, collects glasses); busboy = clears tables in a restaurant
- **podiatrist** (n) – foot doctor; pediatrician = children's doctor

## 52. 25 Most Common Adjectives You NEED to Know
Oxford English Corpus ranking, #1 to #25: 1 **good**, 2 **new**, 3 **first**, 4 **last**, 5 **long**, 6 **great**, 7 **little**, 8 **own**, 9 **other**, 10 **old**, 11 **right**, 12 **big**, 13 **high**, 14 **different**, 15 **small**, 16 **large**, 17 **next**, 18 **early**, 19 **young**, 20 **important**, 21 **few**, 22 **public**, 23 **bad**, 24 **same**, 25 **able**.
- Examples: my very **own** camera; the **other** day; a **long** day; **last** week; **first** date; "How are you?" – "**Good**."; **high** heels; a **public** park.

## 53. 25 Most Common English Verbs You NEED to Know
Base – past – past participle (Oxford Corpus ranking):
1. **be** – was/were – been
2. **have** – had – had
3. **do** – did – done
4. **say** – said – said
5. **get** – got – got/gotten (gotten = American)
6. **make** – made – made
7. **go** – went – gone
8. **know** – knew – known
9. **take** – took – taken
10. **see** – saw – seen
11. **come** – came – come
12. **think** – thought – thought
13. **look** – looked – looked (regular)
14. **want** – wanted – wanted
15. **give** – gave – given
16. **use** – used – used
17. **find** – found – found
18. **tell** – told – told
19. **ask** – asked – asked ("-ed" sounds like /t/ after voiceless k)
20. **work** – worked – worked
21. **seem** – seemed – seemed
22. **feel** – felt – felt
23. **try** – tried – tried (y -> ied)
24. **leave** – left – left
25. **call** – called – called
- Most are irregular and must be memorized; third-person -s: has, does, goes, says.

## 54. 25 Most Common Nouns in English | Words You Need to Know
Oxford Corpus ranking, #1 to #25: 1 **time**, 2 **person**, 3 **year**, 4 **way**, 5 **day**, 6 **thing**, 7 **man**, 8 **world**, 9 **life**, 10 **hand**, 11 **part**, 12 **child**, 13 **eye**, 14 **woman**, 15 **place**, 16 **work**, 17 **week**, 18 **case**, 19 **point**, 20 **government**, 21 **company**, 22 **number**, 23 **group**, 24 **problem**, 25 **fact**.
- Multi-meaning nouns: **point** (you have a point / score points), **case** (solve a case / a case of beer), **company** (business or group of people), **work** (go to work).
- "thing" is vague; teachers advise being more specific.

## 55. 25 Super Useful Phrases You Will Use Again and Again
- **How's it going?** – greeting (the "g" is dropped: "how's it goin'")
- **How's that sound?** – asking if someone agrees with your suggestion
- **Never mind.** – forget it / don't want to repeat or discuss
- **Could you repeat that (please)?** – ask someone to say it again; "Sorry, could you repeat that?"
- **What do you mean?** – when you don't understand an explanation
- **What does ___ mean?** – ask the meaning of a word
- **Where are you from?** – "you" reduced to a schwa
- **What time is it?**
- **I was just about to ___** – I was on the point of doing it ("to" = schwa): I was just about to call you
- **Thanks so much for ___** – "for" sounds like "fer"
- **Be careful.**
- **I don't mean to bother you, but ___** – polite way to interrupt/ask
- **Don't worry about it.**
- **I'm running a little behind.** – I'm late
- **I'm pretty busy.** – "pretty" adds emphasis
- **I haven't heard about that.**
- **Who cares?** – I don't think it matters
- **Next time.** – when you can't do something now
- **Good to know.** – reaction to new information
- **Let me know.** – "let me" blends together
- **That works.** – agreeing to a plan
- **I have no idea.**
- **Let me think about that.**
- **I'm just looking around.** – to a shop assistant offering help
- **I'll be there in a second / in a moment.**

## 56. 25 Useful Words that You Should NOT Mispronounce
(American pronunciation; "-tion" = /shuhn/ with stress on the syllable before)
- **pronunciation** – c sounds like "s"; -tion = /shuhn/; stress on -ci- (pro-NUN-see-AY-shun)
- **characterization** – "ch" = /k/; stress before -tion
- **museum** – /myoo-ZEE-um/; s = z
- **disadvantage** – ends /-tij/, not "-tage"
- **unfortunate** – "tu" = /choo/
- **occupation** – first c = /k/; stress before -tion
- **cucumber** – first c = /kyoo/, second c = /k/ (KYOO-kum-ber)
- **suitable** – "ui" = /oo/; second syllable schwa: SOO-tuh-bul
- **tongue** – one syllable, rhymes with "sung"/"lung"
- **assume** – first syllable schwa (uh-SOOM); "u" = /oo/
- **diary** – three syllables: DAH-yuh-ree
- **category** – flap t (sounds like soft d)
- **schedule** – "ch" = /k/; -dule = /jool/
- **physics** – ph = /f/; short i; final s = z
- **curtail** – cur-TAIL, long a (not "curdle")
- **chemistry** – "ch" = /k/
- **poignant** – "gn" is a nasal sound: POIN-yunt (unlike "pregnant")
- **hippopotamus** – long word, practice syllable by syllable
- **rhinoceros** – silent h; c = /s/
- **science** – silent c; /SAI-uns/ (like scissors)
- **silhouette** – borrowed from French; sil-oo-ET
- **arouse** – "ou" diphthong /ow/; s = z
- **unique** – /yoo-NEEK/; -que = /k/; tongue twister "unique New York"
- **accident** – first c = /k/, second c = /s/
- **dumb** – silent b

## 57. 25 Ways to Say "Thank you" in English
- **Thanks.** – casual; **Thanks so much** / **thanks a ton** / **thanks a million** (more emphasis)
- **Thanks for everything.**
- **Thanks in advance.** – before they have done it
- **Thanks a lot.** – can be sarcastic: "Oh gee, thanks a lot!" (listen to tone)
- **Thanks for nothing.** – sarcastic, angry (not a real thank-you)
- **I'm so grateful (for ___).** – heartfelt
- **I appreciate it.** / **I really appreciate it.**
- **Much obliged.** – old-fashioned/formal in US, still used in UK
- **Cheers.** – casual, mainly British
- **I can't thank you enough.** – strong emphasis
- **Many thanks.** – mostly written (end of emails/texts)
- **I owe you one.** – casual; **I owe you big time** – stronger
- **How can I (ever) repay you?** – truly wanting to return the favor
- **You're too kind.** / **That's so kind of you.**
- **It means the world to me.**
- **I couldn't have done it without you.**
- **Oh, you shouldn't have.** – when someone was generous beyond what's needed
- **Please accept my sincere gratitude.** – very formal, to authority figures

## 58. 25 Words Learners Mispronounce | American Pronunciation + Vocabulary
- **often** /ˈɔfən/ (adv) – frequently; 2 syllables, stress 1st, silent t, schwa 2nd
- **paradigm** /ˈpærədaɪm/ (n) – a model or clear example; 3 syl, stress 1st, final "-gm" silent g
- **caveat** /ˈkæviæt/ (n) – a warning to consider before acting; 3 syllables, stress 1st; "There's one caveat..."
- **cupboard** /ˈkʌbərd/ (n) – storage furniture with shelves; p is nearly "b", 2nd syllable = "-erd", not "board"
- **Alzheimer's** /ˈɑːltshaɪmərz/ (n) – disease causing memory loss; stress 1st, starts like "alts"
- **Heimlich maneuver** /ˈhaɪmlɪk məˈnuːvər/ – emergency method for choking; "ch" = /k/; maneuver stress 2nd syllable
- **asthma** /ˈæzmə/ (n) – breathing condition; "sth" is just "z"; 2nd syllable schwa
- **mischievous** /ˈmɪstʃɪvəs/ (adj) – playfully naughty; 3 syllables (not "mis-CHEE-vee-us"); stress 1st
- **coxswain (cox'n)** /ˈkɑːksən/ (n) – person who steers and directs a rowing crew; sounds like "COK-sun"
- **clothes** /kloʊðz/ (n) – one syllable, sounds like "close" (verb)
- **prescription** /prɪˈskrɪpʃən/ (n) – doctor's note for medicine; stress 2nd; "pre" = /prɪ/
- **perspire** /pərˈspaɪər/ (v) – to sweat; stress 2nd
- **boutique** /buːˈtiːk/ (n) – small shop for clothes, jewelry; "-tique" = "teek"
- **police** /pəˈliːs/ (n) – stress 2nd; "-lice" = "leess"
- **debris** /dəˈbriː/ (n) – scattered broken pieces (after a disaster); stress 2nd, final "ee", silent s
- **marijuana** /ˌmærəˈwɑːnə/ (n) – 4 syllables, stress 3rd ("wah")
- **February** /ˈfɛbjuˌɛri/ (n) – 4 syllables: FEB-yoo-air-ee; stress 1st
- **rural** /ˈrʊrəl/ (adj) – countryside (rural area); two r's tricky
- **sorbet** /sɔːrˈbeɪ/ (n) – frozen dessert of juice, water, sugar; final "t" silent, "-bet" = "bay"
- **valet** /vælˈeɪ/ (n) – person who parks cars at hotels/restaurants; silent t, "-let" = "lay"
- **regime** /rɪˈʒiːm/ (n) – system of government; stress 2nd, "zh" sound, long ee
- **vineyard** /ˈvɪnjərd/ (n) – land where grapes are grown for wine; short i, stress 1st
- **coupon** /ˈkuːpɑːn/ (n) – paper/code for a discount; "cou" = "koo"
- **hyperbole** /haɪˈpɜːrbəli/ (n) – exaggeration; 4 syllables, stress 2nd, "-bole" = "bo-lee"
- **receipt** /rɪˈsiːt/ (n) – proof of payment; silent p, stress 2nd

## 59. 3-WORD PHRASAL VERBS THAT ARE GOOD TO KNOW
Tip: learn each three-word phrasal verb as one phrase; they are inseparable (no object between the words).
- **look forward to** – be excited about a future event – I look forward to speaking with you
- **put up with** – tolerate/endure – I'm not going to put up with it anymore (often negative)
- **get away with** – avoid punishment for something – You won't get away with this
- **break up with** – end a romantic relationship – She broke up with her boyfriend
- **talk back to** – answer rudely/defiantly, esp. to an authority figure (parent, teacher, boss)
- **run out of** – use up the whole supply – We ran out of milk
- **come up with** – think of/suggest an idea or plan – Where does she come up with her ideas?
- **walk out on** – suddenly leave a partner/spouse, ending the relationship
- **make up for** – compensate for a mistake/problem – You more than made up for yesterday's mistake
- **look down on** – think someone is less important than you – They look down on people who aren't wealthy
- **keep up with** – stay level/equal with someone or something – I'm having trouble keeping up with my classes
- **own up to** – admit the truth/responsibility – Own up to your mistakes
- **come across as** – seem to be (impression of a person) – He came across as a stand-up guy
- **stand-up guy** (informal) – a good, honorable person
- **pretentious** (adj) – acting as if more important or classy than others

## 60. 3-WORD PHRASAL VERBS | Build Your Vocabulary
- **live up to** – be as good as expected – live up to expectations / standards
- **get rid of** – remove something unwanted, or make a person leave – get rid of these old socks
- **brush up on** – improve knowledge you've partly forgotten – brush up on my Spanish
- **listen in on** – listen to a private conversation (secretly/intentionally = eavesdrop on, or can't help overhearing)
- **come down with** – catch/show signs of an illness – I'm coming down with a cold
- **get away from** – escape/avoid being caught; leave a place – can't get away from the office; "I want to get away from it all"
- **look up to** – respect and admire – I look up to my father
- **stand up for** – defend a person or idea – stand up for yourself / your rights
- **cut down on** – eat/drink/use less (usually for health) – cut down on chocolate
- **grow out of** – become too big for clothes; outgrow a childish habit; (idea) come into existence – grew out of her passion for human rights

## 61. 3-WORD PHRASES VERBS to Supercharge Your Vocabulary
- **look forward to** – be excited about a future event
- **feel up to** – have the strength/energy to do something (usually negative) – I don't feel up to it
- **do away with** – get rid of, stop using – We should have done away with this department
- **get around to** – finally do something you've intended to do for a long time – I'll get around to it eventually
- **check up on** – see how someone is / make sure they're doing what they should (positive tone) – parents check up on kids' homework
- **look out for** – make sure someone is treated well, take care of; with a reflexive pronoun it's negative: "only looking out for himself"
- **live up to** – meet others' expectations – live up to my mother's expectations
- **put up with** – accept/tolerate an unpleasant situation or person
- **drop out of** – quit before finishing – school, college, a race, a competition
- **get away with** – avoid punishment – You're not going to get away with this
- **pass out from** – faint / become briefly unconscious – pass out from the heat, low blood pressure, anxiety
- **move out of** – stop living in a place – move out of the house at 18
- **pick up after** (also **clean up after**) – clean the mess someone else made – pick up after your dog / kids
- **give up on** – stop believing someone will succeed – refuse to give up on his students
- **walk out on** – leave a job/person/event before finishing, abandon it

## 62. 30+ Ways to Use the Word ASS | American Expressions
Informal/vulgar spoken English; know them for comprehension, be careful using them.
- **ass** – donkey; someone's butt; a jerk/stupid person
- Intensifier "-ass" (meaning unchanged): **broke-ass** (very broke), **long-ass** flight, **lazy-ass**, **cold-ass** night
- **badass** – awesome/impressive (opposite of "bad"); **smartass** – annoying know-it-all (not "smart")
- Insults: **jackass**, **dumbass**; **hard-ass** – very strict; **pain in the ass** – annoying person/situation
- **up my ass** – (boss) constantly pestering me; **have your head up your ass** – not paying attention
- **kiss-ass / ass kisser** – someone who flatters to please; **Kiss my ass!** – screw you
- **tight-ass** – cheap, or overly strict about rules; "a tight ass" = attractive butt (objectifying)
- **bust ass** = fart; **bust your ass** = work very hard
- **My ass!** – no way (five dollars, my ass)
- "my/your ass" as a pronoun for oneself/you: "My ass is gonna teach you grammar"; "They bought my ass a beer"; "I dumped his ass"
- **get some ass** – find a hookup; **kick someone's ass** – beat them up
- **work your ass off** – work very hard; **talk out of your ass** – talk nonsense
- **half-ass(ed)** – lazy, poor effort; **tastes like ass** – disgusting
- **don't give a rat's ass** – don't care at all

## 63. 30 Contractions to Help You Speak Faster
Pronounce them connected and fast; the point is both listening and speaking.
- Be + not: **isn't, aren't, wasn't, weren't**
- Do + not: **don't, doesn't, didn't** – "It doesn't matter."
- Have + not: **haven't, hasn't, hadn't** (hadn't often in 3rd conditional) – "I haven't got a clue."
- **won't** (will not) – "I won't stand for this."
- Modals + not: **wouldn't, couldn't, shouldn't, mustn't** – "I couldn't help myself."
- Modal + have: **could've, would've, should've, might've, must've** (not "must of")
- **that's** (that is / that has), **that'll** (that will), **that'd** (that would – final d-sound): "That'd be nice."
- **there's** (there is/has), **there'd** (there would/had): "Where there's a will there's a way."; "There'd better be a comment."

## 64. 33 Useful Collocations to Build Your Vocabulary
- **rough** + noun: rough day (difficult), rough draft (incomplete version), rough estimate (approximation), rough neighborhood (dangerous)
- **touch**: a touch of (slight amount: a touch of the flu), finishing touch (final details), a nice touch (improves something), have a nice touch (do something well, esp. sports), soft touch (gentle)
- **break**: break a record, break a leg (good luck), break a promise, break the ice (start a conversation/ease tension), break the law
- **do**: do someone a favor, do housework (laundry, dishes), do your best, do harm
- **time**: spend time, waste time, kill time (do something unimportant while waiting), pass the time, right on time
- **run out of**: nothing left (ran out of gas); running out of = a little left; run out of patience / time / money / coffee
- **money**: waste money, invest money, donate money, save money, set aside money, set aside money for a rainy day

## 65. 35 Essential Vocabulary Words for Flying
- **departures / arrivals** (n) – airport areas for leaving / getting in
- **loading and unloading** (n) – temporary drop-off zone (no parking)
- **check-in** (n/adj; "check in" v) – register for your flight; online, curbside, or at the check-in counter
- **curb** (n) – stone edge along the street
- **boarding pass** (n) – ticket document that lets you get on the plane
- **check a bag** (v) – give luggage to the airline
- **security** (n) – screening area; "go through security" (long queue); officers tell you to **remove** your laptop, belt, shoes
- **domestic flight** – within the country; **international flight** – to another country
- **terminal** (n) – airport building where planes arrive/depart
- **gate** (n) – where a specific flight boards
- **moving sidewalk** (n) – conveyor walkway
- **gangway** (n) – elevated passage from gate to plane
- **flight attendant** (n) – crew member serving passengers
- **cockpit** (n) – pilots' compartment
- **overhead compartment / overhead storage** (n) – bag storage above seats
- **armrest** (n); **tray table** (n) – small fold-down table
- **runway** (n) – long strip for takeoff/landing
- **take off** (phr v) – leave the ground (also used like "leave")
- **ascend / descend** (v) – rise / go down; **ascent / descent** (n)
- **altitude** (n) – height above sea level
- **lavatory** (n) – toilet on the plane
- **galley** (n) – crew's food-prep area; **congregate** (v) – gather in a group
- **turbulence** (n) – violent, unsteady air shaking the plane
- **upright position** (n) – straight vertical seat/tray position for landing
- **taxi** (v) – move slowly on the ground after landing
- **disembark** (v) – leave the plane
- **connecting flight** (n) – second flight to reach the final destination
- **immigration** (n) – passport/document check on entering a country
- **customs** (n) – bag/goods inspection
- **baggage claim** (n) – where you collect checked luggage

## 66. 3 Advanced English Phrases Native Speakers Use All the Time!
- **toe the line** (idiom; transcript spells "tow") – do what is expected without upsetting anyone, e.g. follow policy or the boss
- **off the cuff** (adv phrase) – without preparation: speak / give a presentation off the cuff (from notes written on shirt cuffs)
- **the elephant in the room** (idiom) – an obvious problem everyone ignores; "address / talk about the elephant in the room"

## 67. 3 American Idioms Might Surprise You
- **game-changer** (n) – something that significantly alters a situation – Robo-taxis are a game-changer
- **FOMO** (n) – fear of missing out; anxiety about missing events/experiences
- **clapback / clap back** (n/v, informal US) – sharp, quick response to criticism

## 68. 3 Awesome Online Resources for English Learners
Gist: free tools to build vocabulary, then you must actively use the words.
- **Merriam-Webster / Learner's Dictionary** – has simpler "for English language learners" definitions, audio, example sentences, idioms, core vocabulary lists (e.g. air travel), quizzes
- **Google "word definition"** – shows pronunciation, synonyms, translation (use as last resort), and a "use over time" graph to check a word isn't archaic/dated
- **YourDictionary sentence examples** – community example sentences showing how a word is used in context
- Tips: write your own sentences with each new word; reuse it often and review weekly.

## 69. 3 EFFECTIVE WAYS TO IMPROVE YOUR SPEAKING
- Listening and reading = receptive skills (input); speaking and writing = productive skills (output).
- Listening: absorb flow, rhythm, pronunciation, intonation (music, movies, TV).
- Reading: builds vocabulary in context, reading aloud improves delivery, improves grammar/syntax.
- Speaking: the best way is to actually speak (friends, teachers, or speak along with videos); don't fear mistakes.

## 70. 3 Grammar Mistakes Advanced Learners Often Make
- **Subjunctive mood**: after verbs like recommend, demand, insist, suggest, use the base verb (no -s) – "The doctor recommends that she go to bed early"; "She demands that he leave at once"; "I insist the game get rescheduled". (A hidden "should" is in the verb's meaning.)
- **Negative present simple with he/she/it**: "He doesn't eat pizza," not "He don't"; "People don't want..."; "If someone doesn't like it..."
- **-ed vs -ing adjectives**: -ed = a person's (temporary) feeling (I'm bored, excited, frustrated, confused); -ing = the thing/situation/characteristic (The problem is frustrating; I am boring = I'm a boring person).

## 71. 3 Words You Need to Know for a Doctor's Checkup
- **stethoscope** (n) – earpiece device to listen to the heart
- **shot** (n) – injection; "get a shot" (in the arm)
- **thermometer** (n) – measures body temperature (e.g. in the ear)

## 72. 40 Super Useful Phrases to Show that You Care

- Leaving: **Travel safe / Safe travels**; **Have a safe flight / Have a safe trip**; **Let me know when you get there**; **Text me when you get home**
- Missing: **I miss you**; **I wish you were here**; **I'm lost without you**
- Excited to meet: **I can't wait to + base verb** (see you / give you a hug); **I look forward to + -ing** (seeing you)
- Support: **Let me help you with that**; **I'll go with you**; **I've got your back** (informal); **I believe in you**; **Believe in yourself**; **You can do anything if you put your mind to it**; **You're going to do great** (future) / **You're doing great** (now)
- Joy: **I'm so happy for you**; **Congrats on + noun**; **That's wonderful/great/amazing news**
- Love (serious occasions): **You are my everything**; **You complete me**; **You are my strength**; **my better half** (idiom, refers to spouse/partner); **I'm ready to take it to the next level** (more commitment)
- Comfort: **One day at a time**; **It's going to be okay**; **You're going to get through this**; **You will overcome this**
- Appreciation: **I'm so proud of you**; **You're the best**; **You're one in a million**; **You made my day**; **What would I do without you?**
- General care: **Get some rest**; **Sweet dreams** (to loved ones/kids); **Watch your step**; **I hope you feel better**; **I have a surprise for you**; **You have something in your teeth**

## 73. 4 Important Phrasal Verbs with CHECK | Vocabulary Lesson

- **check out** – (1) pay and leave a hotel/hospital; (2) borrow a book from a library; (3) look at / investigate – "Check out that cool dress."
- **check for** – try to find – "The doctor checks for health problems."
- **check into** – (1) register/enter a hotel or hospital; (2) investigate – "I'm checking into who stole my cookie."
- **check off** – put a tick next to an item on a list – "check things off my shopping list."

## 74. 4 Things You Won't Learn in an English Textbook

- Gist: informal English skills textbooks skip.
- **Make up words with suffixes** – **-able** (can be done; "bartable" = reachable by BART), **-ish** (kind of; "purple-ish"; standalone "Is he good? Ish.")
- **Other English varieties** – e.g. Indian English **prepone** (do earlier; opposite of postpone) – "Let's prepone the meeting."
- **Mirroring** – repeat a few of the speaker's words to show interest and invite more detail – "It was crazy." / "It was crazy?"
- **Confidence** – comes only from practice (speaking, presentations), not textbooks.

## 75. 50 American Idioms in 60 Minutes 🔥 | Sound Instantly More Fluent!

**twist someone's arm** – persuade forcefully – "There's no need to twist my arm."
**right up your alley** – exactly what you like – "This lesson is right up your alley."
**under the weather** – slightly sick – "I'm feeling a little under the weather."
**make ends meet** – earn just enough to live on – "barely making ends meet"
**take something with a grain of salt** – don't fully believe it (UK: pinch of salt)
**take a rain check** – decline now, maybe later – "I'll take a rain check."
**play it by ear** – decide as things happen, no plan – "Let's play it by ear."
**wing it** – do something without preparation – "I'll just wing the speech."
**off the beaten path** – not known/popular, uncrowded (positive) – "a beach off the beaten path"
**blow off some steam** – release stress/energy – "I'll go for a run to blow off some steam."
**down to the wire** – the last minute, full of suspense – "The game is coming down to the wire."
**in the home stretch** – final part of an activity (from horse racing) – "The project is in the home stretch."
**in the doghouse** – in trouble because someone is angry at you – "I forgot her birthday; now I'm in the doghouse."
**at a loss for words** – speechless – "I'm at a loss for words."
**in the same boat** – in the same bad situation – "We're in the same boat."
**pick up the tab / check** – pay the bill (restaurant/bar) – "I'll pick up the tab."
**bend over backwards** – try extremely hard to help/please – "I've been bending over backwards to help you."
**behind the eight ball** – in a difficult/worse position (billiards) – "We started late and now we're behind the eight ball."
**elbow grease** – hard physical effort (esp. cleaning) – "It needs a little elbow grease."
**head in the clouds** – not paying attention / unrealistic – "She has her head in the clouds."
**through thick and thin** – through good and bad times – "We've been through thick and thin."
**have your ducks in a row** – be well organized/prepared – "He has his ducks in a row."
**all ears** – eagerly listening – "Tell me, I'm all ears."
**get wind of (something)** – hear something meant to be secret – "She got wind of the surprise party."
**stick your neck out** – take a risk to help someone – "He stuck his neck out for me."
**out of the loop / in the loop** – not informed / informed – "Keep me in the loop."
**when it rains, it pours** – bad things come one after another (UK: it never rains but it pours)
**get something off your chest** – tell someone what has been worrying you
**on thin ice** – in a risky/dangerous position – "I forgot her gift; I'm on thin ice."
**throw caution to the wind** – stop being careful, take risks – "Don't throw caution to the wind."
**hang on someone's every word** – listen with total admiration/attention
**mum's the word** – keep it secret
**put a pin in it** – pause a topic to resume later (unlike rain check, which is for invitations)
**ring a bell** – sound familiar – "That name doesn't ring a bell."
**off the hook** – free from blame or an obligation – "You're off the hook."
**pay through the nose** – pay far too much – "pay through the nose for holiday flights"
**bite the bullet** – force yourself to do something unpleasant – "She had to bite the bullet and give the presentation."
**in a (pretty) pickle** – in a difficult, frustrating situation – "My flight got cancelled; I'm in a pickle."
**red tape** – excessive official rules that cause delay – "so much red tape for the permits"
**hindsight is 20/20** – looking back, it's clear what you should have done
**look on the bright side** – think positively (then say why)
**out of your league** – too good/attractive/expensive for you – "She's out of your league."
**jump on the bandwagon** – join something because it's popular/profitable
**cloud one's judgment** – impair clear thinking – "His emotions clouded his judgment."
**Monday morning quarterback** – someone who criticizes decisions after the fact (American football)
**weather the storm** – survive a difficult period – "The company can weather the storm."
**raining cats and dogs** – raining very heavily (not "dogs and cats")
**pull (some) strings** – secretly use influence to get something – "Can you pull some strings?"
**ballpark figure** – rough estimate – "Give me a ballpark figure."
**toe the line** – do what's expected without causing trouble (caption "tow")
**hit (someone) up** – ask someone for something, usually money – "I'll hit you up for a loan."

## 76. 50 Collocations to QUICKLY Build Your Vocabulary in 2025

Adjective + noun collocations:
- **high**: high school, high ground, high-tech, high standards, a high degree of certainty
- **big**: big mistake, big surprise, big deal, big decision, big money
- **little**: little boy/girl, little thought, a little bit, little time
- **heavy**: heavy drinker/smoker, heavy traffic, heavy rain, heavy sleeper
- **light**: light drinker/smoker, light sleeper, light rain, light breeze, light shade (of a color)
- **strong**: strong argument, strong feeling, strong desire, strong wind/breeze, strong evidence, strong hand (good cards/position of power), strong relationship
- **deep**: deep thought, deep voice, deep sleep, deep breath, deep depression, deep down (inside)
- **fast**: fast start, fast talker, fast asleep, fast pace, fast food, fast learner
- **quick**: quick glance, quick bite, quick shower, quick recap, quick decision
- **slow**: slow progress, slow growth, slow motion, slow day (not busy), slow learner

## 77. 50 Idioms Native Speakers Use Daily | 60 Minutes to Sound American 🇺🇸

Speak/talk idioms:
**speak one's mind** – say what you think openly – "Don't be afraid to speak your mind."
**speaking from experience** – you have credible prior experience
**talk one's ear off** – talk excessively and for a long time – "He talked my ear off."
**actions speak louder than words** – what you do reveals more than what you say
**like talking to a brick wall** – frustration with someone who doesn't listen
**(not) on speaking terms** – (not) talking after a falling out; mostly used negatively
**talk one's way into / out of (something)** – persuade to get / avoid something – "She talked her way out of a ticket."
**as we speak** – right now, with urgency – "The plane is boarding as we speak."
**talk some sense into (someone)** – persuade them to be reasonable

C1/C2 idioms (with pronunciation notes):
**be on the ball** – quick to understand and react (linking: "on the ball")
**with flying colors** – very successfully – "pass with flying colors"
**turn a blind eye (to)** – ignore something you know is wrong
**the easy way out** – the easiest option in a difficult situation – "take the easy way out" (intrusion: way-y-out)
**how/what/why/where on earth** – strong surprise, confusion, anger – "Why on earth are you changing jobs?"
**in light of** – because of / given new information – "In light of recent events..."
**at the end of one's rope** – no strength or patience left
**to the bitter end** – until it is finished, even if unpleasant – "fight to the bitter end"
**get off on the right/wrong foot** – make a good/bad start (more common: wrong foot)
**over the top** – too extreme/exaggerated – "way over the top"
**at the crack of dawn** – very early in the morning
**a change of heart** – change in feelings/opinion, usually with "had" – "a sudden change of heart"

Military expressions:
**boots on the ground** – troops/personnel actually deployed
**bite the bullet** – endure pain/discomfort and just do it
**got your six** – I have your back (six o'clock = behind you); "watch your six"
**bought the farm** – died (informal)
**catch flak (from someone)** – get harshly criticized
**FUBAR / SNAFU / TARFU** – acronyms for things totally messed up (SNAFU = a minor mix-up/snag)
**in the trenches** – in the middle of a tough fight/struggle
**no man's land** – dangerous area or off-limits topic
**nuclear option** – drastic measure that skips debate (US politics)
**on the double** – quickly, immediately
**on the front lines** – at the forefront of a battle/issue
**Roger that** – yes / message received

Winter vocabulary and idioms:
**bundle up** – put on warm clothes
**slush** – partly melted snow
**chilly / nippy** – a little cold; **freezing** – very cold; **as cold as ice**; **cold AF** (slang)
**on thin ice** – in a dangerous situation
**give someone the cold shoulder** – ignore someone
**get cold feet** – become nervous before a big event (often a wedding)
**catch a cold** – get sick with a cold

Summer vocabulary and idioms:
**come rain or shine** – no matter the weather/circumstances
**everything under the sun** – everything imaginable
**Indian summer** – unexpected hot dry spell in autumn
**a ray of sunshine** – someone who brightens your day
**take a shine to someone** – start liking someone
**soak up the sun** – enjoy lots of sun
**summer fling** – short summer romance
**not a cloud in the sky** – perfectly clear sky
**brighten up someone's day** – make their day better
**a scorcher** – very hot day
**rise and shine** – time to get up
**beat the heat** – stay cool in hot weather
**take the heat** – accept criticism/blame
**in a dead heat** – tied/even

## 78. 50 Super Useful Phrasal Verbs to Build Your Vocabulary

**phase out** – gradually stop using/producing – "phase out coal plants"
**wear off** – lose effect/intensity – "The drug is wearing off."
**spell out** – explain very clearly in detail – "The contract spells out both parties' duties." ("Do I have to spell it out for you?" = condescending)
**talk down to** – speak condescendingly
**hammer out** – work out details through discussion – "hammer out an agreement"
**bow out** – withdraw/retire from something (often due to injury/reason)
**frown upon** – disapprove of – "is frowned upon"
**bottle up** – repress feelings – "bottle up your anger"
**check up on** – check what someone is doing / how they are
**follow through (on/with)** – continue to completion – "He didn't follow through on his responsibilities."
**do away with** – get rid of, stop using
**branch out** – expand into a new direction – "branch out into engine repair"
**rattle off** – say quickly and effortlessly from memory – "rattle off all 50 states"
**live up to** – be as good as expected – "live up to expectations"
**latch on to** – follow/cling to someone unwanted; also become very interested in something
**feel up to** – have the strength/energy to do – "I don't feel up to it."
**mull over** – think carefully about for a long time
**buckle down** – get serious and focus on work – "buckle down and study"
**drop out of** – quit before finishing – "drop out of school"
**cash in on** – take advantage of – "cash in on her 15 minutes of fame"
**get around to** – finally do something you intended – "I'll get around to it."
**plow through** – finish something laborious with difficulty – "plow through the reports"
**run through** – rehearse/read quickly – "run through my lines"
**put up with** – tolerate – "I won't put up with this."
**hash out** – discuss to reach agreement (esp. in relationships)
**zero in on** – direct all attention to (also: hone in on)
**listen in on** – secretly listen to; (eavesdrop on = same; overhear = unintentional)
**lash out (at)** – suddenly attack or criticize angrily
**look out for** – make sure someone is treated well (looking out for oneself = selfish)
**eke out** – get/win with difficulty – "eke out a victory"
**get away with** – avoid punishment – "You won't get away with this."
**brush up on** – refresh partly forgotten knowledge – "brush up on my Spanish"
**tone down** – make less forceful/offensive – "tone down the rhetoric"
**move out** – stop living somewhere
**give up on** – stop expecting success/trying with someone or something
**air out** – expose to fresh air; also discuss openly – "air out grievances"
**come down with** – catch an illness – "come down with a cold/flu"
**pick up after** – clean up someone's mess
**pass out** – lose consciousness briefly
**walk out on** – suddenly leave/abandon a person or job
**jazz up** – make more attractive/interesting
**fall behind** – fail to keep pace; opposite: catch up
**look up to** – admire/respect – "I look up to my father."
**goof off** – avoid work, waste time (goof around = same)
**catch on** – understand; "catch on quickly"
**stand up for / stand up to** – defend (someone/yourself)
**burn up** – have a very high temperature
**talk back** – answer rudely (to someone in authority); vs talk down to = condescending
**run out (of)** – use up everything – "run out of milk"
**get rid of** – throw away/discard (not for "stop smoking")

## 79. 50 Useful Collocations with TAKE | Build Your Vocabulary

take part in; take a seat; take a look; take notes; take a step; take a moment; take a deep breath; take comfort; take hostage; take prisoner; take exams/tests/quizzes; take pride in; take advice; take a long hard look in the mirror (examine carefully); take charge (of); take a break; take a shower; take a bath; take turns; take medicine; take drugs; take a risk; take notice (of); take apart (a grammar); take a chance; **take heart** (be hopeful); take seriously; take a trip; take pictures; take time off; take a vacation; **take your breath away** (amaze); take to court; take that decision (British); take responsibility; take a shortcut; take a nap; **take a page out of my book** (do it the same way as someone); take a message; take a bow; take care; take a taxi/bus.

## 80. 5 Advanced Expressions in Spoken English 💬

**without further ado** – immediately; said before introducing a person/event to an audience
**with all due respect** – politely disagree, esp. with authority (formal; also "all due respect", UK "with respect")
**quote-unquote** – highlights a word/phrase someone said; also sarcasm – "She's under quote-unquote intense pressure." / "a quote-unquote expert"
**all downhill from here/there** – two opposite meanings: from now on it's easy, or it'll keep getting worse; "here" = present, "there" = past/future
**agree to disagree** – stop arguing since neither will change their opinion

## 81. 5 Adverbs Native Speakers Use Daily 🔥 Fix Your Pronunciation!

**particularly** – 5 syllables, stress on 2nd (par-TIC-u-lar-ly) – higher than normal
**literally** – 4 syllables, stress on 1st (LIT-er-al-ly); US flap T sounds like soft D – exactly
**regularly** – 4 syllables, stress on 1st; mind R (tongue pulled back) vs L (tongue touches ridge) – often
**eventually** – 5 syllables, stress on 2nd (e-VEN-tu-al-ly); the schwa is often dropped – in the end
**accidentally** – 5 syllables, stress on 3rd (ac-ci-DEN-tal-ly); T after N may elide – not on purpose

## 82. 5 BIZARRE WORDS w/ SPELLING & PRONUNCIATION THAT DO NOT MATCH 🙅‍♂️

**choir** /ˈkwaɪər/ – "ch" sounds like K
**aspirin** – often 2 syllables in US (ASP-rin)
**colonel** /ˈkɜːrnəl/ – sounds like "kernel"
**Q** – just the letter name
**squirrel** – "squ" has a slight W sound

## 83. 5 Common GRAMMAR MISTAKES English Learners Often Make

- **fewer vs less**: fewer + plural countable nouns (fewer bananas, fewer children, fewer people); less + uncountable or singular (less energy, less time, "one less thing to worry about"). Error: "10 items or less" should be "fewer."
- **used to vs use to**: "used to + base verb" = past habit/state, no longer true ("I used to play guitar"); in negatives/questions use "use to" ("I didn't use to have hair like this"); "be used to + noun/-ing" = accustomed ("I'm not used to the heat"). Sound is the same; matters in writing.
- **Noun clauses**: use statement word order (subject before verb), not question order – correct "I don't know where she is" (wrong: "where is she"). Starters: what, where, when, why, how, if, etc.
- **Phrases that take noun clauses**: I don't know / I know / I wonder / ask him + clause ("I wonder if you will write").
- **Polite questions**: "Could you tell me what time it is?" / "Do you know what time it is?" (not "what time is it").
- Idiom: **go out the window** – no longer exists.

## 84. 5 Effective Tips to Become a Confident Speaker

- Find a comfortable environment (classroom, coffee shop, friend's house) to lower anxiety.
- Embrace mistakes; they are part of learning – focus on what you want to say.
- Stop hunting for the perfect word or grammar; if stuck, say "Let me explain it a different way" and move on. Idiom: **on the tip of my tongue** – almost remember a word.
- Maintain eye contact to focus and comprehend, which makes responding easier.
- Speak clearly at your own comfortable speed; don't force reductions or linking.

## 85. 5 Phrasal Verbs with GET | Useful Grammar Lesson

- **get around** – (1) travel/move from place to place ("For her age my grandmother gets around easily"; "That person really gets around"); (2) avoid/bypass ("find a way to get around this traffic"); (3) (gossip) have many sexual partners ("he gets around")
- **get through** – (1) reach someone by phone ("I can't get through right now"); (2) get through to someone = make them understand/connect ("Nobody can get through to him"; "hard to get through to him because he's so stubborn"); (3) survive/endure ("How will I get through my finals?"; "a tough time getting through the divorce")
- **get across** – (1) make someone understand an idea; collocation get your point/message across ("If you'd let me get my point across..."); (2) cross to the other side, implies an obstacle ("We need to get across the street/the destroyed bridge") vs. plain go across
- **get off** – (1) leave work ("What time do you get off work?", "She won't get off until 11 p.m."); (2) exit a bus/train/bike/horse (not a car): "I'm getting off the bus"; (3) stop touching me ("Get off my foot!"); (4) escape severe punishment, collocation get off easy ("They got off easy for cheating")
- **get by** – (1) manage with limited money/knowledge ("He lost his job but is still getting by"; "I barely got by with a passing grade"); (2) pass someone/something blocking your way ("How do we get by the guards?")
- Notes: get through to = 3-word phrasal verb; "have a tough time + -ing"; get around = go around/avoid, get by = slip past.

## 86. 5 SUPER LONG WORDS...VERY DIFFICULT TO PRONOUNCE

- **veterinarian** /ˌvetərɪˈneriən/ – flap T (American), 6 syllables
- **encyclopedia** /ɪnˌsaɪkləˈpiːdiə/ – 6 syllables, all pronounced
- **hierarchy** /ˈhaɪərɑːrki/ – 2nd syllable unstressed schwa
- **surreptitious** /ˌsɜːrəpˈtɪʃəs/ – stress on 2nd-to-last syllable
- **particularly** /pərˈtɪkjələrli/ – hard to say; stress on 2nd syllable

## 87. 5 TRICKY WORDS TO PRONOUNCE IN ENGLISH

- **jewelry** /ˈdʒuːəlri/ – most people say 2 syllables
- **sixth** /sɪksθ/ – voiceless th at the end
- **epitome** /ɪˈpɪtəmi/ – the "e" is a separate syllable (e-pit-o-mee)
- **thoroughly** /ˈθɜːrəli/
- **brewery** /ˈbruːəri/

## 88. 5 Tricky Grammar Rules You Need to Remember

- **Who vs whom**: who = subject, whom = object. Trick: if he/she fits, use who ("Who would like ice cream?"); if him/her fits, use whom ("Whom should I ask?"; "I don't know whom the email is about"). In spoken US English people often use "who" for both.
- **American vs British spelling**: -er/-re (theater/theatre, center/centre), single/double l (traveled/travelled, counselor/counsellor), -or/-our (color/colour, favorite/favourite). Either is fine; just be consistent.
- **Singular "they"**: gender-neutral pronoun for an unknown-gender person ("Why is your friend traveling? Are they taking a vacation?"). Accepted in Cambridge/Merriam-Webster; in formal writing "he or she" is safer.
- **Irregular plurals**: normal -s/-es/-ies; nouns in -f/-fe → -ves (wolf→wolves, leaf→leaves); memorize others: child→children, foot→feet, ox→oxen, index→indices, octopus→octopi.
- **Silent letters**: kn- (knee), wr- (right), gn (foreign). No general rule; learn with subtitles, audiobooks, pronunciation videos.

## 89. 5 Useful Phrasal Verbs with CHECK to Improve Your English

- **check back** – return later to see if things are in order – "I'll check back tomorrow." (inseparable)
- **check on** – make sure someone/something in progress is OK – "Can you check on the cake?" (inseparable)
- **check up on** – check on a person/situation to see they're doing what they should – "She checks up on her kids to make sure they do homework." (inseparable)
- **check over** – examine closely, esp. written work (separable) – "Check your work over before turning it in."
- **check with** – ask someone for confirmation/permission – "I need to check with my parents." (inseparable)
- **check through** – (1) inspect thoroughly to find something – "I checked through every drawer" (inseparable); (2) check luggage through to the final destination (separable)

## 90. 5 Useful Tips on How to Learn & Improve Your English

- Review (spaced repetition): rewatch/reread after a few days to a week.
- Use a variety of resources (video = visual, music = auditory, books, internet quizzes).
- Set short-term, realistic goals (e.g., one new word a day for a month).
- Practice a little each day (about 15 min); a marathon, not a sprint; no cramming.
- Immerse yourself via TV, movies, music, phone apps, no need to live abroad.

## 91. 5 Useful Verbs You May Be Mispronouncing... 😧

- **alleviate** /əˈliːvieɪt/ – make pain/problem less severe; 4 syllables, stress 2nd, schwa first
- **deteriorate** /dɪˈtɪriəreɪt/ – become worse over time; 5 syllables, stress 2nd
- **prioritize** /praɪˈɔːrətaɪz/ – put in order of importance; 4 syllables, stress 2nd
- **emphasize** /ˈemfəsaɪz/ – give special attention/stress; 3 syllables, stress 1st, ph = f
- **speculate** /ˈspekjuleɪt/ – make an educated guess; stress 1st, c = k

## 92. 5 Useful Ways to Improve Your English Fluency

- Fluency = speaking effortlessly and smoothly; matters for sounding native/professional and being understood.
- 1. Stop translating: guess meaning from context; use an English-English dictionary or Google "word definition".
- 2. Don't obsess over grammar: language changes, native speakers err too (e.g., whom is fading); learn grammar in context.
- 3. Listen and repeat: copy songs/shows/podcasts (e.g., Friends, Modern Family, Adele with lyrics) to absorb intonation, stress and structure; you must say it aloud.
- 4. Learn phrases/collocations, not isolated words (make a mistake, do homework, catch a bus, catch a cold).
- 5. Proper register: match formal/informal language to context (email vs resume vs essay; avoid slang like "chillax" in an essay).

## 93. 5 WORDS w/ CRAZY SPELLING & DIFFICULT PRONUNCIATION

- **nauseous** /ˈnɔːʃəs/ – 2 syllables
- **ingenious** /ɪnˈdʒiːnjəs/ – "-ious" is not "-ee-us" here; stress 2nd
- **asthma** /ˈæzmə/ – th is silent
- **indict** /ɪnˈdaɪt/ – c is silent, sounds like "indite"
- **paraphernalia** /ˌpærəfərˈneɪliə/ – long word

## 94. 5 Ways on How to Achieve English Fluency

- "If at first you don't succeed, try, try again": learning has dips; progress feels slower as you advance; keep practicing.
- "You've got to want it": decide, commit, make it part of your lifestyle.
- "Do things differently / think outside the box": mix textbooks, videos, courses, social media, local English groups; pick what fits your needs.
- "Be stronger than your excuses": even 5 min/day adds up; find tutors, online classes, or start a practice club.
- "The sky is the limit": there is always more to learn (new words are added every year).

## 95. 6 Ways to Improve Your Fluency & Become a Better Speaker 💬

- Quote-based tips for speaking: (1) choose a different thought to cut anxiety; (2) confidence = "I'll be fine if they don't like me", so record yourself, sing in the shower; (3) practice until you can't get it wrong, find a language-exchange partner/tutor; (4) own up to mistakes ("sorry, I got sidetracked", "I digress", "That's not what I meant to say, let me say it again"); (5) write down goals, keep them short-term; (6) read a lot for grammar and vocabulary.
- Useful phrases: **own up to** (admit), **I digress** (I'm getting off topic), **get sidetracked**.

## 96. 75 ADVANCED COLLOCATIONS | Supercharge Your Vocab (B2, C1, & C2 Phrases)

B2
- **crucial** (adj) – extremely important – a crucial factor/element/evidence; play a crucial role in; absolutely crucial
- **terms** (n) – conditions/way of expressing – favorable terms; dictate terms; terms and conditions; on friendly/good terms with; in terms of (salary)
- **wrongly** (adv) – incorrectly/unfairly – wrongly accused/convicted/imprisoned/informed
- **academic** (adj) – of school/university – academic achievement/performance/purposes/year/rigor
- **temper** (n) – tendency to get angry – have a temper; lose/control your temper; short temper; vile temper

C1
- **ethnic** (adj) – ethnic group/diversity/minority/background/tensions
- **pose** (v) – present/cause – pose a threat/danger/problem/risk (to); pose a question (formal)
- **immediate** (adj) – immediate effect/surroundings; immediate response/danger/future
- **alternate** (adj) – alternate days/weeks (every other); alternate plan/route/explanation
- **insight** (n) – deep understanding – gain/offer/provide insight into

C2
- **abrupt** (adj) – sudden; abrupt departure/end/change; abrupt manner (curt, rude)
- **paramount** (adj) – more important than anything – of paramount importance/concern; remain paramount
- **invasion** (n) – unwanted entry – invasion of tourists; massive/full-scale/imminent invasion; invasion of privacy
- **toll** (n) – death toll; human toll; heavy cost – emotional toll; exact/take a toll on someone
- **undue** (adj) – more than necessary – undue pressure/stress/attention/influence/hardship

## 97. 75 Difficult Words to Pronounce (Fine-tune Your Pronunciation)

Set 1 (American pronunciation)
- **pronunciation** /prəˌnʌnsiˈeɪʃn/ – "nun" not "noun"; -tion = shun, stress syllable before
- **characterization** – ch = k; -tion = shun; stress before it
- **museum** /mjuˈziːəm/ – s = z
- **disadvantage** /ˌdɪsədˈvæntɪdʒ/ – -age = quick "ij"
- **unfortunate** /ʌnˈfɔːrtʃənət/ – tu = "chu"
- **occupation** – c = k (not s); stress before -tion
- **cucumber** /ˈkjuːkʌmbər/ – first c = "kyoo", second c = k
- **suitable** /ˈsuːtəbl/ – ui = oo; schwa in the middle
- **tongue** /tʌŋ/ – one syllable; rhymes with sung, lung
- **assume** /əˈsuːm/ – schwa first; "oo" sound
- **diary** /ˈdaɪəri/ – 3 syllables
- **category** /ˈkætəɡɔːri/ – flap t (sounds like d)
- **schedule** /ˈskedʒuːl/ – sch = sk; dule = "jool"
- **physics** /ˈfɪzɪks/ – ph = f; s = z
- **curtail** /kərˈteɪl/ – second syllable like "tail"
- **chemistry** /ˈkemɪstri/ – ch = k
- **poignant** /ˈpɔɪnjənt/ – gn = nasal "ny"
- **hippopotamus** – break it up: hip-po-POT-a-mus
- **rhinoceros** /raɪˈnɑːsərəs/ – h silent; c = s
- **science** /ˈsaɪəns/ – the c is not pronounced separately (no "sk")
- **silhouette** /ˌsɪluˈet/ – French origin
- **arouse** /əˈraʊz/ – "ou" = ow; s = z
- **unique** /juˈniːk/ – que = k
- **accident** /ˈæksɪdənt/ – first c = k, second c = s
- **dumb** /dʌm/ – b silent

Set 2
- **often** /ˈɔːfən/ – t silent, schwa second syllable
- **paradigm** /ˈpærədaɪm/ – model/clear example; "-igm" ends as "dime"
- **caveat** /ˈkæviæt/ – warning; 3 syllables
- **cupboard** /ˈkʌbərd/ – p silent-ish, second syllable "bird"
- **Alzheimer's** /ˈæltshaɪmərz/ – stress first
- **Heimlich maneuver** /ˈhaɪmlɪk məˈnuːvər/ – ch = k; stress on "-neu-"
- **asthma** /ˈæzmə/
- **mischievous** /ˈmɪstʃɪvəs/ – 3 syllables, mis-chi-vus
- **coxswain** /ˈkɑːksn/ – "cox-n"
- **clothes** /kloʊðz/ – one syllable
- **prescription** /prɪˈskrɪpʃn/ – stress 2nd
- **perspire** /pərˈspaɪər/ – to sweat
- **boutique** /buːˈtiːk/ – -ique = "teek"
- **police** /pəˈliːs/ – "-lice" = lease
- **debris** /dəˈbriː/ – final long e
- **marijuana** /ˌmærəˈwɑːnə/ – stress on 3rd syllable
- **February** /ˈfebruˌeri/ – 4 syllables
- **rural** /ˈrʊrəl/ – two r's
- **sorbet** /sɔːrˈbeɪ/ – -et = "ay"
- **valet** /vælˈeɪ/ – -et = "ay"
- **regime** /rɪˈʒiːm/ – final long e
- **vineyard** /ˈvɪnjərd/ – short i
- **coupon** /ˈkuːpɑːn/ – cou = koo

Set 3 (silent letters)

- **mortgage** /ˈmɔːrɡɪdʒ/, **nestle** /ˈnesl/, **hasten** /ˈheɪsn/, **moisten** /ˈmɔɪsn/ – silent t
