## 98. 7 Creative Ways to Get More SPEAKING Practice

- Join a speaking group about a shared interest (Meetup, Eventbrite, etc.), not an "English" group.
- Join an online community/book club (Zoom, Skype).
- Take a paid online class.
- Be a volunteer tour guide for international tourists.
- Find a speaking partner (needs a set topic).
- Start a YouTube channel (or just film yourself) on a topic you love.
- Do interactive speaking-activity videos.

## 99. 7 Grammar Mistakes You Should Definitely Correct

- -ed vs -ing adjectives: -ed for feelings ("I'm bored"), -ing for things/people's character ("He's boring").
- "I am agree" -> "I agree" (or "I'm in agreement", formal); don't add "be".
- Comparatives/superlatives: short words -er/-est (hotter, happiest); long words more/most (more beautiful, most successful); never "more hot / more spicier / most strangest".
- Prepositions: "interested in", not "interested about"; read more to absorb them.
- Subject-verb agreement: "he doesn't / she doesn't", not "he don't".
- Gerund vs infinitive: enjoy + -ing (not "enjoy to go"); feel like + -ing; want/ask + to; like + either.
- Pronouns: don't mix up he/she (he ate a salad, she ate the chicken); more speaking practice fixes it.

## 100. 7 Super-Useful Expressions with the Word BRIDGE

- **build bridges** – create friendly relations – "wise build bridges, the foolish build barriers"
- **burn (one's) bridges** – ruin a relationship beyond repair – "He burned bridges when he quit"
- **cross that bridge when we get there** – deal with a problem only when it happens (also "when the time comes")
- **a bridge too far** – a goal too ambitious – "Learning English in a week is a bridge too far"
- **bridge over (something)** – serve as a bridge – "The rocks bridge over the river"
- **water under the bridge** – past problem resolved, forgotten – "Don't worry, it's water under the bridge"
- **bridge the gap** – temporary fix/connection – "I learned Cantonese to bridge the gap"

## 101. 7 Super Useful Idioms to Improve Your Fluency

- **beat around the bush** – speak indirectly, avoid the point – "Stop beating around the bush, tell me"
- **bark up the wrong tree** – make a mistake; pursue the wrong thing/person
- **go with the flow** – accept the situation and relax
- **nip (something) in the bud** – stop something at an early stage before it grows
- **knock on wood** – said to hope for good luck/avoid bad luck
- **live under a rock** – be unaware of widely known news – "Have you been living under a rock?"
- **grassroots** (phrase) – at the basic, local level of people – "a grassroots campaign"

## 102. 7 Things Americans Don't Really Say & What You Should Say Instead

- "How do you do?" (old, formal) -> "How's it going?", "How are you?", "What's up?"
- "I'm fine, thank you, and you?" (robotic) -> "I'm good/great"; don't thank them for asking.
- **shall** (old) -> use "will"/"be going to" for future; "should" for suggestions (shall we is the exception).
- **whom** (rare in speech) -> "Who should we ask?", "Who would you like to speak to?" (avoid "to whom").
- Full forms ("I am going", "I do not like") -> use contractions: I'm, don't, I'd.
- "How's the weather?" (textbook small talk) -> ask about interests (books, activities).
- "It's raining cats and dogs" (rare) -> "It's raining really hard."

## 103. 8 Common Writing Mistakes (AND HOW TO FIX THEM)

- **Subject-verb agreement**: "Neither John nor Mary is at school" (singular subjects = singular verb); "John asks" (3rd-person -s); "he doesn't", not "he don't".
- **Contractions vs possessives**: you're (you are) / your; it's (it is / it has) / its. "It's been a difficult time" = it has been.
- **Article misuse**: "check the weather forecast"; "an umbrella" (vowel sound); no article before "Yosemite National Park".
- **Wrong preposition**: interested **in**; great/good **at** math; depend **on**. Check usage frequency with Google Ngram Viewer ("good at math" > "good in math").
- **Shifting tenses**: use time words (every day = present simple; yesterday = past; tomorrow = future) to pick the tense and stay consistent.
- **Comma misuse**: comma after an introductory phrase ("After going to the dentist, I..."); comma before a quote ("The dentist said, "..."); comma before and/but joining two independent clauses.
- **Wrong word / collocation**: **buy time** (not purchase time) = delay; **give/make/do a presentation** (give = deliver, make = create); **effect** (noun) vs **affect** (verb).
- **Informal language in formal writing**: avoid idioms ("in the nick of time", "a piece of cake") and reductions ("gonna"); write "before the deadline", "straightforward", "going to".
- Pro tips: read backwards (last sentence first) to isolate grammar; read daily to absorb grammar/syntax; read out loud to catch punctuation and run-ons; know your audience (sets tone, content, structure).

## 104. 8 Types of NOUNS | Improve Your Grammar

- Noun = person, place, or thing; a content word (gives meaning/pictures, often the subject or object).
- **Common vs proper**: common = general (book, street, teacher), not capitalized; proper = specific (Harry Potter, Main Street, Wes), always capitalized.
- **Singular vs plural**: singular noun takes singular verb, plural takes plural verb (subject-verb agreement). Spelling: chair-chairs; box-boxes (-x + es); knife-knives (-f/-fe to -ves); city-cities (-y to -ies); quiz-quizzes (double z + es).
- **Irregular plurals**: child-children; tooth-teeth; person-people; sheep-sheep; deer-deer; fish-fish (fishes = species); scissors/pants/glasses are always plural. If singular = plural form, check the verb: "the sheep is white" vs "the sheep are black".
- **Count vs non-count**: count nouns can take -s and numbers (apples, chairs, students); non-count take singular verbs, no plural (water, milk, sugar, rain); use a quantifier to count them (two bottles of water, cartons of milk).
- Quantifiers: **many** (count) / **much** (non-count); **a large/small number of** (count) / **a large/small amount of** (non-count); **a few** (count) / **a little** (non-count); **fewer** (count) / **less** (non-count) - "ten items or fewer", "fewer people"; **some**, **a lot of** work with both.
- **Concrete vs abstract**: concrete = perceived by the five senses (spice, snow, thunder, cologne, lump, money); abstract = ideas/feelings/qualities/events (love, chaos, creativity, knowledge, marriage, happiness, fear, wisdom). Abstract nouns can be subjects with no article ("Love is blind").

## 105. 8 Useful Proverbs to Offer Advice | ADVANCED EXPRESSIONS

- **Strike while the iron's hot** – take advantage of an opportunity now, before it goes away.
- **Don't put all your eggs in one basket** – don't rely wholly on one person/thing (e.g., invest in different things).
- **Look before you leap** – think carefully before deciding, especially when risky.
- **Don't bite the hand that feeds you** – don't disrespect/harm the person who helps or supports you.
- **Don't judge a book by its cover** – you can't judge someone/something by appearance alone.
- **Mind your own business** – don't interfere in other people's affairs.
- **Practice what you preach** – do what you tell others to do.
- **Don't put the cart before the horse** – do things in the proper order.

## 106. 8 Ways to Say "JOIN" | Build Your Vocabulary

- **follow** (v) – join someone's page/updates – "follow someone on Instagram"; "follow these lessons"
- **enroll (in)** (v) – join a class – "enroll in a class"
- **enlist (in)** (v) – join the military – "enlist in the army"
- **take part in** (phr v) – join an activity or event
- **team up with** (phr v) – join a person/group to work together
- **associate with** (v) – join loosely (e.g., partner airlines sharing points)
- **come aboard** (phr v) – join a company/team (e.g., newly hired)
- **network (with)** (v) – meet people/businesses so you can work together or join later (business)

## 107. 8 Words Native Speakers Don't Say & What You Should Say Instead

- **thou / thee / thy** – archaic "you" (subject / object / possessive); use "you / your"; only in old plays and stories.
- **hither** – archaic: to this place ("come hither"); say "come over here".
- **salutations** – fancy greeting; say "hi", "hello", "nice to meet you"; mostly read, not spoken.
- **frolic** (v) – play in a lively, carefree way (children frolicking on the beach); more written than spoken.
- **whippersnapper** (n) – a young, arrogant, overconfident person ("young whippersnapper"); rare, used by older people.
- **so-so** (adj) – neither good nor bad; learners overuse it for feelings ("I'm so-so"); say "okay", "good", "not bad"; better to describe things (a meal, a movie).
- Resources for checking word frequency: Google search "word + meaning" (usage-over-time graph), wordandphrase.info (spoken vs written frequency, trend), Google Ngram Viewer (compare phrases over 200 years).

## 108. A, AN, THE, or NO ARTICLE | English Grammar Quiz

- **the** with unique/specific places: the North Pole, the South Pole.
- **no article** with continents (Antarctica, Asia, Europe) and mountain names (Mount Everest, Mount Denali).
- **a/an** = non-specific singular count noun ("bring me a shirt"); **the** = specific ("the purple shirt").
- **First mention vs second mention**: first = a/an (singular) or no article (plural/non-count); second = the. "I found a dog. The dog was wearing a collar... the collar."
- **Superlatives always take the**: the tallest, the fastest, the most expensive. Comparatives follow normal rules (a more expensive ring / the more expensive ring of two).
- **a/an = per**: "$100 a person" = $100 per person.
- **the** with rivers: the Nile, the Colorado River.
- **Countries**: usually no article (Brazil, China, Indonesia); **the** with plural names (the Bahamas, the Philippines) or names with state/kingdom/republic (the UK, the United States, the Republic of China).
- **Illnesses**: usually no article (COVID); some take the: the flu, the measles, the mumps, the bubonic plague.
- **Jobs**: a/an – "I am an English teacher", "a firefighter".
- **Time expressions**: in the morning/afternoon/evening, during the day/night.
- **a/an depends on sound, not letter**: "a unified voice" (/j/ sound), "an hour".
- **Fixed phrases**: take an interest in / take a strong interest in; keep an eye out; make a statement.
- **Parts of a country**: the Midwest, the South, the North, the East/West Coast.
- **Directions**: on the right, on the left (the after a preposition).
- **a few / a little** = some (positive): "He still has a few tricks up his sleeve"; **few / little** (no article) = hardly any ("We have few choices").
- **the + adjective = group of people**: the rich, the poor, the blind, the elderly ("The rich get richer and the poor get poorer").
- Tip: don't just memorize rules; read and listen a lot.

## 109. ACHIEVE ENGLISH FLUENCY | Here are 5 useful tips... 🎊

- Get more practice than last year, especially active practice: speaking and writing (comments, online class, shadowing = repeating exactly what you hear to copy rhythm and flow).
- Use technology: sites/apps for grammar and vocabulary; prefer active (do something) over passive (just watching) learning.
- Make a habit of reading in English (news articles, captions, comments, memes) - best passive way to absorb grammar and collocations.
- Set short-term goals: decide what to work on, when, and how, instead of just "be fluent by year end".
- Have fun: choose shows, music, and reading you enjoy to stay motivated.

## 110. ADVANCED AMERICAN PHRASES YOU NEED TO KNOW 🇺🇸

- **table (something)** (v) – postpone discussing – "Let's table that suggestion for now."
- **scoot over** (phr v) – slide over to make room (also "scooch over") – "Can you scoot over a little?"
- **someone's two cents** – your (unasked-for) opinion – "That's my two cents."
- **Period.** – added after a statement to end the debate, dramatically – "We're not changing our plans, period."
- **jump the shark** – the point where a TV show declines by doing something ridiculous (from Happy Days) – "Game of Thrones jumped the shark."
- **the American dream** – anyone can succeed through hard work – "Oprah's rise is truly the American dream."
- **bent out of shape** – very angry/upset – "No need to get bent out of shape."
- **Monday morning quarterback** – someone who criticizes decisions with hindsight – "Don't play Monday morning quarterback."
- **Tell me about it.** – I totally agree, I know exactly what you mean.
- **Uncle Sam** – the US government – "We all pay taxes to Uncle Sam."
- **fishing expedition** – a broad search for information hoping to find wrongdoing – "The commission is merely a fishing expedition."
- **sweep (something) under the rug** – hide/conceal a problem (UK: under the carpet) – "He sweeps everything under the rug."

## 111. ADVANCED BANKING VOCABULARY 🏦  | Words and phrases you need to know

- **mortgage** (n) /ˈmɔːrɡɪdʒ/ – loan from a bank to buy a house – "a 30-year mortgage" (the t is silent)
- **borrower** (n) – takes money; **lender** (n) – gives money (borrow = take, lend = give)
- **piggy bank** (n) – container for saving coins, esp. for children
- **deposit** (n/v) – putting money into the bank – "make a deposit"
- **withdrawal** (n) /wɪðˈdrɔːəl/ – taking money out; verb **withdraw** – "make a withdrawal"
- **overdraft fee** (n) – penalty when you spend more than you have in your account
- **safety deposit box** (n) – lockable box in a bank vault for valuables
- **line of credit** (n) – amount of credit the bank extends to a borrower – "extend my line of credit"
- **statement** (n) – monthly bank record of transactions
- **loan** (n) – money borrowed; **interest** (n) – the percentage you pay for borrowing – "take out a loan, pay interest"
- **balance** (n) – difference between credits and debits; money in your account
- **ATM** (n) – automated teller machine
- **Chapter 11** (n) – US bankruptcy protection letting a business restructure and repay creditors – "file for Chapter 11"

## 112. ADVANCED COURTROOM VOCABULARY 👨‍⚖️ | Incredibly Useful Words & Phrases

Pre-trial
- **arraignment** (n) – hearing where the defendant is told the charges and pleads guilty or not guilty
- **deposition** (n) – sworn testimony given outside open court – "give a deposition" (indictment = formal charge, not the same)
- **discovery** (n) – process where each side obtains evidence from the other before trial
- **subpoena** (n) /səˈpiːnə/ – court order to appear/give testimony or evidence (silent b) – "issue a subpoena"

Trial
- **perjury** (n) – criminal offense of lying under oath – "commit perjury"
- **affidavit** (n) – written statement made under oath (4 syllables, stress on 3rd: af-fi-DA-vit)
- **quash** (v) – officially reject/cancel a decision, motion, or evidence – "quash a motion"
- **burden of proof** – obligation to prove the allegations (usually on the prosecution, beyond a reasonable doubt)

Sentencing
- **acquittal** (n) – verdict of not guilty because the prosecution didn't prove its case (verb: acquit)
- **exoneration** (n) – proof the person did not commit the crime (verb: exonerate); stronger than acquittal
- **file an appeal** – ask a higher court to review the decision
- **hung jury** – jury unable to reach a verdict

## 113. ADVANCED DENTAL VOCABULARY 🦷   | Words & phrases you should know

- **decay** (n/v) – damage to a tooth's surface – "your teeth are starting to decay"
- **cavity** (n) – hole in the hard surface of a tooth
- **filling** (n) – material used to treat a cavity
- **dental hygienist** (n) – trained professional who cleans teeth and takes X-rays
- **wisdom tooth** (n) – last tooth at the back of each jaw side
- **floss** (n/v) – string for cleaning between teeth – "floss your teeth"
- **braces** (n) – devices to straighten teeth
- **mouthwash** (n) – liquid for rinsing the mouth
- **dentures** (n) – artificial teeth
- **gums** (n) – soft tissue around teeth
- **anesthesia** (n) /ˌænəsˈθiːʒə/ – drug that stops pain (stress on the long "ee" syllable) – "given some anesthesia"
- **root canal** (n) – procedure treating infection at the center of a tooth
- **panoramic X-ray** (n) – X-ray of the whole mouth in one image
- **dental checkup / oral exam** (n) – routine yearly examination
- **tooth abscess** (n) – pocket of pus from bacterial infection
- **crown** (n) – tooth-shaped cap placed over a tooth
- **enamel** (n) – protective outer covering of a tooth
- **grind / clench your teeth** (v) – bad habit (medical term: bruxism) that wears enamel

## 114. ADVANCED DRIVING VOCABULARY 🚘    | Words & phrases you need to know

Car parts
- **rear view mirror** (n) – mirror in front of you ("side rear view mirror" on the door)
- **bumper** (n) – front/back protective part of a car
- **glove compartment** (n) – storage in the dashboard (also "glove box", more UK/Northeast US)
- **windshield wipers** (n) – clear rain from the windshield (or just "wipers")
- **trunk** (n, US) = **boot** (UK) – rear storage space

Phrasal verbs
- **break down** – stop working – "My car broke down on the side of the road."
- **pull out of / back out of** – leave a driveway (forward onto the road / in reverse)
- **buckle up** – fasten your seat belt
- **cut (someone) off** – drive aggressively into another driver's path – "That car just cut me off."
- **pull over** – stop at the side of the road – "I got pulled over by a cop."

Phrases
- **traffic jam** – many vehicles close together, barely moving – "stuck in a traffic jam"
- **fender bender** (informal) – minor car collision
- **road rage** – anger/violence between drivers
- **rush hour** – time of day when traffic is heaviest (about 5-7 p.m.)
- **parallel parking** – parking alongside the road in line with other cars
- **backseat driver** – passenger who gives the driver unwanted advice

## 115. ADVANCED FLYING VOCABULARY  ✈️  | Words & phrases you need to know

- **cockpit / flight deck** (n) – compartment for the pilot
- **cabin** (n) – section where passengers travel
- **overhead compartment / overhead bins / overhead storage** (n) – storage above the seats
- **galley** (n) – plane kitchen where food is prepared
- **bulkhead** (n) – interior dividing wall between cabins; "bulkhead seats" are right behind it
- **overnight flight / red-eye** (n) – flight departing at night and arriving next morning
- **direct flight / indirect flight** (n) – with / without stops
- **connect through** (phr v) – change planes in another city – "We connected through Frankfurt." (transfer through also works)
- **turbulence** (n) – abrupt irregular air movement shaking the plane
- **layover** (n) – wait between connecting flights (under 24 hours); **stopover** (n) – longer stop (24+ hours, often sightseeing)
- **lug** (v) – carry something heavy with difficulty – "lugging around bags"
- **carry-on bag** (n) – luggage taken into the cabin
- **jet lag** (n) – tiredness after crossing time zones
- **baggage claim** (n) – area where you pick up checked luggage (the conveyor belt = luggage carousel)
- **jetway / gangway** (n) – walkway or ramp between the terminal and plane
- **runway** (n) – surface for takeoff and landing
- **tarmac** (n) – airport ground where planes park (technically the apron/ramp); **taxi** (v) – plane moves on the ground to/from the runway; **taxiway** (n)

## 116. ADVANCED HEALTH VOCABULARY ❤️  | Words & phrases you need to know

**Idioms**
- **bottle (something) up** – suppress/hide emotions – "Don't keep bottling up your anger."
- **pull yourself together** – regain control of your emotions (use reflexive pronoun) – "She pulled herself together and faced it."
- **get your act together** – get organized and act efficiently (organization, not emotion)
- **have a lot on your mind** – be mentally preoccupied/worried – "He couldn't focus; he had a lot on his mind."
- **have a lot on your plate** – be busy with many tasks/responsibilities
- **short fuse / short temper** – gets angry quickly (use with "have") – "He has a short fuse."
- **fit as a fiddle** – in very good health (fixed phrase; not "like a fiddle")
- **back on your feet** – recovered from illness, injury or trouble (also financially) – "You'll be back on your feet in no time."
- **run down** – very tired/exhausted – "I feel run down at the end of the semester."
- **go under the knife** – have surgery (casual; formal: undergo surgery)

**Health acronyms**
- **ADHD** – attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (inattention, hyperactivity, impulsivity)
- **PTSD** – post-traumatic stress disorder (after a traumatic event, not only war)
- **MRI** – magnetic resonance imaging (medical imaging; "an MRI")
- **ER** – emergency room
- **ICU** – intensive care unit
- **ENT** – ear, nose and throat doctor
- **OCD** – obsessive-compulsive disorder (unwanted thoughts + repetitive compulsions; often used loosely)

**Mental health**
- **neurodivergent** (adj) – neurological development is atypical (e.g., autistic people)
- **psychosomatic** (adj) – physical symptoms caused/worsened by mental factors – "psychosomatic headaches"
- **comorbidity** (n) – presence of more than one disorder in the same person – "obesity as a comorbidity of diabetes"
- **resilience** (n) / **resilient** (adj) – ability to recover from setbacks or trauma
- **emotional burnout** / **burnout** (n) – emotional exhaustion from chronic stress; "burnt out"; also "emotional breakdown"
- **grounding techniques** – methods (e.g., breathing exercises) to stay present and reduce anxiety

**Physical health**
- **cardiovascular** (adj) – of heart and blood vessels; "cardio" = short form – "cardiovascular exercise"
- **chronic** (adj) – persisting over time – "chronic back pain / stress"
- **inflammation** (n) – body's response to injury/infection: redness, swelling, pain – "chronic inflammation"
- **in good/great/excellent shape** – physically fit (also: economy in good shape)
- **boost your immune system** – collocation (not "raise")
- **sedentary lifestyle** – little or no physical activity
- **recumbent** (adj) – lying down

## 117. ADVANCED HOSPITAL VOCABULARY 🏨  | Words & phrases you should know

- **ER** – emergency room
- **ICU** – intensive care unit
- **EMT** – emergency medical technician (basic procedures: CPR, oxygen)
- **paramedic** (n) – advanced emergency care (IVs, drugs); more training than an EMT
- **stethoscope** (n) – instrument for listening to heart/breathing
- **gurney** (n) – wheeled stretcher for moving patients (a "stretcher" is carried by people)
- **diagnosis** (n) – identifying the present illness/problem – "You were severely dehydrated."
- **prognosis** (n) – predicted course/outcome and treatment – "Rest and you'll make a full recovery."
- **ward** (n) – separate room/area for a type of patient – "children's ward, psych ward"
- **discharge** (v) – officially let a patient leave – "discharged from the hospital" (also from the military)
- **routine checkup** (n) – regular exam to confirm health; also "annual checkup, wellness checkup"
- **syringe** (n) – hollow tube with a needle for injecting/drawing liquid
- **gown** (n) – robe worn by a patient (doctors/nurses wear scrubs)
- **IV** (n) – intravenous; needle into a vein to deliver liquids – "They hooked me up to an IV."
- **chemotherapy** (n) – drug treatment that kills fast-growing cells, used for cancer
- **malignant** (adj) – likely to grow/spread uncontrollably; cancerous – "malignant tumor"
- **benign** (adj) – mild, not threatening health/life – "benign tumor"

## 118. ADVANCED MILITARY VOCABULARY 🪖 | Words & phrases you should know

(US military vocabulary)

**Slang**
- **Joe / GI Joe** – common term for a US Army soldier
- **snake eater** – US Army Special Forces member (from Vietnam-era survival training)
- **squirter** – person assumed to be an enemy running from a military attack/cordoned area
- **diddy bopper** – Morse code intercept operator
- **shellback** – sailor who has crossed the equator (uncrossed = "pollywog")

**Acronyms**
- **AWOL** – absent without (official) leave; used generally for someone missing – "John's AWOL."
- **DMZ** – demilitarized zone; area where forces agreed not to be (e.g., between the Koreas)
- **CO** – commanding officer
- **MIA** – missing in action; casually: someone who has vanished/not shown up
- **DoD** – Department of Defense (HQ: the Pentagon)

**Other**
- **ten-hut** – contraction of "attention" (stand firm and salute)
- **officer on deck** – call announcing an officer (Navy); from *A Few Good Men*
- **army ranks (low to high)** – second lieutenant, first lieutenant, captain, major, lieutenant colonel, colonel, brigadier general, major general, lieutenant general, general, general of the army
- **deploy** (v) – move troops/equipment into position for military action – "deploy ground troops"
- **barracks** (n) – building(s) where soldiers live

## 119. ADVANCED PHARMACY VOCABULARY 💊  | Words & phrases you should know

- **prescription** (n) – paper from a doctor listing the medicine needed; stress: pre-SCRIP-tion
- **antibiotic** (n) – medicine that kills bacteria or limits their growth; stress on 4th syllable (an-ti-bi-OT-ic)
- **dose / dosage** (n) – amount of medicine to take at one time – "four doses a day"
- **over-the-counter** (adj) – sold without a prescription – "over-the-counter cough medicine"
- **generic** (adj/n) – product without a brand name/trademark (cheaper than e.g. Advil)
- **chronic** (adj) – continuing for a long time – "chronic back pain"
- **fill a prescription** – get the medicine supplied (also: drop off / pick up a prescription)
- **side effects** – unpleasant extra effects of a drug
- **active ingredient** – main ingredient producing the drug's effect (compare brand vs generic)
- **nasal spray** – liquid medicine sprayed into the nose
- **antihistamine** (n) – allergy medicine (hay fever, hives, insect bites)
- **ibuprofen** (n) – drug for fever, pain, inflammation
- **laxative** (n) – eases bowel movements (for constipation)
- **sedative** (n) – calms or helps you sleep
- **ointment** (n) – thick medicated substance applied to skin
- **steroid** (n) – artificial form of a natural chemical; used for medical conditions (e.g., steroid cream for eczema) and athletic doping
- **stimulant** (n) – drug making mind/body more active (opposite of sedative); e.g., caffeine

## 120. ADVANCED SCIENCE VOCABULARY | Words & Phrases You Should Know

- **hypothesis** (n) – idea based on known facts but not yet proved (a theory has been proved)
- **the scientific method** – process of establishing facts via testing and experiment (observation, question, hypothesis, prediction, experiment, results)
- **food chain** – who eats whom: producer, primary/secondary/tertiary consumers, apex predator, decomposers
- **deforestation** (n) – purposeful clearing of forest by people
- **equilibrium** (n) – state in which opposing forces/influences are balanced
- **meteorologist** (n) – studies and forecasts weather
- **paleontologist** (n) – studies the history of life via fossils
- **astronomer** (n) – studies the universe: planets, stars, galaxies, black holes (not astrologer)
- **botanist** (n) – studies plants
- **marine biologist** (n) – studies sea animals and plants
- **solar system** – "There are eight planets in our solar system" (Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune; Pluto demoted to dwarf planet in 2006)
- **herbivore / omnivore / carnivore** – eats plants / plants and meat / meat
- **evaporate** (v) – liquid changes to gas, esp. by heating
- **catalyst** (n) – speeds up a reaction without being changed; figuratively, cause of change – "a catalyst for growth"

## 121. ADVANCED SPRING VOCABULARY 🌼 | Words and phrases you need to know

- **gentle breeze** – collocation for light wind (not "gentle wind")
- **galoshes** (n) – waterproof covers worn over shoes (rain boots replace shoes)
- **spring break** – one-week spring school vacation (US)
- **balmy** (adj) – pleasantly warm (weather)
- **pollen** (n) – flower dust in the air that causes allergies – "The pollen gives me allergies."
- **bulb** (n) – modified stem, resting stage of certain plants (part of a flower)
- **rejuvenate** (v) – make someone look/feel young and energetic again (synonyms: renew, refresh, revitalize)
- **metamorphosis** (n) – change in physical form/appearance – "caterpillar to butterfly"
- **spring cleaning** – thorough cleaning of house/room, clearing out things
- **sprout / germinate** (v) – grow from a seed / produce buds; "sprout" is more common
- **tadpole** (n) – small water creature that develops into a frog or toad

## 122. ADVANCED VERBS FOR WORK 👔 | Vocabulary Lesson

- **expedite** (v) – make something happen more quickly – "The assembly line helped expedite the process."
- **step down** (phrasal v) – give up a position – "The CEO was asked to step down."
- **accrue** (v) – increase in amount over time – "The loan accrued interest each month."
- **cold call** (v/n) – call/visit a possible customer unasked to sell something – "We cold called people to find clients."
- **drum up** (phrasal v) – increase business activity or support – "drum up business with a promotion"
- **procrastinate** (v) – delay doing something unpleasant – "Stop procrastinating."
- **adjourn** (v) – pause/end a formal meeting or trial – "Let's adjourn the meeting."
- **implore** (v) – ask sincerely and urgently (formal "beg") – "She implored her employees to follow the guidelines."
- **vacillate** (v) – go back and forth between options, uncertain – "I vacillate between two good options."
- **enlighten** (v) – give information/understanding – "Enlighten me."
- **run out of steam** (idiom) – lose energy or interest – "He's starting to run out of steam."

## 123. ADVANCED VOCABULARY QUIZ LESSON (Live-Stream)

- **cajole** (v) – persuade with flattery – "They tried to cajole me into delaying the exam."
- **flabbergasted** (adj) – greatly surprised
- **vociferous** (adj) – expressing opinions loudly with energy/emotion; adv *vociferously* – "a vociferous argument"
- **opine** (v) – express an opinion
- **obfuscate** (v) – make something less clear/harder to understand – "obfuscated the primary issue"
- **impertinent** (adj) – lacking respect – "an impertinent question"
- **perfunctory** (adj) – done quickly without care – "a perfunctory greeting"
- **irksome** (adj) – annoying
- **homage** (n) – deep respect shown; collocation "pay homage to"
- **fabricate** (v) – create something false to deceive – "fabricate a story / income"
- **temerity** (n) – willingness to do something that may upset others – "had the temerity to go on vacation without me"
- **panacea** (n) – something that solves all problems – "technology is a panacea for climate change"

## 124. ADVANCED VOCABULARY QUIZ | Do You Know These Words?

- **contrived** (adj) – artificial, fake, unnatural – "a contrived British accent; a contrived storyline"
- **fatigued** (adj) – completely exhausted, drained – "She was so fatigued she hurt everywhere."
- **copious** (adj) – large in quantity – "copious amount; she took copious notes"
- **hypocrisy** (n) – false appearance of virtue; saying one thing, doing another; adj *hypocritical* – synonym: deceitfulness
- **supplement** (n/v) – something added to enhance something else – "vitamin supplements"
- **abhor** (v) – hate extremely – "People who seek peace abhor violence."
- **acquiesce** (v) – accept/comply without protest – "The board will acquiesce to the CEO."
- **polarize** (v) – divide into two opposing groups (often politics); *polarizing*, *polarized*
- **intrepid** (adj) – fearless, adventurous – "an intrepid explorer"
- **demure** (adj) – modest, shy, reserved; antonym: *unreserved*
- **tangible** (adj) – able to be touched; real, actual – "tangible benefits"
- **coerce** (v) – force an unwilling person to do something by force or threats – "coerce me into signing"

## 125. ADVANCED VOCAB FOR 2026! 🎉 Words & Phrases You Should Know

- **leeway** (n) – amount of freedom to move or act/decide (syn. wiggle room) – "The manager gave the team some leeway to adjust the timeline." Collocations: some / little / more / not much / a lot of leeway.
- **performative** (adj) – done for appearance/to impress, not sincere (negative) – "The company's statement felt performative rather than a genuine commitment."
- **read the room** (phrase) – sense the mood/feelings of a group and act appropriately – "He failed to read the room and made a joke during a tense meeting."
- **bandwidth** (n) – mental/emotional capacity to deal with something – "I don't have the mental bandwidth to take on another project." (+ to-verb; emotional / sufficient / enough bandwidth)
- **nascent** (adj) – only recently formed, likely to grow fast – "The nascent startup is attracting investment."
- **echo chamber** (n) – environment where you only hear opinions you already agree with – "Social media creates an echo chamber."
- **unpack** (v) – analyze/break down a complex issue into parts – "We need to unpack this issue." / "There's a lot to unpack."
- **viable** (adj) – capable of working successfully, feasible – "We need to explore all viable options." (viable plan/alternative/option; economically/financially/politically viable)
- **play devil's advocate** (idiom) – argue against an accepted idea just to stimulate debate – "Let's play devil's advocate for a moment."
- **transcend** (v) – go beyond/rise above a limit – "The film aims to transcend traditional storytelling." (transcend borders/limits)
- **zoom out** (phrasal v) – step back to see the big picture/overall context – "Let's zoom out for a moment and look at the long-term implications."
- **cognitive dissonance** (n) – mental discomfort from holding conflicting beliefs/behavior – "His vote was a clear display of political cognitive dissonance."
- **silo** (n/v/adj) – isolated system/department not sharing information; (n) also grain storage tower – "The departments worked in silos." / "Marketing became very siloed."
- **biohack** (v/n) – try to improve body/mind with tech, drugs, supplements – "Many people try to biohack their productivity with supplements."

## 126. ADVANCED WORDS, PHRASES, & IDIOMS ♠️ | Useful Gambling Vocabulary

- **The house always wins** – the casino/business always comes out ahead in the long run.
- **roll the dice** – take a risk to achieve a goal – "Let's roll the dice." (dice = plural; die = singular)
- **deck of cards** (4 suits: hearts, spades, diamonds, clubs)
- **dealer / deal** (n/v) – person who deals cards – "I'll deal the cards."
- **chips** (n) – casino tokens representing money
- **double or nothing** (phrase) – bet all winnings again (UK: double or quits)
- **double down** – double your bet (blackjack); also strengthen commitment to a risky strategy – "He didn't admit he was wrong; he doubled down."
- **bluff** (n/v) – attempt to deceive someone about abilities/intentions – "His bet is just a bluff."
- **hit the jackpot** – win a huge prize / big success – "He played the lottery and hit the jackpot."
- **hedge your bets** – protect against loss by supporting more than one outcome – "They hedged their bets with a big bet on the home team and a small one on the other."
- **in the cards** (US; UK: on the cards) – very likely to happen – "I think marriage is in the cards."
- **put your cards on the table** – be honest, hide nothing.
- **all bets are off** – impossible to predict what will happen – "If he becomes CEO, all bets are off."
- **luck of the draw** – result of chance, not control.
- **have an ace up your sleeve** – a secret advantage/weapon held in reserve.
- **let the chips fall where they may** – allow events to happen without trying to change them.
- **stack the deck** (US; UK: stack the cards) – arrange things dishonestly in your favor.
- Games: **blackjack** (reach 21), **slot machines**, **craps** (two dice; 7/11 win, 2/3/12 lose), **roulette**, **poker** (Texas hold'em), **baccarat** (player vs banker).

## 127. ADVERB CLAUSES | All the Grammar You Need to Know

- Adverb clause = group of words (subject + verb) acting as an adverb; mostly modifies verbs (also adjectives/adverbs). Always a dependent clause; starts with a subordinating conjunction.
- Position: beginning (comma after it), middle (surrounded by commas), or end (no comma). "After he brushes his teeth, he goes to bed." / "I won't continue unless you hit like."
- Place (where): wherever, where – "Wherever you go, that's where you are."
- Time (when): before, after, when, while, as soon as, whenever, by the time – "I watched Netflix while I created this lesson."
- Reason (why): because, since, as, now that, due to – "Can you answer since you're so smart?"
- Condition: if, even if, only if, unless, in case – "Even if you're not on the list, you can comment."
- Purpose: so that, in order that, in case, for fear that – "I'll write a note in case I forget."
- Contrast: although, even though, though, whereas, while – "Although you just started learning, your skills are amazing."
- "Now that + present perfect/present": "Now that we have a car, we don't take the bus."
- "unless" = if not: "I can't help you unless you tell me what happened."
- "By the time" + simple past, main clause in past perfect: "By the time I arrived, my friends had already begun eating."

## 128. ADVERB VOCABULARY QUIZ  | Advanced English Words

- **briskly** (adv) – with great speed, focused – "He walked briskly to work."
- **simultaneously** (adv) – at the same time – "The letters and packages went out simultaneously."
- **generously** (adv) – unselfishly giving/sharing – "People generously volunteered to build houses."
- **deliberately** (adv) – intentionally, on purpose – "He deliberately gave me wrong directions."
- **mockingly** (adv) – in a way that makes fun of someone – "He imitated his boss's voice mockingly."
- **relatively** (adv) – to a certain degree, compared with others – "Tomorrow will be relatively cool." (common in weather talk)
- **wistfully** (adv) – with sad longing for the past/something out of reach (syn. longingly) – "He wistfully remembered his childhood playground."
- **solemnly** (adv) – very serious/formal – "I solemnly swear to tell the truth."
- **sheepishly** (adv) – embarrassed, from shame or lack of confidence – "He smiled sheepishly."
- **surreptitiously** (adv) – secretly, avoiding notice (antonym: openly) – "The politicians surreptitiously passed a law."

## 129. ADVICE for English Teachers & English Learners + Vocabulary

- **take (something) with a grain of salt** (idiom) – don't believe it fully; doubt it.
- **get off on the right foot** (idiom) – start a relationship/job in a positive way.
- **first impression** (n) – the initial opinion someone forms about you.
- **this goes without saying** (phrase) – it's obvious; you already know it.
- **eclectic** (adj) – drawing from a variety of sources – "an eclectic approach / eclectic taste".
- **a fact of life** (phrase) – something true and unavoidable – "Mistakes are a fact of life."
- **tune out** (phrasal v) – stop paying attention.
- **there's always room for improvement** (collocation) – there is space to get better.
- Advice gist: keep a positive attitude (it's infectious); know your audience (needs assessment: student feedback, quizzes); be prepared (syllabus, lesson plans, routine, transparent grading); use varied methods (direct method, Total Physical Response, CLIL) and varied listening input; embrace mistakes and learn from them; "a teacher never says anything once" (repeat); aim for continuous improvement, not perfection; use reflective teaching (peer observation); learn from everyone.

## 130. AMERICAN ENGLISH ACCENT TRAINING 🇺🇸 (Speak Naturally & Easily)

Dialogue: "What's up with the bus? Should we keep waiting around?" / "It's your call, but I think we may want to consider other options. We're going to have to get an Uber if we want to make it on time. I'm going to see if I can find one on the app."
- **what's up with ~** – asking for an explanation of something odd/wrong.
- **it's your call** – it's your decision.
- Linking: final consonant joins next vowel ("what's up", "wait and around", "consider other options", "get an Uber", "make it on time").
- Co-articulation: overlapping sounds ("with the": voiceless th into voiced th).
- G-dropping: "waiting" -> "waitin'"; the t before n can become a glottal stop (kitten, mountain).
- Assimilation: t + y -> "ch" ("it's your" -> "it's chyour").
- Flap T: t between vowels sounds like soft d ("but I" -> "budI", "get an", "make it on").
- Reductions: want to -> wanna ("may want to"); going to -> gonna; have to -> hafta; I'm going to -> "I'mma".
- Elision: d/t after n can drop ("find" -> "fine"; "can't find").
- "the": weak form (thuh) before consonants, strong form (thee) before vowels.

## 131. AMERICAN FAST SPEECH 🇺🇸 (Boost your speaking fluency & listening comprehension)

Dialogue: "Did you know we're not going to finish on time?" / "I should have seen this coming from a mile away." / "What do you think we ought to do now?" / "We're going to have to go back to square one."
- **see something coming from a mile away** (idiom) – foresee it; it was obvious.
- **go back to square one** (idiom) – start over from the beginning (= go back to the drawing board).
- **should have** (modal of lost opportunity) – expresses regret.
- Assimilation: "did you" -> "didja"; "don't you" -> "dontcha".
- Reductions: going to -> gonna; should have -> "shoulda"; ought to -> "otta"; unstressed "do you" -> "d'ya"; unstressed "to" -> "tuh".
- Linking: "finish on", "from a mile away".
- Co-articulation: "on time" (N releases into T); "seen this".
- G-dropping: "coming" -> "comin'".
- Elision: t/d dropped ("what do you" -> "what d'ya"); flap T in "ought to".
- Unstressed function words reduce to schwa.

## 132. AMERICAN FAST SPEECH 🇺🇸 (Improve your speaking fluency & listening comprehension)

Dialogue: "He's been in the kitchen cooking up a storm." / "I don't want to bother him, but I really want a bite to eat." / "Don't you want to wait until he's done?" / "I'm going to try to stay out of his way."
- **(do something) up a storm** – informal: do it with lots of energy, often skillfully (cooking/dancing/talking up a storm).
- **a bite to eat** – a small meal/snack.
- **stay out of his way** – avoid getting in someone's way.
- Linking: "been in", "cooking up a", "wait until", "out of his".
- G-dropping: "cooking" -> "cookin'".
- Reductions: want to -> wanna; going to -> gonna (-> "I'mma"); unstressed "to" -> "tuh".
- Elision: h in he/him/his/her disappears mid-sentence ("bother him" -> "bothe-rim"); t after n drops (interactive, center, internet); "want a" and "wanna" sound the same.
- Assimilation: "don't you" -> "dontcha".
- Flap T: "wait until", "out of".
- Yes/no questions have rising intonation at the end.

## 133. AMERICAN IDIOMS 🇺🇸  Important phrases you need to know

- **blow off some steam** – release strong feelings/energy (e.g., by exercising) – "I go for a long run to blow off some steam."
- **out of your league** – too good/attractive/expensive for someone – "She's definitely out of your league."
- **down to the wire** – the very last minute of a close, suspenseful contest – "The game is coming down to the wire."
- **hit someone up (for something)** – informally ask for something, esp. money – "He's always hitting me up for cash."
- **twist someone's arm** – aggressively persuade someone reluctant – "No need to twist my arm; I'll do it."
- **under the weather** – feeling slightly sick – "I'm feeling a little under the weather."
- **in a pickle** – in a difficult situation – "Can you help me? I'm in a pretty pickle."
- **up your alley** – exactly the kind of thing you know/like – "This game is right up your alley."
- **out of the loop** – unaware of information / not part of decisions (opposite: in the loop) – "Don't ask Wes, he's out of the loop."
- **behind the eight ball** – in a difficult/worse position – "We didn't start immediately and now we're behind the eight ball."
- **Monday morning quarterback** – someone who criticizes past decisions with hindsight – "He likes to play Monday morning quarterback."
- **stick your neck out (for someone)** – take a risk to help – "I stuck my neck out for you at the meeting."
- **get something off your chest** – confess something that has been bothering you – "I have something to get off my chest."
- **ballpark figure** – rough estimate – "Can you give me a ballpark figure?"
- **call it a day** – stop working because you've done enough – "I think it's time to call it a day."

## 134. AMERICAN IDIOM #QUIZ 🇺🇸 3 Idioms to Build Your Vocabulary

- **the home stretch** – the final part of an activity – "The horses are coming down the home stretch."
- **blow off some steam** – release pent-up energy/feelings – "I go for a run to blow off some steam."
- **off the beaten path** – not known or visited by many people – "This beach is off the beaten path."

## 135. AMERICAN SLANG WORDS 🇺🇸  QUIZ LESSON

- **dude** (n) – a guy; mostly male, but can address a woman too – "Dude, what are you doing?"
- **give props** (phr) – give recognition/respect – "I want to give you props for that project."
- **moon** (v) – expose your butt on purpose as a childish joke – "He mooned the cars."
- **be down (to)** (adj) – be willing/okay to do something – "Are you down to go with me?"
- **crash** (v) – go to sleep suddenly from exhaustion – "I'm so tired, I just want to crash."
- **janky** (adj) – messed up, poor quality (East Coast) – "That old phone is janky."
- **sketchy** (adj) – suspicious, untrustworthy – "This plan seems sketchy."
- **call shotgun** (phr) – claim the front passenger seat – "I call shotgun!"
- **bum** (v) – borrow/get something (often not returned) – "Can I bum a few dollars?"
- **lit** (adj) – exciting – "This party is lit."
- **bail on** (phr v) – leave suddenly/unexpectedly – "Don't bail on me." (ditch = leave, no "on": "Don't ditch me")
- **my bad** (phr) – my mistake (small mistakes only; informal) – "Oh, my bad."
- **sweet** (adj) – nice/good/great – "Oh, sweet!"
- **flake** (n) – unreliable person who doesn't do what they say – "Don't be such a flake."
- **bromance** (n) – close non-romantic friendship between two men (said by others) – "Those two are a bromance."
- Also mentioned: **no-show** (n) – someone who doesn't turn up; **weasel** (n) – untrustworthy, tricky person; **jump the shark** (idiom) – a show becomes ridiculous/unrealistic.
