- **herd** – deer (also elephants)
- **caravan** – camels
- **pod** – dolphins
- **murder** – crows
- **parade** – elephants
- **colony** – ants
- **pride** – lions
- **tower** – giraffes

## 137. ANSWERING YOUR DIFFICULT GRAMMAR QUESTIONS ✅

- **Future time clauses:** the future is shown by the main (independent) clause; the dependent clause (after/before/when/while...) uses present simple, not will. "After we eat dinner, the show is going to begin." / "When the lesson ends, I will hit the like button."
- **Prepositions:** most common top five: of, in, to, for, with (plus on/at etc.). Grouped as time/place/direction; also follow verbs and adjectives. Best way to learn: read a lot to absorb correct usage.
- **Sentence transformation (IELTS-style):** rewrite with same meaning using different structures. Simple (S+V): "I study English on Sundays." Compound (2 independent clauses + conjunction): "I work on Saturday and I study English on Sundays." Complex (dependent + independent): "After I work out, I study English." Use a variety and add details.
- **Present perfect:** unspecified past time linked to now – "I have traveled to Thailand." With a specific time use past simple – "I traveled there last year."
- **Past perfect:** earlier of two past events; past simple in the dependent clause, past perfect in the main clause – "By the time they arrived, we had already eaten."
- **Causative verbs** (make, cause, allow, help, let, have, keep, require): verb + person/thing + base verb – "My boss made me work late." "Wes helped Yana cook dinner."
- **Direct vs indirect object** (transitive verbs): direct object answers "what/whom does S verb?"; indirect answers "to/for whom?" – "Wes gives Yana a present": a present = direct, Yana = indirect.
- **That-clause:** dependent clause starting with "that" after verbs/adjectives/nouns; "that" is often optional and can be dropped – "He knows (that) the match is over." "I'm glad (that) you joined me."

## 138. AUTUMN IDIOMS & EXPRESSIONS IN ENGLISH

- **a walk in the park** (idiom) – something easy and pleasant to do – "The test was a walk in the park."
- **turn over a new leaf** (idiom) – start anew, a fresh beginning – "Kids start school in September, turning over a new leaf."
- **the apple never falls far from the tree** (idiom) – a person's traits are similar to their parents' – "I'm bad at remembering place names, just like my father."

## 139. A Complete Advanced English Makeover: 5 Hours, 250+ Words, Native-Level Vocabulary!

Adjectives to describe places (landscape)
- **serene** (adj) – calm and peaceful – "The landscape appeared serene."
- **stunning** (adj) – extremely beautiful – "stunning views"
- **picturesque** (adj) – visually attractive like a picture (-esque = resembling) – "The view was picturesque."
- **desolate** (adj) – empty and unattractive (negative) – "After the fire, the forest looked desolate."
- **unspoiled** (adj) – beautiful because not damaged by people/development – "an unspoiled rainforest"
- **pristine** (adj) – in very good condition, spotless – "pristine condition"

Atmosphere
- **tranquil** (adj) – peaceful and calm, no noise (feeling; serene = look) – "a tranquil beach"
- **vibrant** (adj) – exciting, energetic – "Carnival is a vibrant festival."
- **homey** (adj) – comfortable and cozy (for places like a hotel) – "a homey atmosphere"
- **bustling** (adj) – very active and busy – "Times Square is bustling with tourists."

Cities/towns
- **renowned (for)** (adj) – famous for – "Paris is renowned for the Eiffel Tower."
- **cosmopolitan** (adj) – including people from many countries – "Hong Kong is a cosmopolitan city."
- **walkable** (adj) – easy/safe to walk in – "easily walkable"
- **overcrowded** (adj) – too many people/things – "The old town was overcrowded."
- **spacious** (adj) – large with lots of open space – "Central Park is spacious."
- **charming** (adj) – pleasant, attractive – "a charming little street"
- **quaint** (adj) – attractive because old-fashioned – "quaint little cafes"

Buildings/landmarks
- **iconic** (adj) – very famous, symbolic – "an iconic landmark"
- **contemporary** (adj) – belonging to the present, modern – "contemporary architecture"
- **majestic** (adj) – beautiful, powerful, inspiring admiration – "The Golden Gate Bridge is majestic."
- **gaudy** (adj) – unpleasantly bright/showy – "gaudy casinos"
- **dilapidated** (adj) – old and in poor condition – "dilapidated buildings"

Location
- **out of the way** (adj phr) – far from where people usually go – "an out of the way restaurant"
- **off the beaten path/track** (idiom) – not well known or popular (positive) – "The park was off the beaten path."
- **godforsaken** (adj) – unattractive, nothing pleasant – "I want to get out of this godforsaken place."

Verbs of movement
- **strut** (v) – walk confidently/arrogantly
- **stagger** (v) – walk with great difficulty, almost falling (e.g. drunk) – "He is staggering."
- **bolt** (v) – move very fast, almost run – "I bolted to the airplane."
- **slip (by/past/inside/outside)** (v) – move quietly so as not to be noticed
- **drag one's feet** (idiom) – move/progress reluctantly and slowly – "Stop dragging your feet."
- **hitchhike** (v) – get a free ride from passing vehicles
- **carpool** (v) – travel by car in a small group to save money/environment – "We carpool to work."
- **leapfrog** (v) – take turns moving ahead of each other – "We kept leapfrogging each other on the interstate."
- **swerve** (v) – suddenly change direction – "swerve to the left"
- **soar** (v) – fly high – "The plane is soaring."
- **glide** (v) – smooth continuous motion (bird, swan)
- **slither** (v) – move by sliding and twisting (snake)
- **wobble** (v) – move side to side with poor balance (penguin)
- **gallop** (v) – run with all four feet off the ground (horse)
- **wiggle** (v) – small quick movements back and forth (puppy)

Verbs for situations
- **approach** (v) – get nearer to; deal with a problem – "How are we going to approach this issue?"
- **circumvent** (v) – avoid, usually cleverly – "circumvent the tax laws"
- **smuggle** (v) – move something secretly, often illegal
- **lag (behind)** (v) – make slow progress, be behind others
- **spiral (out of control)** (v) – get worse and harder to control

Nouns for people
- **amateur** (n) – does an activity for fun, not a professional
- **tutor** (n) – gives extra help studying outside school
- **scholar** (n) – studies a subject in great detail, esp. at university
- **big fish / big wig** (n) – powerful, important person – "He's a big wig at the company."
- **one's right hand / right-hand man** (n) – person who supports you at work – "She's my right hand."
- **valedictorian** (n) – student with highest grades, gives graduation speech
- **dilettante** (n) – interested in a subject but no deep understanding – "a political dilettante"
- **aficionado** (n) – very enthusiastic expert/fan – "a soccer aficionado"
- **confidant** (n) – person you trust with secrets
- **proponent (of)** (n) – publicly supports a plan/action – "a proponent of saving dolphins"
- **guru** (n) – spiritual leader/wise teacher
- **gourmet** (n) – knows a lot about food, likes fancy food
- **humanitarian** (n) – helps people who are suffering
- **coward** (n) – easily afraid, not brave
- **introvert** (n) – quiet, reserved, likes being alone
- **extrovert** (n) – very social, talkative
- **perfectionist** (n) – thinks everything must be perfect
- **purist** (n) – believes in doing things the traditional way
- **whiz** (n) – very smart in an area – "a math whiz"

Adjectives for people
- **overzealous** (adj) – too eager/enthusiastic – "overzealous fans ran onto the stage"
- **tenacious** (adj) – refuses to give up (positive) – "a tenacious student"
- **candid** (adj) – truthful, honest – "a candid response"
- **facetious** (adj) – joking, not serious (often inappropriately); /fəˈsiːʃəs/ – "I'm just being facetious."
- **obtuse** (adj) – slow to understand; refuses to see the obvious – "He's being obtuse."
- **boorish** (adj) – rude, insensitive – "boorish behavior" (not related to "boring")
- **jittery** (adj) – nervous, unable to relax
- **capricious** (adj) – impulsive, unpredictable; /kəˈprɪʃəs/ – "capricious weather"
- **gullible** (adj) – easily persuaded to believe things – "I fell for the scam, I was so gullible."
- **adamant (about)** (adj) – refusing to change one's mind – "adamant about protecting the environment"
- **feckless** (adj) – weak, ineffective, lacking character – "feckless leadership"
- **charismatic** (adj) – attracts and influences people easily (not always good)
- **chatty** (adj) – likes to talk a lot, friendly/informal (used about others)
- **surly** (adj) – unfriendly, in a bad mood
- **levelheaded** (adj) – calm, handles difficult situations well
- **easygoing** (adj) – relaxed, not easily worried or upset
- **absent-minded** (adj) – easily forgets, not paying attention
- **above average / below average** (phr) – higher/lower than the usual level – "an above average English teacher"
- **grumpy** (adj) – easily annoyed, complaining

Nouns/phrases for people
- **grump** (n) – unfriendly person who complains (a "grumpy grump")
- **jack of all trades** (n) – capable of doing many different jobs well
- **dark horse** (n) – unexpected winner
- **black sheep** (n) – member who embarrasses/differs from the family or group
- **know-it-all** (n) – thinks they know everything (negative)
- **wise guy** (n) – tries to be clever in an annoying way – "Stop being such a wise guy."
- **stick in the mud** (n) – old-fashioned person who avoids fun and spoils it for others
- **yes man** (n) – always agrees to please a superior
- **sitter** (n) – babysitter, house sitter, dog/cat/pet sitter

C1 phrases (with pronunciation tips)
- **all things considered** (phr) – in general, taking all aspects into account – "The wedding was a success, all things considered." (link "all things")
- **bit by bit** (phr) – gradually (like "little by little", B2) – "growing the channel bit by bit"
- **before you know it** (phr) – very soon – "The baby will be here before you know it." (link "know it")
- **out of the blue** (phr) – completely unexpected (cf. "out of nowhere", B2) – flap T, "out of"
- **at a glance** (phr) – immediately/quickly (also "at first glance")
- **have something against someone** (phr) – dislike someone for a reason – "I have nothing against him." / "What do you have against her?"
- **look on the bright side** (phr) – find good in a bad situation (follow with a reason)
- **take issue with** (phr) – strongly disagree – "I take issue with that."
- **keep a low profile** (phr) – avoid attracting attention
- **in accordance with** (phr) – in a way that follows a rule/law (formal); "-dance" sounds like "dunce"
- **back road(s)** (n) – small road with little traffic
- **bear left / bear right** (phr v) – change direction slightly (bear = homonym, not the animal)

Verbs: C1 with stress
- **differentiate (between)** (v) – find the difference between two things; 5 syllables, stress 3rd – "differentiate between facts and opinions"
- **broaden** (v) – increase the range; /ˈbrɔːdn/ stress 1st – "broaden your horizons / mind / the scope"
- **grasp** (v) – hold firmly; understand; "grasp an opportunity"; "grasp at straws" = try anything though it won't help
- **diminish** (v) – reduce in size/importance; stress 2nd
- **fall flat** (idiom) – (joke/idea) fail to have the intended effect
- **contradict** (v) – say the opposite of; (facts) conflict with; stress 3rd – "contradict yourself"
- **set foot in** (idiom) – enter/go to a place (emotional emphasis) – "I refuse to set foot in his home."
- **allocate** (v) – distribute for a purpose; stress 1st – "allocate resources/funds/blame"; allocate for/to
- **collaborate** (v) – work with others; stress 2nd – collaborate with/on/to/in
- **generalize** (v) – say something is true in all cases based on some cases; stress 1st
- **lose your head** (idiom) – lose control/not stay calm (≠ lose your mind = go crazy)
- **facilitate** (v) – make possible or easier; stress 2nd – "facilitate learning/growth/communication"

C1 adjectives with pronunciation
- **cutting edge** (adj) – very modern, newest features – "cutting edge technology"
- **affluent** (adj) – wealthy; stress 1st (2nd also heard) – "an affluent neighborhood"
- **competent** (adj) – able to do something well – "competent at his job"
- **day-to-day** (adj) – happening every day as a regular part of life/job
- **monotonous** (adj) – never changing, boring; stress 2nd, glottal t
- **alleged** (adj) – believed but not proved – "alleged crime"; /əˈledʒd/ or /əˈledʒɪd/
- **fruitful** (adj) – producing good results – "a fruitful discussion"
- **conservative** (adj) – dislikes/distrusts change; stress 2nd
- **liberal** (adj) – respects many beliefs/behaviors; often 2 syllables
- **idealistic** (adj) – believes very good things can be achieved; words ending in -ic stress the syllable before
- **preliminary** (adj) – coming before a more important event – "preliminary hearing"; stress 2nd
- **genuine** (adj) – honest and sincere; stress 1st, "-ine" sounds like "in"

C2 phrases
- **clear the air** (idiom) – remove bad feelings between people
- **trial and error** (phr) – solve by trying methods and learning from mistakes
- **be at odds over/with** (phr) – disagree/be different from – "I'm at odds with his version."
- **beyond (a shadow of) a doubt** (phr) – with certainty; "beyond a reasonable doubt" (legal)
- **play it by ear** (idiom) – decide as things develop, no fixed plan
- **go with the flow** (idiom) – do what others do/accept what's easiest
- **put one's foot down** (idiom) – use authority to stop something – "I'm putting my foot down."
- **down the drain** (idiom) – work/money wasted
- **come under fire** (idiom) – be criticized
- **jump to conclusions** (idiom) – guess the facts without enough information – "Let's not jump to conclusions."
- **out of one's depth** (idiom) – lacking the knowledge/skills for a situation
- **do someone/something justice** (idiom) – represent fairly/accurately (often negative) – "Pictures don't do the Grand Canyon justice."

C2 verbs
- **relish** (v) – enjoy a lot; + gerund/noun – "relish the thought/prospect/chance of..."
- **coincide (with)** (v) – happen at the same time; be the same – "It coincides with a business trip."; stress 3rd
- **squander** (v) – waste money/opportunities – "squander an opportunity"
- **commemorate** (v) – remember officially with a ceremony/statue; stress 2nd
- **gasp** (v) – take a short quick breath (shock); breathe with difficulty – "gasping for air"; past /gæspt/
- **intervene** (v) – get involved to improve/prevent worsening; stress 3rd
- **label** (v) – describe someone (often unfairly) – "labeled as a troublemaker"
- **imply** (v) – communicate an idea without saying it directly – "What are you implying?"
- **infer** (v) – conclude something from available information – "You'd infer it's raining if I'm soaking wet." (often confused with imply)

**infer** (v) – deduce from evidence; stress 2nd syllable (don't confuse with *imply* = suggest without saying) – "You were inferring; he was implying."
**speculate** (v) – guess answers without enough information – "Journalists are speculating what the president may say."
**succumb** (v) – 1) give in / lose the will to resist; 2) die or suffer badly from illness/injury; silent b, stress 2nd syllable – "succumb to injuries"
**trudge** (v) – walk slowly with great effort (up/down/over/through) – "trudge through the snow"; rhymes with *judge*

**awe** (n) – great respect mixed with fear/surprise; /ɔ/ like *saw* – "in awe of his grandfather"
**bureaucracy** (n) – complicated, unnecessary rules/processes in organizations (negative); 4 syllables, stress 2nd – "too much bureaucracy"
**frenzy** (n) – uncontrolled excited behavior – "shopping frenzy", "feeding frenzy", "whip someone into a frenzy"
**cliche** (n/adj) – overused, unoriginal saying; French loan, stress 2nd, "-che" = /ʃeɪ/ – "I know it's cliche, but time heals all wounds."
**paradigm** (n) – model/clear typical example; silent g, long "I" in last syllable – "paradigm shift", "a new paradigm"
**momentum** (n) – force of moving/developing progress; stress 2nd; US often drops the t after n – "gain/lose momentum"
**morale** (n) – confidence level of a person/group; stress 2nd, short a – "high/low morale", "raise/boost morale"
**withdrawal** (n) – taking money out of the bank; stress 2nd, then dark l – "make a withdrawal"
**deterrent** (n) – something that stops people from doing something out of fear; stress 2nd, true t (not flap) – "act as a deterrent to cheating", "nuclear deterrent"
**ambiguity** (n) – more than one possible meaning; 5 syllables, stress on "gu" (words ending -ity: stress 3rd from last) – "so there's no ambiguity"
**diarrhea** (n) – liquid waste illness; tricky spelling (UK: diarrhoea); stress 3rd syllable
**whereabouts** (n) – the place where someone/something is; stress 1st – "the criminal's whereabouts"; also adverb in questions: "Whereabouts are you from?"

**ambiguous** (adj) – having more than one possible meaning – "The contract is ambiguous."
**complimentary** (adj) – 1) praising; 2) free of charge – "complimentary snacks"
**handy** (adj) – useful, convenient – "come in handy"
**indispensable** (adj) – so useful you can't manage without – "indispensable part of the team"
**legitimate** (adj) – 1) allowed by law; 2) reasonable/acceptable – "a legitimate argument"
**questionable** (adj) – not certain, possibly wrong – "questionable taste", "ethically questionable"
**grim** (adj) – worrying and without hope – "The future looks grim."
**coherent** (adj) – clear, logically connected; (of a person) understandable – "He's not coherent" / "incoherent"
**dubious** (adj) – not completely true or trustworthy – "a dubious email"
**compulsive** (adj) – doing something excessively, can't stop – "compulsive gambler/liar"
**pushy** (adj) – unpleasantly forcing someone – "a pushy salesperson"
**carefree** (adj) – no worries – "carefree days"
**conceited** (adj) – too proud of yourself (syn. arrogant, full of yourself)
**rigid** (adj) – stiff, fixed, not bendable – "rigid rules"
**feeble** (adj) – weak, without strength/power – "a feeble old man", "a feeble government"

**turn over a new leaf** – start fresh, change your attitude/circumstances
**the autumn years** – the later part of someone's life
**drive someone nuts** – make someone crazy
**under the weather** – slightly sick
**neck of the woods** – a particular area/neighborhood – "in my neck of the woods"
**not out of the woods** – not yet out of danger
**shake like a leaf** – tremble, usually from fear
**get wind of (something)** – hear a rumor/information about it
**the apple of someone's eye** – the person loved most
**cold turkey** – quit a bad habit suddenly and completely

**The ball is in your court** – it's your move/decision now
**get off the hook** – escape responsibility/punishment – "You're not getting off the hook that easy."
**go to bat for someone** – defend/support someone
**give it your best shot** – try your hardest
**the home stretch** – the final part of something
**not up to par** – not good enough
**throw in the towel** – give up
**blindsided** (by) – caught off guard by something unexpected
**slam dunk** – a sure thing, easy to accomplish
**call the shots** – make the decisions

**beat around the bush** – speak indirectly, avoid the point
**bark up the wrong tree** – make a mistake, pursue the wrong thing
**go with the flow** – accept the situation and relax
**nip (something) in the bud** – stop it at an early stage
**knock on wood** – hoping for good luck
**live under a rock** – be ignorant of well-known news – "Have you been living under a rock?"
**grassroots** (adj) – at the basic level of a movement/cause – "a grassroots campaign"

**fancy someone** (BrE) – like someone and enjoy their company
**fall for someone** – develop romantic feelings
**head over heels (in love)** – completely, deeply in love
**lovey-dovey** – very affectionate (often too much)
**be smitten (with)** – strongly attracted
**get hitched** – get married
**tie the knot** – get married
**go through a (bit of a) rough patch** – a period of relationship problems
**double date** – two couples going out together
**on the rocks** – (relationship) in difficulty, likely to fail

**pardon my French** – apology after swearing
**weasel out of (something)** – avoid it dishonestly
**hold your horses** – be patient, wait
**the pot calling the kettle black** – hypocrisy
**the whole kit and caboodle** – absolutely everything
**tongue-in-cheek** – not serious, ironic/joking
**wet behind the ears** – inexperienced
**gung-ho** – very eager and enthusiastic
**mind your P's and Q's** – mind your manners
**a bull in a china shop** – clumsy, careless person
**bite the dust** – die, fail, come to an end
**Bob's your uncle** – "and there it is, done" (after instructions; UK/Ireland)

**window-shop** – look in store windows without buying
**rip-off** (n) / **rip (someone) off** – something overpriced; to cheat on price – "$4 for an avocado is a rip-off."
**shop till you drop** – shop until exhausted
**retail therapy** – shopping to feel better
**bargain hunting** – looking for good deals
**shoplift** (v) – steal from a store
**fit like a glove** – fit perfectly
**shopping spree** – buying a lot in a short time
**pay through the nose** – pay far too much

**a piece of cake** – very easy
**run late / run behind** – be late – "I'm running a little behind schedule."
**get the hang of (it)** – learn how to do something
**all set / good to go** – ready
**sound like a broken record** – repeat the same thing again and again
**pitch in** – help as part of a group
**blow off some steam** – release pent-up energy/anger
**it's not rocket science** – it's not difficult
**table (something)** (v) – postpone a discussion (US business)
**jump on the bandwagon** – join something because it's trendy
**sit in the nosebleed section** – cheapest, highest seats far from the action
**fall through the cracks** – get overlooked

**between vs among** – between = two things/one-to-one ("between you and me", "between a rock and a hard place"); among = three or more/a group ("among friends"); *amongst* is older ("talk amongst yourselves")
**reluctant vs reticent** – reluctant = unwilling to do something; reticent = shy/reserved in manner
**achieve vs accomplish** – achieve = succeed in reaching a goal ("achieve fluency"); accomplish = finish a task/mission
**customer vs client** – customer buys products/services; client receives professional services (lawyer); companies can be either
**figuratively vs literally** – figuratively = not real, for effect; literally = really happened ("we were literally just talking about you"); people often misuse *literally* for emphasis
**e.g. vs i.e.** – e.g. = for example (a few examples); i.e. = that is / specifically
**consecutive vs concurrent** – consecutive = one after another without a break; concurrent = at the same time

**burn up vs burn down** – both = destroyed by fire; burn down = a structure collapses/destroyed (past: "burned down"); burn up = consumed, smaller items, or intense heat ("burn up calories", "burning up" with fever)
**walk up vs walk down (the street)** – interchangeable; up/down may follow uphill/downhill; *walk down the street* more common; *walk up to* = approach with confidence
**take off vs lift off** – take off = leave the ground (planes, birds, anything); lift off = rise straight up (rocket, helicopter, drone)
**used to + base verb** – past habit/state no longer true: "I used to live in Korea." Question/negative use "use to": "Did you use to…?", "I didn't use to…" (pronunciation identical); no change with subject
**be/get used to + -ing/noun** – be accustomed to: "I'm used to hot weather", "get used to it"

**homophones** (same sound, different spelling/meaning):
flower / flour; addition / edition; click / clique (exclusive group); patients / patience (/ˈpeɪʃəns/); moose / mousse; metal / meddle (US flap t); horse / hoarse (rough voice); principal / principle; guerrilla / gorilla; core / corps (silent p and s, from French); sweet / suite (/swiːt/); seas / sees / seize; eight / ate

**in / on / at (place)**
- in = enclosed/within larger area: in a town, car/taxi, in water, in class, in a book/dictionary, in the city center, in your pockets
- on = surface/line: on the table, floor (on the 7th floor), road/river/coast (on the east coast), public transport and bikes (on a bus/plane/bike), on the left/right, on a page/slide, on Netflix/YouTube
- at = specific point: at the front door, address (at 44 Main St, but on Main St), shops/public places (at the mall), events (at a party), school/work/home, at the beginning/end, at the front of the plane
- in the lake = in the water; on the lake = on a boat; at the lake = location

**say vs tell** – say = words/information, no listener needed ("The report says…"); tell + person ("She told me to…"); fixed: tell a lie/secret/joke/story/the truth

## 140. A Proven Way to Effectively Build Your Vocabulary | CHUNKING

**chunking** (learn phrases as units, not single words)
- Learn the number of words, their order, and pronounce them together; no need for grammar or each word's meaning ("Que tengas un buen día").
- Chunk can be a partial sentence: "every now and then" = sometimes (only at start or end of sentence).
- Benefits: faster listening/reading comprehension; natural, fluent speaking ("How are you? / I'm doing well" instead of "I'm fine, thank you, and you?"); learn collocations ("strong coffee", "strong thunderstorm", but "powerful film").

## 141. A vs. AN - Learn this ONE rule | Articles in English

- Rule is about sound, not spelling: **a** before consonant sounds, **an** before vowel sounds.
- a: a book, a job, a unicorn (/j/ sound), a URL, a watermelon, a coconut.
- an: an English book, an igloo, an ostrich, an acorn, an X-ray, an honest mistake (silent h).
- "herb": US "an herb" (silent h); UK "a herb".
- "a" is usually the schwa /ə/; stressed /eɪ/ for emphasis ("a disaster", "We need a plan").

## 142. Abbreviations & Acronyms to Build Your Vocabulary

- **Abbreviation** = umbrella term. Types: contraction (Mt. = Mount; Dr.; Mr.; St.; Ave.; Blvd.; Dept.), truncation (Sat., Mon., pt., km, gal., Aug.), acronym (pronounced as a word), initialism (letters said individually).
- Contractions/truncations are written, then read out as the full word (Sat. is not read "sat").
- Acronyms: **ASAP** (as soon as possible), **PIN** (personal identification number), **VAT** (value-added tax), **AWOL** (absent without leave; military, now means disappearing without permission), **POTUS** (President of the United States), **FLOTUS** (First Lady of the United States).
- Initialisms: **FBI** (Federal Bureau of Investigation), **UFO** (unidentified flying object), **DIY** (do it yourself; "a DIY book"), **ADD/ADHD** (attention deficit (hyperactivity) disorder), **BYOB** (bring your own beer; "the party is BYOB", also BYOC etc.).
- Units: F = Fahrenheit, C = Celsius.

## 143. Accent & Pronunciation Training | 25 Sentences to Enhance Your Fluency

- **Reductions:** going to > gonna (> "onna"), want to > wanna, let me > lemme, should/would have > "should of"/"would of" (h elides), do you > "d'you", what do you > "whaddya", to (unstressed) > "tuh".
- **Flap T (US):** t between vowels or before dark l sounds like soft d: water, bottle, later, get a, what are you.
- **Linking:** final consonant moves to next vowel: about it, look at it, think it'll, work out, done it, works at, twice already.
- **Assimilation:** d+y > "j" (did you, could you, would you); s+y > "sh" (this year's); t+y > "ch" (last year's).
- **Elision:** t dropped between consonants: next spring, asked them, first prize.
- **Intrusion:** glide sound between vowels (do I > "do-w-I").
- **Thought groups:** pause between clauses ("I would have done it | if I'd known"; "Next spring | we're planning a trip").
- Practice sentences: What are you going to do about it? / Did you get a chance to look at it? / I should have told you earlier. / Could you pass me the water bottle? / Let me know when you're ready. / What do you want to eat tonight? / I'm going to call you later. / Do you think it'll work out? / I would have done it if I'd known.
- **Clusters:** sixth, next spring, ask Chris, texts, asked them twice already, works at, first prize.
- **Tricky sounds:** th voiceless vs voiced ("three free throws", "the 33 thieves thought they thrilled the throne"); r vs l ("really rarely do I read reviews", "world leaders rarely agree", "the rural area"); s vs sh ("She sells seashells on the seashore").

## 144. Advanced (C1) #Idiom | TO FALL FLAT

**fall flat** (idiom) – a joke/idea/suggestion fails to have the intended effect – "The jokes fell flat."

## 145. Advanced (C1) #Idiom | to set foot in (some place)

**set foot in (a place)** – enter/go to a place, with emphasis or emotion – "I haven't set foot in a bar in years." "I refuse to set foot in his home."

## 146. Advanced (C1) Adjectives to Build Your Vocabulary

**cutting-edge** (adj) – very modern, with the newest features – "cutting-edge technology/research"; stress 1st, flap t, "dge" = /dʒ/
**affluent** (adj) – wealthy – "an affluent neighborhood"; stress 1st (2nd also heard)
**competent** (adj) – able to do something well – "competent at his job"; stress 1st, last syllable schwa
**day-to-day** (adj) – happening every day as part of life/job – "day-to-day responsibilities"; unstressed "to" = /tə/, stress on "day"
**monotonous** (adj) – unchanging and boring – "monotonous tasks"; stress 2nd, glottal stop in -tn- (like *mountain*)
**alleged** (adj) – believed true but not proven (bad/illegal) – "an alleged crime"; 2 or 3 syllables (/əˈledʒd/ or /əˈledʒɪd/)
**fruitful** (adj) – producing good results – "a fruitful discussion"; stress 1st
**conservative** (adj) – dislikes/distrusts change, often political; stress 2nd
**liberal** (adj) – respecting many beliefs/behaviors; stress 1st, often 2 syllables
**idealistic** (adj) – believing very good things can be achieved; 5 syllables, stress 2nd-to-last (words ending -ic)
**preliminary** (adj) – coming before and preparing for a main event – "a preliminary hearing/investigation"; stress 2nd
**genuine** (adj) – honest and sincere – "a genuine apology"; stress 1st, ends /ɪn/

## 147. Advanced (C1) Idioms to Strengthen Your Vocabulary

**set the record straight** – say/write something to make the true facts known (not "put")
**strike a balance (between A and B)** – achieve equal/proper amounts of two things – "strike a balance between work and family"; link "strike a"
**not think twice (about)** – do something immediately without hesitation – "I didn't think twice about helping."; "think twice" = consider carefully first
**at/on short notice** – with little time before it happens – "on such short notice"
**wait and see** – wait to find out what happens (also "you just wait and see" = confident prediction); spoken "wait 'n' see", glottal t
**take (something) at face value** – accept it as it appears without checking
**the best of both worlds** – enjoy the advantages of two different things at once; link "best of"
**in the public eye** – famous, seen in media (opposite: out of the public eye)
**get a grip (on yourself)** – control your emotions and calm down
**off the record** – not to be publicly reported (opposite: on the record)
**up to speed** – having the latest information – "get/bring someone up to speed"
**rule of thumb** – practical rule based on experience – "a general rule of thumb"

## 148. Advanced (C1) Phrases That Will Transform Your Speaking Skills

- **first and foremost** (phr) – most importantly – "First and foremost, build your vocabulary." (and → 'n', links: "first-n-foremost")
- **beat around the bush** (idiom) – avoid talking about the main point – "Don't beat around the bush." (flap T in "beat")
- **the next best thing** (phr) – the best option when you can't have what you want – "No ice cream, so cake is the next best thing." (t in "next/best" elided)
- **easier said than done** (phr) – sounds like a good idea but is hard to do – "I'll hike Kilimanjaro." "Easier said than done."
- **have an ear for something** (phr) – be good at hearing/repeating/understanding sounds (music, language) – "She has an ear for languages."
- **behind the times** (phr) – not fashionable/modern (negative) – "He's behind the times with new technology."
- **down to earth** (adj phr) – practical, realistic, friendly (positive) – "My father is a down-to-earth person."
- **face the music** (idiom) – accept criticism/punishment for what you did – "I broke her mug; I'll have to face the music."
- **have had enough** (phr) – want something annoying to stop (present perfect) – "I've had enough!"
- **make ends meet** (idiom) – have just enough money for needs – "struggle to / barely make ends meet"
- **go hand in hand with** (phr) – be closely related / happen together – "Wealth goes hand in hand with saving."
- **for the most part** (adv phr) – mostly, usually; can go at start/middle/end – "The lesson was, for the most part, helpful."

## 149. Advanced (C1) Phrases to Strengthen Your Vocabulary

- **not think straight** (phr) – unable to use your mind effectively – "No sleep, so I can't think straight." / "You're not thinking straight."
- **in any event** (phr) – whatever happens – "It may rain, but in any event the concert goes on." (BrE also "at all events")
- **in the event of** (phr) – if (something bad) happens – "In the event of an emergency, use the exit." (collocations: emergency, disaster, bad weather, delay)
- **of no/little consequence** (phr) – not important – "The new information is of no consequence." (also not of any/much consequence)
- **from scratch** (phr) – from the beginning, using nothing existing – "I made the lasagna from scratch." (make/build/develop/start from scratch)
- **the last straw / the final straw** (idiom) – the last in a series of bad events; you can't take any more – "That's the last straw!"
- **out of season** (phr) – fruit/veg not grown in an area at that time – "Strawberries are out of season."
- **in no time** (phr) – very quickly – "You'll build your vocabulary in no time."
- **take it from me** (phr) – believe me, I have experience – "Take it from me, practice makes you confident."
- **room for improvement** (phr) – it could get better (polite criticism) – "Your English is great, but there's room for improvement."
- **do/try your utmost** (phr) – make the greatest effort (like "do your best", more formal) – "She'd do her utmost to meet the deadline." (UT-most, stress 1st syllable)
- **put/set your mind to something** (phr) – decide to do it and put in effort – "You can do anything if you put your mind to it."

## 150. Advanced (C1) Verbs to Level Up Your Vocabulary

- **differentiate** (v) – find/show the difference between things (+ between) – "differentiate between facts and opinions" (5 syl, stress 3rd: dif-fer-EN-ti-ate)
- **broaden** (v) – increase the range of something – "broaden your horizons / mind / the scope" (stress 1st; may use glottal stop)
- **grasp** (v) – 1) take and hold firmly; 2) understand – "grasp the main idea"; idiom "grasp at straws" = try hopeless options
- **diminish** (v) – reduce in size/importance – "Muscle strength diminishes with age." (stress 2nd; can also diminish someone = make them seem less important)
- **fall flat** (idiom v) – (joke/idea) not have the intended effect – "My joke fell flat."
- **contradict** (v) – 1) say the opposite of someone/yourself; 2) (facts) be so different one must be wrong – "New evidence contradicts the theory." (stress 3rd)
- **set foot in** (idiom v) – go to/enter a place (emphatic) – "I refuse to set foot in his home."
- **allocate** (v) – distribute for a purpose (+ for/to) – "allocate funds/resources/blame" (stress 1st)
- **collaborate** (v) – work with others for a purpose (with/on/to/in) – "collaborate with a designer" (stress 2nd)
- **generalize** (v) – say something is true in all cases based on some cases – "Don't generalize about Gen Z."
- **lose your head** (idiom v) – lose control, not act calmly (vs "lose your mind" = go crazy) – "Don't lose your head."
- **facilitate** (v) – make possible/easier – "facilitate growth/learning/access/communication" (stress 2nd, c = /s/)

## 151. Advanced (C1) Vocabulary MASTERCLASS in 60 Minutes

**Phrases**
- **all things considered** – in general, taking everything into account – "All things considered, the wedding was a success."
- **bit by bit** – gradually (like "little by little", B2) – "growing the channel bit by bit"
- **before you know it** – very soon, suddenly – "The plane will land before you know it."
- **out of the blue** – completely unexpected (~"out of nowhere", B2) – "Out of the blue, she said we should move in together."
- **at a glance** – immediately/quickly – "I could tell at a glance something was wrong." (at a quick glance / at first glance)
- **have something against someone** – dislike someone for a reason – "I have nothing against him." / "What do you have against her?"
- **look on the bright side** – find good in a bad situation (follow with a reason) – "Look on the bright side: you can buy a new shirt."
- **take issue with** – strongly disagree – "We took issue with the contract changes."
- **keep a low profile** – avoid attracting attention – "I kept a low profile at the party."
- **in accordance with** – in a way that follows (a rule/law), formal – "in accordance with the new law" (-ance = /əns/)
- **backroads** – small roads with little traffic – "Take the backroads to avoid rush hour."
- **bear left / bear right** – turn slightly in that direction – "Bear left, then bear right."

**Verbs**
- **differentiate** – find the difference (between) – "differentiate between facts and opinions" (stress 3rd)
- **broaden** – widen – "broaden your horizons/mind/scope"
- **grasp** – hold firmly; understand – "grasp the main idea"; "grasp at straws"
- **diminish** – reduce in size/importance – "Memory diminishes with age."
- **fall flat** – (joke/idea) fail to have the effect – "The joke fell flat."
- **contradict** – say the opposite; (facts) conflict – "Evidence contradicts the theory."
- **set foot in** – enter a place (emphatic) – "I refuse to set foot in his home."
- **allocate** – distribute for a purpose – "allocate funds"
- **collaborate** – work with others – "collaborate on a project"
- **generalize** – claim true for all from some cases – "Don't generalize about groups."
- **lose your head** – lose control/calm – "Don't lose your head."
- **facilitate** – make easier/possible – "facilitate learning"

**Idioms**
- **on the ball** – quick to understand and react – "You need to be on the ball in the meeting."
- **with flying colors** – very successfully (pass with flying colors) – "She passed the exam with flying colors."
- **turn a blind eye (to)** – ignore something you know is wrong – "The teacher turned a blind eye to cheating."
- **the easy way out** – the easiest option in a hard situation (take the easy way out) – "Don't take the easy way out."
- **how/what/why/where on earth** – expresses surprise/confusion/anger – "What on earth is that?"
- **in light of** – because of, as a result of (new information) – "In light of recent events, we canceled the match."

**Adjectives**
- **cutting-edge** – very modern, newest features – "cutting-edge technology"
- **affluent** – wealthy – "an affluent neighborhood" (stress 1st, sometimes 2nd)
- **competent** – able to do something well (competent at) – "competent at his job"
- **day-to-day** – happening every day as part of life/job – "day-to-day responsibilities"
- **monotonous** – boring because unchanging – "typing numbers is monotonous" (stress 2nd, glottal t)
- **alleged** – believed but not proved (usually of crime) – "the alleged thief" (al-LEGED or al-LEG-ed)
- **fruitful** – producing good results – "a fruitful discussion"
- **conservative** – dislikes/distrusts change – "older people tend to be more conservative"
- **liberal** – respects many beliefs/behaviors – "younger people tend to be more liberal" (2 or 3 syllables)
- **idealistic** – believing very good things can be achieved – "an idealistic view" (stress 2nd-to-last; words ending -ic)
- **preliminary** – coming before something more important – "a preliminary hearing/investigation"
- **genuine** – honest, sincere – "a genuine apology" (-ine = /ɪn/)

## 152. Advanced (C2) Nouns to Build Your Vocabulary

- **awe** – great respect mixed with fear/surprise – "in awe of her grandfather" (/ɔː/)
- **bureaucracy** – complicated, unnecessary rules/processes in organizations – "too much government bureaucracy" (4 syl, stress 2nd)
- **frenzy** – uncontrolled excited behavior – "shopping frenzy"; "feeding frenzy"; "whip someone into a frenzy"
- **cliche** – overused, unoriginal saying – "I know it's cliche, but time heals all wounds." (from French, /kliːˈʃeɪ/)
- **paradigm** – typical model/example – "a paradigm shift / new paradigm" (/ˈpærədaɪm/, silent g)
- **momentum** – force of movement / progress – "gain/lose momentum" (stress 2nd)
- **morale** – confidence of a group in hard times – "high/low/employee morale; boost/raise morale" (/məˈræl/, stress 2nd)
- **withdrawal** – taking money out of the bank – "make a withdrawal" (stress 2nd, ends /ɔːl/)
- **deterrent** – something that stops people from acting out of fear – "a nuclear/effective deterrent; acts as a deterrent to cheating" (true t)
- **ambiguity** – something with more than one meaning, causing confusion – "no ambiguity in the law" (stress 3rd-to-last: -ity)
- **diarrhea** – illness with liquid waste – learn the spelling (4 syl, stress 3rd)
- **whereabouts** – the place where someone/something is – "the criminal's whereabouts" (stress 1st)

## 153. Advanced (C2) Nouns to SUPERCHARGE Your Vocabulary

- **perseverance** – continued effort despite difficulty (positive) – "Learning a language takes perseverance." (stress 3rd)
- **workaholic** – person addicted to work; suffix -holic = addicted (shopaholic, bookaholic) – (stress 3rd; words ending -ic stress the syllable before)
- **hierarchy** – system ranking people/things by importance – "the company's hierarchy; hierarchy of needs" (4 syl, stress 1st)
- **bias** – unfair support/opposition from personal opinion – "bias towards/against; unconscious bias; confirmation bias"
- **scrutiny** – careful detailed examination – "come under close/intense scrutiny" (stress 1st; glottal t in US)
- **concession** – something given up to end a disagreement – "make a concession" (stress 2nd; words ending -sion stress the syllable before)
- **remorse** – sadness and regret for what you did – "feel no remorse; filled with remorse" (stress 2nd)
- **snob** – 1) looks down on lower classes; 2) person with very high standards – "a coffee/wine/art snob"
- **exile** – being forced to live outside your home country – "go into exile; live in exile" (stress 1st)
- **narrative** – story/version of events someone wants others to believe – "change the narrative"
- **xenophobia** – extreme dislike/fear of foreigners (adj xenophobic); -phobia = fear (arachnophobia, claustrophobia, glossophobia = fear of public speaking) – x = /z/, stress 3rd
- **influx** – arrival of many people/things at once – "an influx of tourists" (stress 1st)

## 154. Advanced (C2) Phrasal Verbs to Sound Fluent | Speak Smarter, Not Harder

(Live quiz lesson; answers:)
- **flood with / be flooded with** – receive too many to deal with – "The office was flooded with complaints." (mostly passive)
- **rule out** – decide something is impossible/exclude it – "Police ruled out foul play."
- **drift apart** – gradually become less close (unintentional, less contact; vs "grow apart" = interests differ) – "They drifted apart over the years."
- **loosen up** – relax, become less stiff/reserved – "Loosen up and enjoy the party."
- **dawn on** – suddenly realize – "It finally dawned on me why she acted strangely."
- **hold something against someone** – let a past act affect your opinion of them – "You can't hold that against her."
- **put aside** – C2: temporarily ignore (differences) for a purpose – "put aside our differences"; B2: save money/time – "put aside time to read"
- **resort to** – do something unpleasant as a last option – "resort to legal action/violence"
- **butt in** – interrupt/join uninvited, rude (cf. "cut in") – "Sorry to butt in."
- **chip in** – contribute money/time/effort (cf. "pitch in") – "Everyone chipped in to clean up."
- **come into (money)** – suddenly receive money/property, esp. by inheritance – "come into a lot of money"
- **stumble across/upon/on** – find by accident – "We stumbled upon a charming cafe."
- **live through** – experience and survive a hard situation (cf. "go through") – "what they lived through during the war"
- **back off** – stop being aggressive (cf. "back down") – "The police backed off to avoid a riot."

## 155. Advanced (C2) Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary

- **clear the air** – remove bad feelings between people – "We need to clear the air." ("the" = /ðə/ before consonant, /ðiː/ before vowel; fixed phrase uses /ðə/)
- **trial and error** – solve by trying methods and learning from mistakes – "a process of trial and error"
- **be at odds over / with** – disagree; be different from – "at odds with his version of the story" (flap t in "at" links to "odds")
- **beyond a (shadow of a) doubt** – with total certainty; legal: "beyond a reasonable doubt" – "guilty beyond a reasonable doubt"
- **play it by ear** – decide as the situation develops, no fixed plan – "Let's play it by ear."
- **go with the flow** – do what others do / accept easiest path – "I'll just go with the flow."
- **put one's foot down** – use authority to stop something – "No party. I'm putting my foot down."
- **down the drain** – wasted (work/money) – "All that work went down the drain."
- **come under fire** – be criticized – "The CEO has come under fire."
- **jump to conclusions** – guess facts without enough info – "Let's not jump to conclusions."
- **out of one's depth** – lacking knowledge/skill for the situation – "You're out of your depth."
- **do someone/something justice** – represent accurately/fairly (often negative) – "Pictures don't do the Grand Canyon justice." ("do it" has slight /w/ link)

## 156. Advanced (C2) Verbs to Build Your Vocabulary

- **linger** – 1) continue to exist ("still lingering" cold); 2) take longer than usual to leave – "Customers lingered after closing." (ng+g like "finger", not "singer")
- **dole out** – give (money, etc.) to several people; separable – "Lawmakers dole out surplus; dole it out"
- **pinpoint** – find/say the exact position or exact facts/cause – "Doctors couldn't pinpoint the cause."
- **plague** – cause repeated worry/pain/difficulty (plague by) – "plagued by back injuries" (/pleɪɡ/)
- **prescribe** – (doctor) order a treatment – "prescribe medicine/rest/painkillers" (stress 2nd)
- **utter** – say / make a sound (formal, may be unclear) – "without uttering a word" (flap t)
- **curb** – control/limit something unwanted – "curb my screen addiction"
- **lure** – persuade by offering something exciting (lure into/to) – "lured into working overtime"
- **indulge** – allow yourself something enjoyable, esp. too much (indulge in) – "indulge in sweets" (g = /dʒ/)
- **delegate** – give a job/duty to someone else; verb ends /eɪt/ (noun ends /ət/) – "good at delegating"
- **reconcile** – make opposing ideas/people agree or coexist – "reconcile our differences; reconcile growth with sustainability" (c = /s/)
- **loathe** – hate – "I loathe anchovies." (voiced th at end)

## 157. Advanced (C2) Vocabulary in 60 Minutes | Phrases, Verbs, Nouns, and Adjectives You Should Know

**Phrases**
- **clear the air** – remove bad feelings between people
- **trial and error** – solve by trying methods and learning from mistakes
- **be at odds over/with** – disagree; differ from
- **beyond a (shadow of a) doubt** – with total certainty; "beyond a reasonable doubt" in law
- **play it by ear** – decide as things develop, no fixed plan
- **go with the flow** – do what others do / easiest option
- **put one's foot down** – use authority to stop something
- **down the drain** – wasted (work/money)
- **come under fire** – be criticized
- **jump to conclusions** – guess without enough info
- **out of one's depth** – lacking the skill/knowledge for the situation
- **do someone/something justice** – represent accurately (often negative: "doesn't do it justice")

**Verbs**
- **relish** – enjoy greatly (+ gerund/noun) – "relish the chance/prospect/thought of..."
- **coincide (with)** – happen at the same time; be the same/similar – "The party coincides with my trip." (stress 3rd, co-in-cide)
- **squander** – waste money, supplies or opportunities – "squander an opportunity"
- **commemorate** – honor officially (ceremony, statue) – "commemorate the 70th anniversary" (stress 2nd)
- **gasp** – 1) one short breath from shock; 2) breathe loudly with difficulty – "gasping for air" (past: /ɡæspt/)
- **intervene** – get involved to improve/prevent worsening – "The referee intervened." (stress 3rd; US may drop t)
- **label (as)** – describe someone/something, often unfairly – "labeled as a troublemaker"
- **imply** – communicate indirectly – "What are you implying?" (stress 2nd)
- **infer** – conclude from available information – "I infer it's raining" (imply = speaker, infer = listener)
- **speculate** – guess without enough information – "Journalists speculate about the announcement."
- **succumb (to)** – 1) give in/accept defeat; 2) die or suffer badly from illness/injury – (silent b)
- **trudge** – walk slowly with effort – "trudge through snow/up a mountain" (rhymes with judge)

**Nouns**
- **awe** – respect mixed with fear/surprise – "in awe of"
- **bureaucracy** – needless complicated rules/processes
- **frenzy** – uncontrolled excited behavior – "feeding frenzy; whip into a frenzy"
- **cliche** – overused, unoriginal saying (/kliːˈʃeɪ/)
- **paradigm** – typical model; "paradigm shift" (silent g)
- **momentum** – force of progress – "gain/lose momentum"
- **morale** – group confidence – "boost/raise morale"
- **withdrawal** – taking money out of bank – "make a withdrawal"
- **deterrent** – thing that stops people through fear – "nuclear deterrent"
- **ambiguity** – more than one possible meaning
- **diarrhea** – illness with liquid waste; learn spelling
- **whereabouts** – where someone/something is

**Adjectives**
- **ambiguous** – having more than one possible meaning – "The contract is ambiguous."
- **complimentary** (also spelled complementary in captions) – free, given by a business – "complimentary snacks"; also = praising
- **handy** – useful, convenient – "come in handy"
- **indispensable** – so useful you can't manage without – "an indispensable part of the team"
- **legitimate** – 1) allowed by law; 2) reasonable/acceptable – "a legitimate argument"
- **questionable** – uncertain or wrong in some way – "questionable taste; ethically questionable"
- **grim** – worrying and without hope – "The future looks grim."
- **coherent** – clear and logically connected; (person) understandable – "a coherent argument"; "not coherent" = incoherent
- **dubious** – not completely true/trustworthy – "a dubious email"
- **compulsive** – doing something excessively, can't stop – "compulsive gambler/liar"
- **pushy** – unpleasantly trying too hard to get/make someone do something – "a pushy salesperson"
- **carefree** – without worries – "carefree days"
- **conceited** – too proud of yourself (synonyms: arrogant, proud)
- **rigid** – stiff, fixed, unable to bend – "rigid rules"
- **feeble** – weak, without strength/power – "a feeble old man; a feeble government"

## 158. Advanced (everyday) Idioms that You Should Know for 2024

- **blow off some steam** (idiom) – release pent-up anger/energy, often by activity – "Hit the gym to blow off some steam." (also "let off steam")
- **be/go through thick and thin** (idiom) – endure good and bad times together over a long period – "My wife and I have been through thick and thin."
- **be in the same boat** (idiom) – be in the same unpleasant situation – "I know how you feel, we're in the same boat."
- **Mum's the word** (idiom) – keep it secret – "I'll tell you a secret, but mum's the word."
- **take (something) with a grain of salt** (idiom) – don't fully believe it; collocation "massive grain of salt"; UK: "pinch of salt"
- **pay through the nose (for)** (idiom) – pay far too much – "We paid through the nose for our hotel room."
- **Hindsight is 20/20** (idiom) – in retrospect, what should have been done is clear – "I should never have trusted him." "Hindsight is always 20/20."
- **ring a bell** (idiom) – sound familiar; often negative – "That name doesn't ring a bell."
- **bite the bullet** (idiom) – force yourself to do something unpleasant – "Bite the bullet and go to the dentist."
- **be on thin ice** (idiom) – in a risky/dangerous position – "I forgot her present, so I'm on thin ice." (contrast: break the ice)
- **jump on the bandwagon** (idiom) – start doing something because it's popular – "Fans jumped on the bandwagon when the Yankees started winning."
- **face the music** (idiom) – accept responsibility/punishment for a mistake – "It's better to confess and face the music."

## 159. Advanced Adjectives (C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

- **ambiguous** (adj) – having more than one possible meaning – "The contract is ambiguous."
- **complimentary** (adj) – (1) praising; (2) given free by a business – "complimentary bottle of water" (US often pronounced like "complementary")
- **handy** (adj) – useful, convenient – "come in handy"
- **indispensable** (adj) – so useful you can't manage without it – "She's an indispensable part of the team." (usually after "be")
- **legitimate** (adj) – (1) allowed by law; (2) reasonable/acceptable – "Her argument was legitimate, so we refunded her." (US flap t: "legidimate")
- **questionable** (adj) – not certain; wrong/dubious in some way – "ethically questionable", "questionable taste"
- **grim** (adj) – worrying and without hope – "The future looks grim."
- **coherent** (adj) – (1) clear, logically connected; (2) of a person, understandable (usually negative: "not coherent"/"incoherent") – "a coherent argument"
- **dubious** (adj) – not completely true/trustworthy – "The file size seems dubious."
- **compulsive** (adj) – doing something excessively, unable to stop – "compulsive gambler/liar"
- **pushy** (adj) – annoyingly trying too hard to get someone to do/buy something – "a pushy salesperson"
- **carefree** (adj) – without worries – "those carefree days in high school"
- **conceited** (adj) – too proud of yourself (syn: arrogant, proud, superior) – "You're so conceited."
- **rigid** (adj) – stiff/fixed, not able to bend or change – "rigid rules", "rigid in our chairs"
- **feeble** (adj) – weak, lacking strength/power – "a feeble old man", "a feeble government"

## 160. Advanced Adjectives to Build Your Vocabulary

- **offhand** (adj) – (1) little or no preparation; (2) thoughtless/inconsiderate – "an offhand remark/comment", "offhand manner"
- **forthcoming** (adj) – (1) about to appear/be made available; (2) honest and candid (of a person) – "New evidence will be forthcoming."
- **widespread** (adj) – happening in many places/among many people (often negative) – "widespread chaos", "The disease is widespread."
- **straightforward** (adj) – (1) uncomplicated, easy to understand; (2) honest, sincere – "The instructions were straightforward."
- **so-called** (adj) – (1) named in a particular way; (2) shows the word isn't suitable (ironic, not a compliment); comes before noun – "a so-called expert/doctor/friend"
- **egregious** (adj) – shockingly bad; /əˈɡriːdʒəs/, stress on 2nd syllable – "an egregious abuse of power"
- **frivolous** (adj) – silly, not serious or important – "a frivolous lawsuit/relationship"
- **excruciating** (adj) – extremely painful; collocation "excruciating pain" – "He was in excruciating pain."
- **noxious** (adj) – harmful, poisonous; /ˈnɑːkʃəs/ (not "nauseous" = feeling sick) – "noxious fumes/gas"
- **inveterate** (adj) – doing something habitually, unlikely to change (usually negative) – "an inveterate gambler/liar"
- **nefarious** (adj) – morally bad, wicked – "a nefarious plan"
- **preposterous** (adj) – ridiculous, absurd – "That's preposterous."

## 161. Advanced Adjectives to Describe Personality & Behavior

- **overzealous** (adj) – showing too much eagerness/enthusiasm – "overzealous fans ran onto the stage"
- **tenacious** (adj) – refusing to give up, stubbornly persistent (positive) – "a tenacious effort"
- **candid** (adj) – truthful, honest – "a candid response"
- **facetious** (adj) – joking, not serious (often inappropriately); /fəˈsiːʃəs/ – "I'm being facetious."
- **obtuse** (adj) – slow to understand; refusing to see the obvious – "He can be so obtuse."
- **boorish** (adj) – rude, insensitive; collocation "boorish behavior" (not "boring")
- **jittery** (adj) – nervous, unable to relax – "The boy was too jittery to stay seated."
- **capricious** (adj) – impulsive, unpredictable; /kəˈprɪʃəs/ – "a capricious shopper", "capricious weather"
- **gullible** (adj) – easily persuaded to believe things – "I was so gullible, I fell for the scam."
- **adamant** (adj) – refusing to change one's mind; + about – "I'm adamant about protecting the environment."
- **feckless** (adj) – weak, ineffective, lacking character – "a feckless leader"

## 162. Advanced Adjectives to Impress Your Friends | Build Your Vocabulary

- **so-called** (adj) – word not suitable for the person (ironic) – "a so-called expert"
- **messed up** (adj) – (person) unhappy/emotionally confused; (situation) wrong, bad – "a messed up childhood", "That's messed up."
- **cocky** (adj) – confident in an annoying way – "Well, aren't you cocky."
- **dope** (adj, slang) – very good/cool – "pretty dope"
- **lame** (adj) – (1) weak, unconvincing; (2) boring/uninspiring – "a lame excuse/argument"
- **out of whack** (adj phrase) – not working properly, balance upset – "Public transport is out of whack today."
- **woke** (adj) – aware of social problems (racism, inequality); positive or negative depending on speaker – "woke culture"
- **ballsy** (adj, informal) – brave, determined – "a ballsy move"
- **jaded** (adj) – lost interest from experiencing something too many times; "feel/become jaded"
- **moody** (adj) – mood changes suddenly to upset/angry (temporary) – "I get moody when I don't sleep."
- **legit** (adj, informal) – legitimate – "Is this legit?"
- **clingy** (adj) – sticks too close to someone – "a clingy friend/partner"
- **feisty** (adj) – active, full of determination, willing to push back
- **ballin'** (adj, very informal) – living a wealthy lifestyle, spending lots
- **kinky** (adj) – unusual, strange, exciting (often sexual) – "a kinky idea"
- **cheeky** (adj, UK) – slightly rude but humorous

## 163. Advanced American Idioms 🇺🇸 | LIVE QUIZ LESSON

- **go out on a limb** – say/do something without much evidence; often to start a guess – "I'm going to go out on a limb and say he was lying."
- **drive someone up the wall** – greatly annoy – "He's driving me up the wall."
- **be at the end of one's rope** – so frustrated you can't cope any more – "I'm at the end of my rope."
- **air out one's dirty laundry** – discuss personal issues in front of others; often negative – "Let's not air out our dirty laundry."
- **Speak of the devil** – said when someone just talked about suddenly appears/calls
- **throw caution to the wind** – act without being careful or sensible
- **leave no stone unturned** – do everything possible, search everywhere (often a command)
- **cut to the chase** – get to the point – "Just cut to the chase."
- **beat around the bush** – avoid saying something directly because it's difficult/unpleasant – "Stop beating around the bush."
- **steal someone's thunder** – take their idea/credit/praise – "You totally stole my thunder." (their = whose idea you took)
- **throw someone for a loop** – shock/confuse temporarily – "The rule change threw our team for a loop." (cf. out of the loop = unaware)
- **get out of hand** – become uncontrollable/chaotic – "The mess is getting out of hand."
