Shortened repeat of #163 with the same 12 idioms (go out on a limb, drive someone up the wall, at the end of one's rope, air out one's dirty laundry, speak of the devil, throw caution to the wind, leave no stone unturned, cut to the chase, beat around the bush, steal someone's thunder, throw someone for a loop, get out of hand).

## 165. Advanced Business Vocab to Help you Speak Naturally & Professionally

- **set the tone (at the top)** – leadership establishes character/values/mood – "The CEO's comments set a negative tone at the top."
- **get someone up to speed** – give someone the latest info – "A colleague got him up to speed on the project."
- **instill confidence (in)** – create trust/self-assurance; also build/increase/maintain/restore/shatter confidence
- **pay close attention to detail** – think carefully, avoid sloppy mistakes (positive)
- **level the playing field** – give everyone a fair, equal chance – "The pay equity policy aims to level the playing field."
- **take the initiative** – be first to act, esp. to solve a problem – "She takes the initiative before anyone asks."
- **pull one's weight** – do your fair share of work in a team – "He wasn't pulling his weight."
- **pick up the slack** – do work someone else hasn't done – "Someone has to pick up the slack."
- **take pride in** – feel satisfaction from doing something well – "He takes pride in finishing on time."
- **burn the midnight oil** – work late into the night
- **work around the clock** – work all day and night (emphasis) – "He's working around the clock to meet the deadline."
- **due diligence** – careful investigation before signing a deal/contract – "Do your due diligence before signing."
- **go the extra mile** – make more effort than expected (fixed: "the extra mile")
- **lead by example** – model the behavior you expect from others
- **no-show** (n) – person expected but doesn't arrive – "He's usually a no-show at our weekly meetings."
- **dilemma** (n) – difficult choice between undesirable options (focus on the choice)
- **conundrum** (n) – confusing, difficult problem (focus on the problem) – "Arranging child care is a real conundrum."
- **get the ball rolling** – make something begin – "We need to get the ball rolling on the conference."
- **delegate authority** – assign decision power/responsibility to subordinates; collocations: respect, command, assume, demonstrate, exert, abuse authority
- **business model** – how the parts of a business work together to make money
- **nervous wreck** – extremely anxious/stressed – "I'm a nervous wreck before presentations."
- **loose ends to tie up** – small unfinished things to complete – "I have some loose ends to tie up before I leave."
- **get everyone on the same page** – ensure shared understanding; also be/keep/have everyone on the same page
- **run behind schedule** – happen later than planned – "She's often running behind schedule."
- **a tough act to follow** – so successful the successor will struggle to match (also hard/difficult; "hard" most common)
- **personable** (adj) – agreeable, pleasant personality, gets along with others – "intelligent, hardworking, and personable"
- **keep someone posted** – keep someone updated on a changing situation (vs. get up to speed = for someone behind)
- **hit the nail on the head** – be exactly right – "She hit the nail on the head about poor communication."
- **pivotal role (in)** – central, crucial role; also vital/major/important/unique/essential/starring role
- **take one for the team** – make a personal sacrifice for the group
- **phase out** (phrasal v) – gradually stop using/producing – "phase out coal-powered plants"
- **can't handle pressure situations** – unable to cope with stress
- **hold down the fort** – take responsibility while someone is temporarily away
- **cut corners** – do something less thoroughly to save time/money (negative)
- **frown upon** (phrasal v) – disapprove of – "Public displays of affection are frowned upon."
- **empower employees** – give them authority, resources, autonomy to decide
- **above all** – most importantly – "cheap, attractive, and above all practical"
- **go back to the drawing board** – start over because it's not working
- **empty promise** – insincere commitment unlikely to be fulfilled
- **take the reins** – take control/leadership (spelled "reins")
- **fall on deaf ears** – be ignored – "His requests fell on deaf ears."
- **put on the back burner** – postpone/deprioritize – "Let's put your idea on the back burner."
- **hit the ground running** – be ready to work immediately on something new
- **walk the talk** – back up boastful talk with action; **walk the walk** = simply do what you say; **talk the talk** = talk confidently without necessarily acting
- **on the cusp of** – on the verge of a major development – "We're on the cusp of a technological revolution."
- **branch out** (phrasal v) – expand into a new direction – "He branched out into boat engine repair."
- **hammer out** (phrasal v) – work out details via discussion; hammer out the details/an agreement/a framework
- **bend over backwards** – try extremely hard to help/please
- **overcome obstacles** – succeed in dealing with barriers to a goal

## 166. Advanced Color Vocabulary to Brighten Up Your Conversations 🌈

- **turquoise** /ˈtɜːrkwɔɪz/ – blue-green; named after the gem (French "Turkish")
- **fuchsia** /ˈfjuːʃə/ – strong red-purplish pink; from the fuchsia flower
- **navy** /ˈneɪvi/ – dark blue ("navy blue")
- **crimson** /ˈkrɪmzən/ – strong, bright, deep red
- **beige** /beɪʒ/ – yellowish brown to light brownish gray; clothes
- **auburn** /ˈɔːbərn/ – dark red, slightly darker than crimson; hair color
- **lavender** /ˈlævəndər/ – light purple
- **magenta** /məˈdʒentə/ – purplish red/hot pink
- **teal** /tiːl/ – blue-green; named after the Eurasian teal duck
- **amber** /ˈæmbər/ – yellowish orange; from the material
- **burgundy** /ˈbɜːrɡəndi/ – dark red with purple; from burgundy wine
- **scarlet** /ˈskɑːrlət/ – bright red with an orange tint

## 167. Advanced English #Idiom | to pick up the slack 🏋️‍♂️

- **pick up the slack** – do work someone else hasn't done or completed – "If someone calls in sick, you may need to pick up the slack."
- **slacker** (n, informal) – lazy person who doesn't work hard

## 168. Advanced English Grammar that Will BLOW YOUR MIND 🤯

- **suggest/recommend**: verb + (that) + subject + base verb ("She suggested that I take the train"); or + gerund ("recommended arriving on time") or + noun. Never "suggest me to + verb".
- **to me vs for me**: "to me" = perception/opinion/receiving ("That's news to me", "This is important to me"); "for me" = for my benefit/personal impact ("Can you do it for me?", "too spicy for me"). "new to me" is more common than "new for me".
- **in the end vs at the end**: "in the end" = ultimately/result; "at the end (of)" = specific point/place; "at the end of the day" = after everything considered, ultimately.
- **in/on/at**: in = enclosed/larger area, workplaces, car, water, books; on = surfaces, floors, roads, public transit, media ("on YouTube"); at = specific point (at work/school/home, address, shops).
- **as if + were** (subjunctive, unreal): "He acts as if he were the boss" (colloquial "was" is common).
- **singular "they"** for unknown gender: "My friend is traveling... Where are they going?" (AP-approved 2017).
- **used to**: be used to + noun/gerund = accustomed ("He's used to working late"); used to + base verb = past habit no longer true ("I used to go dancing").
- **"the first time" + present perfect**: "This is the first time I have eaten sushi."
- **double comparatives** (cause and effect): "The harder you work, the more likely you'll succeed."
- **what-cleft sentences** for emphasis: "What I really need is a cup of coffee."
- **neither (of)**: formally singular verb ("Neither of the answers is correct"); plural common in informal speech.
- **from vs since**: from = starting point ("from an early age", "from March to May"); since = point continuing to present ("since 10 a.m.").
- **tag questions**: positive statement + negative tag ("Everyone left, didn't they?"); negative statement + positive tag ("You don't have money, do you?").
- **mixed conditional**: past condition + present result: "If I had eaten earlier, I wouldn't be hungry now."
- **in spite of / despite** – interchangeable, + noun/gerund; more written than spoken (spoken: although/even though).
- **rummage** (v) – look through something quickly and carelessly – "A bear rummages through our garbage cans."
- **that/which/who**: who = people; that = restrictive (animals/things); which = non-restrictive.
- **noun clause word order**: normal subject-verb after question words: "He didn't realize what he was doing", "I don't know who he is."
- **been to vs gone to**: been to = completed visit, returned; gone to = still there ("She's gone to New York").
- **It's high time + past simple**: "It's high time you did your homework" (also "it's about time").
- **inversion after negative adverbials**: "Not until the last moment did we learn..." (adds emphasis).
- **reported speech**: "She said (that) she had visited the museum yesterday" (past perfect).
- **e.g. = for example; i.e. = in other words**.
- **by the time** → perfect tense: future perfect "By the time you arrive, I will have finished my homework"; past perfect for past.
- **some vs any**: some in affirmatives and offers ("Would you like some tea?"); any in negatives, questions, and after if/whether. Phrases: "That's some pig", "and then some", "by any means necessary".
- **stop/try/remember/forget + gerund vs infinitive change meaning**: "stopped smoking" = quit; "stopped to smoke" = paused in order to smoke.

## 169. Advanced English Vocabulary You'll Actually Use (C2 Level)

- **put my finger on (it)** – discover the exact reason; usually negative: "I can't quite put my finger on it." (link: "finger-on-it")
- **kick yourself (for)** – be very annoyed at yourself for a stupid act/missed chance; reflexive pronoun – "I'm kicking myself for not investing in Apple."
- **at your own risk** – warning that you're responsible for the danger – "Swim at your own risk." (/t/+/j/ may blend to "ch": "atchyour")
- **get a move on** – hurry (often a command); flap t: "geda move on"
- **hard to come by** – difficult to obtain, rare – "True friends are hard to come by." (weak form of "to" /tə/)
- **make a run for it** – suddenly run to escape – "It's raining, we'd better make a run for it."
- **lose count (of)** – forget how many; usually past/present perfect – "I've lost count." (silent t in "lost count")
- **from top to bottom** – completely, thoroughly – "clean the house from top to bottom"
- **follow suit** – do the same as someone else – "Other countries may follow suit." (suit = long /uː/)
- **in attendance** – present at an event (important people or large numbers) – "Thousands were in attendance."
- **have a chip on your shoulder** – be angry because of feeling treated unfairly/inferior – "He has a chip on his shoulder."
- **I wouldn't dream of (doing) it** – I would never do it (wrong/silly); + gerund – "I wouldn't dream of quitting."

## 170. Advanced English Vocabulary | Quiz Lesson 📝

- **cajole** (v) – persuade someone with flattery
- **flabbergasted** (adj) – greatly surprised
- **vociferous** (adj) / **vociferously** (adv) – expressing opinions loudly with energy and emotion
- **opine** (v) – express an opinion
- **obfuscate** (v) – make something less clear/harder to understand – "obfuscate the truth"
- **impertinent** (adj) – lacking respect – "an impertinent question"
- **perfunctory** (adj) – done quickly without care – "a perfunctory greeting"
- **irksome** (adj) – annoying
- **pay homage (to)** – show deep respect for someone
- **fabricate** (v) – create something false to deceive – "fabricate a story/income"
- **temerity** (n) – willingness to do something that may upset others – "had the temerity to go on vacation without me"
- **panacea** (n) – something that will solve all problems – "technology as a panacea for climate change"

## 171. Advanced English in Under 2 Minutes 🚀 Try These Phrases

- **strike a chord (with someone)** – resonate emotionally or intellectually – "I hope this lesson strikes a chord with you."
- **a case in point** – a good example of what was just mentioned (like "for example", more formal) – "Many species struggle with climate change; a case in point is the polar bear."
- **go against the grain** – do something different from what is usual/normal – "The new policy goes against the grain of everything the company stood for."

## 172. Advanced Expressions (C1 + C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

- **to say the least** (phrase) – used at end of a statement to understate; you could say much more – "The flight was bumpy, to say the least." (say "ta say the least")
- **give someone the benefit of the doubt** (idiom) – believe/judge someone favorably despite uncertainty – "I'll give you the benefit of the doubt."
- **per se** (Latin, formal) – by/of itself; stresses other factors matter – "Money per se doesn't make people happy." (say "per SAY")
- **to top it all off** (phrase) – introduces the final event that makes a situation better or (usually) worse – "...and to top it all off, I gave a horrible presentation."
- **at the end of the day** (adv phrase) – ultimately; the most important thing considered – "At the end of the day, I'm making the decision."
- **few and far between** (idiom) – rare, not happening/existing often – "Storms stay few and far between."
- **be my guest** (phrase) – permission to go ahead (or, sarcastically, when angry) – "Can I use your pen?" "Be my guest."
- **give someone a run for their money** (idiom) – make someone work hard to win, not let them win easily – "I'll give him a run for his money."
- **sooner rather than later** (idiom) – fairly soon / without much delay – "I need the report sooner rather than later."
- **on the fence** (idiom) – unable to decide, usually between two choices – "I'm on the fence about which applicant to choose."

## 173. Advanced Expressions (C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

- **having said that** (transition) – introduces a contrast to what was just said (bad news after praise, or positive after negative) – "You're hard-working. Having said that, you didn't get the job."
- **wishful thinking** (noun phrase) – imagining an unlikely outcome you just wish for – "Fluent in a week? That's wishful thinking."
- **take something/someone for granted** (idiom) – not appreciate because you assume it will always be there – "I took my car for granted."
- **keep that in mind** (phrase) – be aware of / remember this – "Look, keep that in mind."
- **be that as it may** (formal, sentence start) – accepting the info is true but it doesn't change my view – "Be that as it may, you still broke the law."
- **around the clock** (adv phrase) – continuously, 24 hours a day – "We're open around the clock."
- **toss around ideas** (phrase, informal) – brainstorm with others – "Let's toss around some ideas."
- **in and of itself** (phrase, advanced/slightly old-fashioned) – considered alone, apart from everything else; usually mid-sentence between commas – "The rain, in and of itself, wasn't the cause of the jam."
- **lo and behold** (phrase) – introduces a surprising discovery in a story – "I looked in the drawer and lo and behold, I found my wallet."
- **in hindsight** (phrase) – with understanding gained after the event – "In hindsight, I should have told the truth."

## 174. Advanced Expressions You NEED TO KNOW | Speak Like a Pro

- **get your second wind** – sudden new burst of energy after being exhausted – "I got my second wind near the end of the run."
- **be stretched thin** – overwhelmed, at maximum capacity – "The team is stretched thin after taking on three projects."
- **be spread thin / spread yourself thin** – overwhelmed from taking on too many things – similar to stretched thin
- **catch someone off guard** – surprise someone who isn't prepared – "His question caught me off guard."
- **take someone by surprise** – same meaning, emphasis on the surprise – "His question took me by surprise."
- **for what it's worth** – said before advice/info you're unsure is helpful – "For what it's worth, I think you should build your vocabulary."
- **get back into the swing of things** – return to a routine/activity after a break – "It took days to get back into the swing of things after vacation." (also: get back into the groove; back to the grind)
- **above my pay grade** – beyond my authority/power to decide – "That policy change is above my pay grade."
- **buyer's remorse** – regret after an unnecessary/expensive purchase – "I have buyer's remorse."
- **go through the roof** – rise to a very high level (also: skyrocket, go sky-high, be off the charts) – "My blood pressure goes through the roof in traffic." (not "through the ceiling")
- **overstep one's boundaries** – do something not allowed/acceptable – "Let me know if I'm overstepping my boundaries."
- **see where someone is coming from** – understand their reasoning/feelings – "Once she explained, I could see where she was coming from."
- **love the sound of one's own voice / love hearing oneself talk** – (negative) talk a lot, self-absorbed
- **hang in there** – stay persistent, keep going (encouragement) – "Hang in there."

## 175. Advanced Grammar Quiz (ONLY 2% CAN PASS THIS TEST)

- -ed adjectives = how you feel; -ing adjectives = describe a thing/person/situation: "He's so boring."
- Mixed conditional (3rd + 2nd): "If I had taken my medicine this morning, I wouldn't feel sick right now."
- Noun clause uses statement word order: "I don't know where I should go" (not "where should I go").
- Scheduled future: "The movie starts / is starting / will start at 8." Present simple is most common; present continuous emphasizes the time; will for a sequence of future events.
- Subjunctive after suggest/recommend/wish: base verb – "I recommend that everyone hit the like button." Spoken: "I wish I were/was taller."
- Present perfect continuous for an action started in the past, continuing and likely to continue: "I've been studying English for several months."
- **in spite of** + noun/gerund ("in spite of making progress"); **although** + subject + verb ("although we made progress").
- Preposition: **leave for** a destination – "We left for New York City."
- **who** (subject) vs **whom** (object): "Whom did you invite?" (spoken English usually "who"). Test: answer with him/her -> whom.
- Rule of first mention: first mention a/an, second mention the – "I bought a gift... I hope she enjoys the gift."

## 176. Advanced Grammar Quiz That Will BLOW YOUR MIND 🤯

- **suggest/recommend** patterns: that + subject + base verb ("She suggested that I take the train"); + gerund ("recommended arriving"); + noun ("suggested the restaurant"). Not "suggest me to".
- **It's high time / about time / time (that) + past simple**: "It's high time you did your homework." (high time = more emphasis; "about time"/"time" more common)
- **By the time** -> perfect tense: "By the time you arrive, I will have finished the report." (future perfect); past: "By the time you arrived, I had already finished." (past perfect)
- **prefer X to Y** (idiomatic): "I prefer tea to coffee." (spoken: "over" also heard; not "instead of")
- **stop** + gerund = quit ("stopped smoking"); stop + infinitive = pause to do ("stopped to smoke"). Same shift with **try** (try doing = experiment; try to do = attempt something difficult), **remember** (remember doing = recall past; remember to do = don't forget a task), **forget** (same pattern).
- **Inversion** after negative adverbials, for emphasis/formal: "Not until the last moment did we learn that the event was cancelled."
- **be used to + noun/gerund** = accustomed ("He is used to working late"); **used to + base verb** = past habit no longer true ("I used to go dancing").
- Subject-verb agreement ignores "along with" phrases: "The man, along with his sons, is planning the event."
- **This is the first time + present perfect**: "This is the first time I have eaten sushi."
- Subjunctive after demand verbs (insist): "She insisted that he be present." (spoken: is/was heard)
- **as if + were**: "He acts as if he were the boss." / "If I were you..."
- **Neither (of)** is singular: "Neither of the answers is correct." (plural verb accepted informally)

## 177. Advanced Grammar in 60 Minutes (Improve Your English Fluency)

**12 verb tenses** = 3 times (past/present/future) x 4 aspects (simple/continuous/perfect/perfect continuous).
- Present simple: habits (always, usually), facts ("Fire is hot"), scheduled future ("The movie starts at 8").
- Past simple: finished action, with yesterday/last week/a long time ago – "I taught in South Korea."
- Present perfect (have + p.p.): unfinished with for/since ("since 2017"); experiences/recent past, time unknown – "We have been to Berlin."
- Future simple (will / be going to): "I think it'll rain tomorrow."
- Present continuous (am/is/are + -ing): happening now, temporary situations, annoyances ("You're always asking me for help"), planned future ("I'm leaving tomorrow").
- Past continuous (was/were + -ing): action at a specific past time, story background, overlapping actions, interrupted action – "She was working while he was cooking."
- Future continuous (will be + -ing): action in progress at a future time – "A week from now I will be sitting on a beach."
- Present perfect continuous (have been + -ing): started in the past, continues to now (and likely future), or just-finished action with visible result – "I've been waiting 30 minutes." / "It's been raining."
- Past perfect (had + p.p.): one past action before another; used in the independent clause with when/before/by the time – "When I arrived, the meeting had already begun."
- Past perfect continuous (had been + -ing): action continuing up to a past point – "I had been working there a year when I met my wife."
- Future perfect (will have + p.p.): one future action before another – "By next year you will have achieved your goals."
- Future perfect continuous (will have been + -ing): "By 2067 I will have been teaching for 50 years."
- Quiz cues: "last night at 8" -> past continuous; "a long time ago" -> past simple; "by the time I got there" -> past perfect; "How long have you been waiting?" -> present perfect continuous.

**Active vs passive**
- Only transitive verbs (with an object) can become passive; intransitive ("She laughs at the joke", "We ate in the park") cannot.
- Formula: object to front + be (same tense as active verb) + past participle + (by + object pronoun): "I wrote that email" -> "That email was written by me." Check singular/plural agreement ("those emails were written").
- Use passive to: emphasize the action ("This land was farmed by my ancestors"); hide the actor ("Mistakes were made", "Their dog will be put to sleep"); actor unknown ("My bike was stolen", "Has the cat been fed?"); sound firm but polite ("Talking is not allowed", "Smoking is prohibited"). Most of the time use active.

**Causative verbs**
- **have** someone + base verb / something + p.p. (cause by paying/asking) – "I had my brother paint my house." / "I had my house painted."
- **get** someone + to + base verb / something + p.p. (same meaning, less formal) – "She got a mechanic to fix her car." / "She got her car fixed."
- **make** (force) + object + base verb – "My boss made me work late."
- **let** (permission) + object + base verb – "She let me borrow her car."
- **help** + object + (to) base verb; "to" optional and usually dropped – "I help my friends make dinner."

**Definite article "the" (25 uses, key ones)**
- Second mention (rule of first mention): a bucket... the bucket.
- Only one: the sun/moon/sky, the boss/president/pope; the toilet/park/mall downtown.
- Specific nouns (count or non-count): "Pass me the salt."
- Before superlatives and ordinals: the best, the first time; "the only day/person".
- Decades (the 1960s, the '70s); groups (the French, the elderly, the police, the rich, the poor).
- Rivers (the Nile), mountain ranges (the Alps), oceans (the Pacific), deserts (the Sahara), poles/equator, island groups (the Bahamas).
- Plural-name countries (the Philippines, the Netherlands); countries with kingdom/republic/states (the United States, the United Kingdom).
- Newspapers (the New York Times), buildings/monuments/museums/hotels (the Empire State Building, the Taj Mahal, the Louvre, the Hilton), families (the Kardashians).

**Common mistakes**
- Subjunctive after recommend/demand/insist/suggest (hidden "should"): base verb even for he/she – "The doctor recommends she go to bed early." / "She demands that he leave at once." / "I insist the game get rescheduled."
- Negatives with he/she/it: "doesn't", not "don't" – "He doesn't eat pizza every day."
- -ed = feeling (I'm bored/frustrated), -ing = thing/situation/characteristic (It's boring/frustrating). "I am boring" = I'm a boring person.

## 178. Advanced Idioms (C1-C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

C1:
- **be on the ball** – quick to understand and react, alert – "He didn't sleep, so he's not on the ball today."
- **with flying colors** – (pass) successfully, usually "pass something with flying colors" – "She passed the exam with flying colors."
- **turn a blind eye (to)** – ignore something you know is wrong – "The teacher turned a blind eye to cheating."
- **the easy way out** – the easiest option in a difficult situation; "take the easy way out" – "I try not to take the easy way out."
- **how/what/why on Earth** – shows extreme surprise, confusion or anger – "Why on Earth are you changing jobs?"
- **in light of (something)** – because of / as a result of new info – "In light of recent events, we canceled the match."

C2:
- **at the end of your rope** – no strength or patience left – "I'm at the end of my rope."
- **to the bitter end** – until it is finished, even if the outcome is bad; "fight to the bitter end"
- **get off on the right/wrong foot** – make a good/bad start (esp. projects, relationships) – "We got off on the wrong foot."
- **over the top** – too extreme/exaggerated; "way/a bit over the top" – "Her gift was way over the top."
- **at the crack of dawn** – very early in the morning – "We have to get up at the crack of dawn."
- **a change of heart** – changed feelings/opinion about something meaningful; usually "have/had a (sudden) change of heart" – "She had a sudden change of heart about selling the necklace."

## 179. Advanced Irregular Verbs You Need to Know

- **arise, arose, arisen** – to occur/appear (a problem) – "New problems arose last week."
- **bear, bore, borne/born** – turn slightly (direction); endure/accept (responsibility); produce/give birth – "Bear left." / "He bore the responsibility." / "The trees have borne fruit."
- **behold, beheld, beheld** – see/observe – "Behold my water bottle."
- **creep, crept, crept** – move slowly and quietly to avoid being heard – "I crept into the kitchen and took a cookie."
- **deal, dealt, dealt** – distribute cards; trade/do business – "She has dealt the cards."
- **forsake, forsook, forsaken** – abandon/give up (promises, commitments) – "He has forsaken his vows."
- **lie, lay, lain** – be in a resting position (intransitive) – "I lay in bed all day." (lie = tell untruth is regular: lied)
- **lay, laid, laid** – put something down; prepare (needs an object) – "We laid the baby on the bed." / "laid the groundwork"
- **mean, meant, meant** – intend / refer to – "That's not what I meant."
- **overtake, overtook, overtaken** – come from behind and move in front of (driving) – "We overtook the bus."
- **rise, rose, risen** – move up; get up – "The sun has risen." / "Rise and shine."
- **swear, swore, sworn** – promise solemnly – "He swore to tell the truth." / "Do you swear?"
- **shake, shook, shaken** – move up/down or side to side; tremble – "We shook hands." / "I was shaking in the cold."
- **weep, wept, wept** – cry, shed tears (serious/severe) – "After the breakup, he wept for days."
- **withdraw, withdrew, withdrawn** – remove/take back (money, an application) – "I withdrew my name from consideration."

## 180. Advanced Listening & Speaking Activity | Practice English on Your Own

Listening/speaking drill: listen to a short text read twice, then retell it in full sentences using on-screen clues, then compare with a partner (stories about Steve, 35, in California, and Millie, 9, in Boston). No teachable vocab.

## 181. Advanced Listening & Speaking Practice | Transportation

Guess the noun from a description (then describe nouns yourself). Vocab:
- **gondola** – long narrow boat on canals, e.g. Venice
- **yacht** – big luxurious boat with bedrooms, kitchen
- **snowmobile** – motorbike-like vehicle for snow
- **go-kart** – small motorized car for kids/amusement parks
- **raft** – small inflatable boat on rivers, paddled
- **tram** – public transport on tracks in a city, often with overhead cables
- **jet ski** – fast personal watercraft for fun on water
- (extra: hot air balloon, sled)

## 182. Advanced Phrasal Verbs Natives Use That Textbooks DON’T Teach 📚

- **phase out** (pv) – gradually stop using/producing – "The government will phase out coal power plants."
- **wear off** (pv) – gradually lose effect/intensity (medication) – "The drug started to wear off."
- **spell out** (pv) – explain clearly in detail – "Do I have to spell it out for you?" / "The contract spells out both parties' responsibilities."
- **talk down to** (pv) – speak condescendingly to someone – "A teacher who never talked down to her students."
- **hammer out** (pv) – work out details through discussion/negotiation – "They hammered out an agreement."
- **bow out (of)** (pv) – withdraw/retire from an activity or role – "She'll bow out of the competition because of injury."
- **frown upon** (pv) – disapprove of (not illegal) – "PDA is frowned upon in conservative cultures."
- **bottle up** (pv) – suppress/conceal feelings – "He bottles up his emotions."
- **branch out** (pv) – expand activities/interests in a new direction – "He started as a mechanic but branched out into boat engine repair."
- **follow through (on/with)** (pv) – carry an action to its conclusion – "He didn't follow through on his responsibilities."
- **rattle off** (pv) – say/produce quickly and effortlessly – "She can rattle off all 50 states in alphabetical order."
- **latch on to** (pv) – follow/cling to someone for attention; become very interested in; grip tightly – "He latched on to his friend and followed her around the party."

## 183. Advanced Phrasal Verbs for Everyday Conversation

**eke out** (phr v) – obtain/win with difficulty or great effort – "eke out a victory / gains / growth"
**mull over** (phr v, separable) – think carefully for a long time – "I'd like to mull it over before deciding."
**tone down** (phr v) – make less forceful/offensive – "tone down the rhetoric / your language / expectations"
**run through** (phr v) – rehearse/read quickly – "run through my lines"
**buckle down** (phr v) – get serious and focus on work (esp. study) – "Exams are coming; I need to buckle down."
**air out** (phr v) – expose to fresh air; also discuss openly – "air out the room"; "air out grievances"
**plow through** (phr v) – finish a large/difficult amount with effort – "plow through the data / the pandemic"
**cash in on** (phr v) – exploit a situation for money – "cash in on her 15 minutes of fame"
**15 minutes of fame** (idiom) – brief period of celebrity
**jazz up** (phr v) – make more attractive/interesting – "jazz up the meal / your look" (cf. spruce up, brighten up [colors])
**hash out** (phr v) – discuss to reach agreement – "hash out the details before making an offer"
**zero in on / hone in on** (phr v) – focus attention on – "regulators zero in on AI"
**lash out (at)** (phr v) – suddenly attack or criticize angrily – "He lashed out at me."

## 184. Advanced Phrasal Verbs with ⬆️ UP ⬆️

**crack up** (phr v) – laugh with great enthusiasm – "You crack me up."
**divvy up** (phr v) – divide and share – "How do we divvy this up?"
**grow up** (phr v) – act mature (said as criticism) – "Oh, grow up!"
**slip up** (phr v) – make a mistake (esp. when expected not to) – "US slipped up against Slovakia."
**walk up to** (phr v) – approach someone confidently – "He walked up to me and asked me to dance."
**set up** (phr v) – arrange a date between two people – "set someone up"
**split up** (phr v) – end a relationship/marriage; also a band – "We should never have split up."
**catch up** (phr v) – talk with someone you haven't seen for a while – "Let's catch up over coffee."
**liven up** (phr v) – make more interesting/exciting – "Signs really liven up the place."
**pass up** (phr v) – fail to take advantage of an opportunity – "Don't pass up this opportunity."
**dress up** (phr v) – wear special clothes to change appearance – "dress up as Batman"
**end up** (phr v) – reach an unplanned/unexpected place or situation – "You'll end up drunk."

## 185. Advanced Phrases (C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

**let's face it** – introduces something true but unpleasant/undeniable (start of statement) – "Let's face it, you're the reason I drink." Linking: "face it".
**few and far between** – not happening/existing often – "Good workers are few and far between." "and" reduces to /n/.
**fall into place** – things going satisfactorily with no problems – "Everything was falling into place."
**for the best** – action that improves things though unpleasant now – "Breaking up is for the best." "the" = schwa before consonant.
**conflict of interest** – can't decide fairly because affected by the result – "judging a contest with my daughter in it"; link all three words.
**dawn on (someone)** – realize for the first time – "It suddenly dawned on me I forgot my wallet." Collocations: suddenly/finally dawns on.
**crack down on** – take strong action to stop something – "crack down on password sharing"
**in the blink of an eye** – extremely quickly – "Six years went by in the blink of an eye."
**butt in** – interrupt/get involved in others' business – "Sorry to butt in, but..." (flap T)
**by all means** – certainly; permission – "Could I borrow this book?" "By all means."
**behind closed doors** – in secret, away from the public – "decision made behind closed doors" (d sounds elide)
**come to light** – facts become publicly known – "New evidence comes to light."

## 186. Advanced Phrases That Will Change the Way You Communicate

**on the cusp of** – at the beginning/transition point of a major development – "on the cusp of a technological revolution" (cf. verge, brink); not for small events
**play devil's advocate** – argue a view you don't hold, to debate/test
**beyond the pale** – outside acceptable behavior – "His comments were beyond the pale."
**a bitter/hard/tough pill to swallow** – difficult, unpleasant fact to accept (noun phrase)
**watershed moment** – critical turning point – "The ruling was a watershed moment for civil rights."
**in the same vein** – in a similar style/theme – "The follow-up lecture continued in the same vein."
**the jury is still out** – no decision/conclusion reached yet – "Will Bitcoin reach a million? The jury is still out."
**open a can of worms** – create a situation causing many problems when dealt with – "Let's not open that can of worms."
**add fuel to the fire** – make a bad situation worse
**a double-edged sword** – has both good and bad effects – "Fame is a double-edged sword." Noun phrase; don't use as adjective.
**put on the back burner** – postpone/deprioritize – "Let's put it on the back burner."
**hit the nail on the head** – say exactly the right thing – "Her analysis hit the nail on the head."

## 187. Advanced Verbs (C2) to Build Your Vocabulary

**relish** (v) /ˈrɛlɪʃ/ – like/enjoy; + gerund or noun (relish the thought/prospect/chance) – "I relish eating ice cream on a hot day."
**coincide** (v) /ˌkoʊɪnˈsaɪd/ (stress 3rd) – happen at same time (with); be the same – "It coincides with a business trip."
**squander** (v) /ˈskwɑːndər/ – waste money/opportunities – "squander an opportunity / taxpayer money"
**commemorate** (v) /kəˈmɛməreɪt/ (stress 2nd) – honor officially, e.g. by ceremony/statue – "commemorate the 70th anniversary"
**gasp** (v) – take a short sharp breath from shock; or breathe loudly with difficulty – "gasping for air"; past "gasped" /gæspt/
**intervene** (v) – get involved to improve/prevent worsening – "The referee intervened." (stress 3rd; US may drop the t)
**label** (v) – describe someone/something (often unfairly) – "labeled as a troublemaker"; link "label as"
**imply** (v) – communicate without saying directly – "What are you implying?"
**infer** (v) – conclude from available information – "I infer it's raining." (imply = say indirectly; infer = deduce)
**speculate** (v) – guess answers without enough info – "Journalists speculate what he'll say."
**succumb** (v) – give up/accept defeat; or die/suffer from illness/injury; silent b – "succumb to injuries"
**trudge** (v) – walk slowly with effort (through snow, up a mountain); rhymes with "judge"

## 188. Advanced Verbs to Describe the Way Things Move

Walking/running:
**strut** – walk confidently/arrogantly
**stagger** – walk with great difficulty, almost falling (e.g. drunk)
**bolt** – move very fast, like running – "bolted to the plane"
**slip (by/past/inside/outside)** – move quietly so no one notices
**drag one's feet** (idiom) – move/progress reluctantly and slowly; procrastinate
Driving/flying:
**hitchhike** – get a free ride by standing on the road
**carpool** – travel by car in a small group to save money/environment
**leapfrog** – take turns passing each other
**swerve** – suddenly change direction
**soar** – fly high
Animals:
**glide** – smooth continuous motion (bird, swan)
**slither** – move by sliding and twisting (snake)
**wobble** – move side to side with poor balance (penguin)
**gallop** – run with all four feet off the ground (horse)
**wiggle** – small quick back-and-forth movements (squirming puppy)
Situations:
**approach** – get nearer; also deal with a problem – "How do we approach this issue?"
**circumvent** – avoid cleverly, go around – "circumvent tax laws"
**smuggle** – move something secretly (often illegal)
**lag (behind)** – make slow progress behind others
**spiral (out of control)** – get worse and harder to control

## 189. Advanced Vocabulary (slang) that You Should Know for 2023

**gaslighting** (n/v: gaslight) – psychological abuse making someone doubt their sanity/reality – "You're crazy, you're imagining things."
**metaverse** (n) – imaginary internet space/virtual world where people meet (Facebook renamed itself Meta)
**finfluencer** (n, slang, portmanteau) – finance + influencer giving financial advice online
**staycation** (n, portmanteau) – holiday spent at/near home
**gig worker** (n) – temporary/freelance on-demand worker; **gig economy** (Uber, Airbnb, Fiverr)
**mansplain** (v, portmanteau) – explain condescendingly (man to woman) what she already knows
**de-platform** (v) – deny someone a platform to share views (ban from social media)
**whataboutism** (n) – deflecting criticism by asking "what about...?"
**oligarch** (n) – extremely rich, powerful person, esp. Russian after Soviet collapse – "Russian oligarch"
**cringe** (adj, slang) – so embarrassing it's hard to watch – "That video is so cringe."
**GOAT** (n, acronym) – greatest of all time – "Michael Jordan is the GOAT."
**woke** (adj) – socially aware (race, inequality); can be positive or negative/sarcastic depending on speaker
**epic** (adj, slang) – amazing/spectacular – "The trip was epic."
**snowflake** (n, derogatory) – person seen as too easily upset/offended
**simp** (n, slang) – silly/unintelligent person (short for simpleton); more used by younger people

## 190. Advanced Vocabulary (slang) that You Should Know for 2024

**bed rotting** (n) – spending many hours in bed during the day (snacks/phone) as retreat from stress
**range anxiety** (n) – fear an electric vehicle's battery will run out before reaching destination/charger
**kennel cough** (n) – contagious mild respiratory infection in dogs (dry hacking cough)
**greedflation** (n, portmanteau) – price rises driven by corporate profit-seeking, blamed on inflation – "McDonald's accused of greedflation"
**Barbiecore** (n) – style of playful pink outfits/accessories/decor
**shacket** (n, portmanteau) – shirt + jacket: thick button-down jacket worn over shirts
**fire science** (n) – study of fire (composition, suppression, prevention, wildfires)
**delulu** (adj/n, slang) – short for delusional; esp. obsessed fans/dating partners with extreme behavior
**mid** (adj, slang) – mediocre, unimpressive, disappointing – "Most restaurants are mid."
**soju** (n) – clear distilled Korean alcoholic drink (rice/sweet potato etc.)
**skiplagging** (n) – buying a flight with a layover at your real destination and skipping the last leg (to save money)
**dry powder** (n) – cash reserves/liquid assets held to cover obligations or seize opportunities (finance)

## 191. Advanced Vocabulary About the Housing Crisis | Read & Put Words in Context

**tenant** (n) – person who rents a home
**landlord** (n) – owner of rented property
**eviction / evict** (n/v) – forcing someone to leave their home (e.g. unpaid rent) – "eviction notice"
**forbearance** (n) – payment delayed or not required for a time – "student loan forbearance"
**ban** (n/v) – official prohibition – "eviction ban"
**reprieve** (n) – relief that makes a bad situation easier to handle
**phase out** (phr v) – gradually end
**hamper** (v) – make something more difficult to happen – "hamper their ability to invest"
**tenure** (n) – legal ownership/holding of land (or period of holding)
**land rights** (n) – land ownership rights (not "social rights")
**seize** (v) – take by force
**grapple with** (v) – try to deal with a difficult problem
**pronounced** (adj) – very noticeable – "a pronounced problem"
**funds** (n) – money; **raise funds** – collect money
**exacerbate** (v) – make worse
**fallout** (n) – rapid bad consequences/downturn – "economic fallout"
**susceptible** (adj) – easily affected/likely to get (e.g. a virus)
**disparate** (adj) – different in every way; unequal – "disparate impact on groups"

## 192. Advanced Vocabulary Lesson | Take the Quiz & Learn New Words

Tips: learn context of use not just meaning; search a word in Google News (in quotes) to see correct usage; practice repeatedly to move words into active memory.
**zenith** (n) – highest point/peak – "retired at the zenith of his career"
**cocky** (adj) – arrogantly confident – "He thinks he's always right."
**admonish** (v) – warn/reprimand firmly – "admonished for skipping class"
**klutz** (n) – clumsy person – "He broke another mug; he's such a klutz."
**vaguely** (adv) – not clearly – "I vaguely remember it."
**quintessential** (adj) – the most perfect/typical example – "the quintessential superhero"
**embezzle** (v) – secretly steal money entrusted to you (company funds)
**preposterous** (adj) – contrary to reason; absurd – "You think I stole your wallet? Preposterous!"
**dilettante** (n) – person with superficial interest/knowledge, not a professional
**ubiquitous** (adj) – found everywhere – "the company's ubiquitous influence"
**impugn** (v) – cast doubt on someone's character/reputation by criticizing – "impugn her character"
**capricious** (adj) – subject to sudden, unpredictable change – "he's so capricious"
**fervently** (adv) – with great enthusiasm/intensity – "worked fervently around the clock"
**presumptuous** (adj) – showing lack of respect by overstepping – "taking food from our fridge is presumptuous"
**meticulous** (adj) – showing great attention to detail – "meticulous down to the last detail"

## 193. Advanced Vocabulary Talking About University Life

- **tuition** (n) – the cost of your classes for a year (excludes books) – "The tuition is insane."
- **scholarship** (n) – financial aid covering part or all of tuition – "She got a scholarship."
- **dorm / dormitory** (n) – student housing on campus – "I lived in the dorms."
- **freshman / sophomore / junior / senior** (n) – 1st / 2nd / 3rd / 4th year student – "I'm a junior" = third year.
- **major** (n/v) – main field of study – "I majored in business."
- **minor** (n) – secondary focus requiring fewer classes – "a minor in history"
- **prerequisite** (n) – class you must take before another (also general: something required first) – "Elementary chemistry is a prerequisite."
- **faculty** (n) – professors/teaching staff of a university
- **TA (teaching assistant)** (n) – usually a grad student who helps the professor and grades papers
- **lecture** (n) – large class where the professor presents, little interaction – "a lecture class"
- **academia** (n) – the world/lifestyle of higher education and research – "part of academia"

## 194. Advanced Vocabulary for Talking About Babies

- **conceive** (v) – become pregnant (formal); noun **conception** – "The baby was conceived on vacation."
- **maternity leave** (n) – time off work for the mother before/after birth; **maternity** = period around birth (maternity clothes)
- **paternity leave** (n) – time off for the father after birth (less common phrase)
- **due date** (n) – date the baby is expected
- **go into labor** (phr) – the birth process begins – "She went into labor."
- **deliver the baby** (v) – doctor helps with the birth
- **pediatrician** (n) – doctor for infants and children
- **vaccination / vaccinate** (n/v) – shot protecting against disease
- **nursing** (n/v) – feeding a baby breast milk
- **formula** (n) – artificial baby food instead of breast milk
- **teething** (n/v) – teeth coming in, baby chews on things
- **swaddle** (v) – wrap a baby tightly in a blanket
- US vs UK: **stroller** (also baby carriage) = **pushchair / pram / buggy**; **diaper** = **nappy**; **onesie** = **baby grow**; **drool** = **dribble**; **pacifier** = **dummy** (Canada: binky, soother); **tired** = **knackered** (UK, "neck 'red" in caption; informal for very tired); **crib** = **cot**

## 195. Advanced Vocabulary in 30 Minutes (Descriptive words you should know)

Movement verbs
- **glide** (v) – move with smooth, quiet, continuous motion – "A swan glides through the water."
- **plunge** (v) – jump/dive energetically into water (plunge into) – "He plunged into the lake."
- **trudge** (v) – walk slowly with effort in harsh conditions – "trudge through the snow/mud"
- **swerve** (v) – change direction abruptly – "She swerved to avoid the deer."
- **creep** (v) – move slowly and carefully to avoid being noticed (creep up on; cf. sneak, tiptoe) – "The cat crept up on the bird."

Places
- **desolate** (adj) – empty, bleak, unpleasant, no people – "a desolate landscape"
- **opulent** (adj) – expensive and luxurious – "an opulent hotel"
- **verdant** (adj) – covered with healthy green plants/grass – "verdant countryside"
- **pristine** (adj) – immaculately clean or never used – "Her office is always pristine."
- **enclave** (n) – a separate group/area within a larger one – "a wealthy enclave in an impoverished city"

People
- **disheveled** (adj) – very untidy (hair, clothes, appearance); syn. unkempt – "He looked disheveled."
- **radiant** (adj) – showing great happiness, love or health – "a radiant smile"
- **wistful** (adj) – slightly sad thinking of something you can't have – "a wistful song about lost love"
- **serene** (adj) – calm, peaceful, tranquil – "peaceful and serene"
- **frail** (adj) – physically weak/unhealthy (often elderly) – "looking particularly frail"
- Tips: learn synonyms (thesaurus), associate via Google Images, compare word frequency with Google Ngram Viewer.

## 196. Advanced Vocabulary in 60 Minutes (Precise words you need to know!)

Adjectives to sound smarter (stress marked with caps)
- **ubiquitous** /juːˈbɪkwɪtəs/ (adj) – existing everywhere – "Coffee shops are ubiquitous here."
- **cumbersome** /ˈkʌmbərsəm/ (adj) – difficult to move/carry, burdensome – "Moving the furniture was cumbersome."
- **facetious** /fəˈsiːʃəs/ (adj) – not meant seriously, teasing – "He's being facetious."
- **arduous** /ˈɑːrdʒuəs/ (adj) – requiring great effort – "an arduous task"
- **presumptuous** /prɪˈzʌmptʃuəs/ (adj) – overstepping, showing no respect for others – "It was presumptuous of him to think it was a date."
- **pensive** /ˈpensɪv/ (adj) – in deep thought – "She looks pensive."
- **circuitous** /sərˈkjuːɪtəs/ (adj) – roundabout, indirect – "a circuitous route"
- **conscientious** /ˌkɑːnʃiˈenʃəs/ (adj) – careful, responsible, fair to others – "conscientious of her health"
- **meticulous** /məˈtɪkjələs/ (adj) – paying very close attention to detail – "meticulous with his designs"
- **convoluted** /ˈkɑːnvəluːtɪd/ (adj) – extremely complicated, hard to follow – "a convoluted story"
- **germane** /dʒərˈmeɪn/ (adj) – relevant, closely related – "not germane to the central argument"
- **preposterous** /prɪˈpɑːstərəs/ (adj) – absurd, contrary to reason – "That's preposterous."
- **perfunctory** /pərˈfʌŋktəri/ (adj) – done out of routine, without thought – "a perfunctory smile"
- **truculent** /ˈtrʌkjələnt/ (adj) – aggressively hostile, cruel – "truculent with the media"
- **austere** /ɔːˈstɪr/ (adj) – severe, strict, without excess – "her austere manner"
- **capricious** /kəˈprɪʃəs/ (adj) – subject to sudden unpredictable change – "capricious weather/person"
- **defamatory** /dɪˈfæmətɔːri/ (adj) – falsely harming someone's reputation – "defamatory remarks"
- **esoteric** /ˌesəˈterɪk/ (adj) – understood by only a small group – "an esoteric experience"
- **nascent** /ˈneɪsnt/ (adj) – just beginning to develop – "a nascent stage"
- **quintessential** /ˌkwɪntɪˈsenʃl/ (adj) – the most perfect example – "the quintessential channel"

Verbs
- **admonish** (v) – scold/advise against, mildly – "admonished the child for lying"
- **advocate** (v, + for) – strongly support – "advocated for better pay"
- **bemoan** (v) – express distress/complain about – "bemoaned having to find another flight"
- **compel** (v) – force/drive someone to do something – "The police compelled him to tell the truth."
- **embezzle** (v) – steal money fraudulently from an employer/bank – "embezzled millions"
- **extol** (v) – praise highly – "extolled the beauty of modern art"
- **impugn** (v) – challenge as false, cast doubt (impugn someone's character)
- **obfuscate** (v) – deliberately make confusing – "obfuscate the benefits"
- **placate** (v) – appease by giving something up – "Nothing could placate him."
- **repudiate** (v) – reject with disapproval – "repudiated the students' request"

Nouns
- **quid pro quo** (n) – something given in return for something else – "We'll need some quid pro quo."
- **catch-22** (n) – unsolvable problem because the solution is part of the problem – "It's a catch-22."
- **epitome** /ɪˈpɪtəmi/ (n) – the perfect typical example – "the epitome of excellence"
- **dichotomy** (n, between) – contrast between two opposed things – "dichotomy between development and environment"
- **myriad** (n) – a great number of things – "a myriad of trees"
- **credence** (n) – belief in truth of something (give credence to) – "gave credence to the claims"
- **malaise** (n) – vague discomfort, early sign of illness – "headaches and malaise"
- **sycophant** /ˈsɪkəfənt/ (n) – person who flatters the powerful – "sycophants in the media"
- **dilettante** (n) – dabbler with shallow knowledge – "She's really a dilettante."
- **zenith** (n) – the peak/highest point – "His career reached its zenith."

Adverbs
- **annually** – once a year – "file taxes annually"
- **reluctantly** – unwillingly – "came in reluctantly"
- **unabashedly** – without embarrassment/apology – "unabashedly asked for a raise"
- **vaguely** – not clearly (vaguely remember) – "I vaguely remember."
- **fervently** – with great enthusiasm/intensity – "worked fervently"
- **diligently** – with hard, focused effort (work diligently) – "working diligently on this"
- **vicariously** – through another person's experience – "live vicariously through you"
- **hastily** – too fast, with little care – "hastily completed the report"
- **utterly** – completely – "utterly ridiculous"
- **intently** – with sharp focus – "listening intently"

15 irregular verbs (present – past – past participle)
- **arise – arose – arisen** – appear/occur – "New problems arose."
- **bear – bore – borne/born** – turn slightly (bear left); endure; give birth/produce – "bore the responsibility"
- **behold – beheld – beheld** – see/observe – "Behold!"
- **creep – crept – crept** – move slowly/quietly – "I crept into the kitchen."
- **deal – dealt – dealt** – distribute cards; trade – "She dealt the cards."
- **forsake – forsook – forsaken** – abandon – "forsaken his vows"
- **lie – lay – lain** – be in resting position – "I lay in bed all day."
- **lay – laid – laid** – put down; prepare – "laid the groundwork"
- **mean – meant – meant** – intend to refer to – "That's not what I meant."
- **overtake – overtook – overtaken** – pass from behind – "We overtook the bus."
- **rise – rose – risen** – go up; get up (rise and shine) – "The sun has risen."
- **swear – swore – sworn** – promise – "He swore to tell the truth."
- **shake – shook – shaken** – move up/down; tremble – "We shook hands."
- **weep – wept – wept** – cry – "He wept for days."
- **withdraw – withdrew – withdrawn** – take back/remove – "I withdrew my name."

## 197. Advanced Vocabulary that will BLOW YOUR MIND 🤯

Verbs
- **bamboozle** (v) – trick or deceive by confusing – "I was bamboozled into buying it."
- **dwindle** (v) – become smaller/fewer (syn. decline, diminish, fade) – "Our membership has been dwindling."
- **jeopardize** (v) – put at risk of loss/harm/failure – "jeopardize your chances/health"
- **entertain** (v) – give consideration to an idea/offer – "He refused to entertain my offer."
- **articulate** (v) /-leɪt/ – express clearly and effectively; adj /-lət/ = able to express clearly – "He articulated the policy changes."

Adjectives
- **sanctimonious** – self-righteous, hypocritical – "a sanctimonious speech"
- **preposterous** – ridiculous, absurd – "That's preposterous."
- **levelheaded** – practical, balanced, composed – "very levelheaded under pressure"
- **daunting** – intimidating, discouraging – "the daunting prospect of three exams"
- **egregious** – outstandingly bad, glaring – "an egregious mistake"

Nouns
- **superiority** – being better/stronger than another – "an air of superiority"
- **custody** – legal right/duty to care for someone (child) – award/get/share/grant/lose custody
- **novelty** – quality of being new and unusual – "the novelty wore off"
- **comeback** – becoming popular/successful again – make/mount/stage a comeback
- **scene** – public display of anger – "Don't make a scene."

Adverbs
- **blindly** – without thinking or understanding – "blindly follow orders"
- **sparsely** – in small numbers – "sparsely populated"
- **expeditiously** – with speed and efficiency – "act expeditiously"
- **auspiciously** – in a way suggesting a successful future – "didn't start auspiciously"
- **blithely** – happily, without worry/indifferently – "blithely ignored the evidence"

## 198. Advanced Vocabulary to Describe People (like politicians)

Negative
- **unscrupulous** (adj) – dishonest, unethical, unfair (opposite "scrupulous" is rarer) – "an unscrupulous politician"
- **vulgar** (adj) – lacking good manners/taste, crude – "vulgar remarks"
- **sycophant** (n) – insincerely flatters people in authority for advantage – "a Trump sycophant"
- **glib** (adj) – speaking confidently but insincerely, without careful thought – "a glib explanation / glib speaker"
- **partisan hack** (n) – person who backs their party over what's right or true; **hack** = unprofessional, derogatory
- **smooth talker** (n) – very persuasive speaker, often not sincere – "He's such a smooth talker."

Positive
- **charismatic** (adj) – energetic, likable personality that draws people in (not necessarily moral) – "a charismatic leader"
- **shrewd** (adj) – good at judging people/situations, makes advantageous decisions (can imply callousness) – "a shrewd and calculated politician"
- **diplomatic** (adj) – skilled at negotiating/settling disputes – "diplomatic strengths"
- **principled** (adj) – strong beliefs about right and wrong, consistent – "a principled politician"
- **eloquent** (adj) – expressing yourself readily, clearly, effectively – "an eloquent speaker"
- **resolute** (adj) – determined in character, actions or ideas – "resolute in her convictions"
- Also: **fiscally conservative but socially liberal** (phr) – frugal with money, liberal on social issues

## 199. Advanced Vocabulary to Talk About the ECONOMY 💵

Collocations
- **economic downturn** – significant decline in economic activity lasting months (less extreme than a depression)
- **economic indicators** – figures (unemployment, inflation, GDP) showing the economy's condition
- **economic outlook** – expected future state of the economy
- **economic impact** (of/on) – financial effect of something – "Tourism has a strong economic impact."
- **economic environment** – condition of a country's economy and how it affects business
- **economic data** – data describing the economy
- **economic growth** – increase in production of goods/services vs. a previous period
- **cut back on** (phrasal v) – reduce – "cut back on spending"

Terms
- **GDP (gross domestic product)** – standard measure of value added by a country's production in a period
- **monetary policy** – central bank/government control of the money supply and its availability
- **QE (quantitative easing)** – central bank buys securities to lower interest rates, increase money supply, drive lending
- **bubble** (n) – rapid rise in asset prices followed by a collapse – "tech bubble, housing bubble"; the bubble bursts
- **market crash** – sudden dramatic fall in stock prices
- **debt** – cumulative money a government has borrowed over history
- **deficit** – one year's gap between government spending and income
- **recession** – decline in economic activity, technically GDP falling two quarters in a row (depression = more severe)
- **appreciate** (v) – increase in value (appreciate against the euro) vs **depreciate** (v) – decrease in value
- **monopoly** (n) – exclusive control of a product/service market supply
- **standard of living** – level of wealth and comfort in a society

## 200. Advanced Vocabulary to Understand American Politics 🇺🇸

- **necessary evil** (phr) – something disliked but essential (government, or any unpleasant but needed thing)
- **impeachment / impeach** (n/v) – serious charge of misconduct against a public official, may lead to removal
- **quid pro quo** (n) – something given in exchange for something of value – "a favor for a favor"
- **compromise** (n/v) – work together/meet in the middle to reach an agreement
- **leak** (v) – make secret information public – "leak documents"
- **whistleblower** (n) – person who exposes wrongdoing to an authority
- **Big Brother** (n) – government/authority with total control that limits freedom (from Orwell's 1984) – "Big Brother is watching you."
- **lobby** (v/n) – try to influence an official to act or change a law; **lobbyist** (n) – does this as a job
- **campaign** (n/v) – organized effort to get someone elected – "campaigning for votes"
- **pundit** (n) – expert commentator asked for opinions, esp. on politics – "political pundits"
- **red tape** (n) – unnecessary rules/processes causing delay – "a lot of red tape to open a business"
- **shady** (adj, informal) – dishonest, untrustworthy – "a shady politician"
- **cynic / cynical** (n/adj) – believes people act only out of self-interest
- **treason** (n) – working with an enemy to undermine/overthrow the government – "commit treason"

## 201. Advice to achieve your fluency goals in 2021 | Interactive Q&A with Wes

Tips
- Accent is how people from a region sound; pronunciation is producing correct sounds so people understand you (mutual intelligibility). Pronunciation matters more; reducing accent is an optional personal goal.
- Listening: listen at your level or slightly above; slow down videos (YouTube speed) if too fast; music is hard because you can't control speed.
- Reading: read at your level on topics you like; Breaking News English (free, levels 0-3) lets you control difficulty. Aim for ~30 min/day.
- Reading builds vocabulary through natural repetition and improves writing passively (you absorb grammar and usage).
- Movies feel harder than news because of faster speech, idioms, slang; learn informal vocabulary and idioms.
- Practice all four skills; reading, writing, listening can be done alone; for speaking find a partner or local speaking club, language exchange, record yourself, give pretend presentations, or think to yourself in English (inner voice).
- Improve writing: proofread for one type of mistake at a time (articles, spelling, prepositions, punctuation) and keep a list of your recurring errors.
- Beginners: children's books and nursery rhymes (simple words, rhythm); don't jump to texts far above your level.
- You don't need to live in an English-speaking country or study alone; it helps with motivation and accountability, but self-motivated learners can do it alone.
- Exams (IELTS/TOEFL): you can take it anytime; study the test format and do practice tests to feel comfortable.
- Phrasal verbs: don't stress; pick them up gradually through reading/listening; one can have many meanings; replacing with a single word is fine.
- Translation is OK for beginners; later use an English-English dictionary (Cambridge Dictionary) instead of translating.

Vocab
- **conflate** (v) – treat two different things as the same
- **mutual intelligibility** (n) – speakers of different languages/accents understand each other
- **enunciate** (v) – pronounce words clearly
- **pick up** (phr v) – learn informally; lift; collect someone; pay (the check)
- **pollyannish** (adj) – always optimistic
- **vociferous** (adj) – expressing opinions or complaints loudly and repeatedly
