- **skyscraper** (n) – very tall building, typically 40-50+ floors – "the Empire State Building"
- **jaywalk** (v) – cross the street where/when you're not supposed to – "Everyone jaywalks in New York."
- **pond** (n) – small body of water surrounded by land (smaller than a lake)
- **drizzle** (v/n) – very light rain – "It's drizzling."
- **ferry** (n) – boat for public transport between places – "the Staten Island Ferry"
- **the Big Apple** – nickname for New York City

## 203. American Accent Workout 🇺🇸 Repeat These Sentences Every Day!

- Drill lesson (listen, repeat, shadow). English is stress-timed: stressed syllables at regular beats, everything else reduced.
- Unstressed **to** -> schwa "ta"; **for** -> "fer/fur"; **and** -> "n".
- Flap T: T between two vowels sounds like a soft D ("go to the" -> "go da the", "get a" -> "ged a").
- Use "the" (thee) before vowel sounds: go to the airport / office / artwork.
- Reductions: **going to** -> gonna; **got to** -> gotta; **want to** -> wanna; **have to** -> hafta.
- Linking: final consonant joins next word's initial vowel (get a, pick up a, take out, meet up, work out, and meet his [h dropped]).
- Drill frame: "I'm going to go to the store (and get a few groceries / to pick up a few things / for some essentials)"; same pattern for pharmacy, bank, gym, mall, airport, doctor, office, theater, museum.

## 204. American Accent Workout 💪 Linking, Reductions & Real Dialogues 🇺🇸

- **Assimilation**: did you -> "didja" (D+Y = J); don't you -> "donchu" (T+Y = ch); what's your -> "whachur" (S+Y = sh).
- **Reductions**: gonna, wanna, should have, have to -> hafta, I'm gonna -> "amina" (further reduced); unstressed "you", "to", "for" go to schwa.
- **Flap T**: T between vowels -> soft D (what do you, ought to, get out of, go to the, bite to eat).
- **Linking**: from a mile away, get out of, waiting around, find one on the app, come up with an excuse.
- **Elision** (sounds dropped): T/D between consonants vanishes (just taking, slipped my, find one).
- **G-dropping**: -ing -> -in' (coming -> comin', taking -> takin').
- **Glottal stop**: T before N or in dropped-G verbs (waiting -> "wai'in", kitten, mitten, mountain, certain, "uh-oh").
- **Intrusion**: added y/w glide to link vowels (to do -> "to-w-do").
- **Co-articulation**: neighboring sounds with same tongue position overlap (n+t in "going to finish").
- Stressed "the" (thee) before vowel sound: the app.
- Yes/no questions have rising intonation.
- **see something coming from a mile away** – to foresee something obvious
- **go back to square one** – start over after failure (= go back to the drawing board)
- **slip your mind** – to forget ("it slipped my mind")
- **get an earful** – be spoken to angrily; **give someone an earful** – speak angrily to someone
- **What's up with ...?** – informal, "why is this happening / what's wrong with ...?"
- **It's your call.** – it's your decision
- **make it on time** – arrive in time
- **a bite to eat** – a quick meal
- **take it easy** – relax; **What's your hurry?** – why rush?

## 205. American Culture Quiz 🇺🇸  (ONLY 3% CAN PASS THIS TEST)

Gist: live US-culture trivia quiz. Answers:
- Thanksgiving: 4th Thursday of November.
- Queen of Pop: Madonna (Michael Jackson = King of Pop).
- City with Coca-Cola Museum and MLK birth home: Atlanta (capital of Georgia).
- West Coast fast-food favorite: In-N-Out Burger.
- US senators: 100 (2 per state).
- "What's up, Doc?": Bugs Bunny.
- Longtime Jeopardy host: Alex Trebek (28 years, died 2020).
- Lin-Manuel Miranda's musical: Hamilton (Alexander Hamilton, Founding Father).
- "Roll Tide": University of Alabama (Crimson Tide).
- Min. age to buy tobacco: 21 (raised from 18 in 2019); alcohol 21.
- Voting age 18; driving age typically 16 (learner's permits ~15 in some states).
- The Fourth: July 4th, Independence Day.
- Rice Krispies Treats: cereal + butter + marshmallows.
- Event watched for commercials: the Super Bowl (American football championship; new ads debut).
- Open question "Americans love ...": food, flag, donuts, burgers, baseball, happy hour, etc.

## 206. American English Pronunciation for Intermediate to Advanced Learners

- **Schwa** /ə/ – most common English sound; unstressed vowel (ba-**nə**-nə; alphabet). Reduce "to" to schwa: "I'm going tə the mall".
- **IPA** – International Phonetic Alphabet; used in dictionaries to show pronunciation. Phonetic spelling = spelling words as they sound.
- **Syllables** – chin test (hand under chin; each chin touch = one syllable). **humanitarian** = 6 syllables, stress on 4th (-tar-).
- **Flap T** – T between vowels or before dark L or after R sounds like soft D: water, Italy, metal, little, forty, "I bought a car". Contrast: true T in "time", stop T in "mountain", "sh" in "pronunciation".
- **Silent letters** – **rendezvous** (silent z, French).
- **-ed endings** – /t/ after voiceless sounds (helped, watched); /d/ after voiced sounds (played); /ɪd/ only after t/d (waited, needed, wanted). Common mistake: adding extra syllable.
- **Minimal pair** – words differing by one sound: right/light, sheep/ship, fan/van, think/sink, coffee/copy, bus/buzz. (profession/obsession = rhyme; eight/ate = homonyms.)
- **Noun/verb stress** (2-syllable words): noun stressed on 1st syllable, verb on 2nd: PREsent/preSENT, OBject/obJECT, CONduct/conDUCT.

## 207. American Idioms & Expressions | English Quiz Lesson 🇺🇸

- **no worries** – informal "you're welcome / no problem"
- **plead the fifth** – refuse to answer so as not to incriminate yourself (also joking: "I'm not telling")
- **break a leg** – good luck (before a performance)
- **game changer** – something that changes how things are done
- **bread and butter** – your main source of income ("teaching is my bread and butter")
- **catnap** – a short daytime sleep
- **a piece of cake** – very easy
- **hold your horses** – wait / slow down (a bit impolite)
- **the icing on the cake** – something that makes a good thing even better
- **shoot the breeze** – chat casually
- **have your head in the clouds** – be unrealistic
- **off your rocker / fall off your rocker** – crazy
- **have a lot on your plate** – be very busy
- **The ball is in your court.** – it's your turn to act/decide
- **have a sweet tooth** – love sweet food
- **get your ducks in a row** – get organized/prepared
- **get hitched** – get married (informal; also "tie the knot")
- **take a rain check** – politely postpone an invitation to an unspecified later time

## 208. American Slang vs. Informal Vocab vs. Formal Vocab | English Class #Shorts 👨‍🏫

- very hungry: **famished** (formal) / hungry (informal) / **hangry** (slang, hungry + angry)
- immature: **infantile** (formal) / **childish** (informal) / **little** (slang)
- goodbye: **Good day to you, sir/ma'am** (formal) / **See you later** (informal) / **Bye, Felicia** (slang, from the movie Friday)

## 209. Americans Try to Guess British Idioms 🇬🇧️

- **kerfuffle** (n) – fuss, commotion, skirmish over differing views
- **all mouth and no trousers** – all talk, no action
- **knees up** (adj/n) – lively party/dancing
- **blinding** (adj) – excellent
- **know your onions** – know your stuff, be well informed
- **chuffed** (adj) – very pleased (chuffed to bits)
- **miffed** (adj) – mildly annoyed
- **corker** (n) – outstanding person/thing
- **numpty** (n/adj) – stupid person
- **doofer** (n) – unnamed object, thingamajig
- **odds and sods** – miscellaneous bits and pieces
- **earwig** (v) – eavesdrop
- **spawny** (adj) – lucky
- **eating irons** – cutlery
- **steaming** (adj) – extremely drunk (or extremely angry)
- **the fuzz** – the police
- **wag off** (v) – waste time, skive
- **honk** (v) – vomit
- **warts and all** – including all flaws
- **idiot box** – TV

## 210. Answering ALL Your Questions about English | Q&A w/ Interactive English

Gist: live Q&A. Key takeaways:
- Listen to many accents (US, UK, non-native) to improve comprehension and vocabulary.
- Level-graded sites: Breaking News English (levels 0-3), British Council vocabulary, English Club.
- **especially / particularly** – interchangeable for "more than usual"; "especially" also singles out one thing ("everyone must study, especially you").
- Writing: treat as reflective (review and correct); reading complements writing by showing correct grammar/prepositions in context.
- Krashen's **i+1**: practice at your level plus a bit more; slow audio to 0.75x, use subtitles for listening.
- **vanish / disappear** – synonyms (disappear more common).
- **gotta / wanna / gonna** – reductions of got to / want to / going to.
- **talk trash / trash talk** – insult an opponent to distract them (same meaning).
- **a bummer / a downer** (n) – something disappointing.
- "I have had to ..." = present perfect of main verb "have".
- "When did you buy ..." – use base verb after "did", not "bought".
- **Shadowing** – repeat exactly, copying reductions, intonation, emotion (more than listen-and-repeat).
- Remember vocabulary via spaced repetition and reading several articles on one topic.
- Plateau: keep practicing or maintain; for motivation, use intrinsic reasons (travel, work).
- **fingers crossed** – hoping for luck; **pithy** – brief and to the point; **if you don't use it, you lose it**.

## 211. Are You Making These Common GRAMMAR MISTAKES?

- **didn't use to** (not "didn't used to"): verb "used to" = past habit (I used to wake up early; negative: I didn't use to). Adjective "be used to + -ing" = accustomed (I'm used to running in heat).
- **to me vs for me**: "to me" = perception/opinion/impact ("news to me", "to me it won't work", "read this story to me"); "for me" = for my benefit/need ("do the homework for me", "too spicy for me", "important for me"). Often interchangeable ("new to me" more common).
- **from vs since**: "from" = starting point of a span (from an early age; open 10am onward; March to May); "since" = point in time with present perfect (has been here since 10am).
- **in spite of / despite** – both correct, mean "although"; used with noun or gerund; despite slightly more formal; more common in writing.
- **been to vs gone to**: "been to" = visited and returned; "gone to" = went and hasn't returned (She's gone to NYC, back tomorrow).
- **in / on / at**: in = enclosed area (in the city); on = surface, media (on the table, on YouTube, on TV); at = specific point (at the library).
- **that / which / who**: who = people; that/which = animals/things; "that" preferred in restrictive clauses (needed info, no commas); "which" for non-restrictive (between commas); "which" in restrictive clauses is more British.
- **e.g. vs i.e.**: e.g. (exempli gratia) = for example; i.e. (id est) = that is / in other words. Mostly writing.
- **must vs have to**: must = formal obligation, deduction ("you must be thirsty"), strong recommendation; have to = everyday necessity, past/future ("had to wait", "will have to"). Rules in formal text: "applicants must submit".
- **split infinitive** – adverb between to and verb ("to boldly go", "to immediately quit"); acceptable, adds emphasis.
- **some vs any**: some = affirmative and offers/requests (Would you like some tea?); any = negatives, questions, after if/whether ("if you have any questions"). Phrases: **some pig!** (impressive), **and then some** (and more), **by any means necessary**.
- **who vs whom**: who = subject (he/she test); whom = object of verb/preposition (him/her test: "to whom", "whom do you believe"). Whom is formal; "who" now common in speech.
- **rummage through** (v) – search carelessly through something.

## 212. As Soon As You Learn Adverb Clauses, Your English Will Instantly Improve!

- **Adverb clause** = dependent clause (subject + verb) starting with a subordinating conjunction; modifies verb/adjective/adverb; needs a main clause (I went home **because it was raining**).
- **Time**: when, before, after, while, until, as soon as, by the time. "By the time" -> main verb in future perfect/past perfect (By the time I arrived, everyone had finished).
- **Condition**: if, unless, provided (that) (You can go out if you finish your homework).
- **Reason**: because (most common), since, as (They canceled the marathon since it was raining).
- **Purpose**: so that, in case, in order to + base verb, in order that + subject (more formal) (He woke up early so that he wouldn't be late).
- **Contrast**: although, though, even though (adds emphasis), whereas (I prefer novels, whereas my friend prefers movies).
- **Place**: where, wherever (This is the house where I live).
- **Position/commas**: clause first -> comma (When the rain stopped, we went outside); clause last -> no comma; mid-sentence = nonrestrictive extra info.
- Answers when, why, where, how.

## 213. Ask me ANYTHING about learning English | Q&A w/ Wes

- **advice** (n, non-count) – no plural; use "a piece of advice"; **suggestion** is countable ("three suggestions"). Pronounce noun /ədˈvaɪs/ (s), verb **advise** /ədˈvaɪz/ (z).
- Passive learning (movies, music) -> make it active: write a short summary afterwards.
- Past simple vs present perfect: often interchangeable; "had a car" = finished; "I've had a car" = link to present.
- Intermediate plateau is normal; find motivation (travel, classes) to keep going.
- **effective** – meaningful, works well ("chunking is effective") vs **efficient** – gets it done in the quickest way, saves time ("do things efficiently").
- **jail** (short-term holding, e.g. police station) vs **prison** (long-term institution) vs **penitentiary** (~prison).
- Improve writing: write a lot (emails, summaries) + read a lot (see grammar, prepositions in context); keep a mistake journal: write a paragraph, self-correct one error type per pass (spelling, capitalization, articles, subject-verb agreement), chart counts to find weak spots.
- Fluency without moving abroad: possible; need discipline, daily listening/reading, and find speaking partners.
- Find a topic niche and read several articles on it; same words recur and stick.
- **thrice** – three times (old-fashioned; just say "three times").
- Mixing US/UK pronunciation in one sentence is fine (advertisement, tomato, aluminum/aluminium); exposure to many accents is good.
- Confidence in speaking: vocabulary + repeated discussions on the same topic.
- **cut down on / cut back on** – reduce the amount ("cut down on chocolate").
- **hold your horses** – wait a moment, be patient.
- **real** (exists) vs **realistic** (practical, doable): "swimming the English Channel isn't realistic."
- **out of the blue** – suddenly, unexpectedly.
- Learn idioms through dialogues/listening and use them in conversation (book: Speak English Like an American).
- **ripple effects** – side effects/knock-on results that keep spreading from an action.
- **flirt** (n) – person who is overly friendly romantically; opposite ~ "cold".
- **when all is said and done** – when everything is finished (learn as one chunk).
- Grammar checkers (Grammarly, Gmail) are useful for spotting mistakes.

## 214. Athletic Words about Sports & the Olympics that You Need to Know 🏅

**anthem** (n) /ˈænθəm/ – song of a nation/group – "national anthem plays before games"
**amateur** (n) /ˈæməʃər/ (US) or /ˈæmətʃʊr/ – does it for pleasure, not a professional – "amateur skiers"
**banned** (v/adj) – prohibited from taking part – "banned from the competition"
**doping** (n) /ˈdoʊpɪŋ/ – using drugs to boost performance – "caught doping"
**medal** (n) /ˈmedl/ – piece of metal awarded as honor (US flap T: sounds like "metal")
**participant** (n) /pɑːrˈtɪsɪpənt/ (stress 2nd) – person taking part – "marathon participants"
**podium** (n) /ˈpoʊdiəm/ – platform where medal winners stand
**preliminaries** (n) /prɪˈlɪmɪneriz/ – early rounds to decide finalists; "preliminary round" (adj)
**representative** (n) – person who represents a country/company/school; stress on 3rd syllable (-zen-)
**spectator** (n) /ˈspekteɪtər/ – person who watches an event
**sponsor** (n) – company that pays an athlete to promote its products
**sportsmanship** (n) – fairness and honesty in sport – "show good sportsmanship"
**standings** (n) – ranking of teams/individuals at a given time – "Norway leads the standings"
**torch** (n) – large flame on a stick (UK: also flashlight) – "Olympic torch relay"
**venue** (n) /ˈvenjuː/ – place where an event is held – "stadium, field, concert venue"

## 215. Avoid Saying "I disagree." | Use these phrases to build your vocabulary

- **I beg to differ** – polite disagreement (more common than "I beg to disagree").
- **I don't see it that way / That's not the way I see it** – polite.
- **I have a different perspective / point of view** – for serious discussions, not trivial opinions (pizza).
- **With all due respect, ...** – very polite/formal, esp. to authority (judge, boss).
- **I see your point, but... / That's a good point, but... / I understand where you're coming from, but...** – acknowledge then counter.
- **I don't think that's (quite) right** – when something is incorrect.
- **I'd say the exact opposite / It's the exact opposite** – more confrontational (collocation: *exact opposite*).
- Intensified: **absolutely / totally / completely / strongly / vehemently disagree** (*vehemently* – in a strong, emotional way).

## 216. Avoid Saying ...UM...ER...UH 🚫  #interactiveenglish #shorts

Replace filler words (um, uh) with stalling phrases:
- **How can I say this...** / **I need a moment to think about this.**
- **What I was going to say is...**
- **I'm drawing a blank** – can't think of it.
- **It's on the tip of my tongue** – almost remember it.
- **I lost my train of thought** – forgot what I was saying.

## 217. Awesome Listening & Speaking Practice | Transportation

Transportation vocabulary quiz (describe-and-guess): **plane**, **taxi** (yellow car, you pay), **elevator**, **subway / metro** (underground train), **motorcycle** (motorized bicycle, two wheels), **bus**; also **car**, **train**, **boat**.

## 218. BEACH or BITCH? | How to Pronounce These Words Correctly

- Minimal pair: long **/iː/** (beach) vs short **/ɪ/** (bitch); don't avoid "beach" out of fear.
- Long /iː/: mouth slightly open, smile ("say cheese"), tongue arched high, tip behind bottom teeth.
- Short /ɪ/: relaxed jaw/lips, tongue slightly lower; unstressed-feeling vowel.
- Practice pairs: **sheep/ship**, **piece/piss**, **beat/bit**, **each/itch**, **beach/bitch**.
- Sentences: "Let's go lay out on the beach." "We're going to the beach for vacation." "I saw the sunset from the beach."

## 219. BEAUTIFUL IDIOMS & EXPRESSIONS ABOUT ART 🎨

- **paint yourself into a corner** – put yourself in a situation with few/no good outcomes
- **a picture is worth a thousand words** – an image expresses a complex idea better than words
- **paint the town red** – go out partying/celebrating – "Let's paint the town red."
- **see the big picture** – see the situation as a whole, not just details
- **What's wrong with this picture?** – (sarcastic) asking someone to spot what's wrong
- **Do I have to paint you a picture?** – frustrated: must I explain more clearly?
- **like watching paint dry** – extremely boring, slow, tedious
- **paint a (terrible / bleak / rosy) picture** – describe something in a certain way, often negative

## 220. BECOME FLUENT IN 2020 | 7 Things You Need to Do 🎉

1. Identify one mistake (grammar/pronunciation) and fix it; revisit after 1 week, then after 1 month.
2. Commit to reading a whole (longer) book; choose something interesting or already familiar (e.g. book of a movie you saw); a few pages a day.
3. Do an active learning exercise (grammar practice, listening comprehension, quizzes, answering in comments) vs passive (just listening/reading).
4. Add one more form of listening exposure (podcast, audiobook, more YouTube).
5. Add more time and make it a routine (commute, walk, lunch break, cooking).
6. Start a diary (events, goals, feelings); even a few sentences help.
7. Have a conversation: record a video or audio introducing yourself and share it.

## 221. BINOMIAL PAIRS QUIZ | Useful & Important Phrases

Binomial pair = two words joined by a conjunction (usually *and/or*, sometimes *by*).
**loud and clear** – clearly understandable – "I hear you loud and clear."
**give or take** – approximately – "100,000 people, give or take."
**all or nothing** – completely or not at all
**the ins and outs** – detailed/complicated facts – "know/learn the ins and outs of..."
**fair and square** – honestly, by the rules – "We won fair and square."
**wear and tear** – damage from ordinary use – "normal wear and tear"
**safe and sound** – unharmed, safe – "back home safe and sound"
**by leaps and bounds** – to a great degree/very fast – "improving by leaps and bounds"
**pros and cons** – advantages and disadvantages
**sick and tired (of)** – can't tolerate any more (~ fed up with)
**little by little** – gradually (also: slow and steady; step by step = one step at a time)
**do's and don'ts** – instructions/warnings about what to do and not do

## 222. BORROW vs LEND | Confusing English Words

- **borrow** – take something temporarily and give it back ("Can I borrow your car?")
- **lend** – give something temporarily and get it back ("Can you lend me some money?")
- Trick: borrow = take, lend = give. Never "borrow me money".

## 223. BRITISH 🇬🇧 and AMERICAN 🇺🇸 Idioms to Improve Your English

American:
**shoot the breeze** – chat casually about nothing serious
**take a rain check** – politely decline now, do it later
**on the john** – on the toilet
**flip a bitch** (slang, US) – make a U-turn
**jonesing (for)** – craving something – "jonesing for coffee"
**a Hail Mary** – desperate last-ditch long shot (from American football)
**put up your dukes** – raise your fists, let's fight
**John Hancock** – your signature
British:
**chin wag** – chat/gossip
**skive** – skip work/school
**thick** – stupid
**get a bollocking** – be scolded/told off angrily
**chuffed (to bits)** – very pleased
**cock up** – a screw-up (noun); to cock up (verb)
**bugger all** – nothing

## 224. BUSINESS ENGLISH | Quiz for Working Professionals

**go the extra mile** – do more work/effort than required
**The customer is always right** – motto: prioritize customer service/satisfaction
**declare bankruptcy / file for bankruptcy** (collocations); a company can "go through bankruptcy" or "go bankrupt"
**HR** – Human Resources (hiring, training, benefits)
Interview weakness question: be honest and humble, name a real weakness, then say how you are improving it.
**regards / best regards** – safest formal email closing (avoid "yours faithfully" for unknown people; "best wishes", "take it easy" don't fit)
**pull one's weight** – do your fair share of work (often negative: "not pulling their weight")
**pick up the slack** – do work someone else hasn't done
**CYA** – "cover your ass": protect yourself (also "cover your assets")

## 225. Become Fluent in English | You MUST do these 3 things...

1. Exposure: create an "English bubble" (daily reading + listening, from morning to night); keep English materials close and easy to access.
2. Practice: "practice puts brains in your muscles"; get a variety of the same review (same vocabulary/grammar in different conversations or articles); do active learning (quizzes, conversations, writing).
3. Mental toughness: stay focused (know your goals), motivated (know why you learn), confident (don't fear mistakes), and **stay on your toes** (be ready to react/respond).

## 226. Brilliant Business Phrases You Should Know for Work

**on the same page** – in agreement about how something should be done
**get someone up to speed** – give someone the latest information
**go back to the drawing board** – start over from the beginning
**pick up the slack** – do work others haven't done – "She's not working hard, so someone must pick up the slack."
**burn the midnight oil** – work late into the night (usually continuous tense)
**go the extra mile** – make more effort than expected
**cut corners** – do something quickly and less thoroughly than you should
**pass the buck** – avoid responsibility and make someone else deal with it
**get the ball rolling** – begin/start something
**work around the clock** – work all day and night – "We've been working around the clock."
**pull your weight** – work as hard as others in the group
**keep me posted** – keep me updated as the situation changes
**up in the air** – undecided/uncertain – "Our plans are up in the air."
**hit the ground running** – start working effectively immediately
**due diligence** – careful investigation before a contract/deal – "do your due diligence"

## 227. Build Academic Vocabulary and Practice: SIGNIFICANT

**significant** (adj) /sɪɡˈnɪfɪkənt/ (stress 2nd syllable) – important; or large enough to be noticed – "a significant event"; "significant other"
**insignificant** (adj) – not important – "an insignificant detail"
**significance** (n) – importance – "What's the significance of this?"

## 228. Build Your English Vocabulary 👉 PROCESS  (Word of the Day)

**process** (n) – series of steps to reach a result – "the recruiting process", "in the process of..."; stress 1st syllable (UK /ˈprəʊses/, US /ˈprɑːses/)
**process** (v) – go through steps/handle information – "I couldn't process what was happening."
**processed** (adj) – artificial/treated, esp. food – "processed cheese"
**process of elimination** – cross out wrong answers to find the right one
**ongoing process** – continuing process

## 229. CAT'S OUT OF THE BAG | Simple & Easy English Idiom

**let the cat out of the bag** – reveal a secret; "The cat's out of the bag" = the secret is out.

## 230. CAUSATIVE VERBS - Have, Get, Make, Let, Help | Super Important Grammar

- Causative verbs: the subject causes someone/something to do or have something done.
- **have someone + base verb** – "I had my brother paint my house." **have something + past participle** – "I had my house painted." (usually by paying; more formal than get)
- **get someone + to + base verb** – "She got a mechanic to fix her car." **get something + past participle** – "She got her car fixed."
- **make + object + base verb** – force/cause: "My boss made me work late." "The movie made me cry."
- **let + object + base verb** – permission: "She let me borrow her car."
- **help + object + (to) base verb** – assist; "to" optional and usually omitted: "I help my friends make dinner."
- Quiz: I had a plumber fix my toilet; she let her friend borrow her jacket; the weather made us alter our plans; we got a lawyer to look over the contract.

## 231. CHALLENGING RIDDLES IN ENGLISH | Critical Thinking Lesson

Riddle-game lesson (listening/creative thinking). Riddles and answers: two hands but can't clap = clock; dog that never bites = hot dog; word pronounced "poorly" = poorly; months with 28 days = all of them; "20 ate chickens, how many didn't" = 10 (eight/ate wordplay); thing tigers have that no other animals have = baby tigers; share it and you no longer have it = a secret; hold in left hand but not right = your right hand/elbow; loses its head in the morning, gets it back at night = pillow.

## 232. CLEAR THE AIR | Advanced (C2) #Idiom to Build Your Vocabulary 💪

**clear the air** – remove bad feelings between people (e.g. by talking it out) – "We really need to clear the air."

## 233. COLLECTIVE NOUN QUIZ 📝 Can you name these groups of people? 🤔 #interactiveenglish #shorts

students = **class**; singers = **choir**; musicians = **band**; many people gathered = **crowd**; sailors = **crew**; players = **team**; employees = **staff**; rioters = **mob**

## 234. COMMONLY CONFUSED WORDS IN ENGLSH | Prefixes Un-  vs Dis-  | Build English Vocabulary

- Prefixes *un-* and *dis-* both mean "not"; un- can also mean the opposite action (undress); dis- can mean lack of (dishonesty) or opposite action (disappear).
**unqualified** (adj) – lacks skill/experience for a job
**disqualified** (adj) – barred from a competition for breaking rules/cheating
**unable** (adj) – lacking the ability/opportunity to do something
**disable** (v) – make something stop working – opposite of *enable*
**unarmed** (adj) – without weapons
**disarmed** (v, past) – gave up/had weapons taken away
**uninterested** (adj) – bored, indifferent
**disinterested** (adj) – impartial/unbiased, or doesn't care about outcome (traditional meaning; often used loosely like uninterested)
**unlike** (prep) – not similar to – "She is unlike her mother." **unlike** (v) – withdraw a "like" online
**dislike** (v) – not enjoy
**ungraceful** (adj) – not elegant in movement
**disgraceful** (adj) – shameful (from *disgrace*, bring shame on)

## 235. COMMON ACRONYMS IN AMERICAN ENGLISH 🇺🇸

Initialism = acronym where you say each letter (LOL, FBI); capitalize in formal writing.
- **BRB** – be right back
- **IMHO / IMO** – in my humble opinion / in my opinion
- **NVM** – never mind
- **BTW** – by the way
- **THX** – thanks
- **B4** – before
- **2nite** – tonight
- **STBY** – sucks to be you (joking sympathy)
- **JK** – just kidding
- **TTYL** – talk to you later
- **LOL** – laugh out loud
- **IDK** – I don't know
- **FAQ** – frequently asked questions
- **SUV** – sport utility vehicle
- **DOA** – dead on arrival
- **MD** – medical doctor
- **SEO** – search engine optimization
- **DOB** – date of birth
- **CTA** – call to action
- **MBA** – Master of Business Administration
- **EST / CST / MST / PST** – Eastern / Central / Mountain / Pacific Standard Time
- **OT** – overtime
- **HR** – human resources
- **ETA** – estimated time of arrival
- **CIA** – Central Intelligence Agency
- **DNC** – Democratic National Committee
- **MIA** – missing in action (also casual: "he's MIA")
- **POW** – prisoner of war
- **FBI** – Federal Bureau of Investigation
- **CSI** – crime scene investigators
- **CDC** – Center for Disease Control
- **NBA / NFL / PGA** – National Basketball Association / National Football League / Professional Golfers' Association
- **MLA** – Modern Language Association (citation style; also APA)
- **NAACP** – National Association for the Advancement of Colored People
- **AA** – Alcoholics Anonymous
- **WWE** – World Wrestling Entertainment

## 236. COMMON TONGUE TWISTERS | Practice Your English Pronunciation 😜

- How much wood would a woodchuck chuck if a woodchuck could chuck wood? (keep lips rounded for /w/)
- She sells seashells by the seashore. (/s/ vs /ʃ/, both voiceless)
- If two witches were watching two watches, which witch would watch which watch? (/w/)
- Unique New York. (mouth forward, jaw back, forward again)
- If Stu chews shoes, should Stu chew the shoes he chews? (/s/ /ʃ/ /tʃ/; "ch" tongue touches the roof, "sh" tongue lower)

## 237. CONFUSING ENGLISH WORDS....EVEN FOR NATIVE-SPEAKERS

- **see** – notice with your eyes – "I see a TV"
- **watch** – observe something happening over time – "watch TV"
- **look (at)** – direct your eyes toward – "Look at me"
- **borrow** – take; **lend** – give – "Can I borrow money?" / "Can you lend me money?"
- **empathize** – you've had the same experience; **sympathize** – you feel sorry for them without sharing it
- **imply** – express indirectly (tone/words); **infer** – conclude from evidence – "He's wet, so I infer it's raining"
- **who's** – who is; **whose** – possessive – "Whose coffee is that?"
- **e.g.** – for example (list of examples); **i.e.** – in other words (specify)
- **resign** (stress 2nd syllable) – quit formally; **re-sign** – sign again
- **uninterested** – not interested; **disinterested** – impartial/neutral

## 238. CONFUSING GRAMMAR IN ENGLISH | Advanced Quiz Lesson

- -ed adjectives = how you feel (frustrated, bored); -ing adjectives = person's character/thing/situation (boring, exciting). "I'm boring" = you are a boring person.
- Quick decision made on the spot: use "will" ("The phone is ringing. I'll answer it."); pre-planned: "going to".
- **farther** = physical distance; **further** = figurative distance ("further my education").
- "None of" + plural noun takes plural verb: "None of the bananas are ripe." (none/some: verb depends on the noun)
- Adjective order: opinion, size, shape, condition, age, color, pattern, origin, material, purpose – "my raggedy blue basketball jersey". **raggedy** (adj) – old and torn.
- Divided quote: "The photo," she said, "was taken by me." Passive = be + past participle.
- Present perfect continuous for action started in past, still continuing: "We have been waiting for the bus for over 20 minutes."
- Noun clause keeps statement word order (no inversion): "I wonder where they will eat dinner tonight."
- Subjunctive after suggest/recommend/demand/insist: base verb – "I suggest that everyone join."
- Prepositions: leaving **from** New York; delayed **by** 3 hours; complain **about**; wait **for**; incapable **of** (doing); laugh **about** it.

## 239. CONFUSING WORDS FOR ENGLISH LEARNERS 🤔

- **a / an** – depends on first SOUND: a university, a house; an hour, an honor, an MP3
- **abbreviation** – shortened form (Dr., St.); **acronym** – initials pronounced as one word (NASA, NATO, YOLO, AWOL); FBI/LOL (letters said) are technically abbreviations
- **AWOL** – absent without official leave
- **affect** (v) – influence; **effect** (n) – result/impact – "The storm affected our plans"; "a devastating effect"
- **emigrate** – leave a country (from); **immigrate** – enter a country to live (to)
- **every day** (two words, adverb) – each day; **everyday** (adj) – ordinary – "everyday exercises"
- **compliment** – nice words; **complement** – goes well with/enhances – "mustard complements the hot dog"
- **exacerbate** (v) – make worse; **exaggerate** (v) – stretch the truth
- **criminal** – anyone who breaks the law; **thief** – a criminal who steals
- **farther** – physical distance; **further** – figurative/greater degree ("the furthest thing from my mind")
- **lay** (lay, laid, laid) – put something somewhere (needs object); **lie** (lie, lay, lain) – recline; **lie** (lied, lied) – say untrue. Common error: "lied in bed" for recline. "You should go lie in bed."

## 240. CONFUSING WORDS IN ENGLISH (Even for native speakers!)

- **achieve** – reach a goal through hard work ("achieve fluency"); **accomplish** – complete/finish ("mission accomplished")
- **borrow** – take (borrow from); **lend** – give (lend to)
- **convince** – make someone believe something is true; **persuade** – get someone to do something through reasons
- **between** – clearly separate things; **among / amongst** – part of a group ("a traitor among us", "talk amongst yourselves"); "among other things" – as well as other things
- **figuratively** – metaphorically, not real ("stress washes away"); **literally** – actually happened (often misused for emphasis: "I literally died laughing")
- **throw someone under the bus** (idiom) – betray someone

## 241. CONFUSING WORDS IN ENGLISH 📝  | Vocabulary Quiz Lesson

- **borrow** (take) vs **lend** (give)
- **every day** (two words, = each day) vs **everyday** (adj before noun: everyday chores); "each and every day" = binomial pair
- **empathize** – you've been through the same ("never been in my shoes"); **sympathize** – feel for them
- **be in someone's shoes** (idiom) – understand/experience their situation
- **imply** – say indirectly; **infer** – conclude from evidence
- **fun** – enjoyable ("a fun time"); **funny** – amusing/makes you laugh (predicate adj)
- **burn down** – large structure (house, bridge); **burn up** – smaller items consumed (books, asteroids in atmosphere)
- **convince** – make believe; **persuade** – get to take action
- **fewer** – count nouns (fewer people); **less** – non-count nouns (less water)
- **book** – reserve and pay (hotel room); **reserve** – keep/save, maybe unpaid (restaurant table)
- **tell** someone something; **say** something to someone – "I'll tell you a secret. Don't say anything to anyone."

## 242. COOKING PHRASAL VERBS 👨‍🍳 Build Your Vocabulary

- **heat up** – make hotter, quickly (microwave) – "I'll heat it up when I get home"
- **warm up** – make warmer, slower (stove)
- **turn up** – increase heat; **turn down** – decrease heat
- **pass up** – not take advantage of – "I can't believe you're passing up the cookies"
- **eat up** – eat all of it; also command "Eat up!" (start eating)
- **pig out** – eat excessively and fast
- **chop up** – cut into small pieces – "chop up the onions"
- **thaw out** – let frozen food soften – "thaw out the chicken"
- **boil over** – water spills over the side
- **eat out** – eat at a restaurant; **eat in** – eat at home
- **whip up** – make food quickly
- **cut down on** – reduce eating – "cut down on chocolate"
- **pick at** – eat tiny amounts, not hungry – "stop picking at your food"
- **water down** – dilute; also "tastes watered down"
- **wash down** – drink after food to help swallow – "wash it down with water"
- **drink to / toast to** – wish someone/something health, success – "toast to the groom"

## 243. COPY MY AMERICAN ACCENT! 🇺🇸 (How to 100% Improve Your Speaking Fluency)

- Reductions: going to -> "gonna"; want to -> "wanna" (before a base verb).
- Unstressed function words: to -> /tə/ (schwa); for -> /fər/ ("fur"); and -> /n/ ("n").
- **Flap T** (soft "d"): T between two vowels (water, city, better, get over, put up); before/after dark L (little, bottle, battle); before/after controlled R (daughter, party, turtle). Practice: "Betty bought some butter, but she said the butter's bitter."
- **American R**: tongue pulled back, sides touch upper teeth, tip up and not touching, lips slightly rounded (great, red, car, star).
- English is stress-timed: stressed words at regular intervals, others reduced -> musical rhythm. Tip: read children's books/poetry aloud.
- Linking: final consonant joins next word's initial vowel (give us an update; take it away; got out of; and if; but I).
- Assimilation: t+y -> "ch" (don't you, why don't you); d+y -> "j" (did you, what did you).
- Intonation: statements fall; yes/no questions rise.
- Be dynamic: vary pauses, emphasis, speed; avoid monotone.
- Shadowing: speak simultaneously with native audio (TV shows, TED talks), matching intonation, speed, linking.
- **mellifluous** (adj) – pleasing/soothing to hear.

## 244. CORRECTING GRAMMAR MISTAKES 🚫 (Lesson Notes Included)

- Passive (was opened, be + past participle) vs active ("John opened the door"); active is more common in stories.
- Word order: place before manner – "I accompanied my mother to the hospital as a chaperone." Think acceptable/unacceptable, not right/wrong.
- Subject pronoun "I" vs object pronoun "me": "My friend and I are working"; "They invited my friend and me." Polite to list yourself last.
- recommend/suggest -> subjunctive: "I recommend that Susie do her homework." ("is recommended to do" sounds impersonal, used on signs.) Be concise; drop unnecessary "to".
- Subject-verb agreement: "Today is a holiday"; "Thanksgiving and Christmas are holidays."
- Past simple: "I started playing cricket when I was around five or six." Past perfect for before a point in time: "I had started playing cricket by the age of five."
- like + gerund or infinitive, both fine: "I like playing / to play soccer." (begin, can't stand, continue, hate, love, prefer, start work both ways.)
- Present perfect = present perfect continuous for duration; continuous suggests staying: "I have lived / have been living in Spain for 3 years."
- "used to": "I used to go for a jog" (noun) / "go jogging"; in questions drop the -d: "Did you use to jog?"
- "different from" preferred in writing over "different than" (but "than" is heard in speech).
- "I've never been paid so quickly (before)" – "before" optional; use past simple "I got the money" when answering about a past receipt.
- Mixed conditional: "If I were you, I would have wanted to know if he had done it." Capitalize "I".
- "Who art in heaven" – archaic English: "art" goes with singular "thou" (Romeo, where art thou?); don't over-analyze.
- **stickler** (n, informal) – person strict about rules.

## 245. CORRECT YOUR GRAMMAR MISTAKES 🚫 (Lesson Notes Included)

- **flammable** = **inflammable** (catches fire easily); **nonflammable** = does not burn. "Flammable" is now more common (Google Ngram Viewer).
- "I did a workshop **to improve** / **in order to improve** my consulting skills." Workshop **for** (audience), workshop **on** (topic).
- Contract have/has/had only when auxiliary ("I've gone", "she'd been"), not as main verb ("I have a pen"); avoid contractions in formal writing.
- "I have to be in bed **by** 9:00 p.m." – **by** = deadline; **before** = any time earlier. **must** = self-imposed; **have to** = external reason.
- **stunt** (v) – prevent from reaching full potential (collocation: stunt your growth); for slower progress use "slow" or "hinder".
- **under the weather** (idiom) – slightly sick; "a little under the weather" is more natural than "kind of". **veg out** – relax and do nothing. Avoid "kind of", "gonna" in writing.
- Put yourself last: "My son and I are a little under the weather." "It's a great day to veg out" instead of "the best day".
- Collocations: **set** short-term/long-term goals; "I set a long-term goal for the next few years, which is to ..." (use a noun after academic/formal adjectives).
- Everything is going to be **okay / all right** (adjective, not "well"); "a little / a bit / somewhat nervous" instead of "kind of"; avoid run-on sentences; keep future tense: "because I will do my best".

## 246. Can I say "I'M L❤️VING IT"? | Stative verbs in the continuous tense? (Advanced Grammar)

- Stative (non-action) verbs describe a state (love, like, see, have, own...) and normally are NOT used in continuous tense; dynamic verbs describe actions.
- Some stative verbs have a second, action meaning and CAN be continuous: "Are you seeing anyone?" (dating); "I'm having a bad day" (experiencing); "We're having friends over" (hosting).
- Spoken, informal English increasingly breaks the rule: "I'm loving this" (McDonald's slogan), "How are you liking retirement?" Rarely written; if written, usually "lovin'".
- **gray area** (n) – situation where right/wrong is unclear.

## 247. Can You Say These Words Right? 😬 Identical Spelling, Different Sounds!

Same "-ough" ending, different sounds:
- **dough** – /doʊ/ ("oh")
- **tough** – /tʌf/ ("uff")
- **plow** (also "plough") – /plaʊ/ ("ow")
- **cough** – /kɔːf/ ("off")
- **through** – /θruː/ ("oo")

## 248. Can you become fluent in 3 months? | Natural Conversation + Vocabulary

- **foundation** (n) – base that supports a building; basics you build on – "Start with a foundation, then build vocabulary and grammar."
- **plateau** (v/n) – reach a state of little or no change after growth – "My learning started to plateau."
- **well-rounded** (adj) – skilled/knowledgeable in many areas
- **dramatically** (adv) – greatly – "Your skills improve dramatically."
- **at the end of the day** – (phrase) before stating the most important fact
- **pay off** (phrasal v) – bring a benefit, be successful – "Hard work pays off."
- **no pain, no gain** – you must work hard to achieve goals
- Gist: fluency means different things to different people; study every day (read, speak, write), be consistent, "language learning is a marathon, not a sprint."

## 249. Can you correctly pronounce these 3 words? (mischievous, choir, veterinarian)

- **mischievous** /ˈmɪstʃɪvəs/ – 3 syllables, stress on first (not "mis-CHEE-vee-ous")
- **choir** /ˈkwaɪər/ – "ch" is a k-type sound (kwire)
- **veterinarian** /ˌvetərɪˈneriən/ – 6 syllables, stress on third-to-last (nair)

## 250. Can you solve these RIDDLES? 🤔

- What has to be broken before you can use it? – An egg.
- What month has 28 days? – All of them.
- Where does "today" come before "yesterday"? – In a dictionary.

## 251. Casual WORK EXPRESSIONS to Sound More Natural & Professional

- **put two and two together** – draw an obvious conclusion from known facts – "Her car's not there, so I put two and two together." ("and" reduces to "n")
- **make headway** – make progress – "I'm making some headway on the project." / "I haven't made much headway."
- **in a nutshell** – very briefly, main point only – "In a nutshell, everything went wrong." (links "in a")
- **circle back (to something)** – return to a topic later – "Let's circle back to that later." (can be a stalling tactic)
- **in light of (something)** – because of; formal – "in light of recent events/developments" (flap T, links "light of")
- **put one's best foot forward** – make a good impression / try your best (t in "best" elided)
- **jump the gun** – do something too soon – "I jumped the gun and started before approval."
- **hit the ground running** – start a new activity immediately and effectively (d in "ground" elided)
- **put something on the back burner** – postpone because not urgent – "Let's put it on the back burner."
- **the pros outweigh the cons** – more good than bad (**outweigh** v – be greater/more important than)
- **make up for lost time** – do something faster/more to compensate for delay
- **come up short** – fall short of expectations/goal – "We came up short on sales."

## 252. Collective Nouns to Describe People | Vocabulary Lesson

Collective nouns take a singular verb ("The band is playing tomorrow"); can be plural for multiple groups (bands).
- **band** – group of musicians
- **board** (of directors) – top decision-makers of a company
- **cast** (of actors/actresses) – actors in a show/movie
- **choir** – group of singers
- **class** – group of students
- **crew** – sailors/people working on a boat
- **company** (of dancers) – dance group (troupe/troop also possible for performers)
- **crowd** – many people in one area (adj: crowded)
- **faculty** – university instructors/academics
- **gang** – group of troublemakers/criminals
- **majority / minority** – larger / smaller number of people
- **mob** – chaotic, hectic crowd (negative) – "There was a mob at the mall."
- **orchestra** – classical musicians
- **panel** (of experts) – experts in discussion
- **posse** – historically a group of police/sheriffs; informally, your loyal group of friends
- **slate** (of candidates) – list of candidates
- **staff** – group of employees
- **team** – group of players
- **troop** – group of scouts

## 253. Collocations to Emphatically Build Your Vocabulary (B2 + C1 + C2)

Tip: Google Ngram Viewer shows which collocation is more common.
- **a little / a bit chilly** – (not "a little bit"; redundant)
- **hit / smash / crush the like button** – more energetic than "press"
- **cut corners** – do something the quickest/cheapest way, harming quality – "Don't cut corners."
- **a form of discrimination** – more common than "a kind of"
- **from my point of view / from my perspective / in my view / in my opinion** – don't mix "from" and "in" ("in my point of view" sounds awkward)
- **give someone the benefit of the doubt** – trust them though unsure
- **satisfy the conditions** (slightly more common) / **meet the criteria** (use meet with criteria)
- **grab (or get) a bite to eat** – "grab" implies hurry
- **in layman's terms** – in simple language anyone understands
- **take on more responsibility** – accept more duties
- **a stark difference** – very clear, obvious difference
- **file an appeal** – legal context
- **out of the loop** – not informed about something

## 254. Collocations to Quickly & Effectively Build Your Vocabulary

- **a high / large / great percentage of** (high most common)
- **make an appointment** (= schedule); **have an appointment** = already scheduled
- **respect someone's personal space / boundaries / privacy**
- **ended abruptly** (adv) – suddenly, unexpectedly (more common than "ended suddenly")
- **inside joke** – understood only by people with specific knowledge; **running joke** – repeated many times; **bad joke** – not funny; **little joke** – harmless
- **take a look at** (more common than "have a look at")
- **do / file your taxes** ("do" more casual/common; "pay" taxes = sales/import/income tax payment)
- **a considerable / tremendous / enormous / massive / substantial amount of** – vary your wording
- **boost morale** (stress: mo-RALE) – most common; also lift/raise
- **make a living / make a good living** (more common than "earn a living")
- **save your strength** – don't waste energy; also **save someone a seat**, **save yourself the trouble**, **save the best for last**
- **make a decision** (not "take a decision"; make is overwhelmingly common, also in UK)

## 255. Collocations to Supercharge Your Vocabulary (B2 + C1 + C2)

B2
- **considerable** (adj) – very large – considerable amount of / difference / progress / influence over
- **feedback** (n) – give/provide/offer/get/receive feedback (not "tell feedback"); positive/negative feedback
- **deeply** (adv) – extremely – deeply worried/concerned about, deeply flawed, deeply sorry, deeply moved by, deeply in love
- **controversial** (adj) – causing disagreement – controversial issue/decision/figure; highly/extremely controversial
- **wisdom** (n) – conventional wisdom (long-held common opinions); wisdom tooth/teeth
C1
- **call** (v) – call a meeting / an emergency meeting; call for a strike/election/halt
- **tedious** (adj) – boring – tedious job/work/task
- **bubble over with** (phrasal v) – be very excited – bubble over with excitement/enthusiasm/emotion
- **burning** (adj) – burning desire/ambition (very strong); burning issue/question (urgent)
- **capture** (v) – capture the imagination/attention/interest (of someone)
C2
- **underlying** (adj) – real but not obvious – underlying cause/condition/principle/motivation
- **framework** (n) – system of rules/ideas – legal/regulatory/theoretical framework
- **expenditure** (n) – total money spent – government/annual/personal expenditure; cut/reduce expenditure
- **literal** (adj) – original/basic meaning – literal meaning/interpretation; in a literal sense
- **finite** (adj) – having a limit – finite resources/number/amount

## 256. Collocations w/ Make, Have, Take, Give | Supercharge Your Vocab 💪

- **make a lasting impression** – be remembered long; **make a good impression**
- **have a change of heart** – change opinion/decision (about something important)
- **give rise to** – cause/result in – "His comments gave rise to rumors."
- **make amends** – do something to correct a mistake or injury
- **take a rain check** – decline now, suggest later (American idiom)
- **give someone a wide berth** – keep your distance from someone/something
- **have the upper hand** – be in an advantageous position
- **have a knack for** – natural skill or talent for
- **take a toll on** – have a bad effect over time – "stress is taking a toll on her health"
- **give someone the cold shoulder** – intentionally ignore/be unfriendly
- **make a snap decision** – decide very quickly (not "take a decision")
- **give someone the benefit of the doubt** – believe them though unsure
- **take precedence over** – be more important than
- **make a mockery of** – make something seem ridiculous/useless
- **take someone/something for granted** – fail to appreciate because it's always there
- **have a clear conscience** – feel you've done nothing wrong
- Common error: "do a mistake" -> **make a mistake**

## 257. Common Expressions Using the Word BABY 👶

- **cry like a baby** – cry/complain (often of an adult)
- **(as) helpless as a baby** – very helpless
- **sleep like a baby** – sleep very well
- **(as) soft/smooth as a baby's bottom** – very soft or smooth
- **food baby** (n) – swollen stomach after eating too much (joking)
- **throw the baby out with the bathwater** (idiom, old-fashioned) – discard something valuable by accident along with the worthless – "Let's not throw the baby out with the bathwater."
- **be one's baby** – a project/business/thing very important to someone – "This car is my baby."
- **leave someone holding the baby** (more UK) – leave someone to take the blame/responsibility

## 258. Common Expressions to Sound More American  🇺🇸

- **whatever** – (pronoun) anything you want – "Say whatever you want"; (informal exclamation) I don't care / I'm done talking
- **my bad** – informal "my mistake" for small errors, common in sports
- **lose touch (with someone)** – stop communicating; opposite **stay in touch**
- **plead the fifth** – refuse to answer to avoid incriminating yourself (US 5th Amendment); used jokingly too
- **I couldn't care less** – I don't care at all (NOT "I could care less")
- **like** (filler) – meaningless interjection among young Americans; recognize, don't use
- **give someone props** – (slang) give recognition/praise – "My boss gave me props."; **mad props**
- **John Hancock** – (US) your signature – "I need your John Hancock here."
- **get ahead of oneself** – plan prematurely or be overconfident
- **it's all good** – everything is fine, don't worry

## 259. Common Grammar Problems | Tricky Prepositions

Prepositions are function words (unstressed, easy to miss); learn verb + preposition pairs by reading and practice.
- **about**: anxious, argue, care, excited, upset (about a situation), serious, sorry, worry about
- **with**: agree, angry (with someone; also "at"), bored (with/of), busy, fed up, presented, pleased, satisfied with; upset with someone
- **for**: apologize (for something; apologize TO someone), apply (for a job), blame, famous, prepare, ready, responsible, stand for (beliefs) ("stand with/by someone" = support)
- **to**: apply (to a university), accustomed, addicted, attached, refer, respond, similar to; apologize to someone
- Addicted/accustomed to + noun or gerund (-ing).
- "Prepare for" (not "prepare my interview"); "get prepared for" also OK.

## 260. Common Idioms & Expressions with the Word OUTSIDE

- **think outside the box** – think creatively/innovatively
- **color outside the lines** – break/bend rules, act unconventionally
- **step outside / let's step outside** – challenge to a fight
- **take it outside** – command to leave and continue an argument elsewhere
- **an outside chance** – an unlikely possibility
- **on the outside looking in** – excluded from a group/situation
- **detached from the outside world** – away from society, not participating
- **it's cold outside** – literal, adds emphasis
- **outside the law** – breaking the law; operate outside the law

## 261. Common Work Expressions That You Need to Know

- **let's get down to business** – let's begin seriously
- **on the up and up** – honest, sincere, legitimate – "Is everything on the up and up?"
- **What's the game plan?** – what's the strategy/plan of action?
- **my hands are tied** – I'm not free to act because of rules/constraints
- **get your foot in the door** – succeed at the first/initial stage (e.g., get an interview)
- **go belly up** – (business) go bankrupt/fail
- **get the short end of the stick** – receive unfair/unfavorable treatment
- **in the red** – spending more than you have; losing money
- **under the table** – done secretly, e.g., paid in cash off the books
- **there's no I in team** – teamwork matters, don't be selfish

## 262. Common verbs followed by GERUNDS or INFINITIVES

- Gerund = verb + -ing used as a noun; infinitive = to + base verb.
- Verb + infinitive: **regret to inform** (you), **wait to see**, **manage to keep** (one's nose clean = stay out of trouble), **expect to miss**
- Verb + gerund: **consider taking**, **advise flossing** (but "advise me to floss" with object), **postpone training**, **admit cheating** (also "admit to cheating")
- Either, same meaning: **like, love, prefer, hate, can't stand** (stative; no continuous). Ngram: "like to watch" slightly more common; "love watching" more common.
- **begin / start / continue**: either form, same meaning. If the verb is continuous, use infinitive ("is beginning to believe", "continuing to monitor"). Infinitive can imply incomplete action ("started to learn Korean but dropped it"); "continue" more often takes gerund.
- Meaning changes:
  - **stop to do** (pause one activity to do another) vs **stop doing** (quit permanently)
  - **remember to do** (not forget a task) vs **remember doing** (recall a past event)
  - **forget to do** (didn't do it) vs **forget doing** (did it but forgot)
  - **try to do** (attempt, maybe fail) vs **try doing** (experiment to see if it works)

## 263. Confusing Grammar in English | Lesson for Advanced Learners
- -ed adjectives = feelings ("I get so frustrated"); -ing adjectives = person's character/thing/situation ("the movie is exciting").
- Quick decision at the moment of speaking -> "I'll answer it"; "going to" implies a prior plan.
- **farther** = physical distance; **further** = figurative ("further my education").
- "None of the bananas are ripe": indefinite pronoun *none* takes a verb matching the noun after "of" (plural noun -> plural verb).
- Adjective order: opinion, size, shape, condition, age, colour, pattern, origin, material, purpose + noun ("my raggedy blue basketball jersey").
- Divided quote: "The photo," she said, "was taken by me."
- Present perfect continuous for action started in the past and still going: "We have been waiting for the bus for 20 minutes."
- Noun clause keeps statement word order (no inversion): "I wonder where they will eat dinner."
- Subjunctive after suggest/recommend etc.: base verb, no -s ("I suggest that everyone join").
- Prepositions: leaving **from** New York; delayed **by** three hours; complained **about** waiting **for** so long; incapable **of** taking off; laugh **about** it.

## 264. Confusing Phrasal Verbs with Similar Meanings | Improve English Fluency
- **burn down** – structure (house, building, bridge) destroyed by fire; past events usually "burned down" ("my home burned down").
- **burn up** – smaller items consumed by fire ("photo albums burned up"); also burn calories; intense heat ("your forehead is burning up"). Both OK for a house burning right now.
- **walk up / walk down (the street)** – interchangeable (uphill -> up, downhill -> down); "walk down the street" is more common; "walk up to" someone = approach confidently ("walked up to her boss and said I quit"). Same for drive up/down.
- **take off** – aircraft/bird/insect leaves ground; far more common.
- **lift off** – leaves the ground going straight up (rocket, helicopter, hot air balloon, drone); more precise/specific.

## 265. Confusing Words You Need to Know | Improve Your Fluency
- **lose** /luːz/ (v) – misplace; fail to win – "I don't want to lose my keys." vs **loose** /luːs/ (adj) – not tight – "loose shirts".
- **maybe** (adv) – perhaps – "Maybe I'll go." vs **may be** (modal + be) – "She may be in her office."
- **stationary** (adj) – not moving – stationary bike; **stationery** (n) – paper/writing supplies.
- **breathe** /briːð/ (v) – take air in/out; **breath** /breθ/ (n) – "take a deep breath", "bad breath".
- **advice** (n, /s/) – "great advice"; **advise** (v, /z/) – "I advise students to study daily."
- **less** + non-count nouns (less sugar, less water); **fewer** + count nouns (fewer apples). Signs saying "10 items or less" are technically wrong -> "10 items or fewer".
- **story** (n) – tale/account; **storey** (UK) / **story** (US) – floor of a building ("a two-story building").
- **desert** /ˈdezərt/ (n) – dry sandy place; **desert** /dɪˈzɜːrt/ (v) – abandon; **deserted** (adj) – abandoned/empty (deserted island); **dessert** (n) – sweet course (double s).

## 266. Correct Your Grammar Mistakes | Lesson for Beginner, Intermediate, & Advanced Learners
- Use **the** with specific count nouns: "hit the like button" (not "a like button").
- Adjective order: condition before colour: "a brand new blue shirt", "an old red truck".
- Subject-verb agreement: he/she/it **doesn't** (not "he don't").
- -ed adjectives = feelings (excited, bored, tired); -ing = situations/things (exciting, boring, tiring): "I'm really excited about this lesson."
- Present perfect = have + past participle: "Have you ever **been** / **gone** to Thailand?" (not "ever went").
- First conditional: if + present simple, will + base verb: "If it rains tomorrow, we'll plan a picnic" (no "if it will rain").
- Passive: be + past participle: "My bike was **stolen**" (steal-stole-stolen).
- Gerund vs infinitive: avoid/enjoy/regret + -ing ("I try to avoid eating junk food"); hope/learn/need + to-infinitive.
- Tag questions: positive statement -> negative tag ("You're going to watch another lesson, aren't you?"); negative statement -> positive tag.
- Past perfect for earlier of two past actions: "By the time I arrived, everyone **had** already ordered."
- Subjunctive after recommend/suggest/propose/insist/demand: base verb ("The doctor recommends (that) she get some sleep").
- Mixed conditional: if + past perfect, would + base: "If I **had taken** some medicine this morning, I wouldn't feel sick now."

## 267. Correcting Celebrity Twitter Mistakes | Grammar Lesson
- it's (= it is) vs its (possessive): "says it's run by me".
- Capitalize sentence starts and proper nouns (UK, Monday); watch missing prepositions ("story **about** a massive amount").
- waste (not "wast"); countries (plural -ies, not "countrys").
- **loose cannon** (n phrase) – unpredictable, uncontrollable person; don't confuse loose/lose. Spelling: instincts.
- Complete sentences need subject + verb: "but we're here".
- **sore** (adj) – aching muscles vs **soar** (v) – fly high.
- intelligence (spelling); **underestimate** (v) – not understand/estimate (word missing "under-").
- Subject-verb agreement: "if this gets 100,000 retweets".
- their (possessive) vs there; plural subject needs plural verb: "their blood, sweat and tears **were** a waste of time"; "the opposite" (article).

## 268. DESCRIBING GRAPHS IN ENGLISH
**Going up**
- **rise** (rose) – go up steadily or a lot – "Gas prices rose significantly."
- **increase** – synonym of rise.
- **soar** – go up fast and significantly, suddenly – "Electricity use soared last month."
**Going down**
- **decrease** – general, safe term.
- **decline** – steady decrease over time.
- **drop** – general, sudden or steady – "The crime rate dropped."
- **fall** (fell) – more sudden/bigger than a slight decrease.
- **plummet** – fall far and fast, only for very big changes – "The stock plummeted."
**Up and down**
- **fluctuate** – go up and down (big or small) – "Blood pressure fluctuated wildly."
- **zigzag** – very big swings between highs and lows; rare.
**Staying the same**
- **remain constant / stay the same / level off** – no change.
- **stabilize** – becomes constant after earlier fluctuation – "The economy finally stabilized."
**Adverbs**: gradually, dramatically, steadily, significantly. Avoid repeating "rise" -- vary verbs.

## 269. DIFFERENT WAYS TO SAY "GO AWAY!"
**Polite**
- "Would you please leave?" (anyone)
- "I think it's time for you to go." (gets the hint, e.g. party)
- "Could you please give me some space?" (family/friends/partner)
- "I just need to be alone." (family/friends)
**Condescending (rude, adult to child)**
- **run along** – go away (condescending)
- **skedaddle** (caption: skidaddle) – go away quickly; less common, less offensive.
**Rude**
- **Get lost!** – common
- **Beat it!** – (Michael Jackson song)
- **Scram!** – one word
- **Take a hike!** – needs emphasis/intonation
- **Get the hell out of here!** – stronger than "Get out of here"
- **Leave me the (expletive) alone!** – profanity adds emphasis
- **Bugger off!** – British English; may not be understood in the US

## 270. DON'T SAY THIS! You Should Say This Instead. | Upgrade Your Small Talk
Principle: keep small talk going with open-ended, positive, non-intrusive questions; avoid yes/no questions, politics-first, money, and complaints.
- "What do you do?" (predictable, like an interview) -> "What's keeping you busy these days?" / "What do you do for fun when you're not working?" / "What passion projects are you working on?"
- "Can you believe this weather?" (cliche) -> "What are your plans this weekend?" / "How did you spend your weekend?" / comment on a local observation.
- "Do you like Biden/Trump?" (too direct, polarizing) -> "What's the most important local issue in the community right now?" / "What political or social issues are going on in your country?" / "What's one thing about politics that worries you about the future?"
- "Are you having a good time?" (yes/no) -> "What's been the highlight of the event so far?" / "How do you know the host?" / "What's your take on the venue/music/atmosphere?"
- "Have you traveled anywhere lately?" (can make others feel bad) -> "If you could vacation anywhere right now, where would you go?" / "If you could revisit a place...?" / "If you could live anywhere...?"
- "Why are you still single?" (intrusive) -> "What are you looking forward to next month?" / "How do you usually decompress after a busy weekend?" / "What book, podcast or show completely changed your perspective?"
- "That sounds expensive." -> "That sounds like a unique experience." / "That sounds like an incredible investment in your happiness." / "You clearly have good taste."
- "My English is not that good." (too early undermines you) -> "Could you say that again, a bit more slowly?" / "Could you explain what you mean by...?" / "I'm better at understanding than speaking. Please bear with me."
- "I don't care." (dismissive) -> "I don't have a strong feeling either way. What's your take?" / "Both options sound great. Which one are you leaning towards?" / "I'm easy. I'm happy to go with whatever you prefer."
- "I'm just tired." -> **have a lot on my plate** (very busy); **get a second wind** (new burst of energy); "I'm a little low on caffeine today, but I'm hanging in there."; "It's been one of those whirlwind weeks."
- "That's not true." (blunt) -> "I'm not sure that's always the case." / "I see where you're coming from, but..." / "My experience has been quite the opposite." / "I had a different impression of that." (soften disagreement)
- "This place is pretty crowded." (complaint) -> "This is the place to be." / "I'm amazed at the big turnout." / "It's a busy one today." (reframe complaint as observation)

## 271. Descriptive (C2) Verbs that will Supercharge Your Vocabulary!
**stagger** (v) – walk/move with difficulty as if about to fall – "He staggered out of the bar at midnight."
**cling** (v, clung, clung) – hold tightly; refuse to let go; + to/onto – "She clung onto the railing." (clingy = emotionally dependent, informal adj)
**merge** (v) – combine/join – "The two lanes merge into one." (driving, business mergers)
**shudder** (v) – shake suddenly in small movements from an unpleasant thought/feeling – "One shudders at the thought."
**spit** (v) – force saliva/contents out of the mouth; "spit it out" – "He spat the food out."
**giggle** (v) – laugh repeatedly, quietly, uncontrollably (silly/nervous) – "Why are you giggling?"
**glare** (v) – look at someone angrily for a long time – "They just glared at me."
**carve** (v) – cut hard material (wood/stone) to make an object/design/inscription; carve a turkey – "carved my name on the tree"; carve into/out of.
**intervene** (v) – step into a difficult situation to improve/stop it getting worse – "The teacher intervened."
**revolt** (v) – rise up against authority, + against – "Employees revolted against management."
**sniff** (v) – smell by taking air in through the nose (nose close to the thing) – "The dog sniffed my hair."
**blow** (v) – make a sound by forcing air out of the mouth: blow a whistle, blow out candles, blow on coffee. (blow someone's mind = amaze)

## 272. Descriptive Collocations to Give Your Vocabulary a BOOST
- **become increasingly difficult** (adverb + adj) – "It has become increasingly difficult to work for him."
- **catch / get a whiff of** – catch a faint smell – "I caught a whiff of alcohol on his breath."
- **business model** – how a company operates/makes money.
- **exert (your) influence on/over** – use your power to affect an outcome – "exert your influence on the election."
- **absolutely necessary** – "unless it is absolutely necessary."
- **scare tactic** – threat meant to frighten – "the legal threat is just a scare tactic."
- **conveniently located** – positively situated – "Our seats were conveniently located near the exit."
- **empty promise** – promise that won't be kept.
- **full / complete / speedy / quick recovery** – "expected to make a full recovery."
- **overcome obstacles** – "overcome many obstacles."
- **nervous wreck** (also complete/total wreck) – extremely anxious person.
- **keep an eye on** – watch/monitor – "keep an eye on the weather."

## 273. Descriptive Nouns to Talk about Profession, Personality, or Characteristics
**Profession**
**amateur** (n) – does an activity for fun, not a professional.
**tutor** (n) – gives extra one-on-one help in a subject.
**scholar** (n) – person who studies a subject in great detail, esp. at university.
**big fish / big wig** (n) – powerful, important person, usually in a company.
**one's right hand (right-hand man)** (n) – person who supports someone, esp. at work.
**valedictorian** (n) – top student at the end of high school; gives a graduation speech.
**Personality**
**dilettante** (n) – interested in a subject but without deep knowledge.
**aficionado** (n) – very enthusiastic expert fan – "soccer aficionado".
**confidant** (n) – trusted person you share secrets/feelings with.
**proponent** (n) – publicly supports a plan/idea; + of – "a proponent of saving the dolphins."
**guru** (n) – spiritual leader/wise teacher.
**gourmet** (n) – person knowledgeable about fine food.
**humanitarian** (n) – person who helps people who are suffering.
**Characteristics**
**coward** (n) – easily afraid; not brave.
**introvert** (n) – quiet, reserved, likes being alone.
**extrovert** (n) – very social, talkative.
**perfectionist** (n) – thinks everything must be perfect.
**purist** (n) – insists on doing things the traditional way/by the rules.
**whiz** (n) – highly intelligent/skilled in an area – "math whiz".

## 274. Different Ways to Say "I'm Busy" Build Your Vocabulary
- **I'm preoccupied (at the moment)** – polite synonym of busy.
- **My schedule is full.** – business-friendly.
- **I've got too much going on.** – vague, polite.
- **I've got a lot to do.**
- **I've got a lot on my plate.** – plate full of food = very busy.
- **busy as a bee** (simile) – always working hard.
- **I'm tied up (right now).** – can't help.
- **I'm swamped.** – too many things at once.
- **I'm overwhelmed.** – too much; invites sympathy.
- **I'm buried in work/research/comments.** – buried in + the thing.
- **I'm knee deep in (work/editing).**
- **I'm up to my ears in (work).**

## 275. Different Ways to Say "Sorry!" | Useful English Expressions
**Small mistakes**
- "Sorry (about that)." – passing apology.
- "My bad." – very informal, almost a non-apology.
- "My mistake." – small error you take responsibility for; fine at work ("My mistake, I forgot to call you").
**Serious mistakes**
- "I'm so sorry." – more genuine, medium-sized mistake.
- "I'd like to apologize." – sincere and professional.
- "I take full responsibility (for my actions/any damage)." – most formal; ok in work emails/letters.
- "It's all my fault." – serious but less formal; close relationships (spouse, family, friends).
- "I regret..." – wish you'd never done it; reflection, often romantic relationships.
- "Forgive me." / "I hope you can forgive me." / "I beg you to forgive me." – big mistakes, family/romance.

## 276. Different Ways to Say "Thank You!" | Useful English Expressions
- **grateful** (adj) – showing appreciation; + for – "grateful for your help."
- **gratitude** (n) – state of being thankful; show/express gratitude.
- **appreciate** (v) – recognize the full worth of something.
- **appreciation** (n) – recognize and enjoy good qualities; show appreciation.
- **I'm (forever) in your debt.** – e.g. someone gives you a ride home.
- **Cheers.** – esp. British; also US (server brings food).
- **I owe you one.** – e.g. someone buys you a drink.
- **Thanks a million.** – "I don't know what I would have done without you."

## 277. Different Ways to Say "Yes" & "No" | Useful English Expressions
**Yes**
- **okey-dokey** – informal "okay".
- **by all means** – of course, certainly.
- **very well** – okay, agreed.
- **absolutely / totally** – completely, yes.
- **sure** – yes; a delayed "sure..." means not a full yes.
**No**
- **out of the question** – impossible, not worth discussing.
- **no way (Jose)** – not at all.
- **under no circumstances** – never; inverts word order: "Under no circumstances **am I** drinking wine tonight / **is he** coming."
- **fat chance** – informal; certainly won't happen.
- **not likely** – certainly not.

## 278. Different Ways to Say "You're Welcome" | Useful English Expressions
- **You're quite / very welcome.** – very polite, respectful.
- **Don't mention it.** – informal, humble; friends/colleagues.
- **No worries.** – informal, short for "don't worry about it".
- **No problem (no problemo).** – informal; it wasn't a hassle.
- **Sure.** – shortest, informal; often with another expression.
- **(It was) my pleasure.** – polite, enjoyed helping.
- **Happy to help.** – formal or informal.

## 279. Difficult Words to Pronounce in English
- **daughter** /ˈdɔːtər/ – stress 1st syllable; US flap T sounds like soft D; UK true T and schwa ending.
- **obvious** /ˈɑːbviəs/ – 3 syllables, stress 1st; "-ous" = schwa.
- **serendipity** /ˌserənˈdɪpəti/ – 5 syllables, stress on "dip"; words ending in -ity stress the 3rd-to-last syllable. Meaning: happy coincidence.
- **jewelry** /ˈdʒuːəlri/ or /ˈdʒuːlri/ – 3 or 2 syllables, stress 1st; middle schwa elides.
- **literally** – 4 or 3 syllables, stress 1st; flap T; schwa can drop.
- **schedule** – US /ˈskedʒuːl/ (k sound); UK /ˈʃedjuːl/ (sh sound).
- **distribute** /dɪˈstrɪbjuːt/ – stress 2nd; **distributed** – 4 syllables, stress 2nd; **distribution** – stress shifts to "-bu-" (words ending -tion/-sion stress the second-to-last syllable).
- **choir** /ˈkwaɪər/ – ch = "kw", long i.
- **particularly** /pərˈtɪkjələrli/ – 5 syllables, stress 2nd.
- **adjective** /ˈædʒɪktɪv/ – 3 syllables, stress 1st; "d" elides lightly (objective is stressed on 2nd).
- **obfuscate** /ˈɑːbfəskeɪt/ – stress 1st; make something harder to understand intentionally.
- **quinoa** /ˈkiːnwɑː/ – "KEEN-wah", stress 1st.
- **receipt** /rɪˈsiːt/ – silent p, long e, stress 2nd.
- **bear** /ber/ (like "air") vs **beard** /bɪrd/ (long e) vs **hear** vs **heard** /hɜːrd/ – "ea" spelling varies.
- **oil** and **rural** – dark L at end of word/syllable (ball, pillow).
- **subtle** /ˈsʌtl/ – silent b, dark L, flap T, stress 1st; not loud/obvious.
- **months** /mʌnθs/ – voiceless th + s; fast speech may drop the th.
- **anonymity** /ˌænəˈnɪməti/ – 5 syllables, stress on "nim" (3rd-to-last).
- **metastasize** /məˈtæstəsaɪz/ – stress 2nd; cancer spreading in the body.
- **rendezvous** /ˈrɑːndɪvuː/ – French loan word, stress 1st.
- **lingerie** /ˌlɑːnʒəˈreɪ/ – French loan word (women's underwear/nightwear).
- **genre** /ˈʒɑːnrə/ – French loan word, 2 syllables, stress 1st.
- **sophomore** /ˈsɑːfəmɔːr/ or /ˈsɑːfmɔːr/ – 3 or 2 syllables; second-year student (freshman, sophomore, junior, senior).
- **photography** /fəˈtɑːɡrəfi/ and **photographer** /fəˈtɑːɡrəfər/ – ph = f, stress 2nd.
- **totally** – 3 syllables, stress 1st, flap T, dark L.
- **pheasant** /ˈfezənt/ – ph = f, stress 1st; a bird.
- **persuade** /pərˈsweɪd/ – stress 2nd.
- **mischievous** /ˈmɪstʃɪvəs/ – 3 syllables (not "mis-CHEE-vee-us"), stress 1st.
- **doll** /dɑːl/ vs **dull** /dʌl/ – minimal pair of short vowels.
- **conscientious** /ˌkɑːnʃiˈenʃəs/ – 4 syllables, stress on "en".

## 280. Do You Make These English Mistakes When Speaking?

- Tense slips in stories: stay in past tense for past events ("yesterday we went", "we didn't see the owner"). Fix: record yourself, or write the story and hunt present-tense verbs.
- Noun clauses use statement order, not question order: "I don't know where I should go" (not "where should I go"). Drill: "I know who you are", "I know how you can fix it".
- some- vs any-: something/someone/some in affirmative; anything/anyone/any in negatives and questions ("I didn't eat anything"). Fix: read a lot.
- make vs do collocations. do: the laundry, shopping, cooking, chores, housework, homework, research, a project, business, your best, someone a favor, harm, damage, a good job. make: a decision, plan, effort, mistake, suggestion, announcement, promise, excuse, money, a deal, an investment, friends, an impression, dinner, a mess.
- Adverb vs adjective: "she sings beautifully", "do it well" (not good).
- Reported speech shifts tense back: "he said he was coming" (not "is coming"); "she was looking for her keys".
- Prepositions: "on the team" (sports teams: on), "in the morning/afternoon/evening", "afraid of". Learn by reading.
- Irregular plurals: person -> people (never "persons"), child/children, tooth/teeth, foot/feet, woman/women, man/men.
- fewer + count nouns (fewer children/items/opportunities); less + non-count (less money/water/time). "10 items or fewer".

## 281. Do You Want to Reduce Your English Accent?

- An accent is fine; the goal is being understood. Three ways to reduce it:
- 1) Listen to the accent you want to imitate (movies, TV, music): rhythm, intonation, stress. Then imitate it.
- 2) Record yourself, compare with native speakers, find weak sounds/word stress; research common issues for speakers of your language (e.g., R/L for Korean speakers).
- 3) Become a student of pronunciation: -tion = /shun/, PH = /f/, initial WR = /r/ (W silent). Use pronunciation books/sites.

## 282. Does having an accent matter? | Natural English Conversation + Vocabulary

**slight** (adj) – small in degree – a slight accent
**spoiler alert** (expr) – warning that future info is about to be revealed; also sarcastic for stating the obvious
**impede** (v) – make something harder to happen – impede progress
**crucial** (adj) – of great importance – it is crucial that you listen
**exposure** (n) – experiencing something in your environment – exposure to English
**get someone down** (expr) – make someone feel sad/demoralized – failure gets people down
**put a spin on something** (idiom) – interpret an event favorably/unfavorably – put a positive spin on it
- Gist: an accent matters only when it impedes communication; lots of exposure naturally reduces it; keep it as part of your identity.

## 283. Don't Always Say 'I THINK' | Useful Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary

**I really / definitely / certainly think** – add an adverb to "I think" for emphasis ("I really think" is most common)
**I feel (that)** – opinion/attitude from the heart; "think" = from the brain – I feel that I let you down
**It seems that / It seems to me** – appears true to you (that can be omitted) – It seems you enjoy building vocabulary
**as far as I know / so far as I know** – what you think is true, though unsure – As far as I know, English is increasingly important
**as far as I can tell** – what you have noticed/understood – As far as I can tell, your English is great
**I have no doubt (that)** – expresses confidence – I have no doubt you can learn these
**I was under the impression (that)** – I believed it, but it turns out not true – I was under the impression you'd hit like
**It goes without saying (that)** – obviously true – It goes without saying that fast food isn't healthy
**I strongly feel / I'm of the opinion that** – stronger/formal opinion
