- Non-teaching game: guessing sentences written in emojis. No new vocabulary.

## 285. ENGLISH SPEAKERS USE THESE EXPRESSIONS ALL THE TIME | Binomial Pairs

- Binomial pair = two words joined by "and"/"or".
**pros and cons** – advantages and disadvantages – weigh the pros and cons
**sick and tired (of)** – very annoyed, at breaking point – I'm sick and tired of the rain
**give or take** – approximately (with numbers) – 100 km, give or take
**short and sweet** – brief and to the point – keep it short and sweet
**odds and ends** – small unimportant things/tasks – I have some odds and ends to do
**hustle and bustle** – lots of busy activity – the hustle and bustle of the city
**back and forth** – moving in one direction then the other; also exchange of talk – texting back and forth
**by and large** – in general/overall – By and large, it's a safe area
**more or less** – approximately/mostly (abstract things) – Did you have fun? More or less
**loud and clear** – easily and clearly understood – I hear you loud and clear

**a cappella** – singing without instruments
**nepotism** – favoritism toward relatives
**extravaganza** – spectacular, expensive entertainment/event

## 287. EPONYMOUS ADJECTIVES | Advanced Vocabulary You Probably Don't Know

- eponymous (adj) – a name is given to something else (e.g., Harry Potter title, Washington DC).
**Shakespearean** (adj) – like Shakespeare; amazing writing
**chauvinistic** (adj) – originally excessively patriotic (from fictional French soldier Nicolas Chauvin); now biased against women – a chauvinistic remark; also chauvinist (n), chauvinism, male chauvinism (not capitalized)

## 288. EXERCISE PHRASAL VERBS 💪 Build Your Vocabulary

**work out** – exercise to improve health
**warm up** – light exercise/stretches to prepare (also a class activity)
**stretch out** – stretch muscles; separable – stretch my legs out
**bend over** – move downward at the waist
**bend over backwards** (idiom) – try very hard to help
**try out** – test to see how useful/effective – try out new exercises
**work on** – spend time improving – work on my arms / my Spanish
**bulk up** – get bigger and heavier (muscles)
**lift up** – lift to a higher position (separable) – couldn't lift it up
**tire out** – become very tired
**cool down** – let body return to normal after hard exercise
**cool off** – become cooler after being hot
**pick up (the pace)** – increase speed/intensity
**burn off** – get rid of fat/calories through activity
**cut down on** – consume/do less – cut down on chocolate
**do in** – make extremely tired (past tense) – that really did me in

## 289. Easy & Important Word Stress Rules for Better Pronunciation

- Syllable = one unit of sound with one vowel sound; count with hand under chin. Stress = louder/higher pitch on one syllable (don't overdo it).
- banana: ba-NA-na; Germany: GER-ma-ny; refrigerator: 5 syllables, stress on 2nd (re-FRIG-er-a-tor). Check dictionary.com.
- 1) Most 2-syllable nouns: stress 1st – GARden, ROCket, OCean, CHICKen, OBject, BREAKfast, FRIday.
- 2) Most 2-syllable adjectives: stress 1st – EAsy, CLEver, ANXious, SPLENdid.
- 3) Most 2-syllable verbs: stress 2nd – enJOY, reLAX, sugGEST, beCOME. (Exception: OPen.)
- 4) Words that are both noun and verb: noun = 1st, verb = 2nd – PREsent/preSENT, OBject/obJECT, CONduct/conDUCT. (address as verb: adDRESS; noun can be either.)
- 5) Words ending -tion/-sion: stress the second-to-last syllable – amBItion, poSItion, deCIsion, colLIsion, pronunciAtion.
- 6) Words ending -cy, -ty, -phy, -gy, -al: stress the third-to-last syllable – deMOCracy, geOGraphy, techNOLogy, ilLOGical, phiLOSophy, soCIety, proFESsional.
- Practice sentence: "My phiLOSophy TEAcher preSENted me with this AWEsome PRESent."

## 290. English Idioms That Will Blow Your Mind 💥

**hit the ground running** – immediately work hard and successfully at something new – We need to hit the ground running
**under the radar** – unnoticed (be/fly/go/stay under the radar) – her music went under the radar
**rain on someone's parade** – spoil someone's enjoyment – the bad news rained on my parade

## 291. English Phrases They Never Taught You in School 😏

**throw shade** (informal, US) – subtly disrespect/criticize someone
**break the internet** – cause a major viral sensation online
**the new normal** – a state of things now accepted as usual – remote work is the new normal

## 292. English spelling is crazy! Let me tell you why ...

- GH at start of word: /g/ (h silent); GH at end often /f/ (some exceptions silent); in compounds (pighead, bighorn) both pronounced.
- PH = /f/ anywhere. PS at start: P silent.
- Double O: usually /uu/ or /ʊ/ (and others) – no rule, learn each word.
- -tion/-sion = /shun/; stress the syllable before (pronunciAtion, comprehENsion).
- -ology = /ol-uh-jee/ (short o + schwa). -ture = /cher/.
- RL blend (e.g., world, girl) is hard: move tongue from R (mid mouth) to L (behind top front teeth).
- Tip: use subtitles to match sounds to spellings.

## 293. English words that are actually German 🇩🇪 #interactiveenglish #shorts

**doppelganger** – someone who looks exactly like you
**kindergarten** – early childhood education ("children's garden")
**blitz** – fast, quick attack (also a football play)

**do your business** – poop (polite)
**powder my nose** – (women) go to the toilet
**big-boned** – fat (less rude; "overweight" also better than "fat")
**love handles** – excess fat around the waist
**make love** – have sex (more romantic)
**lose your lunch** – vomit
**not the sharpest tool in the shed** – not smart
**pass away** – die (polite)
**kick the bucket** – die (very informal)

## 295. Euphemisms in English that You Need to Know

**over the hill** – old
**better half** – one's spouse
**hearing impaired** – deaf (politically correct)
**hard of hearing** – having poor hearing
**downsizing** – eliminating jobs/firing people
**early retirement** – (often) being forced out of a job
**the birds and the bees** – sex education
**carnal knowledge** – sexual relationship (often legal context)
**expecting** – pregnant – we're expecting
**a bun in the oven** – pregnant (humorous, informal)
**put down** – euthanize (a pet)
**in a better place** – dead / gone to the afterlife

## 296. Everyday Words & Phrases You REALLY Must Know

**off the beaten path / track** (idiom) – not known or popular, remote (travel) – a beach off the beaten track
**through thick and thin** (idiom) – supporting through good and bad times (present perfect) – we've been through thick and thin
**get/have your ducks in a row** (idiom) – be well prepared/organized
**off the hook** (idiom) – free from blame or obligation – you're off the hook
**the home stretch** (idiom) – the last part of an activity (horse racing) – I'm in the home stretch
**buckle down** (phrasal, inseparable) – get serious and focus on work – you need to buckle down
**fall through** (phrasal) – (plans/deal/sale) not happen – our plans fell through
**mull over** (phrasal, separable) – think carefully for a long time – mull it over
**eke out** (phrasal) – obtain with great difficulty/just barely – eke out a victory
**hash out** (phrasal) – discuss to reach agreement – hash things out
**pick up the tab** (phrase, US) – pay the bill – drinks are on me, I'm picking up the tab
**at a loss for words** (phrase) – speechless
**moot point** (phrase) – irrelevant, no longer worth debating
**full of oneself** (adj phrase, informal) – annoyingly arrogant – she's so full of herself
**short and sweet** (binomial) – pleasantly brief

## 297. Exercise Vocabulary | Important Words for Working Out

**calisthenics** – body-weight exercises like gymnastics
**stretch** – loosen muscles before exercise
**jumping jacks** – jump with arms/legs spread; warm-up
**jogging** – running at a slower pace
**sprint** – very fast run over a short distance
**circuit** – series of exercises repeated in order
**pushup** – body-weight arm exercise on the floor
**sit-up** – lying-to-sitting abs exercise
**crunch** – smaller abs exercise, similar to sit-up
**squat** – bend knees with back straight, work the legs/buttocks
**plank** – hold body straight on forearms/hands; ab exercise
**lunge** – step forward and bend the knees
**burpee** – squat, push-up, jump in one move
**chin-up** – pull yourself up on a bar, palms facing you

## 298. Expressions Native Speakers Use ALL THE TIME | Binomial Pairs

**bits and pieces** – assortment of small things – told us the truth in bits and pieces
**wine and dine** – entertain someone with fine dining – wine and dine her
**safe and sound** – free from harm/danger
**sooner or later** – definitely will happen, time unknown
**ups and downs** – good and bad periods
**wear and tear** – damage from ordinary use – normal wear and tear on the car
**little by little** – gradually, step by step
**dos and don'ts** – advice on what to do and not do
**rock and roll** – music genre; also "let's rock and roll" = let's begin
**live and learn** – learn from a (bad) experience
**peace and quiet** – freedom from noise/stress

## 299. FAKE NEWS! | True Misconceptions about Learning English

- Gist: misconception = a wrong belief. Takeaway: "no excuses"; success depends on your effort, attitude, and practice, not on these myths.
- Mistakes are good: you learn and correct from them.
- No fixed finish line: learning never ends; focus on progress. Everyone has 10-15 min a day.
- Accent matters only if it blocks understanding; pronunciation also includes intonation, rhythm, word stress.
- Grammar: needs balance. Zero correction leads to error fossilization (mistakes become permanent); grammar-only teaching doesn't give speaking. Use grammar to complement other skills.
- Children don't learn effortlessly; it takes years and many mistakes. Adults can copy their fearlessness and willingness to experiment.
- Talent: "attitude not aptitude"; many people are multilingual because of environment.
- You don't need a native speaker to practice (self-talk, friends, online).
- Living in the country doesn't guarantee fluency; effort and practice do.
- Classroom: good teacher and structure help, but "languages cannot be taught, only learned"; it depends on you.
- Vocab: misconception (n) – wrong opinion/view; fossilization; "attitude not aptitude".

## 300. FAMOUS MOVIE QUOTES EXPLAINED | Improve Your English Fluency

**"We're not in Kansas anymore"** (The Wizard of Oz) – we're in a strange, unknown place
**"There's no place like home"** (The Wizard of Oz) – expressing missing home
**"Go ahead, make my day"** (Dirty Harry) – dare someone to continue so you can react
**"Say hello to my little friend"** (Scarface) – said before firing a weapon
**"I'll be back"** (Terminator) – I will return
**"You can't handle the truth"** (A Few Good Men)
**"Houston, we have a problem"** (Apollo 13) – used for any problem
**"O captain, my captain"** (Dead Poets Society; Walt Whitman poem) – respect/reverence for a leader
**"Carpe diem"** (Dead Poets Society) – Latin: seize the day
**"It's alive!"** (Frankenstein) – used when your creation works
**"I'm going to make him an offer he can't refuse"** (The Godfather)
**"Keep your friends close but your enemies closer"** (The Godfather; attributed to Sun Tzu)
**"May the force be with you"** (Star Wars)
**"You talking to me?"** (Taxi Driver) – confrontational
**"Nobody puts Baby in the corner"** (Dirty Dancing) – don't sideline/ignore someone (swap in other names)
- Tip: use quotes for shadowing practice (intonation, emphasis).

## 301. FAST SPEECH in the United States 🇺🇸 (Speak smoothly & naturally)

- Connected speech features (mainly American accent), using dialogue: "What did you do last night? Did you go to the movie?" "Yes, I got a ticket and then called in sick to work." "I'm gonna go this weekend if you want to see it again." "Let's do it, I'm up for watching it again."
- Assimilation: sounds blend into a new one: "what did you" -> "wha-dja" (d + y = /j/); also why/how/when did you.
- Elision: sounds dropped, e.g., t/d between consonants: "las(t) night".
- Flap T: t between vowels sounds like soft d: "go to the" -> "go da the", "it again", "got a" -> "gotta".
- Weak forms/reductions of function words with schwa: to -> /tə/, the -> /thə/, for -> /fər/, and -> "n".
- Linking: final consonant joins next word's initial vowel: "called in", "got a", "up for", "watching it again" ("watchin' nit again").
- Intrusion: added glide between vowels: "do it" -> "do-w-it" (like "Just do it").
- Reductions: going to -> gonna, I am going to -> "I'm gonna"/"Imma", want to -> wanna, got to -> gotta.
- Dropping the -g of -ing before vowels ("watchin' it").
- Rising intonation on yes/no questions.
- **call in sick** – phone work to say you're ill and won't come in
**be up for (something)** – be willing to do it

## 302. FIND YOUR MISTAKES 🚫 Learn How to Correct Yourself

- Use writing to find mistakes: it is reflective (you can look back), always reread, and hunt ONE mistake type per pass (spelling/capitalization, verb tense, word order, prepositions, redundancy, word choice). Track counts over ~10 pieces to see your weakest area.
- Sample paragraph had 16 errors: spelling/caps 5, tense 3, word order 2, prepositions 3, redundancy/word choice 3.
- **bury the hatchet** (idiom) – stop fighting and make up – "Let's bury the hatchet." (not "barry")
- **down to the wire** (idiom) – almost out of time/about to end
- **add insult to injury** (idiom) – make a bad situation worse (not "add injury to insult")
- **wake up on the wrong side of the bed** (idiom) – be in a bad mood all day (on, not in)
- **cologne** (n) – men's scent (women: perfume); spelling differs from pronunciation
- Rules: you're (you are) vs your; subjunctive "if your girlfriend were..." / "he wishes he were taller"; "my English has dramatically improved, thanks to..."; "hard-working" (adj); late for/to work (not "at"); "step in" poop; adjective order size before colour ("giant brown stain"); "important" spelling; add (extra) vs ad (advertisement).
- Use -ed adjectives for feelings ("I was frustrated"), -ing for things ("frustrating situation"); replace overused "so/very" with precise adverbs ("incredibly frustrated").
- Passive question rewrite sounds awkward; prefer active. "lives by the bus stand" (next to) vs "across from".

## 308. Fine-Tune Your Pronunciation - 25 Advanced Words with Silent Letters

Silent w (wr-): **wrangle** (v) – argue/struggle; **wrench** (n) – tool; **wretched** /ˈretʃɪd/ (adj) – miserable, 2 syllables
Silent k (kn-): **knuckle** (n); **knot** (n) – "tie a knot"; **knead** (v) – work dough
Silent p (p-): **pneumonia** /nuˈmoʊniə/ – stress 2nd syllable; **pseudonym** /ˈsuːdənɪm/ – stress 1st; fake name of author
Silent c (sc): **crescent** (n) – moon shape; **fluorescent** (adj) – stress 2nd; **obscene** (adj) – stress 2nd; **resuscitate** (v) – revive, stress 2nd, 4 syllables
Silent t (middle): **mortgage** /ˈmɔːrɡɪdʒ/ – home loan; **nestle** /ˈnesəl/ – snuggle; **hasten** /ˈheɪsən/ – speed up; **moisten** /ˈmɔɪsən/ – make slightly wet
Silent b (-mb): **catacomb** (n) – underground tombs, stress 1st; **crumb**; **limb** (arm/leg); **womb** /wuːm/
Silent t (end, French): **debut** /deɪˈbjuː/; **gourmet** /ɡʊrˈmeɪ/ – high-quality food; **rapport** /ræˈpɔːr/ – good relationship; **ricochet** /ˌrɪkəˈʃeɪ/ – rebound, stress 3rd
Silent z: **rendezvous** /ˈrɑːndɪvuː/ – meet at a place, stress 1st

## 309. Fluency Hack: Using Comparatives & Superlatives to Sound More Native

- Basic rule: short adjectives add -er/-est; 2+ syllables use more/most. "than" signals comparative; "the" signals superlative.
- **furthest** vs **farthest** – both correct for distance; "furthest" more common, esp. abstract ("the furthest thing from my mind"). Collocations: furthest away/apart/point/corner.
- **much more + uncountable noun** – emphasis – "much more work than yesterday"
- Two-syllable adjectives ending in -y take -ier, not "more": spicier, busier, happier, sunnier, prettier, sillier. ("more spicy" only means "additional spicy", e.g. "one more sunny day".)
- Absolute (non-gradable) adjectives can't be compared: impossible, perfect, empty, dead. Use modifiers: utterly impossible, absolutely perfect, totally empty, completely dead.
- Both forms acceptable; more common option (Ngram): clever→cleverer/more clever (cleverer written, "more clever" easier to say); gentle→**gentlest** (more common); humble→**humbler** (more common); common→**more common** (not "commoner"); subtle→subtlest ≈ most subtle (tie).
- Tip: use Google Ngram Viewer to check which form is more frequent.

## 314. GRAMMAR QUIZ | 15 Questions for Intermediate to Advanced Learners

- **a/an**: use "an" before vowel sounds – "an hour"
- **-ed vs -ing adjectives**: -ed for feelings (bored); -ing for things/situations (boring) – "I'll keep it interesting."
- **ago vs before**: "ago" = unknown time before now (a few days ago); "before" counts back from a past moment
- **at/in/on**: at + clock time (at 7:30), in + parts of day (in the morning), on + days (on Monday)
- **for vs since**: for + period of time; since + point in time (present perfect)
- **some vs any**: some in affirmatives (would like some juice); any in negatives/questions
- **there is/are** depends on the noun: "there are a lot of children" (irregular plural)
- **pull yourself together** – regain control of emotions (reflexive pronoun)
- **active vs passive**: passive = subject receives the action – "The money was stolen (by Wes)."
- **good vs well**: good = adjective; well = adverb – "speak English really well"
- **stop to V vs stop V-ing**: "stopped to buy" = paused in order to buy; "stopped exercising" = quit
- **second conditional**: if + past, would + base – "If I were you, I would ask for help."
- **forbid – forbade – forbidden** (irregular verb)
- **fewer vs less**: fewer + count nouns ("10 items or fewer"), less + non-count
- **clearer vs more clear**: one-syllable adjectives take -er; "clearer" is more common

## 315. GRASPING AT STRAWS | Advanced #Idiom

- **grasp at straws** – try desperately for any way to succeed even though it probably won't help (like grabbing a weak straw to save yourself) – "We both know you're grasping at straws."

## 316. Gerunds vs Infinitives | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Gerund = verb+ing; infinitive = to + base verb. No hard rule; memorize through reading/listening/practice (gerund often = real/completed/fixed action).
- Followed by GERUND: avoid, consider, dread (don't look forward to), enjoy, finish, feel like, quit, miss, postpone, recommend – "I dread going to the dentist." "She finished doing her homework."
- Followed by INFINITIVE: agree, decide, hope, learn, plan, promise (and similar) – "He promised to take her to dinner." "I decided to stay in tonight." "He hopes to become a teacher."
- Either one, SAME meaning: begin, continue, hate, like, love, start – "We like studying / to study English." (gerund slightly more common in spoken US English)
- Either one, DIFFERENT meaning: forget, remember, stop
  - remember doing = memory of past; remember to do = not forget a task ("She remembers buying a present" vs "She remembered to buy a present")
  - forget doing = forgot about having done it; forget to do = didn't do it ("I forgot to set the timer")
  - stop doing = quit; stop to do = pause in order to do ("I stopped smoking" vs "I stopped to smoke")

## 318. Grammar Quiz - prepositions, verb tenses, adverbs, & more

Adjective + preposition:
- **convicted of** a crime; **accustomed to** + -ing; **devoted to** + -ing; **superior to**; **safe from** danger
- **engaged in** (paying attention to an activity); **grateful for** (= thankful for); **devoid of** (lacking, none at all)
- **concerned about** (= worried about); **acquainted with** (familiar with); **addicted to**; **pleased with**
- **notorious for** (known for something bad); **fed up with** (tired of, won't take it anymore); **crowded with**; **eligible for** (meets the requirements)
- **incapable of** + -ing; **keen on** + -ing (want to do it); **amazed by**; **comfortable in** a seat

Verb tenses (rules):
- Modal **ought to** + base verb: He ought to know better.
- Scheduled future events use present simple: The plane arrives at 8 pm.
- Unspecified time -> present perfect: I have been to China twice.
- Action at a point in the past -> past continuous: Last year we were planning a trip.
- Sudden decision -> will ("I'll get the door"); evidence/sign something will happen -> going to ("Our team is going to win").
- Action continuing from past into future -> present perfect continuous: I have been learning English for four years.
- Subjunctive after recommend: The doctor recommends that she get some rest (speech often: gets).
- Two past events: By the time I arrived (simple past) everyone had already eaten (past perfect).

Articles:
- No article: languages (English), mountains (Mount Everest), lakes (Lake Superior), sports/games (play tennis), people's names, most countries, non-specific plural nouns.
- **the**: superlatives (the most northern), bridges (the Golden Gate Bridge), plural/collective country names (the Philippines, the United States), when there is only one (the toilet), second mention of something.
- **a/an**: first mention; depends on sound not letter (a university, an apple, an mp3 player, an embassy).

Conditionals:
- Zero: if + present, present (general truth) - If you commit a crime, you go to jail.
- First: if + present, will + base (real future) - If I go, I'll call you.
- Second: if + past simple, would + base (imaginary); use "were" for all persons in formal writing - If I were you, I would join.
- Third: if + past perfect, would have + past participle (unreal past) - If I had taken the bus, I would have arrived on time.
- Mixed: if + past perfect, would + base (past condition, present result) - If I had eaten earlier, I wouldn't be hungry now.
- Result clause can come first: I'll fail if I don't study.

Adverbs:
- Frequency scale: always (100%), usually/often (75%), sometimes (50%), rarely (25%), never (0%)
- **every now and then / from time to time** (= sometimes); **once in a blue moon** (very rarely; hardly ever)
- Degree: **Is it hot enough for you?**; collocations: fully aware, deeply sorry/regret, utterly ridiculous/insane
- Manner: speak English **well/fluently/clearly**; take the test **carefully**
- **hard** (with great effort) vs **hardly** (barely): "Are you working hard or hardly working?"
- Time: three days **ago** (not "three days before")

Tag questions:
- Positive statement -> negative tag; negative -> positive tag: He finished, didn't he? / He didn't finish, did he?
- Let's -> **shall we**; I am -> **aren't I**; imperatives -> **will/would you** (Give me a hand, will you?); used to -> **didn't**; this/that -> **isn't it**; these/those -> **aren't they**; main-verb have: doesn't she (US) / hasn't she (UK).

## 319. Grammar Quiz on English Articles | A - An - The - No Article

- No article: languages (English), mountains (Mount Kilimanjaro), lakes (Lake Superior), names of people, sports/games (play tennis, Monopoly), non-specific plural nouns (basketball players are tall), most countries.
- **the**: assume only one (where the toilet is), superlatives (the most northern capital: Reykjavik), bridges (the Golden Gate Bridge), plural/collective countries (the Philippines, the United States/Kingdom), specific noun (the doctor in "An apple a day keeps the doctor away").
- **a/an** depends on SOUND: a university professor (consonant /j/), an mp3 player, an embassy, an apple.
- Rule of first mention: a first time, the second time (a plant ... the plant).

## 320. Greet People Like a Native Speaker | Pronunciation Lesson

- **How are you?** -> /hou-ahr yuh/: link "How" + "are", reduce "you" to schwa /yuh/. Reply "you" is not reduced. Not a real question, just acknowledgment (esp. US).
- **How are you doing?** -> drop the g: "doo-in"; further reduced "How ya doin'" / drop "are" -> "How you doin'". Full form is more consoling (when someone is sad).
- **What's up?** -> always contracted, final -s blends into "up"; very informal (friends); super-informal "Sup?". Reply "Not much." / "Nothing." / "Hey." (not "Good"). Can also mean "What's wrong?" or "What do you need?"

## 323. HOW TO EXPRESS THAT YOU'RE ANGRY 😡

Anger scale (little to a lot):
**upset** (adj) – a little angry – upset with someone / about a situation
**annoyed** (adj) – a little angry – annoyed with someone, at/by a situation
**bothered** (adj) – a little angry – bothered by someone/something
**exasperated** (adj) – angry after something fails or continues again and again – exasperated by the situation
**irate** (adj) – beyond angry – irate about/with a situation
**enraged** (adj) – very angry – enraged by someone/something
**furious** (adj) – super angry – furious at someone/something
**pissed off** (slang) – very angry (informal, American) – "I'm so pissed off at my mom."

## 324. HOW TO GIVE A GREAT PRESENTATION 💬

Based on an American/English-speaking audience.
- Visuals: **10-20-30 rule** = max 10 slides, max 20 minutes, min 30 pt font. **6x6 rule** = max 6 words per line, max 6 bullets per slide. Few words, audience should look at you, not read the slide.
- Why use visuals: they complement the talk, keep you focused/organized, help the audience remember.
- Content: start with a **hook** (question, interesting fact, story, quote, historical event). Structure: introduction (~10-15%, includes hook), body (~75%), conclusion (~10%).
- Make it easy to follow: logical order (chronological, importance); use **signposts** (first, then, next, finally, however, in addition, in conclusion); breathe and pause, don't rush when nervous.
- Delivery: practice many times (10-15+); make eye contact; use gestures/walk around; smile naturally.
- TED = Technology, Entertainment, Design; talks are about 10-20 min.

## 325. HOW TO IMPROVE YOUR ENGLISH IN 2019

Six tips:
- Use technology: set phone/computer language to English; search a new word in quotes on Google News to see usage in context (e.g. "jubilant"); meetup.com, Facebook groups, apps, podcasts, ebooks, social media; find what you like.
- Find fun ways to learn (riddles, movies, games) to stay engaged.
- Set a schedule: consistency (even once a week), organizes learning needs, sense of accomplishment.
- Practice all four skills (reading, writing, listening, speaking); they are connected (reading shows grammar, prepositions, words in context); e.g. English book club.
- Set short-term, specific goals (1 new word a day for a month, read 5-10 min a day, watch a show with subtitles); boosts confidence and motivation.
- Subscribe to the email list for free resources.
Riddles: "30 cows and 28 chickens, how many didn't?" -> 10 (20 ate = 28 eight). "Face and two hands, no arms or legs" -> a clock.

## 326. HOW TO IMPROVE YOUR LISTENING SKILLS

Five tips:
- Be attentive and engaged: eye contact; listen for tone (happy/sad/excited) and intonation (falling = statement, rising = question).
- Picture what the person is saying: focus on key words/adjectives and visualize.
- Put the conversation in context: guess unknown words/idioms from surrounding words; listen for who/what/where/when/why/how; ask yourself "what will they say next?" to check comprehension.
- Use a variety of materials: conversation, radio, podcasts (no visual cues = harder), TV, movies, YouTube, music, online listening practice, subtitles, ebooks with transcripts.
- Ask questions (shows engagement) and ask people to repeat ("Can you please repeat that?").
Vocab: **jet lag** (tiredness after travel across time zones); **comprehension** = understanding; **FAQ** = frequently asked questions.

## 327. HOW TO PRACTICE SPEAKING ENGLISH ON YOUR OWN

Three ways to practice alone (don't worry about mistakes, just practice):
- Talk to yourself: imagine conversations (meeting someone, talking about a job, describing what you're cooking).
- Read out loud (a speaking skill): practice rhythm and cadence by following punctuation (comma = short pause, period = breath); choose books with dialogue.
- Speak to a video/recording: answer questions aloud (past simple for "Where did you go yesterday?"; will / be going to for "What will you do tomorrow?").

## 328. HOW TO SPEAK WITH A STANDARD AMERICAN ACCENT 🇺🇸

- Pronounce the final **R**: father, car, water (British drops it).
- **Flap T** (sounds like a soft D) between vowels or before dark L: water, metal (sounds like "medal"), little, bottle, rattle, get a -> "geta".
- **Linking**: final consonant joins next word's initial vowel: bottle of water, in a hole in the ground there lived a Hobbit. Practice by marking articles/prepositions (in, on, at) after consonants.
- **Reductions** (spoken, not emphasized): want to -> wanna, going to -> gonna, have to -> hafta; **for** -> /fər/ ("wait here for a moment").
- **Schwa /ə/**: unstressed vowel, any letter (a, e, i, o, u: about, pencil, address, syringe).
- Weak function words: **you** -> /yə/, **to** -> /tə/ (do you want to -> "do ya wanna").
- Practice sentence: "Do you want to get a bottle of water for your father before he goes to the airport?" (pause after "father"); use shadowing.

## 329. Halloween English Lesson | Pronunciation + Culture + Vocabulary 🎃

- Pronunciation: /w/ is voiced, lips rounded (as if blowing a kiss). Tongue twister: "If two witches were watching two watches, which witch would watch which watch?"
**All Hallows' Eve** – another name for Halloween (Oct 31)
**jack-o'-lantern** (n) – carved pumpkin with a face, placed outside
**trick-or-treat** – kids' phrase at doors for candy
**candy apple / toffee apple / caramel apple** – apple coated in candy
**pumpkin pie** – traditional dessert
Creatures: ghoul, ghost, vampire, goblin, (grim) reaper, banshee, witch, devil, zombie, mummy, werewolf, Dracula, skeleton, pixie/fairy, Frankenstein, spirit
**apparition** (n) – ghost – "an apparition appeared"
**cackle** (v/n) – loud harsh laughter (witch)
**superstition** (n) – belief without scientific evidence (black cat, number 13, broken mirror = bad luck; coin in fountain, four-leaf clover, shooting star = good luck)
**stab someone in the back** (idiom) – betray someone – "He stabbed his friend in the back."
**eerie** (adj) – strange and frightening – "an eerie feeling about this place"

Channel announcement about Patreon support (rewards: video of the month, behind-the-scenes feed, lesson notes, ebook). No teaching content.

## 331. Helpful Dating Phrasal Verbs about Love & Relationships 💖

**ask (someone) out** – invite someone for a date (drinks, movie, dinner) – "He asked her out."
**go out (with)** – be dating, in a relationship – "They've been going out for a year."
**hook up** (informal) – casual start of a relationship/sexual encounter – "They hooked up at the party."
**make out** – kiss and touch sensually
**come on to** – show someone you are attracted to them
**flirt with** – behave playfully to show romantic interest – "He flirts with everyone."
**break up with / split up with** – end a relationship – "She broke up with him."
**get over** – feel happy again after a relationship ends – "It took months to get over him."

## 332. Helpful Study Tips to Improve Your English Skills

- Practice what you learn: use each new word immediately in sentences/dialogue; don't just memorize lists.
- Spaced repetition: review a word right away, next day, ~3 days later, ~1 week, ~1 month. Don't learn too many words at once.
- Daily practice: even 10-20 minutes at the same time every day beats long irregular sessions. "If you don't use it, you lose it."
- Know your learning style (visual, auditory, kinesthetic) and your best time of day; then also challenge your weak style (e.g. turn off subtitles for 10 minutes).
- Act like a child: listen a lot first (TED Talks, YouTube), speak out loud (in the mirror), don't fear mistakes, have fun with topics you like (lower the "affective filter").
- Be a turtle: slow and steady beats fast cramming; aim for progress, not perfection.

## 333. Heteronyms to Build Your Vocabulary & Improve Speaking Fluency

Heteronym = same spelling, different pronunciation and meaning (differs from homonym/homophone).
Rules of thumb: 2-syllable words: noun stressed on 1st syllable, verb on 2nd; noun ends in /s/, verb in /z/.
- **Polish / polish** (n/adj) – the Polish language/people vs. to shine something
- **Nice / nice** – city in France vs. pleasant
- **Reading / reading** – town in the UK vs. the act of reading
- **dove** /dʌv/ (bird) vs. **dove** /doʊv/ (past of dive)
- **conflict** (n, stress 1st) vs. **conflict** (v, stress 2nd) – to clash
- **house** /haʊs/ (n) vs. **house** /haʊz/ (v, to provide housing)
- **abuse** /əˈbjuːs/ (n) vs. **abuse** /əˈbjuːz/ (v) – drug abuse; people abuse opioids
- **use** /juːs/ (n) vs. **use** /juːz/ (v) – we get a lot of use out of the boat
- **lead** /liːd/ (v, to guide) vs. **lead** /lɛd/ (n, the metal); past of lead = led
- **close** /kloʊs/ (adj, near) vs. **close** /kloʊz/ (v, to shut)
- **tear** /tɛər/ (n/v, a rip) vs. **tear** /tɪər/ (n, drop from the eye)
- **produce** (v, stress 2nd, to make) vs. **produce** (n, stress 1st, fresh fruit/vegetables)
- **separate** /ˈsɛprət/ (adj, not shared) vs. **separate** /ˈsɛpəreɪt/ (v, to divide)
- **attribute** (n, stress 1st, a characteristic) vs. **attribute** (v, stress 2nd, to regard as caused by)

## 334. Homonyms & Homophones to Build Vocabulary

Homonym = same spelling and/or pronunciation, different meaning (umbrella term). Homophone = same pronunciation, different spelling and meaning.
- **bat** – baseball bat / flying animal
- **right** – correct / direction
- **well** – in good health / water source
- **bark** – tree skin / dog's sound ("barking up the wrong tree" = pursuing the wrong approach)
- **bright** – shining / smart
- **rock** – music genre / stone
- **their / there / they're** – possessive / place / they are
- **rain / reign / rein** – water from sky / rule of a monarch / horse's strap
- **eight / ate** – number / past of eat
- **meet / meat** – to encounter / food
- **tail / tale** – dog's tail / story
- **knight / night** – armoured man with sword / dark time
- **match** (pure homonym) – a pair/equal or a stick for lighting fire
- **idle / idol** – doing nothing (or engine idling) / representation of a god ("idle hands are the devil's workshop")
- **quarts / quartz** – units of liquid / hard colorless mineral
- Idiom: **go over someone's head** – be too hard for them to understand

## 335. Hot Summer Idioms to Burn Into Your Vocabulary ☀️

- **in the heat of the moment** – saying/doing something rash when suddenly angry – "I said it in the heat of the moment."
- **be in hot water** – in trouble – "I'm in hot water with my boss; I missed the deadline."
- **take the heat** – accept blame/criticism – "I'll take the heat for it."
- **take the heat off (someone)** – reduce criticism aimed at them
- **if you can't take the heat, get out of the kitchen** – if you can't handle the pressure, step aside (attributed to Harry Truman)
- **the heat is on** – there is high pressure to achieve something – "Two weeks to the deadline, the heat is on."
- **in the hot seat** – responsible for answering hard questions / under pressure (e.g. game show contestant)
- **hot under the collar** – easily angered, esp. over small things
- **hot shot** – overly self-confident person (derogatory or playful)

## 336. How I Became FLUENT in English...

Gist: Fluency comes from immersion (movies, music, reading, speaking with everyone).
- Immersion teaches idioms, phrasal verbs, colloquial English (different from textbook English).
- English learning takes time and never ends; accepting that removes stress.
- Notice how people speak and mentally note new expressions, then use them (e.g. weak vowel "tuh" for "to").
- It's OK to make mistakes; native speakers make them too. Strive to speak, not to craft one perfect sentence.
- **fewer** (countable) vs. less (uncountable): "fewer mistakes."

## 337. How Important is Grammar to Becoming Fluent

Vocabulary:
- **subjective** (adj) – based on personal opinion/feeling, not objective
- **reinforce** (v) – strengthen an existing idea/habit/skill
- **in conjunction (with)** – together with
- **exception** (n) – something that doesn't follow the rule
- **bash** (v, informal) – criticize
- **nag** (v) – keep annoying someone about something
- **impede** (v) – delay/obstruct (impede progress)
- **takeaway** (n) – key point to remember
- **fossilization** – a repeated mistake becomes permanent and hard to correct

Gist: Grammar matters (especially early on), but studying it alone slows speaking; combine it with reading, speaking, writing; don't fear mistakes, and correct known ones before they fossilize.

## 338. How good are your listening skills? | Comprehension Quiz

Listening tips:
- Listen for key details: names, places, dates, numbers/quantities.
- Use content words (nouns, adjectives, adverbs) to build mental pictures of the story; focus on the main idea first.
Vocab/phrases:
- **on foot** – walking
- **replete (with)** – supplied with, full of
- **block** – one street length
- **ladle** – cooking utensil (vs. grater, spatula)
- Questions often reword the text (approximately -> "around how many"; died -> "passed away").

## 339. How long does it take to learn English? | Natural Conversation + Vocabulary

- **modest** (adj) – not bragging about your abilities
- **legitimate** (adj) – reasonable, acceptable – "a legitimate question"
- **take the edge off** – reduce the intensity (of stress, etc.)
- **credentials** (n) – qualifications/achievements listed on a CV
- **It's not about the destination, it's about the journey** – the experience matters more than the end goal
- **allocate** (v) – set aside (time/resources) for a purpose
- **fall into this camp** – belong to a group with a shared view/trait
- **atrophy** (v) – decline and gradually diminish through disuse
- **wrap up** (phr v) – bring to a conclusion
Gist: No fixed timeline; depends on the person and exposure. Study a bit daily, focus on progress; "if you don't use it, you lose it."

## 341. How to Describe Yourself in a Job Interview | 20 Awesome Adjectives

- **diligent** – hard-working, careful about duties
- **motivated** – determined to do something
- **driven** – determined (same as motivated)
- **ambitious** – strongly wants to succeed
- **collaborative** – working together with others
- **punctual** – on time
- **efficient** – quick and effective, organized
- **detail-oriented** – pays close attention to details
- **personable** – easy to approach and talk to
- **enthusiastic** – shows excitement and interest
- **adaptable** (flexible) – easily changes with the situation
- **innovative** – creates new methods and original ideas
- **organized** – arranged systematically
- **experienced** – has a lot of knowledge from doing it
- **methodical** – follows a procedure, thinks through each step
- **responsible** – answerable for what you control
- **reliable** – can be trusted to get the job done
- **insightful** – shows deep understanding
- **articulate** – speaks clearly and expresses yourself well

## 342. How to Dramatically Improve Your Grammar

- Get more exposure: listen (podcasts, music, TV) and read to see correct structures.
- Use the grammar actively, especially in speaking; spot target grammar in articles you read.
- Think "acceptable vs. unacceptable," not just right/wrong (e.g. "If I were" vs. informal "If I was").
- Review old grammar concepts regularly, not only new ones.
- Rules get broken in real usage (e.g. "I'm loving it" with a stative verb; "10 items or less" instead of "fewer").
- Grammar evolves over time.
- Don't let fear of mistakes stop you from speaking/writing.

## 343. How to Find & Correct Your Grammar Mistakes

- **sugarcoat** (v) – make something sound better than it really is (not "sugar coat" misspelled as "sugar code")
- **bird's-eye view** (idiom) – overview from above; "I didn't get much of a bird's-eye view."
- **fail miserably** – collocation (adverb after verb)
- **older / elder** – older = adjective ("he's older than me"); elder = noun ("he's my elder")
- **at church** (general) vs. **in the church** (inside a specific building)
Method to find mistakes:
- Write regularly (reflective; fixes carry over to speaking), then reread, hunting one mistake type per pass.
- Passes: punctuation, spelling/capitalization, subject-verb agreement and verb tense, articles, prepositions.
- Track mistake types by category to see your weakest area.
Rules/examples:
- Comma after an introductory dependent clause: "After eating a snack, I began walking."
- Past participle vs. past: began (not begun).
- No article before mountain names: Mount Everest; the top (specific point) takes "the".
- Prepositions: hike up a mountain; "not much of a view."
- this + singular (this class), these + plural (these classes... they give).
- Word order: "I captured how they were fighting on video."

## 344. How to Improve Your Reading Skills

- Reading boosts receptive vocabulary and grammar (notice patterns).
- Four ways:
  - Read as much as possible, things you like; reread.
  - Check comprehension: write the main idea of each paragraph or paraphrase in your own words.
  - Read at the right speed: guide with your finger; try speed-reading exercises.
  - Interact with the text: ask questions before reading, then look for answers.

## 345. How to Improve Your Speaking Fluency | Q&A w/ Interactive English

- **stubble** (n) – short facial hair from not shaving for a few days
- **bucket list** (n) – things you want to do before you die
- **on the fence** (idiom) – undecided
- **minimal pair** – bathroom /θ/ vs. bedroom /ð/
- **-ed endings** – baked, dressed, blessed all end in /t/ (after voiceless consonants)
- **inshallah** – "if God wills"
Tips:
- Fluency is subjective: comfortable free-flowing conversation on many topics, few awkward pauses.
- Reading builds vocabulary/grammar better than movies (you can reflect and re-read); pick material at your level and interest.
- Get as much English exposure as possible; you don't need to live in an English-speaking country.
- Shadowing (e.g. YouGlish) improves pronunciation and intonation; singing lyrics helps too.
- Practice grammar in speaking, not only in books; avoid fear of using new words; for IELTS/TOEFL, study the test format.

## 346. How to Listen for Details & Improve Your Skills

- Details = specific info: names, dates, places, colors, ages, feelings, numbers, descriptive adjectives.
- Tips: focus and visualize what you hear; don't worry about catching everything at first, aim for progress; practice without subtitles/visuals; audiobooks/podcasts help.
- Questions often rephrase the text (approximately -> "around how many"; died -> "passed away").
- **passed away** – died (polite)
- **approximately** – about

## 347. How to Listen for Inferences | Improve Your Listening Fluency

- **Inference** (n) – an educated guess/conclusion drawn from clues or evidence in what you read or hear, not stated directly.
- Pre-reading tip: infer what a text is about from its title/headline, then check yourself as you read.
- Tip: form a mental image of the scene; it helps you work out why things happen.
- Clues include cultural knowledge (e.g., "Jim and Pam" = a couple).
- Examples: soaking wet + forgot umbrella = it was raining; "no idea what it's like to be first-time parents" = he has no children; two heavy bags of food = she came from the supermarket; hears a faint "goal" but ignores calls = **selective hearing** (hears only what he wants to hear).

## 348. How to Listen to New Vocabulary & Understand the Meaning

Strategy: put an unknown word in context (the surrounding words/situation) and guess; an exact meaning is not needed to follow the main idea.
- **perilous** (adj) – full of danger or risk – "a perilous journey"
- **bibliokleptomaniac / biblioklept** (n) – someone who steals books ("klept" = stealing)
- **defecate** (v) – to poop / relieve oneself – "The baby defecated again."
- **recalcitrant** (adj) – defiant of authority, stubborn, difficult to deal with – "recalcitrant students"
- **lamprophony** (n) – loudness and clarity of voice (rare) – "compliment me for my lamprophony"
- **hullabaloo** (n) – loud noise, commotion – "the hullabaloo in the classroom next door"
- **woebegone** (adj) – looking very sad – "Why do you look so woebegone?"
- **curmudgeon** (n) – bad-tempered person – "My boss is a real curmudgeon."
- **gobbledygook** (n) – unintelligible nonsense – "a bunch of gobbledygook"
- **flummoxed** (adj) – confused, perplexed – "flummoxed by their behavior"
- **ephemeral** (adj) – lasting a very short time – "their ephemeral lives" (mayflies)
- **lollygag** (v) – waste time, fool around (informal; synonym: drag your feet) – "stop lollygagging and finish your chores"
- **repast** (n) – a meal (rare/formal) – "wash your hands before our repast"

## 349. How to Overcome Your Anxiety and Fear of Speaking

Gist: Alcohol seems to help speaking only because it lowers anxiety; the real cause is confidence, and drinking has drawbacks (effects are temporary, mistakes made while drinking can become habits, unhealthy).
- **Affective Filter Hypothesis** (Stephen Krashen) – negative emotions (anxiety, stress) raise a filter that blocks both input and output; lower the filter to learn and speak more.
- **liquid courage** (idiom) – courage from alcohol.
- 4 ways to lower speaking anxiety:
  - Don't expect perfection; mistakes are part of learning. If corrected, move on; if not understood, rephrase.
  - Turn nervousness into excitement; each time you speak is practice.
  - Don't translate every word beforehand; think of the idea and let it come out naturally and unrehearsed.
  - Identify what makes you anxious (e.g., journal about it, reflect) to reduce it.

## 350. How to Pronounce -ED Words Correctly | Speak English

Rule: the -ed sound depends on the final consonant SOUND (not spelling) of the base verb. Also applies to -ed adjectives and past participles.
- Voiceless final sound (p, f, s, sh, ch, k, x) -> /t/: **hoped, laughed, kissed, danced, fixed, washed, watched, liked**
- Voiced final sound (b, g, l, m, n, r, v, z, vowels...) -> /d/: **played, borrowed, begged, called**
- Final /t/ or /d/ -> /ɪd/ (extra syllable): **wanted, needed, weighted/waited, blended, ended**
- Check voiced vs voiceless by touching your throat: vibration = voiced (b), none = voiceless (p).
- Practice: read dialogues aloud (e.g., rained, worked, frustrated, wanted, opened, tried, waited, showed up, changed, learned, enjoyed, hoped, ended).

## 351. How to Pronounce -ed Endings | Improve Your Pronunciation

Same rules as above; mispronouncing (e.g., "walk-it" for walked) impedes fluency.
- Voiceless (p, k, f, s, sh, ch, x) -> /t/: **helped, walked, laughed, kissed, danced, watched, hoped, dropped, missed, splashed**
- Voiced -> /d/: **rubbed, called, cleaned, loved, marveled, disappeared, enjoyed, amazed, traveled, tried, dried, worried, stayed, whispered, exercised** (final sound /z/ -> /d/)
- Ends in /t/ or /d/ -> /ɪd/ (adds a syllable): **wanted, needed, waited, blended, ended, descended, accepted, visited, landed**
- Vowel-ending verbs (enjoy, try, dry, stay) take /d/ with no extra syllable.
- Self-study: listen to songs/podcasts in simple past, read simple-past stories aloud, shadow.

Native English (American) pronunciations; people just need to recognize what you say.
- **IKEA** – /aɪˈkiːə/ (eye-KEE-uh)
- **Nutella** – native: /nuːˈtɛlə/ (noo-TEL-uh); Americans often say /nʊˈtɛlə/
