- **Tommy Hilfiger** – /ˈhɪlfɪɡər/
- **Hyundai** – /ˈhʌndeɪ/ (HUN-day)
- **Nike** – /ˈnaɪki/ (NY-key), not "Nike" rhyming with bike-less
- **Adidas** – /əˈdiːdəs/ (portmanteau of Adi + Das, from Adolf Dassler)
- **Sephora** – /sɛˈfɔːrə/ (ph = f)
- **Porsche** – /ˈpɔːrʃə/ (POR-shuh; two syllables)
- **Louis Vuitton** – /ˈluːi vwiːˈtɒn/ (Americans: LOO-ee vwee-TAWN)
- **Samsung** – /ˈsæmsʌŋ/ (SAM-sung)
- **Versace** – /vɛrˈsɑːtʃi/ (ver-SAH-chee)
- **Chevrolet** – /ˌʃɛvrəˈleɪ/ (shev-ruh-LAY; t silent); short: Chevy
- **Stella Artois** – /ˈstɛlə ɑːrˈtwɑː/ (short: Stella)
- **Adobe** – /əˈdoʊbi/ (uh-DOH-bee)
- **Abercrombie & Fitch** – /ˌæbərˈkrʌmbi ən ˈfɪtʃ/ (not "Finch")
- **Volkswagen** – /ˈvoʊlksˌwɑːɡən/ (Americans: "Volks-wagon")
- **Anthropologie** – /ˌænθrəˈpɒlədʒi/ (ends like "-ology")
- **Huawei** – /ˈhwɑːweɪ/ (WAH-way)
- **Levi's** – /ˈliːvaɪz/
- **Budweiser** – /ˈbʌdwaɪzər/ (short: Bud)
- **H&M** – "H and M" (letters)
- **Jack Daniel's** – /ˈdænjəlz/ (Jack and Coke; "on the rocks" = with ice)
- **Nestlé** – /ˈnɛsəl/ (NES-ul; no T sound)
- **Corona** – /kəˈroʊnə/ (kuh-ROH-nuh)

## 353. How to Pronounce the R & L Sounds Like a Native Speaker

- /r/ (American): tongue tip curled up but not touching the roof of the mouth; tongue pulled back (smiling helps); sides of tongue touch back teeth; breathe in and feel air along the roof of the mouth.
- /l/: tongue tip pressed on the ridge behind the front teeth; air flows around the sides of the tongue (feel cool air on the sides when breathing in).
- Practice: minimal pairs (right/light), imitation of sentences, University of Iowa phonetics site, use a mirror.

## 354. How to Quickly & Effectively Build Your Vocabulary

Gist: learn phrases, not isolated words, so you remember more. Three methods:
- **Collocations** – words frequently used together. take: a rest/shower/bath/chance/note; have: fun/breakfast/lunch/dinner/a drink/a meeting; make: a mistake/noise/a wish; do: homework/business/nothing/laundry; also get some rest. Search "English collocations" online for lists.
- **Expressions** (idioms, proverbs, sayings) – learn the whole phrase:
  - **When in Rome, do as the Romans do** – follow local customs when traveling.
  - **running late** – behind schedule (not "getting late").
  - **once in a blue moon** – very rarely.
  - **Where there's smoke, there's fire** – a hidden problem likely exists if there are signs of it.
  - **think outside the box** – think creatively/differently.
  - **fall into place** – things sort themselves out.
- **Chunking** – learn groups of words as one unit and add endings: **If you have a moment...** (do you have time), **I was wondering...** (polite question opener), **I would like...**, **Would you like to grab some...** (go get food/drink).
- Tip: use Google Images searches ("English idioms") to make flashcards.

## 355. How to Read New Vocabulary & Understand the Meaning 📖

Method: find an unknown word, look for context clues (same sentence, or the sentence before/after), guess the meaning. Then keep a vocabulary list with meanings; read stories/books so the word repeats (repetition builds memory).
- **arduous** (adj) – difficult, demanding – "the arduous climb"
- **downtime** (n) – time to relax, not active – "when I have some downtime I relax"
- **flabbergasted** (adj) – very surprised – "flabbergasted: her bed was in the pool"
- **nonplussed** (adj) – surprised and confused, unsure how to react
- **cascada** (Spanish) – waterfall

## 356. How to Say Useful and Difficult Numbers in English

- Cardinal numbers (1, 2, 3) show quantity; ordinal numbers (1st, 2nd) show position.
- Dates: use ordinals. American: "March twenty-first"; British: "the twenty-first of March".
- Years:
  - 3 digits: "seven eighty-six" (1 + 2 digits, more common) or whole number "nine hundred twelve".
  - 4 digits: two + two: 1492 = "fourteen ninety-two".
  - 2000-2099: whole number: "two thousand eighteen" (2018); 1800 = "eighteen hundred"; 2100 = "twenty-one hundred"; then 2101 = "twenty-one oh one".
- Decimals: cardinal digits, "." = point; 0 = "oh" or "zero": 6.07 = "six point oh seven"; 2.0 = "two point oh".
- Fractions: cardinal numerator + ordinal denominator (plural if numerator > 1): 1/3 = "a/one third", 3/5 = "three fifths", 1/2 = "a half", 3/4 = "three fourths/three quarters", 3/2 = "three halves".
- Teens vs tens: 13 vs 30, 14 vs 40, 15 vs 50, 19 vs 90 – listen for the stress on the -teen (long ee) and the flap T in "thirty" (sounds like D).
- Big numbers: commas mark thousand / million / billion; skip zeros: 115,045 = "one hundred fifteen thousand forty-five"; 123,456,789,123 = "123 billion 456 million 789 thousand 123".

## 357. How to Speak Fast & Sound Natural – Copy My American Accent 🇺🇸

Connected-speech features (dialogue: "I've been waiting around all day for the mail. / Did you buy something off of Amazon? / I got a new phone because my old phone crapped out. / I'm going to have to get a new phone as well."):
- **Stress timing** – English stressed syllables/words come at roughly regular intervals; gives rhythm and flow ("What did you DO LAST NIGHT?").
- **Flap T** – /t/ between two vowel sounds sounds like a soft D (water, waiting, got a).
- **G-dropping** – -ing -> -in' in continuous verbs (waiting -> waitin').
- **Glottal stop** – /t/ before an /n/ sound can become a catch in the throat (mountain, kitten, button, waitin').
- **Linking** – final consonant joins the next vowel (waiting around all day; off of Amazon).
- **Weak forms/reductions** – "for the" -> /fər ðə/ (schwa); because -> cuz; going to -> gonna -> "I'mma"; have to -> hafta; got a -> gotta.
- **Assimilation/palatalization** – "did you" -> "didja".
- **Elision** – T/D dropped between consonants (old phone, crapped out).
- Rising intonation on yes/no questions.
- **off of** (informal) – from/off – "bought something off of Amazon".
- **crap out** (phrasal verb, slang) – stop working/break down – "my phone crapped out."
- Practice by shadowing.

## 358. How to Speak with Different American Accents 🇺🇸

Stereotypical regional features:
- **New York** – drops final/post-vowel R (water, park, later); "al" sounds like "aw" (talk, call); you -> "ya".
- **Boston** – drops R (car keys -> "cah keys", Star Wars); "or" sounds like "aw" (chicken or beef, divorce, force -> "foss"); upward inflection at sentence end. **packie** (package store) – liquor store.
- **Southern drawl** – slower; short vowels become diphthongs with a schwa glide (have -> "hay-uv"); pen -> "pin", get -> "git". **y'all** – you all.
- **Southern twang** – more normal speed, sharper and more nasal (Florida panhandle, Louisiana).
- **Minnesota** – pure long "o" (Minnesota, boat, soap); "ag" sounds like "eg" (bag -> "beg", flag).
- **California valley** – over-stressed content words with others reduced and run together; fillers "like" ("I was like..."), **hella** (very).

## 359. How to Speak with a Southern Accent | American English

- **Drawl** – drag out vowels: short vowel + off-glide schwa diphthong (hat, pet, sit, have -> "hay-uv"); "dog" -> "dawg"; prolong the stressed syllable (believe, mama).
- **Stress shifts to first syllable**: July (JU-ly), insurance (IN-surance), umbrella (UM-brella), police (PO-lice), guitar (GUI-tar), Halloween (HAL-loween).
- Vowel mixing: e -> i before n/g (get -> "git", pen -> "pin", ten -> "tin").
- "I"/"my" -> "ah"/"mah" (monophthong, no long i).
- Soft R after vowels: fire -> "fahr"/"fah-uh".
- Words ending -llow -> -la: yellow -> "yella", fellow -> "fella".
- Drop the g in -ing; reductions: gonna, wanna, gotta, hafta.
- **y'all** – you all (plural "you").
- **ain't** – contraction for am/is/are/has/have + not – "Halloween ain't in July."
- **fella** (n, informal) – a man/boy.
- **pitch a fit** (idiom) – throw a tantrum, complain loudly.

## 360. How to Start a Conversation | 3 Things You Need to Remember 💬

- Put the conversation on the other person: ask open-ended (not yes/no) questions; people like talking about themselves.
  - Starter: "So what do you do (for a living)?"
  - Ask for help/advice: "Do you know how to...?" / "Would you happen to know...?"
  - Share something, then ask: "I really love ___. How about you?"
- Find shared experiences: surroundings ("What brings you here?", "Who do you know?") and time of year (holidays: "Do you have any plans for Thanksgiving/the 4th of July?").
- Keep it going; avoid silence by switching topics: "What are you doing this weekend?", "What have you been up to lately?", "What have you been watching lately?", "Could you recommend...?", or current events you can discuss.
- **break the ice** (idiom) – start a conversation with someone you don't know.
- **get the ball rolling** (idiom) – start something.

## 361. How to Use Everyday Idioms | Bird's Eye View

- **a bird's eye view** (idiom) – a view of an area from a high position, like a bird. Used with be/have/get: "There is a bird's eye view from the mountaintop." / "I have a bird's eye view of Budapest." / "We hope to get a bird's eye view."

## 362. How to Use Mixed Conditionals | Advanced English Grammar

Review: zero (general truths: if + present, present), first (likely future: if + present, will), second (hypothetical/unlikely: if + past, would + base; "If I were you, I would..."), third (unreal past: if + past perfect, would have + past participle).
Mixed conditionals: focus on the situation and when each part happens (past/present/future), not on the grammar.
- Past condition -> present result: if + past perfect, would + base. "If I had gotten the job, I would live in New York." / "If he had taken care of himself, he wouldn't be sick."
- Past condition -> future result (time marker like next month/tomorrow): if + past perfect, would + be + -ing/base. "If I had gotten the job, I'd be moving to New York next month." / "If Wes hadn't squandered his money, he'd travel to Brazil for his birthday."
- Present condition -> past result: if + simple past, would have + past participle. "If I didn't have a bike, I would have taken the bus." / "If she didn't have to work, she would have given us a ride."
- Future condition -> past result: if + simple past (were for formal), would have + past participle. "If he weren't going to come to the wedding, we would have invited someone else." / "If they weren't going to bring food tonight, we would have made a reservation."
- Method: state the real situation first, then reword with "but if...": "I'm not a billionaire, but if I were, I would have stopped working."
- **squander** (v) – waste (money) foolishly.

## 363. How to Use Modal Verbs | Should - Could - Would

- Modals show likelihood, ability, permission, obligation; they add information about the main verb. Pattern: subject + modal + bare infinitive (no "to").
- Modals never change form: no -s, -ed, -ing ("he should go", not "he shoulds").
- **should** – obligation, recommendation, polite request (~75-90% "you probably ought to"): "You should exercise." Also rhetorical self-questions ("Should I do this?") and likelihood: "He should be here by now." / "The party should start at 9." / "We should get going."
- **could** – possibility/suggestion: "We could eat Italian or Thai." Polite request (softer than can): "Could you please write to us?" Past of can (past ability): "When I was younger I could run faster."
- **would** – with wh-questions: "Where would you like to go for dinner?"; polite request: "Would you please help me?"; "I would like a glass of water" (better than "I want"); wish/desire: "I would rather go on vacation than work"; hypothetical/conditionals: "If I were you, I would watch another lesson." / "If I had gotten up earlier, I would have started sooner."
- Tone matters: "You should try to study each day" = recommendation; "You could try..." = possibility.

## 364. How to Use Social Media to Learn English (Facebook, Instagram, Twitter, Snapchat)

- Gist: Social media gives daily exposure to English. Three benefits: time/flexibility (a few minutes anytime), you produce the language (comment, answer, ask) rather than passively listen, and it is learner-directed (you control when/how, so motivation is internal). It complements classes/self-study; it does not replace them. Follow teachers and learning groups.

## 365. How to Use the 1st Conditional | Grammar Lesson

- Use: real or possible situations likely to happen.
- Structure: if-clause (condition) in present simple + main clause (result) in future (usually "will"; "be going to" is fine but less common): "If you study with us, you will learn English."
- Condition first -> comma; result first -> no comma: "I'll give you ten dollars if you win the match."
- Subject + will can be contracted: "I'll".

## 366. How to Use the 2nd Conditional | Useful Grammar Lesson

- Use: hypothetical/unreal situations ("If I were you, I would watch this lesson." / "If I had a million dollars, I would...").
- Structure: if-clause in simple past + would + base verb. "Could" can replace "would" for present/future outcomes.
- Subjunctive: use "were" for all subjects with "to be" ("If I were..."), though native speakers often say "If I was". Other verbs are unaffected.
- Comma when the condition comes first; no comma when the result comes first.

## 367. How to Use the Definite Article (THE) & Zero Article (X) | Grammar Lesson

- **the** (definite): specific singular/plural nouns both speaker and listener can identify ("the car is in the shop"); goes before adjectives ("the new iPhone"); always with superlatives ("the tallest mountain in the world", "the best").
- **zero article** (X): general plural or non-count nouns ("Apples are good for your teeth", "I like to drink water"). Compare: "The boys play soccer" (specific) vs "Boys play soccer" (general).
- Use "the" with: rivers (the Nile), oceans/seas (the Pacific, the Black Sea), bridges (the Golden Gate Bridge), regions (the west coast, the Everglades), buildings/landmarks (the Empire State Building, the Eiffel Tower, the Taj Mahal), groups of lakes (the Great Lakes).
- Use no article with: people's names (George Washington), street names (Park Avenue, Main Street), languages (I'm learning Spanish), single lakes (Lake Malawi), mountains (Mount Everest), companies/cities/countries/brands (YouTube).

## 368. How to Use the Past Perfect | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Use: shows which of two past events happened FIRST (the earlier one takes the past perfect; the later one is simple past). Also for duration before a past event.
- Form: subject + had + past participle (same for all subjects): "He had studied English before he moved to London."
- Order in the sentence doesn't matter; the tense shows sequence: "She understood the movie because she had read the book."
- Duration: "I had lived in California for 15 years before I moved to Europe."
- Common adverbs: **just** (shortly before) "I had just finished when my boss called"; **yet** (expected but not done) "I hadn't finished my report yet"; **already** (earlier) "I had already eaten when Fred arrived"; **still** (not finished) "We still hadn't booked a hotel".
- With "before/after" the simple past is often enough: "Wes left before I got home." (past perfect also correct).
- Irregular participles must be memorized: go-gone, do-done, come-come, give-given, know-known.
- Practice: "I had already left for work when the mailman knocked." / "Everyone had already left the stadium when the match ended." / "I had never had Ethiopian food before I moved to LA."

## 369. How to Use the Present Perfect | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Use: past events with no specific time that connect to the present. Form: subject + have/has + past participle (main verb). Contractions: I've, she's. Regular participles = -ed; irregulars must be memorized (be-been, go-gone, see-seen, begin-begun, eat-eaten).
- Four main uses:
  1. Unfinished actions continuing to now, with **for** (duration: "We've lived in Europe for almost two years") or **since** (point in time: "I've taught English since 2006").
  2. Finished actions with a present result: "I've lost my phone." / "They must have gone shopping."
  3. Places gone to and returned from (been): "Have you been to Italy?" "She's been to Spain."
  4. Adverbs: **already** (sooner than expected; end of sentence or between have and participle: "I've already seen it"); **just** (very recent; only between have and participle: "We have just bought a kitten"); **yet** (expected; questions/negatives, end of sentence: "I haven't finished yet").
- Present perfect vs simple past:
  - "I have lived in California since 2015" (still live there) vs "I lived in California in 2015" (finished).
  - "I haven't written the email (yet)" (will do soon) vs "I didn't write the email" (no sense of intention).
  - "I've been to Thailand" (may go again) vs "I went to Thailand" (no such hint; older speakers use past simple more).
  - "What have you done today?" (accomplishments/results) vs "What did you do today?" (activities).

## 370. How to Use the Word LIKE in English

Seven parts of speech (the 54th most common English word):
- **like** (verb) – enjoy: "She likes eating/to eat pizza" (gerund or infinitive, same meaning). Polite want: "I would like the filet mignon." Perform well: "My shoes don't like the rain." Click approval online: "I like it when people like our videos." Preference (end of clause): "You can leave anytime you like." Normally stative (no -ing), but informal "I'm liking my odds / How are you liking your classes?"
- **like** (noun) – "My likes include hiking"; "The video got 1,000 likes."
- **like** (preposition) – resemble: "I look like my father"; simile: "sly like a fox" (= as sly as a fox); "feel like doing something" (want to); approximately: "It cost like $50"; such as: "shows like Peaky Blinders"; as though: "It looks like it's going to rain."
- **like** (adjective, rare) – similar: "responded in like fashion" (same way); "astronomy, physics and like sciences".
- **like** (conjunction) – as if/in the way that: "Stop acting like a spoiled brat." / "He acted like he was afraid of rain." / "The movie ended like you'd expect." / "I felt like I was falling from a plane."
- **like** (adverb) – approximately: "The airport is more like 15 minutes away."; degree, spoken: "He was acting all tough, like."
- **like** (interjection/filler) – pause or intensifier in speech: "Your comments are, like, amazing."

## 371. How to Use the Word SO | Everything You Need to Know

- **so + adjective/adverb** – to a great extent, ~"very": "I'm so happy to see you." / "The test was so easy." / "Thank you so much." (Colloquial "walking so slow"; formal "slowly".)
- **so much / so many / so little / so few** – much/little with non-count, many/few with count: "The puppy has so much energy." / "The store carries so few products."
- **so (conjunction)** – result/consequence, joins two clauses: "It's raining outside, so we will reschedule the match."
- **so (discourse marker, spoken only)** – starts a sentence to link to previous talk: "So, what are we going to do now?" / "So, are you going to like this lesson?" (not used in formal writing).
- **so that** – purpose ("that" can be dropped): "I wear glasses so (that) I can see long distances." / "He worked late so that he wouldn't have to come in on the weekend."
- **so as substitute for a clause** (after think/believe/hope): "Is that the right time?" "I think so." / "I believe so." / "I hope so." / negative "I don't think so."
- **so am I / so do I / so are they** – "me too": "I'm learning English." "So am I." / "We have a cat." "So do we."
- **so-so** – neither good nor bad (boring; use more specific words).
- **so far** – up until now, usually with present perfect: "So far, I have attended all the live lessons."
- **Is that so?** – expresses mild surprise/disbelief.

## 372. How to Use the Word OF

(4th most common English word; best learned through reading and listening.)
- Possession/origin: "a friend of mine" (looser than "my friend"), "the colors of the rainbow".
- After amounts/numbers/quantifiers/containers: "20 kilos of Nutella", "thousands of people", "most of us", "a bag of rice", "a cup of coffee", "a box of chocolates", "a liter of milk".
- Position: "the top of his head", "the back of your shirt", "the front of the line", "in front of you".
- Characteristic: "the look of a criminal", "the sound of an angel", "the personality of a tree" (insult).
- Dates: "the 12th of November", "the fourth of July", "the end of the month".
- Made of/having: "a necklace made of gold", "a woman of great beauty", "the Land of Fire".
- Adjective/verb + of: tired of, afraid of, fond of, full of, sick and tired of, accused of, scared of.
- Judging behavior: "That's so thoughtful of you." / "It's rude of you." / "It's awfully nice of you." (awfully nice = collocation)
- About/relating to: "speak of the devil" (person appears just after being mentioned), "the advantage/disadvantage of", "the issue of poverty", "the difficulty of learning English", "at the age of twenty-one".
- Action done to something: "the destruction of the rainforest", "the oppression of minorities", "the firing of employees".
- Felt/experienced by others: "earn the respect of others", "the suffering of others".
- Comparison/superlative: "the best of all", "the worst of all", "of all the teachers".
- US informal time = before: "five of five" = 4:55, "ten of two" = 1:50.
- Loss: "deprived of", "robbed of" (a job, money).
- Idioms: **a man of my word** – keeps promises; **in the nick of time** – at the last possible moment.

## 373. How to Use the Zero Conditional | English Grammar Lesson

- Use: general truths, habits, scientific facts ("If you use the bathroom, you wash your hands." / "If you heat water to 100 C, it boils.").
- Structure: present simple in BOTH the if-clause and the result.
- Comma only when the if-clause comes first; no comma when the result comes first ("Water boils if you heat it to 100 C").

## 374. How to Write Formal & Informal Messages

- Formal writing tips: when in doubt be polite (formal first; let the other person set the tone); no reductions in writing (write "going to" / "want to", not "gonna/wanna"); no emojis; avoid idioms; no text-speak ("u" for "you"); check spelling; do not write in ALL CAPS (or all lowercase); use proper capitalization; say "attached", not "clipped", for attachments; be consistent (formal greeting with formal closing).
- Formal greetings: **Dear Sir or Madam** (unknown recipient); **Dear Mr./Ms. Smith**, **Dear Professor Davis**, **Dear Dr. ...** (use name and title when known); **To Whom It May Concern:** (capitalized, colon; a bit dated); **Dear Hiring Manager**.
- Informal greetings: Hey, Hi, Hello, What's up.
- Formal ending lines: "I look forward to hearing from you." / "Thank you for your consideration."
- Formal closings: Sincerely, Best regards, Respectfully. Informal closings: Later, Ciao, Cheers, Thanks.

## 375. How to pronounce the longest word in English

**pneumonoultramicroscopicsilicovolcanoconiosis** – a lung disease; say it slowly in chunks; stress falls on the second-to-last syllable (-co-ni-O-sis).

## 376. IDENTIFY THE MAIN IDEA | Practice Your Listening Skills

- Find the main idea: (1) identify the topic (usually one word); (2) what is said about it (a statement, not just a noun); (3) listen for information repeated more than once; (4) often (not always) it is in the first sentence.
- Main idea = one sentence, no details (no numbers, minor facts). Tests (IELTS/TOEFL) paraphrase with synonyms, so match meaning, not words.
- Other true statements may appear as options; pick the most important one, not just any true one.
- Practice texts: Spain's cabinet majority women; Earth losing wildlife/biodiversity from human consumption; air pollution may reduce intelligence; Blackfly flying vehicle; foreign accent syndrome; "French Spider-Man" banned from climbing UK buildings.

## 377. IDIOMS to Express Hope & Inspiration | Vocabulary Lesson

**keep your chin up** (idiom) – stay positive in a hard time – "Keep your chin up, things will get better."
**hang in there** (idiom) – keep trying, don't give up – "Just hang in there a little longer."
**break a leg** (idiom) – good luck (before a performance/competition) – "Good luck, break a leg."
**in the bag** (idiom) – success is certain – "Don't worry, it's in the bag."
**the sky's the limit** (idiom) – no limit to what you can achieve – "Keep learning; the sky's the limit."
**the world is your oyster** (idiom) – you have many opportunities (often said to young people) – "You have so many options; the world is your oyster."
**light at the end of the tunnel** (idiom) – a difficult situation will improve/end – "I finally see the light at the end of the tunnel."
**there's plenty of fish in the sea** (idiom) – said after a breakup: you'll find another partner – "You'll get over her; plenty of fish in the sea." (fish is singular/plural)
**look on the bright side** (idiom) – stay cheerful about a bad situation (then give the reason) – "Lost your job? Look on the bright side, you can move now."
**pull yourself together** (idiom) – regain control of your emotions, act normal – "I need to pull myself together."
**fingers crossed** (idiom) – hoping for a good outcome – "I applied for the job; fingers crossed."
**blessing in disguise** (idiom) – something bad that turns out good – "Getting fired was a blessing in disguise."

## 378. IELTS Listening Practice for Advanced Learners

- IELTS = English proficiency exam used by universities/organizations; sections: listening, reading, writing, speaking.
- Tips: study the test format; understand the directions (audio plays only ONCE); practice a lot to reduce anxiety.
- Answers usually follow the order of the questions; wording in the audio is paraphrased from the question (e.g., "developed" = "built"; "from tiny beginnings to huge enterprise" = "remember the history").
- Gap-fill answers need exact info from audio (place names, numbers); compound adjectives before nouns are hyphenated (first-aid centers).
- Vocab: **roller coaster** – amusement-park ride on a track; **petting zoo**; **fumble** – to speak unclearly/make a slip.

## 379. IMPROVE YOUR LISTENING | Check out these summer songs... 🎵

Gist: listening to songs with a known theme (summer) helps comprehension because you listen for key details. Songs: Summer of '69 (Bryan Adams, rock), School's Out (Alice Cooper), Summer Nights (Grease), Summertime (DJ Jazzy Jeff & Fresh Prince, rap), Summer Breeze (Seals & Crofts), Long Hot Summer (Keith Urban, country).
**fling** (n) – a short romance, usually in summer – "a summer fling"
**breeze** (n) – a light wind – "a summer breeze"
**reminisce** (v) – remember past times fondly – "reminiscing about summer"

## 380. IN / ON / AT - How do I use prepositions of place?

- **IN** = enclosed space / within a larger area: in this town, in the car/taxi/van, work in HR/fashion, in the pool/water, in class, in a book/dictionary (printed materials), in the city center, in your pockets.
- **ON** = surface / touching: on the table, on the sidewalk, on the 7th floor, on the road/river/coast ("on the east coast"), on a bus/plane/bike/motorcycle (larger transport or ride-on), on the left/right, on Netflix/YouTube, on a page/slide.
- **AT** = a specific point: at the front door, at 44 Main Street (but "on Main Street"), at the mall/shops, at a party, at school/university, at work/home, at the beginning/end of, at the front of the plane, at the lake (location).
- Meaning shifts: in the lake (in the water), on the lake (on a boat), at the lake (location).
- A mistake on the page; a word in the book.

## 381. IT DOESN'T DO IT JUSTICE. (Advanced C2 Phrase) #interactiveenglish #shorts

**do (something/someone) justice** (phrase) – represent it accurately/fairly; usually negative: "doesn't do it justice" – "Pictures don't do the Grand Canyon justice." / "The movie doesn't do the book justice."

## 382. Idioms for Casual Conversation (improve your speaking fluency)

**look on the bright side** (idiom) – try to be cheerful about a bad situation (follow with the reason) – "Look on the bright side, it's not so bad."
**pick up the tab/check/bill** (phrase) – pay for a meal or drinks (tab = drinks at a bar) – "I'll pick up the tab."
**in the home stretch** (idiom) – in the last part of something (from horse racing) – "The project is almost done; we're in the home stretch."
**be at a loss for words** (idiom) – too shocked/surprised to speak – "I'm at a loss for words."
**in the doghouse** (idiom) – in trouble because someone (often your partner) is angry at you – "I forgot her birthday; I'm in the doghouse."
**make ends meet** (idiom) – have just enough money to live – "struggling/barely making ends meet"
**hang on someone's every word** (idiom) – listen very carefully with admiration/respect – "I hung on his every word during the lecture."
**play it by ear** (idiom) – decide as things happen, no advance plan – "Let's just play it by ear."
**off the beaten path / track** (idiom) – not well known or visited (positive, travel) – "This beach is off the beaten path."
**bend over backwards (to do something)** (idiom) – try extremely hard to help/please – "I've been bending over backwards to help you."

## 383. Idioms for Everyday Conversation (develop your speaking fluency)

**below the belt** (idiom) – unfair or insulting remark – "That comment was below the belt."
**elbow grease** (n) – hard physical effort, esp. cleaning – "Give it a little elbow grease."
**bite the dust** (idiom) – fall; come to an end; be killed – "Another one bites the dust." (cold for people)
**put a pin in it** (idiom) – pause a topic and return to it later – "Let's put a pin in it." (similar: table something)
**be off the hook** (idiom) – free from blame/an annoying obligation – "You don't have to come; you're off the hook."
**toe/tow the line** (idiom) – do what's expected without causing trouble – "I'm just toeing the line."
**stick your neck out** (idiom) – bravely say/do something that may be criticized or wrong – "He stuck his neck out for me."
**be all ears** (idiom) – eagerly waiting to hear – "Tell me, I'm all ears."
**get/have your ducks in a row** (idiom) – be well organized and prepared – "Get your ducks in a row before the audit."
**move the goalposts** (idiom) – change rules/conditions to make it harder – "My boss keeps moving the goalposts."
**throw caution to the wind** (idiom) – act without worrying about risk – "Don't throw caution to the wind."
**have skin in the game** (idiom) – be directly involved/affected, esp. financially – "Investors have skin in the game."
(Also: fish out of water – uncomfortable in a new environment.)

## 384. If I Had Known This… I Would Sound Fluent in English!

- Zero: if + present simple, present simple (general truth): If you touch fire, you get burned.
- First: if + present simple, will + verb (likely future): If we work hard, we will finish on time.
- Second: if + past simple, would + verb (unlikely/hypothetical): If I won the lottery, I would buy a car.
- Third: if + past perfect, would have + past participle (unreal past): If I had studied, I would have passed.
- Mixed 1 (past condition, present result): if + past perfect, would + verb: If you had checked the map, we wouldn't be lost now.
- Mixed 1B (past condition, future result): if + past perfect, would + be + -ing: If I had taken the job, I'd be moving to New York next month. (continuous only with action verbs, not stative)
- Mixed 2 (hypothetical present condition, past result): if + past simple, would have + p.p.: If I were afraid of snakes, I wouldn't have gone camping.
- Mixed 2B (future condition, past result): if + past continuous, would have + p.p.: If my parents weren't coming this weekend, I would have planned something.
- Result clause can come first (no comma). Slacker – lazy person.

## 385. If You ACE This Quiz, Your English is Excellent! 💯

Gist: keyword transformations (rewrite a sentence with a given word, same meaning) test vocab + grammar.
- most beautiful sunset I've ever seen (superlative + present perfect)
- **make sense of** something – "We couldn't make sense of what he was saying."
- **wish + past perfect** – "She wishes she had spoken up during the meeting." (**speak up** – say your opinion out loud)
- **turn out (to be)** – end result – "The results turned out (to be) completely different."
- **blame someone for something** – "The manager blamed her assistant for the mistake."
- **too young = not old enough** – "He isn't old enough to understand."
- **prevent someone from + -ing** – "Breaking his leg prevented him from taking part in the race."
- **have no intention of + -ing** – "She had no intention of offending you."
- **be surprised at how much** – "I was surprised at how much I enjoyed the concert."
- **due to** – "The meeting was postponed due to the director's absence."
- **Inversion: Had you not told me, I wouldn't have found out.** (formal)
- **be credited with + -ing** – "The new CEO is credited with transforming the business."

## 386. If You Want to Succeed at Work, Learn These English Idioms

**connect the dots** (idiom) – piece together info to see a pattern/reach a conclusion (= put two and two together) – "I saw her packing and started to connect the dots." (start/try/fail to connect the dots)
**give the green light** (idiom) – formal permission to proceed (also verb "green-light") – "The board gave the green light for the merger."
**pick someone's brain** (idiom) – ask an expert/experienced person for advice – "Can I pick your brain about your career?"
**stay on top of** (idiom) – stay informed and in control of something/someone (too much = micromanaging) – "I need to stay on top of the contractors."
**bring something to the table** (idiom) – offer useful skills/resources – "She brings 10 years of experience to the table."
**weigh the pros and cons** (idiom/binomial) – consider advantages and disadvantages – "I weighed the pros and cons before quitting."
**the bottom line** (idiom) – the key point; also final total/profit – "Just give me the bottom line."
**call the shots** (idiom) – make the decisions, be in charge – "Who is calling the shots?"
**see/look at the big picture** (idiom) – the overall perspective, not details (lose sight of the big picture) – "A good leader sees the big picture."
**(back) at square one** (idiom) – back at the beginning after a failure (= go back to the drawing board) – "The contract was rejected; we're at square one."
**read between the lines** (idiom) – understand an unstated meaning – "Read between the lines of the press release."
**move the needle** (idiom) – make a noticeable difference (business speak) – "The ads didn't move the needle on sales."
(Also: hold down the fort – be in charge while someone is away.)

## 387. Important Christmas Vocabulary | English Cultural Lesson

**the holidays** (n) – the Christmas/New Year season (not a vacation); "Happy holidays" – "Are you going home for the holidays?"
**white Christmas** (n) – Christmas with snow on the ground
**wreath** (n) – circular arrangement of leaves/flowers hung on a door
**holiday spirit** (n) – the warm, cheerful feeling of the season – "Gift shopping puts me in the holiday spirit."
**holly / mistletoe** (n) – two different Christmas plants; kiss under the mistletoe – "Meet me under the mistletoe."
**Santa Claus** (n) – from St. Nicholas; also Saint Nick, Father Christmas
**eggnog** (n) – drink of milk, cream, sugar, eggs, often with rum/brandy; (mulled wine – warm spiced wine)
**stocking** (n) – big sock hung by the fireplace and filled with small gifts; **lump of coal** – gift for bad behavior
**stocking stuffer** (n) – small gift – "Small toys make great stocking stuffers."
**ornament** (n) – decoration hung on the tree
**with bells on** (idiom) – very enthusiastically (any time of year) – "I'll be there with bells on."
**bells and whistles** (idiom) – fancy extra features – "a car with all the bells and whistles"
**merry** (adj) – cheerful (mostly Christmas context); **the more the merrier** – more people, more fun
**Scrooge** (n) – a stingy person who hates spending money – "Don't be a Scrooge." (opposite: generous)
**the holiday blues** (n) – sadness around the holidays; **beat the holiday blues** – find ways not to be sad
**Good things come in small packages** (proverb) – something small can be good (quality over quantity)

## 388. Important Phrasal Verbs for When You Get Sick 😷

**come down with** – start to become ill (not serious) – "I think I'm coming down with a cold."
**burn up** – be very hot, have a fever – "You're burning up."
**throw up** – vomit – "I threw up last night."
**cough up** – cough something out of the lungs (mucus, blood) – "I'm coughing up blood."
**puff up** – swell – "My eyes puff up with allergies."
**flare up** – a condition returns/gets worse – "My skin condition is flaring up."
**break out** – (rash/spots) appear suddenly – "I started breaking out."
**fight off** – resist an illness – "I'm trying to fight off a cold."
**get over** – recover from an illness – "I finally got over the flu."
**pass out** – lose consciousness
**come to / come around / come round** – regain consciousness
**put (someone) on** – prescribe/start medication or treatment – "The doctor put me on antibiotics."

## 389. Important Phrasal Verbs for Work and School

- **hand in / turn in** – give something to an authority (teacher, boss) – "Turn in your report."
- **hand out** – give something to each person in a group – "She handed out the homework."
- **look through** – skim a document quickly to find information/mistakes – "Look through this and tell me if anything's wrong."
- **look over** – examine/review quickly (also your own work) – "Can you look over this contract?"
- **lay off** (someone) – end someone's employment because there's not enough work – "They laid off 100 people."
- **take on** – accept more work/responsibility – "I can take on more responsibility."
- **knock off / clock out** – stop working (informal; hourly jobs) – "I knock off at five."
- **burn out** – become exhausted from overwork/study – "I'm burned out."
- **work on** – spend time creating/improving something – "I'm working on an essay."
- **work out** – (1) solve a problem; (2) end well/succeed ("it all worked out"); (3) agree on details ("work out the details/our differences"); (4) add up to an amount ("it works out more expensive")
- **work through** – deal with a problem by thinking/talking it over; or keep working continuously – "work through the weekend/lunch."
- **call off** – cancel – "The meeting/merger was called off."
- **slack off** – not work hard – "Students who slack off."
- **fill in for** – take someone's place while they're away – "I'm filling in for Kirsten."
- **run (something) by (someone)** – tell someone your idea/work to get their opinion – "Run it by a colleague first."
- **branch out** – do something new/different from what you did before – "Companies branch out into new products."
- **sort out** – (1) solve a problem/disagreement; (2) organize – "sort out my desk."

## 390. Important Travel Idioms So You Don't Miss the Boat

- **bad news travels fast** – bad news spreads quickly – "Pam failed art school and everyone knew next day."
- **a mile a minute** – very fast (talking/thinking) – "My mind was going a mile a minute."
- **off the beaten track** – little-known, remote place – "A tiny restaurant off the beaten track."
- **hit the road** – set out on a journey – "We're packed, let's hit the road."
- **jump on the bandwagon** – join something that has become popular – "Many jumped on the keto bandwagon."
- **in the same boat** – in the same (usually difficult) situation – "I only slept two hours." "I'm in the same boat."
- **travel light** – travel with little luggage – "I like to travel light."
- **miss the boat** – miss an opportunity (may not come again) – "He missed the boat by applying late."
- **train of thought** – sequence of thoughts – "I lost my train of thought when the doorbell rang."
- **right up my alley** – exactly what I like/am passionate about – "A cat cafe is right up my alley."

## 391. Impressive B2 Vocab That Makes You Stand Out

- **simultaneously** (adv) – at exactly the same time (more formal than "at the same time")
- **preferably** (adv) – ideally; one choice is more desired – "Preferably by Friday / in silence."
- **eagerly** (adv) – with excitement/enthusiasm – collocations: eagerly await / anticipate / accept
- **willingly** (adv) – because you want to, not forced – "He willingly accepts."
- **patiently** (adv) – waiting a long time without getting angry/upset – "wait patiently"

## 392. Improve Your Listening Skills to Effectively Learn English | Live-Lesson

- **chide** (v) – scold/reprimand angrily – "The teacher chided me for texting in class."
- **cast** (n) – hard mould around a broken arm/hand
- **southpaw** (n, informal) – left-handed person (originally boxer/baseball pitcher)
- **jokingly** (adv) – in a teasing, not serious way
- **the Big Apple** – nickname for New York City
- **bachelor party** – party for the groom with close friends before the wedding
- **bequeath** (v) – give property/money to someone after you die (formal, old) – "independence bequeathed by your fathers"
- **fetters** (n) – chains/shackles
- **heinous** (adj) – terrible, despicable
- **sacrilegious** (adj) – deeply disrespectful
- **irony** – situation with the opposite of the expected effect
- Skills: guess meaning of unknown words from context; identify main idea and key details; make inferences (wet umbrella -> it's raining).
- Practice text: Frederick Douglass's speech "What to the Slave Is the Fourth of July?"

## 393. Improve Your Listening to Easily Understand Fast Speakers

- Variety: mix TV/movies (natural speech), educational videos (clear speech), conversation (active listening), podcasts, audiobooks, music (rhythm/flow), radio (flexible, interviews, sports).
- Speed: "slow and steady wins the race." Listen to material at or slightly above your level; you should understand at least 80%. Use YouTube/media-player speed controls to slow down or speed up.
- Your listening level can be lower than your other skills; choose material by listening level.
- Exposure: quality beats multitasking; give full focus to what you listen to.
- Beginners: control speed. Busy intermediate: short video lessons, radio. Advanced: faster speed, podcasts on topics you like, music with full focus.

## 394. Improve Your Speaking Fluency | 11 Ways to Get More Practice

- Speak English with a partner/significant other.
- Take online classes, ideally daily (one-on-one with a native speaker).
- Join a speaking club/community (e.g., Toastmasters) or start your own (Facebook group, flyers).
- Role-play situations (airport, kitchen) with others or alone; it reveals missing words/phrases.
- Language exchange: search "English language exchange" for a partner.
- Talk to yourself like giving a presentation on a serious topic (climate change, social media); aim for several minutes.
- Record yourself; find mistakes, awkward pauses, check fluency.
- Shadowing: listen to a speaker (movie, TV, speech), repeat exactly, copying pronunciation, intonation, emphasis.
- Sing along to songs.
- Talk to your phone assistant (Siri); practice questions and check it understands you.
- Answer questions aloud (e.g., "Where do you plan to go on your next vacation?").

## 395. Improve your Speaking and Conversation Skills with me

- Common spoken reductions: going to -> **gonna**; want to -> **wanna**; because -> **cuz**; got to -> **gotta**; give me -> **gimme**; let me -> **lemme**.
- Not required for speaking, but helps listening comprehension and sounds more natural.
- Practice: "I'm gonna get some rest." / "Do you wanna eat out tonight?" / "I gotta go to the bathroom." / "Could you please give me a hand?" / "Let me think about it."

## 396. Innuendos in English | Examples to Improve Your Comprehension

- **innuendo** (n) – indirect remark/gesture suggesting something sexual or unpleasant without saying it directly; to insinuate/imply.
- **derogatory** (adj) – insulting, not nice.
- **rhetorical question** – a question not meant to be answered.
- Clues: "you know what I mean?" / "if you know what I mean"; air quotes; emphasis.
- Examples: "I found a way to get 'extra help' on tomorrow's exam" (cheating); "Dave has been spending a lot of time with Kim, if you know what I mean" (romance); a newspaper ad about "pleasing 15 women" (sexual double meaning; paper apologized); kids' movie jokes (Aladdin "earth moving until the honeymoon", Toy Story wings popping up, Cars flashing headlights, Frozen "foot size doesn't matter").
- **to flash someone** (informal) – briefly expose one's body.
- Mostly for comprehension (TV, movies, sitcoms), informal; no need to use them.

## 397. Intermediate Listening & Speaking Activity | Describe Words Around the Home

- Activity: guess nouns from descriptions, then describe words yourself.
- **desk** – furniture you sit at to work
- **garage** – where you park your car and store tools
- **living room** – room where families relax, watch TV, play games
- **pillow** – soft item you rest your head on
- **stairs** – go up/down between floors
- **sink** – where you wash dishes/hands (running water)
- Also: window (glass you look through), microwave, shower.

## 398. Intermediate to Advanced Grammar (B1 - C2) + Lesson Notes

- **for** = duration (for 10 years); **since** = point in time; both signal present perfect ("I've been studying English for 10 years").
- remember/forget/stop change meaning: **remember to + base verb** = not forgetting to do it ("Did you remember to lock the door?"); **remember + -ing** = recalling a past action.
- Tag questions: positive statement -> negative tag; negative statement -> positive tag ("You don't want me to quit, do you?").
- Relative clauses: **who** = people; **which** = animals/things; **that** = people/animals/things. Restrictive clause (essential, no commas) uses that/who; non-restrictive (extra info, commas) uses which/who. "There is a raccoon that always goes through our garbage."
- Interjections express emotion, often with "!" – wow, oops, oh my God.
- Prepositions: **in** = enclosed/surrounded (in the lake/pool); **on** = surface/touching; **at** = point/location ("We're going swimming at the lake").
- Present perfect links past events to now: "I'm confident I'll win even though she has beaten me twice."
- Correlative conjunctions come in pairs: **not only ... but also** ("Not only do you hit like, but you also write comments" - inversion after "not only").
- Sentence transformation: active -> passive = object first + be + past participle + by ("The house was built by my grandfather").
- "too small" = "**not big enough**."
- Third conditional: if + past perfect, would have + past participle ("If you had studied, you would have passed").
- **hardly any** = almost none ("There is hardly any difference between the two applicants").

## 399. Key Word Transformation Quiz | How to change sentences?

- Sentence/key word transformation: rewrite a sentence with the same meaning (often using a given word); tests grammar + vocabulary (TOEFL/IELTS style).
- Adjective -> noun: "He was responsible for painting" = "Painting the fence was his **responsibility**."
- "There's nothing better than X" = "X is **the best**."
- Direct vs indirect speech: "I asked Wes what he ate" -> "I asked Wes, 'What did you eat for breakfast?'" (change pronouns/tense).
- "You can miss class if you have a good reason" = "You can't miss class **unless** you have an excuse."
- Regret -> wish: "She regrets not taking the job" = "She wishes she **had taken** the job."
- Third conditional: "I didn't stretch so I pulled a muscle" = "If I **had stretched**, I **wouldn't have pulled** a muscle."
- **look forward to + -ing**: "He can't wait to go" = "He **looks forward to going** on vacation."
- almost always -> **hardly ever**: "Wes almost always does live lessons" = "Wes **hardly ever misses** a live lesson."
- "When did you start studying English?" = "**How long have you been** studying English?"
- Active -> passive: "My father took this picture" = "This picture **was taken by** my father."
- **by the time**: "I arrived late; everyone had finished eating" = "By the time I arrived, everyone had finished eating."

## 400. LEARN -ED & -ING ADJECTIVE ENDINGS | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- **-ed adjectives** describe how a person feels (temporary emotion): bored, tired, confused, excited, worried, shocked, insulted. "I'm bored."
- **-ing adjectives** describe a thing/situation/event that causes the feeling, or a person's characteristic: boring, exciting, confusing, interesting, disappointing, insulting. "The movie is boring." "I'm boring" = I am a boring person.
- Both usually follow a linking verb (be) or come before a noun ("an exciting movie").
- Examples: "I find English grammar confusing." / "She's bored of her job." / "Some of his comments were insulting." / "He's so boring." / "I'm worried my writing isn't interesting." / "My results were disappointing so I was shocked."

## 401. LEARN ALL 12 VERB TENSES IN ENGLISH

- 12 tenses = 4 aspects (simple, progressive/continuous, perfect, perfect progressive) x 3 times (past, present, future).
- **Present simple**: habits/facts; adverbs of frequency (always, usually, sometimes, rarely, never); 3rd person -s. "We usually play soccer." "Nobody stands up to her" (**stand up to** = resist someone).
- **Past simple**: finished action in the past; time words (yesterday, last night, a long time ago); regular -ed or irregular (bought, went). "I lived in South Korea for 3 years" (don't live there now).
- **Present perfect** (have/has + past participle): past action at an unspecified time or still connected to now; for/since. "She has played piano for six years." "Where have you been?" "I've been to China." (Specific time -> past simple: "I went to Japan this summer.")
- **Future simple** (will + base verb): certain facts/predictions. "It will rain all week." "The door won't open."
- **Present progressive** (am/is/are + -ing): happening now. "The phone is ringing." "What are you doing?"
- **Past progressive** (was/were + -ing): continuous action at a specific past time or interrupted by another event. "Last week we were visiting Slovenia." "When I started, people were talking."
- **Future progressive** (will be + -ing): continuous action at a specific future time. "At this time next week, I'll be doing another live lesson." "Will you be joining us for dinner?"
- **Present perfect progressive** (have/has been + -ing): started in the past, continues now and likely future; lately/recently/for/since. "I've been waiting for hours." "Has it been raining all day?"
- **Past perfect** (had + past participle): the earlier of two past events. "When you knocked, I had already left for work."
- **Past perfect progressive** (had been + -ing): ongoing action up to a past point. "He had been talking nonstop when you interrupted."
- **Future perfect** (will have + past participle): done before a future point. "By the time you go to bed, I will have changed several diapers."
- **Future perfect progressive** (will have been + -ing): ongoing action up to a future point. "By 2067 we will have been teaching on YouTube for 50 years."
- Note: "enjoy watching" - -ing there is a gerund, not a continuous tense.

## 402. LEARN ANIMAL VOCABULARY AT THE ZOO

- emu, ostrich, otter, butterfly, alligator, iguana, Argentine horn frog, marmoset, anaconda (snake), clown fish, eel, stingray, bat, swan, kangaroo, wallaby, peacock, polar bear, seal, goat, penguin, lemur, lion, hyena, gorilla, giraffe, meerkat, rhinoceros, flamingo, gecko, turtle, guinea pig, parrot, bunny (rabbit), lizard

## 403. LEARN SUBJECT-VERB AGREEMENT | Advanced Grammar

- One subject (he/she/it) -> singular verb (goes); two or more (we/they) -> plural verb (go).
- Subjects joined by **and** -> plural: "Wes and Ioana are teachers."
- Singular subjects joined by **or/nor** (or either...or / neither...nor) -> singular: "Wes or Ioana teaches."
- Singular + plural joined by or/nor -> verb agrees with the **nearer** subject: "The students or Wes plays"; "Wes or the students play." Tip: put the plural subject last.
- Compound subject meaning one thing/person -> singular: "Spaghetti and meatballs is my favorite meal." "My wife and best friend (same person) loves to travel." "PB&J is the best."
- Words between subject and verb don't change agreement: "Wes, who loves cookies, needs to cut back."
- Singular indefinite pronouns take singular verbs: each, either, neither, no one, anybody, anyone, someone, something, everyone, everybody, everything. "Everybody needs to answer."
- Plural indefinite pronouns take plural verbs: several, few, both, many. "Many have tried and few have succeeded."
- **some, any, none, all, most** + of: uncountable -> singular ("Some of the water is..."); countable plural -> plural ("Some of the plates are...").
- Inverted subjects (there is/are, how many): verb agrees with the noun after it: "There is a kitten"; "There are cats"; "There is a group of people in the elevator."
- Collective nouns (group, jury, crowd, team) -> singular: "The team plays in the World Cup." (groups -> plural)
- **the number of** + plural noun -> singular verb ("The number of people has decreased"); **a number of** -> plural verb ("A number of people have smartphones").
- Tips: watch for slips when speaking fast (he go, she do); read a lot to absorb correct grammar.

## 404. LEARN THE FUTURE TENSE  | Will + (Be) Going To

- **will + base verb**: predictions (~50% sure: "I think it'll rain tomorrow"); spontaneous decisions ("There's a knock - I'll get it"); willingness ("I'll help you with the project"); refusal ("He won't eat his vegetables"; won't = will not).
- **be going to**: plans already decided ("I'm going to the doctor's next week"); likely predictions (~75% sure: "It's going to rain"). Only "be" conjugates; negative: "I'm not going to...".
- Spontaneous decision = will; plan = be going to. Learners over-rely on will, so mix both.
- Pronunciation: "going to" + base verb reduces to "gonna" (speaking only, don't write it); not before a place ("going to the doctor's").
- **Future continuous** (will be + -ing / be going to be + -ing): unfinished action at a future point – "I'll be doing my homework later this evening."
- **Future perfect** (will have + past participle): action completed before a future point; rare. "By September she will have been with the company for six years." "By the time you arrive, we will have finished eating." (by the time -> perfect tense)
- **Future perfect continuous** (will have been + -ing): look back from a future point at an unfinished action; very rare. "When it gets to midnight I will have been waiting for two hours." "By 2019 I will have been studying English for five years."
- **Simple present for scheduled events** (near future): "Her plane arrives next Monday." "The movie starts at 8."
- **Present continuous for planned arrangements** (someone's plan): "He's leaving work early tomorrow." "They're getting married on a beach."

## 405. LISTENING & SPEAKING ACTIVITY | Speak English on Your Own

Activity: listen to a short story twice, picture it in your mind, pause and retell it from on-screen clues, then compare with a partner. Tip: visualizing details helps memory. (Stories: summer morning routine in San Francisco; a silly dream about an Uber driver in Brazil.)

## 406. LISTEN & LEARN | Build Vocabulary, Identify Key Details, Make Inferences

Skills: guess new words from context, find main idea and key details, make inferences (use known info to infer more, e.g. wet umbrella -> it's raining).
- **chide** (v) – scold or reprimand angrily – "The teacher chided me for texting in class."
- **the Big Apple** (n) – nickname for New York City
- **bachelor party** (n) – party for a groom with close friends before the wedding
- **cast** (n) – hard mold around a broken arm/hand
- **jokingly** (adv) – in a teasing way, not seriously
- **southpaw** (n) – left-handed person (originally boxer/baseball pitcher) – "He's a southpaw, so he can't write with a cast on his left hand."

## 407. LIVE ENGLISH LESSON 🚨 | Q&A with Wes, Ioana, and Emi

Live Q&A; gist: practice consistently across all four skills, get lots of exposure; make a word memorable by linking it to a picture or sentence (Google the word's usage chart/pronunciation); lower speaking anxiety by practicing aloud; use YouTube captions/speed settings for listening; read to improve punctuation; idioms matter less for academic English, slang is for comprehension.
- **on the brink of** (idiom) – about to start something – "She's on the brink of crawling."
- **pulchritude** (n) – beauty (rare)
- **under your belt** (idiom) – learned/achieved already – "a second language under your belt"
- **jot down** (phr v) – write quickly/informally (write down is more common)
- **acquit** (v) – declare not guilty of a crime
- **pardon** (v) – official forgiveness of a crime (e.g. by the president)
- **up in the air** (idiom) – undecided – "Our plans are up in the air."
- **I shudder to think** – I don't want to think about something bad
- **meet someone halfway** (idiom) – compromise
- **ain't** (informal/Southern US) – contraction of "am not" ("I ain't gonna do this")
- **TPR (Total Physical Response)** – learning by acting out what you hear
- Pronunciation: "the" before a consonant is usually /ðə/ (schwa).
- Tip: TOEFL/IELTS don't grade accent type (British vs American).
- Tip: describe education in interviews chronologically and connect it to the job.

## 408. LIVE ENGLISH Q&A ANSWERING YOUR QUESTIONS

- **because / since / as** (conjunctions, reason): use **because** (focus on the reason, most common, informal "cuz"); since = less common; as = formal/rare in American English. "I watch Interactive English because I want to achieve fluency."
- **regarding / concerning / in terms of / with regard to** (prepositions introducing a subject): regarding = concerning; "in terms of" is more common than "with regard to". "Regarding your raise, we need to review your performance." "We give the best in terms of quality and cost." Note: concerning can also mean "worrying".
- **red flag** (idiom) – warning sign of a problem
- Tool: Google Ngram Viewer to compare how common phrases are.
- **tall** = height; **long** = length. **freedom** (very common, general) vs **liberty** (political). **suburbs** (residential area outside city) vs **outskirts** (any area on the edge).
- Pronunciation: **sentence** (stress 1st), **cooperative** /koʊˈɑːpərətɪv/ (stress 2nd), **antibiotics** (stress "bi"), **quadrilateral** and **parallelogram** (5 syllables each), **ship** /ɪ/ vs **sheep** /iː/; close /kloʊz/ (verb) vs close /kloʊs/ (near); close/clothes homophones.
- **homonyms / homophones**: here/hear, there/their, whole/hole.
- **might / may** – interchangeable in everyday speech ("I may/might go outside").
- Tips: create an "English bubble"; mix passive (music, TV) and active (respond, write) practice; read at slightly above your level on topics you like; 10 min/day beats cramming; shadowing/imitation technique for pronunciation; think in English during daily tasks.

## 409. LIVE: Q&A with Interactive English + Big Announcement

Gist: Q&A with Wes and Ioana (English teachers, married, living in Budapest; announce expecting a baby girl in January). Advice: fluency is a process - practice daily, exposure possible without moving abroad; learning more languages makes the next easier; use movies/TV at your level with English subtitles, mix passive and active viewing; pick content you're interested in; listening/speaking/reading/writing are connected; for IELTS/TOEFL, learn the exam format, write timed essays, read model answers; examiners care that you give supporting reasons, not which side you pick. Book: "Compelling Conversations" (speaking questions).

## 410. LOSE YOUR HEAD | Advanced (C1) #Idiom to Build Your #Vocabulary

- **lose your head** (idiom) – lose control, stop acting calmly because of anger/upset – "Everyone loses their heads."
- **lose your mind** (idiom, different) – go crazy / become mentally ill

## 411. LOVE VOCABULARY ❤️ | Words and phrases you need to know

- **have a crush on** (phrase) – strong but temporary liking for someone – "I have a crush on you." / "She is my crush."
- **secret admirer** (n) – person secretly attracted to you (gifts, letters)
- **love is in the air** (expression) – feeling of romance around – "I think love is in the air."
- **knight in shining armor** (idiom) – rescuer/hero who saves someone (usually a man saving a woman)
- **sweep someone off their feet** (idiom) – suddenly make someone very romantically attracted to you
- **romantic nicknames** (for partners only, not colleagues): babe, darling, sweetheart, honey, sugar, boo, doll, handsome, baby, sweetie
- **hit it off** (idiom) – like each other and become friendly immediately – "We really hit it off."
- **flirt with** (v) – show romantic interest playfully; also n. flirt, adj. flirtatious
- **fall head over heels (in love)** (idiom) – fall suddenly and deeply in love
- **woo** (v) – try to win someone's love, aiming at marriage
- **better half** (idiom) – informal term for your spouse/partner – "Meet my better half."
- **bouquet of flowers** (n) – arranged bunch of flowers; /buːˈkeɪ/ (French)
- **hit on** (phr v) – make romantic/sexual advances toward someone
- **serenade** (v) – sing/play music for someone you love
- **candle-lit dinner** (n) – romantic meal lit by candles
- **blush** (v) – turn red in the face from shyness/embarrassment
- Note: "propose" takes "to" (propose to someone).

## 412. Learn & Practice English with Listening Games | Fun Activity for Learners

Activity: road-trip game "20 Questions" (here 10) - think of a person or place; the other player guesses by asking only yes/no questions ("Is it in North America?", "Is it a city?"). Good speaking/listening practice with friends.

## 413. Learn All 12 Verb Tenses + Mini-quiz | Grammar You Need to Know

3 times (past, present, future) x 4 aspects (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous) = 12 tenses.
- **Present simple** (V/Vs): habits (always, usually), facts ("Fire is hot"), schedules ("The movie starts at eight").
- **Past simple**: finished past action (yesterday, last week, a long time ago) – "I taught in South Korea a long time ago."
- **Present perfect** (have + past participle): unfinished with for/since ("We have lived in Hungary since 2017"); experiences ("We have been to Berlin"); recent past ("We recently traveled to Italy").
- **Future simple** (will / be going to): "I think it'll rain tomorrow." "The US is going to win."
- **Present continuous** (am/is/are + -ing): happening now; temporary ("this month I'm working from home"); annoyance with always ("You're always asking for help"); planned future ("I'm leaving on a jet plane").
- **Past continuous** (was/were + -ing): action at specific past time/interrupted; story background ("The sun was shining"); overlapping actions; changed plans ("We were planning a road trip but the car broke down").
- **Future continuous** (will be + -ing): action in progress at future time ("A week from now I will be sitting on a beach").
- **Present perfect continuous** (have been + -ing): started in past, continues now ("I've been waiting for the bus for 30 minutes"); just-finished action with visible result ("It's been raining").
- **Past perfect** (had + past participle): earlier of two past actions ("When I arrived, the meeting had already begun"). Trigger words: when, before, by the time.
- **Past perfect continuous** (had been + -ing): ongoing until a past point ("I had been working at the school for a year when I met my wife").
- **Future perfect** (will have + past participle): done before a future point ("By the end of next year you will have achieved your goals").
- **Future perfect continuous** (will have been + -ing): ongoing up to a future point ("By 2067 I will have been teaching for 50 years").

## 414. Learn Awesome English Vocabulary....at IKEA!

**couch / sofa**, **armchair**, **coffee table**, **coaster** (protects furniture from drinks), **shelving unit**, **bookshelf**, **crib** (baby bed), **changing table**, **oven** (bake) / **stove** (heat on top), **cupboard** (cups, plates), **ottoman** (footrest), **shot glass**, **flat-screen TV**, **strainer**, **mallet**, **shower curtain**, **silverware** (spoon, fork, knife), **cushions**, **bar stools**, **cutting board**, **refrigerator / fridge**, **Tupperware** (brand = generic name), **swivel chair**, **vanity mirror**, **throw pillows**, **slippers**, **loofah**, **pan** (shallow) vs **pot** (deep), **cheese grater**, **oven mitt**, **pitcher**, **hook**, **hangers**, **laundry basket / hamper**, **lamp / lampshade**, **welcome mat**, **rug** (smaller than a carpet), **frame** (for photos).

## 415. Learn English & Stay Motivated 🚴

- **Intrinsic motivation** – comes from within (love of the language, travel, job opportunity, interests); more likely to keep you going; find it by linking English to your real interests (cars, cooking).
- **Extrinsic motivation** – external reward or avoiding a bad consequence (school requirement, exam, university/work requirement); helps you get started but only takes you so far.
- Both work together: use extrinsic to get the ball rolling, then find intrinsic motivation. Teachers: don't let rewards be the sole driver; understand students' interests.
- **get the ball rolling** (idiom) – get started.

## 416. Learn English with Friends - Ross & Rachel Sex Tape | Improve Your Listening

- **Reductions**: want to -> "wanna", going to -> "gonna".
- **hell yeah** (informal) – emphatic yes.
- **Elision** (sound dropped): final /t/ in "just" before a consonant ("just have to" -> "jus have to").
- **see** (multiple meanings): look at / understand ("Do you see?", "I see your point").
- **please** (multiple meanings): request vs. exasperated "come on".
- **Flap t** (US): /t/ between vowels sounds like /d/ ("forgot about"); **linking** consonant-final word + vowel-initial word ("forgot about him").
- **go on and on about** (phrase) – talk about something so long it becomes boring.
- **do it / done it** (informal euphemism for sex); **intrusion**: added /w/ sound links "do it" ("do-w-it").
- **come on to** (phr v) – make a romantic/sexual advance on someone.
- **Stress-timed rhythm**: English stressed syllables come at regular intervals, unstressed words get reduced; practice with **shadowing**.

## 417. Learn Future Time Clauses Correctly | Advanced Grammar

- Clause = subject + verb. Independent clause stands alone; dependent (time) clause doesn't.
- Time markers: after, before, when, while, by the time, since, whenever, until, as soon as, every time, the first time.
- **Rule**: in a future time clause use the simple PRESENT; use future (will) in the main clause. Never "will" after a time marker.
  - "After I get home, I will eat dinner." (not "after I will get home")
  - "Wes will do the dishes when he gets home."
  - "I'm going to wait until the train comes." "As soon as the rain stops, I will go to the store." "He'll get here before we eat all the pizza."
- Comma after the time clause when it comes first; no comma when the main clause comes first.

## 418. Learn Ordinal Numbers in English

- Ordinal numbers show position/order in a list, dates, and names ("March 15th", "Henry the 8th").
- Suffixes: ends in 1 -> **-st** (twenty-first); 2 -> **-nd** (forty-second); 3 -> **-rd** (sixty-third); all others -> **-th** (eighty-fourth).
- Exceptions: **eleventh, twelfth, thirteenth** (all -th).
- First 10: first, second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth.

## 419. Learn THIS - THAT - THESE - THOSE | Demonstrative Pronouns & Adjectives

- Same four words serve as pronouns (replace the noun) and adjectives (come before the noun).
- **this** (singular, near) / **these** (plural, near); **that** (singular, far) / **those** (plural, far). Distance is relative to the speaker.
- Pronoun: "This has gone bad." "These taste incredible." "I like those." Adjective: "Do you see this chair / those burgers?"
- "I like these" (pronoun) = "I like these glasses" (adjective) - same meaning, different part of speech.
- Asking about something on another person: "What's that on your face?" (on yourself: "What's this on my face?").
- Something happening now: "this music we're listening to"; something past/remote: "that music", "Those were the days" (nostalgia idiom).
- Plural nouns (headphones, documents, pretzels) take these/those: "Would you mind signing these documents?" "These pretzels are making me thirsty." "What the hell is that?" (unidentified, far away)

## 420. Learn Tag Questions to Improve Your Fluency | Advanced Grammar

- Tag question (question tag) = statement + short question; used mostly in spoken English; asks for confirmation (yes/no answer). Rising intonation = real question; falling intonation = rhetorical/accusing ("You stole the money, didn't you.").
- Positive statement -> negative tag: "He finished the report, didn't he?" Negative statement -> positive tag: "He didn't finish the report, did he?"
- Let's... -> **shall we**: "Let's eat, shall we?"
- I am -> **aren't I**: "I'm the favorite to win, aren't I?"
- Polite request: "You'll help, won't you?"
- Imperative (command) -> **will you / would you**: "Give me a hand, will you?"
- **used to** -> **didn't**: "You used to play football, didn't you?"
- Main verb **have**: American "She has friends, doesn't she?"; British "hasn't she?" - both fine.
- this/that -> **it**: "This is it, isn't it?"; these/those -> **they**: "These are your shoes, aren't they?"
- Practice: "He's always studying, isn't he?"; "I told you what happened, didn't I?"; "This isn't the last question, is it?"; "They used to live in San Francisco, didn't they?"

## 421. Learn Thanksgiving Vocabulary | Fun English Lesson

- Thanksgiving: US family holiday on the 4th Thursday of November (Canada: 1st Monday of October; Liberia: 1st Thursday of November); originally giving thanks for the harvest. Typical food: turkey, potatoes, corn, stuffing, pumpkin pie, rolls, carrots, mac and cheese.
- **feast** (n) – large meal celebrating something – "a Thanksgiving feast"
- **gravy** (n) – sauce of meat juices and flour – "gravy on the mashed potatoes"
- **cornucopia** (n) – symbol of plenty; "a cornucopia of" = plenty of – "a cornucopia of activities"
- **pilgrim** (n) – person who journeys far for religious reasons; the early settlers in the US – "pilgrims walk the Camino"
- **gratitude** (n) – quality of being thankful; collocation: *show gratitude to/for* – "She showed gratitude for the help"
- **carve** (v) – cut meat into slices; cut/shape an object or writing into something – "carve the turkey", "carve your name in a tree"
- **abundance** (n) – large quantity – "an abundance of food"
- **leftovers** (n) – food remaining after a meal – "We ate leftovers for a week"
- **wishbone** (n) – forked bone from a turkey; the person with the larger piece after pulling it apart makes a wish

## 422. Learn Useful English Vocabulary 👉 Ignorant 👈

- **ignorant** /ˈɪɡnərənt/ (adj) – lacking knowledge or awareness (rude to call a person this; describe things instead) – "an ignorant comment / statement / behavior"; "I'm a bit ignorant when it comes to art history." Stress on 1st syllable; 3rd syllable is a schwa.
- **ignorance** (n) – lack of knowledge – "Ignorance is bliss."
- **ignore** (v) – refuse to notice or acknowledge – "Don't ignore me."

- **coast** (n) – land next to or near the sea (also waterfront, shoreline) – "the Barcelona coast"
- **street vendor** (n) – person selling things on the street – "street vendors sell scarves and sunglasses"
- **balcony** (n) – raised platform on a building's side, with a railing or wall – "Barcelona is the city of balconies"
- **architecture** (n) – the way a building is designed and constructed – "Gaudi's architecture"

- **peninsula** (n) – land surrounded by water on three sides – "Dubrovnik is on a peninsula"
- **infamous** (adj) – well known for something bad – "You're infamous for forgetting stuff"
- **alley** (n) – narrow pathway between or behind buildings – "easy to get lost in the alleys"
- **engraved** (adj/v) – written or cut onto a hard surface – "names engraved on love locks"
- **cable car** (n) – transport on a cable that carries people up and down mountains – "we took the cable car down"
- **fortified** (adj) – protected with defenses – "fortified walls"
- **overcast** (adj) – cloudy – "it's a bit overcast today"
