## 425. Learn about July 4th in English | Vocabulary + American Culture

- July 4, 1776: Declaration of Independence from Britain; US celebrates with fireworks, barbecues, picnics.
- Fireworks adjectives: **dazzling, sparkling, shimmering, glittering, booming** (sound).
- Firework types: **sparklers, poppers, smoke bomb, mortars** (shoot up and explode), **Roman candles, M-80s** (very loud).
- **founding fathers** (n) – the people who created the United States (Washington, Jefferson, Madison, Franklin, Hamilton...)
- **John Hancock** (idiom) – your signature – "Can I get your John Hancock?"
- **liberty** (n) – freedom; "at liberty to do something" = free to; Statue of Liberty (Lady Liberty)
- **Revolutionary War** (n) – war of US colonists against Britain for independence
- US flag nicknames: **the red, white and blue**, **Old Glory** (13 stars for the original colonies; 50 now)
- **national anthem** (n) – a country's official song; US: "The Star-Spangled Banner"
- **salute** (v) – gesture of respect to a flag or person
- **patriotism / patriotic** – pride in your country, its people and way of life (positive)
- **nationalism / nationalistic** – pride tied to national power or might (can be dangerous)
- **pay tribute** (v phr) – show respect/honor – "pay tribute to the veterans"
- **veteran** (n) – former soldier
- **valor** (n) – bravery
- **love of country** (n phr) – pride in your country
- **fire up the grill** (idiom) – start the grill to cook outdoors; **barbecue** (event or food); **cookout** (outdoor meal)
- Flag colors: white = purity/innocence, red = valor, blue = vigilance and justice.

## 426. Learn the Difference...TOO vs ENOUGH | Grammar Lesson

- **too** (adverb) = more than needed/excessive: too much (non-count) / too many (count) / too + adjective – "too much food", "too many cars", "The water is too cold to swim", "I'm too old for this."
- **too** also = also ("Me too"); = very, in negatives – "I'm not too pleased with the result"; shows surprise – "...and by a child, too!"
- **enough** = sufficient: enough + noun / adjective + enough – "We don't have enough time", "She's smart enough to figure it out", "old enough to drive" vs "too young to drive".
- **way too much time on their hands** – too much free time, nothing to do
- **old enough to be her father** – much older than her
- **You're too kind.** – polite response to a compliment
- **too little, too late** – help/effort that came too late to matter
- **go/take it too far** – behave in an upsetting or annoying way (e.g. a joke)
- **not too bad / not too shabby** – pretty good (often surprisingly)
- **You can never be too careful.** – do everything possible to avoid problems
- **Enough is enough.** – I want this to stop
- **sure enough** – as expected – "Sure enough, the dog peed everywhere." (can't replace with "for sure")
- **Enough said.** – I understand, no need to say more

## 427. Learn the SCHWA SOUND /ə/ the Most Common Sound in English

- **schwa** /ə/ – the "uh" sound; most common sound in English; looks like an upside-down "e".
- Occurs only in unstressed syllables, at the start, middle or end of words; any vowel letter can be a schwa.
- **banana** /bəˈnænə/ – 1st and 3rd syllables are schwa.
- Native speakers reduce unstressed words (articles, prepositions) with schwa, e.g. "Why learn English?" - the middle words reduce.
- Learning it improves listening comprehension and makes your speech smoother and more confident.

## 428. Learn to Use ACTIVE and PASSIVE VOICE | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Only sentences with a transitive verb (verb followed by an object) can become passive; intransitive verbs ("She laughs at the joke", "We will eat in the park") cannot.
- Active: subject performs the action ("Wes teaches English"; direct, most common). Passive: subject receives the action.
- Formula: move the object to the front + **be (same tense as the original verb) + past participle** (+ *by* + object pronoun): "I wrote that email" -> "That email was written by me."
- Subject pronouns become object pronouns after *by* (I -> me, he -> him, we -> us, they -> them); watch agreement with a plural object ("Those emails were written").
- Use passive to: (1) emphasize the action over the actor – "This land was farmed by my ancestors."
- (2) avoid naming the actor – "Mistakes were made." (politicians); "Their dog will be put to sleep."
- (3) when the actor is unknown – "My bike was stolen."; "Has the cat been fed?"
- (4) sound firm but polite in rules – "Talking is not allowed." "Smoking is prohibited."

## 429. Learn to Use SHADOWING and Improve Your Pronunciation

- **Youglish** – search engine that finds YouTube clips of any word/phrase in context; filter by accent (US, UK, Australian).
- **Yarn** – site of short movie/TV/music clips for practicing a word or phrase.
- **Shadowing** – hear something and repeat it exactly, copying intonation, rhythm and emphasis.
- Pronunciation = mouth position for individual sounds + imitating the rhythm/flow/intonation of connected speech.
- Same sentence changes with context ("What's the matter with you?" = concern vs. "What's your problem?").
- Record yourself and compare with the original to spot problem sounds, intonation, or stress.
- Example words/phrases: **colossal** – "a colossal understatement"; "Where do we go from here?"

## 430. Learning English On Your Own?... NO PROBLEM | How to Practice By Yourself

- **Hangman** – improves spelling and vocabulary (games-to-learn-english, justhangman, hangmanwordgame sites; categories like food, Christmas).
- **Crossword puzzles** – spelling and vocabulary; print themed ones (rooms, body parts, colors, adjectives, actions) from ESL Games Plus; dictionary.com has a free daily crossword for a challenge.
- **Spot the difference** – Google Images search; describe each difference in a full sentence ("In the second picture the cow has black spots; in the first it has brown spots").
- **Emoji stories** – write a short story using 3 random emojis (creativity, vocabulary, sentence structure); a story generator site keeps adding emojis so the story continues; also emoji guessing-game apps (compound words, movies, pop culture).
- Vocab: **corn dog** – hot dog in cornmeal batter on a stick, fair food; "blind as a bat" (idiom) – can't see well without glasses.

## 431. Level Up Your English in Under 3 Minutes (B2 Adjectives)

- **forthcoming** (adj) – about to happen soon; or willing to share information/help (opposite: "not forthcoming") – "We expect their approval to be forthcoming."
- **sharp** (adj) – intelligent, quick to notice and think; idiom *sharp as a tack* – very intelligent – "She's sharp."
- **worthwhile** (adj) – useful/important enough to justify the time or money – "a worthwhile endeavor"
- **last-minute** (adj) – at the latest possible time – "a last-minute change / decision / cancellation"
- **self-confident** (adj) – sure of your own abilities (positive; not the same as *arrogant*) – "smart, self-confident, and articulate"

## 432. Level Up Your English: Smart Ways to Describe Someone’s Appearance

Positive
- **effervescent** (adj) – active, positive, full of energy – "her effervescent smile"; collocations: personality, charm, energy, spirit
- **dapper** (adj) – neat and stylish (usually a man; "look dapper") – "he looked dapper in his tailor-made suit"
- **glowing** (adj) – radiant, happy, healthy (skin/complexion; "pregnancy glow") – "You look amazing, you're glowing."
- **voluptuous** (adj) – curvy and sexually attractive (usually a woman) – "the model's voluptuous figure"
- **striking** (adj) – exceptionally noticeable or attractive; collocations: striking beauty/appearance/features/eyes/resemblance; adverb *strikingly* beautiful/handsome – "his striking blue eyes"
- **chiseled** (adj) – clear, well-defined features (usually a man) – "chiseled jawline / cheekbones / physique"

Neutral
- **broad-shouldered** (adj) – having wide shoulders (vs. sloping/narrow shoulders)
- **stocky** (adj) – short and solidly built, with muscle – "a short, stocky build"
- **petite** (adj) – small and delicate (usually a woman; also a clothing size) – "her petite stature"
- **prominent** (adj) – noticeable, said neutrally of features – "a prominent nose"
- **slender** (adj) – thin and graceful – "slender waist / build / figure / legs"
- **unassuming** (adj) – not drawing attention (look or behavior) – "an unassuming appearance"

Negative
- **disheveled** (adj) – messy, untidy overall appearance – "tired and disheveled after the flight"
- **haggard** (adj) – worn out, exhausted-looking – "haggard after night shifts"
- **scruffy** (adj) – untidy, a bit dirty (hair, unshaven face) – "a scruffy man who hadn't shaved in weeks"
- **frail** (adj) – weak or unhealthy (old or ill people) – "the frail old woman"
- **tattered** (adj) – torn, ragged (clothing) – "tattered clothing"
- **slouch** (v/n) – stand, sit or move in a lazy, drooping way – "Don't slouch."

## 437. Live English Lesson to Improve Your Fluency | Q&A Conversation
- **run out (of)** (phrasal v) – supply gets used up; works for anything (cheese, gas, time, battery, patience): "running out of time". Not "sell out of time".
- **sell out / sold out** (phrasal v) – goods/tickets all bought; only for things you buy; "the concert sold out". Interchangeable with "ran out" for stores/stock; for event tickets "sold out" is more natural. "Ran out" may imply more later.
- **inventory** (n) – goods a store keeps in stock – "we're running out of inventory"
- **done vs finished** – interchangeable for tasks ("I've done/finished all my work"); strictly "done" = brought to a conclusion (food is done; "I'm done with this" may mean you quit, not completed), "finished" = completed. "Finish" + -ing: "finished doing the laundry".
- **bother** (v) – annoy; **bore** (v) – make someone uninterested.
- **a piece of cake** (idiom) – very easy (US and UK)
- **once in a blue moon** (idiom) – very rarely (blue moon = 2nd full moon in a month)
- **over my head** (idiom) – too hard to understand – "that math problem was over my head"
- **feel blue** (idiom) – feel sad
- **beat the Sox** – baseball reference (Boston Red Sox)
- **slow down vs calm down** – "slow down" is the polite way to ask someone to speak slower; "calm down" = too hyped up.
- **figure out** (phrasal v) – solve
- **overwhelmed vs overcome** – overwhelmed = too much going on; overcome (by/with) = a feeling takes over temporarily: "overcome with joy/sadness"
- **keep in touch** (phrase) – stay in contact
- **take a hit** (idiom) – be reduced/damaged – "my Spanish has taken a hit"
- **the cat's out of the bag** (idiom) – the secret is revealed
- **discourse markers** (n) – filler words like "um", "like", "right on" that people overuse
- **sign off** (phrasal v) – end a broadcast/session
- Tips: slang/idioms are most useful for listening comprehension (TV, movies, music); be careful using them yourself. Focus on rhythm and flow, not speed. Test prep: know exactly what is on the test, practice until comfortable, retaking helps. Practice a little daily, 1-2 new words/day.

## 438. MANY, MUCH, A LOT OF, & LOTS OF | Grammar Deep Dive with Common Quantifiers
- Affirmative: many + plural noun; much + uncountable (sounds formal, rare: "great concern" instead of "much concern"). **a lot of / lots of** work with both (lots of = more informal).
- Negative: many (plural), much (uncountable), a lot of / lots of (both). "I don't have many contacts / much money / a lot of photos."
- Questions: "how many" (plural) and "how much" (uncountable) are fixed phrases – cannot replace with a lot of. Other questions allow either: "Were there many/a lot of children?"
- Negative questions: "Are there not many...?" expects "no"; "Are there not a lot of...?" expects "yes". "Didn't you drink much water?" vs "lots of water?"
- Without noun (noun obvious): "Did you catch many?" / "How much?" / "There were a lot." / "Lots." (no "of" when noun is dropped)
- many of / much of + possessive/article/pronoun: "many of your comments", "much of my life"
- much + comparative for emphasis: "much faster", "much better"; so many / so much (= very): "so much work"; too many / too much = more than necessary
- as many as / as much as = any amount up to a limit: "write as many comments as you like", "as much food as you want"

## 439. MASTERING QUESTIONS (everything you need to know)
- Yes/no questions: rising intonation when the answer is unknown ("Are you hungry?"); falling when you already know / want confirmation ("Does that make sense?").
- Yes/no structure: be-verb + subject + info ("Are you okay?"); otherwise do/does/did + subject + main verb.
- Statement questions: a statement said with rising intonation = genuine question ("You're from Chicago?"); falling = confirming something you suspect.
- Open-ended: **who, what, where, when, why, how**; **which** = choice; **whose** = possession; **how often** = frequency; also how much/how many.
- "did you" assimilates to "didja" (d+y -> j): "what did you", "where did you"; "would you", "could you" likewise.
- Question order: wh-word + auxiliary + subject + main verb ("What does he eat for lunch?").
- Noun/wh-clause inside a statement keeps normal order, no inversion: "I don't know what he eats for lunch" (not "what does he eat"). Common spoken mistake.
- Politeness: direct -> indirect. "Do you want...?" -> "Would you like...?"; "Where's the toilet?" -> "Could you please tell me where the toilet is?" (clause order: subject + verb). Use "please".
- Tag questions: positive statement + negative tag ("You like pizza, don't you?"); negative statement + positive tag ("She didn't see the movie, did she?").
- **rhetorical question** (n) – asked for effect, no answer expected – "Can't you do anything right?"
- **loaded question** (n) – contains a hidden trap/implication; any answer admits it – "Why aren't you married yet?" / "Do you still drive that ugly car?"
- **leading question** (n) – nudges the respondent to answer a certain way – "Objection, leading the witness" (courtroom: judge says "sustained"/"overruled")
- **trick question** (n) – question with a catch.

## 440. MASTER the American Accent with 9 Simple Words
- **to** – weak form /tə/ when unstressed (function word): "I'm going to the mall"
- **for** – weak form /fər/ ("fur"): "what are you going to do for dinner"
- **water** – flap T (t between vowels sounds like soft d: water, city, political, quality) + pronounced final R (car, weather, chair, better, butter, writer)
- **mountain** – glottal stop for t before n: mountain, kitten, written, gotten, bitten (not a fixed rule; true t also heard)
- **center** – t after n often dropped: center, wanted, interview, twenty, entertain; but kept at start of stressed syllable: internship, anticipate, intensive
- **can't** – US /kænt/ (UK /kɑːnt/); ends with glottal stop on "nt"; hard to distinguish from "can"
- **schedule** – US "sked-yool" vs UK "shed-yool"; also advertisement (US ad-ver-TIZE-ment), zebra (US ZEE-bra), dynasty (US DIE-nuh-stee)
- **entrepreneur** – Americanized French word; French loanwords (fiancé, connoisseur, café, sorbet) often have final stress – don't stress the wrong syllable
- **supercalifragilisticexpialidocious** – 14 syllables; practice stress-timed rhythm (English stress at regular intervals; unstressed syllables shortened): "cats chase mice" / "the cats will chase the mice"
- Tip: shadowing is the best way to practice; you don't need to change accent, only be understood.

## 441. MINIMAL PAIRS | Helpful Pronunciation Practice to Improve Your Fluency
- **Minimal pair** – two words that differ by one sound (desk/disc); practice improves clarity and listening.
- Short e vs short i (mouth slightly wider for i): fell/fill, set/sit, bless/bliss, sex/six
- /s/ vs /sh/ (s: tongue near teeth ridge, like a snake; sh: lips rounded, tongue arched): sock/shock, sign/shine, said/shed
- /s/ vs voiceless /th/ (tongue between teeth, no vibration; finger test feels air): sink/think, some/thumb, symbol/thimble; sank/thank
- Tongue twister: "She sells seashells by the seashore."
- Methods: repeat/shadow sentences, then identify the word you hear (hold up fingers); "going to" -> "gonna".

## 442. Make Sure You Learn These Useful Clothing Phrasal Verbs
- Separable rule: transitive phrasal verbs (with an object) are often separable ("take out the trash" / "take the trash out"), but some are not ("got on the plane"). Intransitive (no object) are never separable ("wakes up at 9"). Best learned through reading/exposure.
- **have on** (sep) – be wearing – "she has a blue dress on"
- **take in** (sep) – make clothes narrower – "I'll have the seamstress take it in"
- **let out** (sep) – make clothes wider/larger – "have the seamstress let it out"
- **slip on** (sep) – put on quickly/easily (shoes, pajamas) – "slip your shoes on and go"
- **slip off** (sep) – remove easily – "she slipped off her robe"
- **come off** (inseparable) – detach/be removable – "the ring wouldn't come off"
- **zip up** (sep) – fasten with a zipper – "zip your jacket up"
- **hang up** (sep) – put on a hanger/hook – "she hung up her clothes"
- **kick off** (sep) – remove (shoes) quickly to relax – "she kicked off her heels"
- Earlier lesson: **dress up** (put on formal clothes), **try on** (test clothes while shopping).

## 443. Make Your English More Impressive with These 12+ Letter Words
- **multifaceted** (adj, 12) – having many aspects/sides – "a multifaceted approach/issue/career/personality" – stress: mul-ti-FA-ce-ted
- **unimaginable** (adj, 12) – very hard to imagine/believe – "unimaginable pain/suffering" – stress 3rd syllable
- **illuminating** (adj, 12) – providing insight/clarity – "an illuminating journey" – stress 2nd syllable
- **opportunistic** (adj, 13) – taking advantage of situations, often negative – "an opportunistic politician/deal", "highly opportunistic" – stress before -tic
- **inconceivable** (adj, 13) – impossible to imagine/believe – "It is inconceivable to me that..." – stress 3rd syllable
- **contextualize** (v, 13) – put in background/circumstances for understanding – "contextualize the results"
- **accountability** (n, 14) – accepting responsibility for actions – "corporate/personal accountability", "lack of accountability", "demand/avoid accountability"
- **wholeheartedly** (adv, 14) – completely and sincerely – "I wholeheartedly agree" – stress 1st syllable
- **impressionable** (adj, 14) – easily influenced, esp. young people – stress 2nd syllable
- **confrontational** (adj, 15) – aggressive/argumentative – "confrontational approach/style/attitude/situation" (negative) – stress 3rd syllable
- **disillusionment** (n, 15) – disappointment from discovering something isn't as good as thought – "voters' disillusionment"; adj disillusioned
- **reconfiguration** (n, 15) – new arrangement of related things – "reconfiguration of the floor plan"
- **disproportionate** (adj, 16) – too large or too small compared with something – "a disproportionate share of the work"; "grossly/vastly/wholly disproportionate"
- **uncharacteristic** (adj, 16) – not typical of a person/thing – "an uncharacteristic mistake"
- **incomprehensible** (adj, 16) – impossible to understand or make sense of – "it seems incomprehensible that..."; also unfathomable
- **indistinguishable** (adj, 17) – cannot be told apart – "indistinguishable from the real thing"; "virtually/practically/almost indistinguishable"
- **misinterpretation** (n, 17) – understanding something wrongly – "open to misinterpretation" (vs "open to interpretation" = different views possible)
- **interrelationship** (n, 17) – close connection where things influence each other – synonyms: interconnectedness, correlation
- Stress rules: words ending in -tic or -tion stress the syllable before the ending. Tip: Google Ngram Viewer compares word frequency (written English).

## 444. Make vs Do | How to Build Your Vocabulary with Useful Collocations
- **Collocation** – words frequently used together (adjective+noun, noun+noun, verb+noun).
- **make** (create/build): make coffee/breakfast/lunch/dinner/a cake/soup; made of gold / made from grapes / made in (country); make a plan / a decision / up your mind; make you happy (reactions); make money / a profit / a living; make a mistake / a phone call / a sound / a promise / an excuse
- **do** (activities/work): do the dishes / exercise / the laundry; do homework / your job / chores / the assignment / work; do something / anything / everything / nothing; do hair / nails

## 445. Master This DIFFICULT GRAMMAR to Improve Your Fluency
- **in the end vs at the end**: "in the end" = ultimately/outcome ("In the end, we decided to wait"); "at the end (of)" = specific point in time/place ("at the end of the story"); "at the end of the day" = all things considered.
- **being**: use gerund "being" after verbs/prepositions, not "to be": "I don't remember my teachers being so strict"; "tired of being wrong". Continuous: "you're being silly".
- **Not only ... but also**: emphasizes two (surprising) things; invert subject-verb after "not only": "Not only did he miss the meeting, but he also forgot to submit his report."
- **Double comparatives** (cause and effect): "The more you work, the more likely you'll succeed"; "the faster the car, the more dangerous it is".
- **What-cleft sentences** (emphasis): "What I really need is a cup of coffee"; "What they did was give us a refund"; "What we need to do is get batteries."
- **Mixed conditional** (past condition -> present result): if + past perfect, would + base verb: "If I had eaten earlier, I wouldn't be hungry right now."
- **Noun clauses** keep statement word order (no inversion): "He didn't realize what he was doing"; "I don't know who he is"; "tell me how you got lost."
- **Tag questions**: positive statement + negative tag ("Everyone left, didn't they?"); negative statement + positive tag ("You don't have any money, do you?").
- **Subject-verb agreement**: neither = singular ("Neither is fit to lead"); none = singular or plural ("None were available"); everyone = singular ("Everyone is a suspect"); both = plural ("Both are from New York").
- **Subjunctive with recommend/suggest**: use that-clause + base verb: "I recommend that you talk to a specialist" (not "recommend you to talk"); "He suggested that I apply".

## 446. Military Idioms & Expressions that YOU Can Use
- **boots on the ground** – troops/police physically present in an operation
- **bite the bullet** – endure pain/discomfort and just do it – "I'll bite the bullet and confess"
- **got your six / watch your six** – I've got your back (six o'clock = behind you); mostly in military/law enforcement/TV
- **bought the farm** – died (informal)
- **catch flak** – be harshly criticized (from someone) – "I caught a lot of flak from my boss" (from German anti-aircraft fire)
- **FUBAR** – messed up beyond all recognition; **SNAFU** – situation normal, all messed up; a problem/mistake ("we hit a snafu"); **TARFU** – things are really messed up
- **in the trenches** – in the thick of a tough fight/struggle (war, sports, business)
- **no man's land** – dangerous area or off-limits topic
- **nuclear option** – drastic action that destroys everything, e.g. skipping debate in US politics
- **on the double** – very quickly – "get over here on the double"
- **on the front lines** – at the forefront of a battle/cause/movement
- **Roger that** – yes / message received (from old NATO alphabet "Roger" = R)

## 447. Minimal Pairs | Useful Pronunciation Practice

Minimal pair = two words differing by one sound (e.g. sheep/ship). Practising them trains you to hear and produce small differences.
- **R vs L**: R = tongue raised, curled toward the back, not touching anything. L = tongue tip touches the ridge behind the upper front teeth. Pairs: **right/light**, **arrive/alive**, **rear/real(reel)**, **correct/collect**, **royal/loyal**, **crown/clown**. e.g. "Take a right at the next light."
- **Long /iː/ vs short /ɪ/**: /iː/ = wide, smiling mouth, tongue high and forward ("cheese"). /ɪ/ = relaxed lips, tongue central. Pairs: **sheep/ship**, **seat/sit**, **beach/bitch** (do not avoid "beach" – practise the long /iː/), **eat/it**, **deep/dip**, **sleek/slick**, **sleep/slip**. e.g. "I just want to take a deep dip in the pool."
- **B vs P**: both lips closed with a puff of air; /b/ is voiced (throat vibrates), /p/ is voiceless (no vibration). Pairs: **big/pig**, **by/pie**, **cab/cap**, **bear/pear**, **beer/pier**, **robe/rope**, **bull/pull**, **butt/putt**. e.g. "A big pig wearing a robe pulled a bull with a rope."
- Tip: practise out loud, with a partner, and check they understand which word you meant.

**buck** (n) – informal dollar – "Can I borrow a few bucks?"
**cost an arm and a leg** (idiom) – very expensive (too much) – "That car cost an arm and a leg."
**pony up** (phrasal verb) – pay money you owe (informal) – "You're the CEO, so pony up."
**put your money where your mouth is** (idiom) – prove you mean it by spending/acting – "Stop talking and put your money where your mouth is."
**make ends meet** (idiom) – earn just enough to live – "I struggled to make ends meet."
**loaded** (adj) – very rich – "These guys must be loaded."
**look like a million bucks** (idiom) – look great/elegant (compliment) – "You look like a million bucks!"
**shell out / fork over** (phrasal verbs) – pay for something (informal) – "You have to shell out cash for that."
**rags to riches** (idiom) – from poor to wealthy – "It's a true rags-to-riches story."
**bread and butter** (idiom) – main source of income – "Antiques are my bread and butter."
**foot the bill** (idiom) – pay for something – "Who's footing the bill?"
**break the bank** (idiom) – cost more than you can afford – "It won't break the bank."
**penny pincher** (n) – a person who hates spending money – "He's a penny pincher."
**pick up the tab / pick up the check** (idiom) – pay the bill (restaurant) – "Don't worry, I'll pick up the check."

## 449. Most Difficult Poem to Say in English | THE CHAOS

Gist: Wes reads Gerard Nolst Trenité's poem "The Chaos" to show how unpredictable English spelling and pronunciation are; he makes many mistakes, and the point is that nobody's pronunciation is perfect and that many poem words are rare. Some tricky pairs it contrasts:
**sword** /sɔːrd/ (silent w) vs **sward**; **plaid** /plæd/ vs **bade** /bæd/; **choir** /ˈkwaɪər/; **heard** /hɜːrd/ vs **beard**; **wind** /wɪnd/ vs **wind** /waɪnd/ (verb); **tortoise** /ˈtɔːrtəs/; **Heather** vs **heathen**; **billet** vs **ballet**; **blood**/**flood** /ʌ/ vs **food**; **sieve** /sɪv/; **soldier** /ˈsoʊldʒər/; **Worcester** /ˈwʊstər/; **viscount** /ˈvaɪkaʊnt/ (silent s); **isle** (silent s); **cough/tough/though/through/hiccup** – same letters "ough", different sounds.

## 459. Only Advanced Learners Can Score 10/10 on This Verb Tense Quiz 🔥

- **Present continuous for confirmed future arrangements**: "I can't meet you Friday; I am meeting the board."
- **"Be" in continuous = temporary behavior**: "You are being difficult today" (vs "You are difficult" = permanent trait).
- **Present simple for fixed schedules** (trains, flights, events): "The keynote starts at 9:00 a.m."
- **Present perfect for life experience** (no specific time): "I have visited Tokyo three times." With a past time expression use past simple: "I went last year."
- **Present perfect continuous** for action from past continuing to now, emphasizing duration (for/since): "We have been negotiating for 6 hours."
- **Past perfect** for the earlier of two past events (by the time...): "By the time the CEO arrived, the staff had already decided."
- **Past perfect continuous**: duration before a past point: "He was exhausted because he had been working since 5 a.m."
- **Future continuous** for an action in progress at a future moment: "This time next week I will be lying on a beach."
- **Future perfect** for completion before a future deadline: "I will have finished it by the time you wake up."
- **Future perfect continuous**: "By December, our team will have been developing this software for 2 years."

**off the beaten path** (idiom) – lesser-known, hard-to-reach place
**concrete jungle** (n) – city full of buildings
**melting pot** (n) – place with people of many cultures
**in awe** (phrase) – amazed and impressed
**wow factor** (n) – the amazing/impressive quality of something
**cheesy** (adj) – tacky, cliched
**antebellum** (adj) – before the US Civil War

## 461. PERPLEXING GRAMMAR Mistakes That Are Holding You Back

- **Subjunctive**: use "were" in hypotheticals: "If I were you...", "I wish he were here" (not "If I was you" in correct usage).
- **Word families**: verb **differentiate** (between), noun difference, adj different, adv differently. "How can we differentiate between the two?"
- **Parallel structure**: keep list items in the same form: "She likes reading books, writing essays, and studying history."
- **would rather + person + past tense**: "She would rather you didn't smoke here." ("would rather you not" also occurs.) Tip: Google Ngram Viewer compares phrase frequency.
- **Present perfect continuous** for duration up to now: "I have been waiting for the bus for over 20 minutes."
- **Who vs whom**: who = subject ("Who is going to the party?"), whom = object.
- **Third conditional**: if + had + past participle, would have + past participle: "If I had known, I would have helped you."
- **Singular they** for unknown gender in casual speech: "Where are they going?" (accepted by AP style).
- **Reported speech**: backshift to past perfect: "She said that she had visited the museum yesterday."
- **Future perfect after "by the time"**: "By the time you arrive, I will have finished my work."
- **Neither...nor**: verb agrees with the nearer subject: "Neither the teacher nor the students were aware."
- **Time clauses about the future use present simple**: "We'll leave as soon as the train arrives."

## 462. PICK UP has 20 Different Meanings | Phrasal Verb Vocabulary

**pick up** – 20 meanings:
1. lift – "I pick up my baby."
2. make feel better – "Coffee picks me up." (noun: **pick-me-up**)
3. get up after falling / recover – "Pick yourself up and keep going."
4. improve/develop – "Business is picking up."
5. collect someone (by car) – "Pick me up at 8."
6. tidy – "Pick up your room."
7. arrest – "Police picked up a suspect."
8. try to start a romance with someone (informal) – "pick up girls"
9. pay for – "Who's picking up the bill?"
10. buy – "I picked up a TV on sale."
11. understand – "Are you picking up what I'm putting down?"
12. learn a skill/language gradually – "I'm picking up guitar."
13. catch an illness – "I picked up a cold."
14. continue/resume – "Let's pick up where we left off."
15. receive/detect a signal or sound – "My phone isn't picking up a signal."
16. identify – "Doctors pick up early signs of a heart attack."
17. notice something not obvious (scent) – "The dog picked up the scent."
18. accelerate – "We picked up the pace."
19. answer the phone – "Nobody is picking up."
20. defend/mark an opposing player – "Who's picking up number 10?"

## 463. POLITICAL VOCABULARY 🇺🇸 Advanced Words & Phrases You Should Know

- **bipartisan** (adj) – supported by two political parties – "bipartisan support/agreement/vote/committee"
- **constituent** (n) – voter in a particular area; a representative answers to constituents – "answer to their constituents"
- **incumbent** (n) – person currently holding an official position – "the incumbent has the advantage"
- **demagogue** (n) – politician who wins support by stirring emotions rather than sound ideas – "accuse him of being a demagogue"
- **polarized** (adj) – divided into two opposing groups – "a polarized issue"
- **filibuster** (n/v) – long speech to delay or block a vote on a law – "I plan to filibuster the bill"
- **impeach** (v) – formally accuse a public official of serious misconduct (impeachment, impeachable offense); political process, not jail
- **referendum** (n) – public vote on one important question; "referendum on + topic"
- **ballot** (n) – secret voting system / paper you vote on – "secret ballot", "cast your ballot", "elected by ballot"
- **winner take all** (phrase) – winner gets the entire prize; US Electoral College states give all electors to the popular-vote winner (270 votes needed)
- **think tank** (n) – group of experts studying problems and proposing solutions (can be biased) – Brookings, Heritage Foundation, Pew
- **lobby** (v/n) – try to persuade officials to do or change something; (n) the group doing so – "the gun lobby"
- **poll** (n/v) – survey of opinions – "the polls show he's ahead"; **exit poll** – asking voters how they voted as they leave the polling station

## 464. POPULAR AMERICAN SLANG 🇺🇸  (Millennials & Gen Z)

- **keep it 100** – be truthful/authentic – "Let me keep it 100 with you."
- **extra** (adj) – dramatic, attention-grabbing, too much (negative) – "She's so extra."
- **vibe check** – friendly check on someone's mood/attitude – "Vibe check: how are you?"
- **rent free** – "live rent free in your head": obsessive thought about someone/something
- **stan** (n/v) – obsessive fan (from Eminem's song "Stan") – "Taylor Swift stans"
- **simp** (n) – person (usually a man) who over-desires another's attention/affection
- **based** (adj) – being yourself without caring what others think; original, unaffected
- **sus** (adj) – suspect/suspicious – "That guy is sus."
- **bougie** (adj) – luxurious, fancy, upper-class lifestyle – "bougie outfit"
- **basic** (adj) – boring, uncool (insult) – "This party is basic."
- Tip: don't force slang into conversation; avoid simp/stan with older generations.

## 465. PREPOSITIONS OF PLACE - IN, ON, AT | Quiz Lesson

- **IN**: enclosed/larger area (in this room, in the building); car/van/taxi; workplaces/fields (in marketing, in HR); water (in the sea/ocean/shower/pool); in class; printed or written material (in a book, newspaper, dictionary, article, picture – even online)
- **ON**: surfaces (on the table, on the sand, on the floor); floors of a building (on the 30th floor); along a road/river/coast/lake (on the east coast, on the river); public transport (on the bus/train/plane); left/right (on the right); media (on TV, on the radio, on YouTube, on the news); on the water (boat); on university property
- **AT**: a point/location (at the door, at the traffic light, at the gym, at the mall, at the beach); specific address (at 44 Main Street; but "on Main Street"); group activities (at a party, at a meeting); school/university (at school, at university); at home, at work
- "Where are you?" → "at the mall" (general point); "in the food court" (more specific, enclosed).
- Examples: "drive over the bridge to get into the city"; "no firearms on university property" vs "in the building"; "first heard it on the radio, read it in the newspaper".
- Tip: don't memorize rules only; read and practice until it feels natural.

## 466. PREPOSITIONS QUIZ | Advanced English Grammar

- concentrate **on** – "concentrate really hard on the lecture"
- jealous **of** someone/something
- Time: **in** the morning/afternoon/evening and months (in November); **at** a specific time (at 5 a.m., at noon, at midnight) and holidays (at Christmas); **on** days (on Sunday)
- accustomed **to** (+ noun or gerund) – "accustomed to the hot weather / to living abroad"
- addicted **to** (+ noun or gerund) – "addicted to playing video games"
- agree **on** something (topic/terms); agree **with** a person
- prohibit someone **from** + -ing – "prohibit people from smoking"
- standing **at** the window (a point); also near/by
- drive **over/across** the bridge to get **into** the city
- renowned **for** – "renowned for its natural beauty"
- together **at/for** Thanksgiving, **on** the fourth Thursday **in** November
- object **to** + noun/gerund – "I hope you won't object to hitting the like button"
- **toot your own horn** (idiom) – brag, promote yourself

## 467. PREPOSITIONS THAT FOLLOW VERBS | Advanced Grammar

- long **for** – desire something strongly – "longing for a beach vacation"
- escape **from** (a place/person); escape **to** (a destination)
- dream **of** (hope for the future) vs dream **about** (what you see in sleep)
- refer someone **to** – "the doctor referred me to a specialist"
- coincide **with** – happen at the same time
- suffer **from** – "suffers from amnesia"
- wink **at** someone
- **bring up** (phrasal) – introduce a topic – "don't bring up his divorce"
- stem **from** – be caused by, originate from
- consist **of** – be made up of
- apologize **to** a person **for** something
- argue **with** a person **about/for** something
- Note: phrasal verbs change meaning (grow up); verb + preposition keeps verb meaning (wait for).
- **quick on the draw** (idiom) – answer immediately, no hesitation

## 468. PREPOSITIONS that follow ADJECTIVES | Advanced Grammar

- convicted **of** (a crime)
- accustomed **to** – used to
- devoted **to** – committed to
- superior **to** – better than
- safe **from** – protected from danger
- engaged **in** – involved in an activity
- grateful **for** (= thankful for)
- devoid **of** – lacking – "devoid of laughter"
- concerned **about** – worried about
- acquainted **with** – familiar with
- addicted **to**; pleased **with**
- notorious **for** – known for something bad
- fed up **with** – tired of, won't take it anymore
- crowded **with**; eligible **for** – meets requirements for
- incapable **of** (capable of); comfortable **in** a seat
- keen **on** – eager to do; amazed **by**

## 469. PREPOSITION QUIZ | How advanced is your grammar?

- **in** the morning/afternoon/evening; **in** November, **on** a Thursday
- scarf **around** her neck
- learn **with** someone
- fed up **with** (annoyed/bored of) – three-word phrasal verb
- "Where are you?" – "I'm at the gym" (location point; "in" = inside)
- **on time** (when scheduled) vs **in time** (early enough, before deadline)
- **between** a rock and a hard place (idiom) – stuck in a difficult situation with no good option
- dream **of/about** hiking – "of" = hope for the future
- captivated **by** – held your attention
- better to ask **for** forgiveness than permission (saying)
- thinking **of** a number/animal (imagine to guess) vs thinking **about** (consider)

## 470. PRONUNCIATION & EMPHASIS LESSON | Interactive Quiz

- Syllable = single unit of sound with a vowel. coconut = 3; **idiosyncrasy** = 6; **scrumptiousness** = 3. Tip: hand under chin, count chin drops.
- **idiosyncrasy** (n) – behavior/trait peculiar to one person
- **scrumptious** (adj) – delicious
- Silent letters: **mechanic** (h, sounds /k/), **fasten** (t), **wreck** (w), **Wednesday** (d), **honest** (h), **knight** (k), **rendezvous** (z, s), **gnome** (g), **castle** (t)
- **Schwa** /ə/ – most common English sound, reduced unstressed vowel ("uh"): cel-e-brate (2nd syllable), pho-tog-ra-phy (1st and 3rd)
- Emphasis changes meaning of "I didn't say you stole the money": stress I (someone else said it), say (I implied it), you (someone else stole), money (stole something else); "This IS the last lesson" = emphasizes fact; stressing "the" (/ði/) = the one everyone knows. Practice by shadowing.
- Hard words: **thorough** /ˈθɜːroʊ/; **listened** /ˈlɪsənd/ (-ed adds a d sound, no extra syllable); **democracy** (schwa in 3rd syllable); **world** (hear the rl + d); **sixth** (x then th); **entrepreneur** (4 syllables, French loan); **conscientious** /ˌkɒnʃiˈenʃəs/ (4 syllables); **queue** = "Q"; **eerie** /ˈɪri/ (strange, creepy); **months** (slight th); **literally**; **hypocrisy** (4 syllables, schwa in 3rd); **massage** (schwa in 1st syllable)

## 471. PRONUNCIATION HACKS | Speak with the American accent 🇺🇸

- Hack 1 – Reductions in speech (not writing): gonna (going to), wanna (want to), hafta (have to); only reduce "going to" before a base verb ("I'm gonna play"), not a noun ("I'm going to work" = place).
- Hack 2 – Use the schwa: "a" /ə/, "you" /yə/, "to" /tə/ – "Do ya need ta go see a doctor?" Stress content words (need, go, see, doctor).
- Hack 3 – Flap T: /t/ sounds like soft d between vowels (water, city, beautiful), after r before a vowel (party, dirty), before dark l (bottle, little), and across words ("get in the car").
- Hack 4 – US vs UK word pronunciation: advertisement, vitamin, tomato, aluminum (UK: aluminium).
- Hack 5 – Rhythm and flow: speak in phrases with slight pauses, not word-for-word.
- You don't need an American accent; clarity matters most.

## 472. PUNS IN ENGLISH | Examples of a Play on Words

- **pun / play on words / wordplay** – humorous use of a word with several meanings or that sounds like another word
- Types: **homophonic** (sound the same, e.g. toad/towed: "Frog parking only, all others will be towed"); **homographic** (spelled the same, pronounced differently: "You can tune a guitar but you can't tuna fish, unless you play bass" – bass fish vs bass guitar); **homonymic** (same spelling and sound, different meaning: "I got fired" – bullet); **compound** (more than one pun); **recursive** (need first half to get second: "May the Fourth be with you" / force)
- **no pun intended / pardon the pun** – said after an accidental pun
- **over my dead body** (idiom) – completely against something
- **watchdog** (n) – guard dog; also a person/organization that monitors others
- **catch up (with someone)** (phrasal) – meet to talk and hear news; "nice to meet (meat) you ... catch up (ketchup)"
- **then it hit me** – I finally realized (or literally got hit)
- **bright** – giving light / intelligent
- **utter devastation** (collocation) – "otter devastation" headline
- Book to try: The Phantom Tollbooth (full of wordplay).

## 473. Phrasal Verb Quiz | Advanced Grammar & Vocabulary

- Phrasal verbs are hard: one verb + different particles = different meanings, and one phrasal verb has many meanings.
- **pick up** – learn (pick up English); tidy; collect someone by car; pay (pick up the bill)
- **turn in** – go to bed; submit (homework); turn yourself in (to police)
- **take after** – resemble a parent/relative in character – "She takes after her mother."
- **get over** – recover from/overcome (a breakup); **get over with** – finish something unpleasant; **get through** – survive, endure
- **hold on** – wait (on the phone); grip; "hold on for dear life"; survive/win narrowly
- **read through** – read from beginning to end (contracts, documents)
- **make up** – do work/time you missed; invent (excuses, stories); form a part (women make up half the workforce); "kiss and make up"
- **put off** – delay (separable: put it off); be put off by = dislike
- **go off** – alarm sounds; gun/bomb fires; wander away; power goes out
- **make out** – kiss passionately; understand (can't make it out); write (make a check out to); "made out like a bandit" – profited a lot

## 474. Phrasal Verbs Q&A with Interactive English

- Phrasal verb = verb + preposition/particle; meaning differs from the verb alone. Many have several meanings; can be separable or inseparable (intransitive ones, e.g. "get up", cannot be split).
- **look after** (pv) – take care of – "look after the kids"
- **get through** – endure a challenge/hardship – "just trying to get through this class"
- **go through** – look through/search or revise something – "go through a book"
- **finish up** – complete work/activity – "I have to finish up my work"
- **finish off** – complete the rest; eliminate – "finish off the water"
- **end up** – arrive somewhere / find yourself in a place (unplanned) – "we ended up at a dance club"
- **brush up (on)** – review a skill you already learned – "brush up on my English"
- **hurry up** – go faster, time is short – "we need to hurry up"
- **open up** – (store) open for business; (person) express feelings – "hard for him to open up"
- **lean back** – recline – "lean your seat back"
- **bail (someone) out** – rescue from a problem; pay bail to release from jail – "my friend bailed me out"
- **pass out** – faint / fall asleep suddenly (e.g. drunk) – "he passed out"
- **pass away** – die (gentle way) – "my grandmother passed away"
- **double down (on)** – repeat/refuse to change your position despite criticism – "he doubled down"
- **fight (something) off** – resist an illness – "fighting off a cold"
- **pitch in** – help out as part of a group – "everyone needs to pitch in"
- **call (someone) out (on)** – publicly point out a mistake/lie/hypocrisy – "I'm calling you out on that"
- **call out** – shout to someone – "call out to the dog"
- **buckle down** – get serious about work/study – "you need to buckle down"
- **doze off** – start falling asleep, nap briefly – "students dozing off in class"
- **run around** – go to many places doing errands/be busy – "running around all day"
- **come up with** – think of/create an idea or plan – "come up with new ideas"
- **use (something) up** – finish the rest of it (separable) – "let's use this up before buying more"
- Stress: usually on the verb; prepositions are function words and get less stress.
- "head back to" a place vs "head back for" + purpose (e.g. "for a rest").

## 475. Phrases Native Speakers Use ALL THE TIME | Binomial Pairs

- Binomial pair = two words joined by and/or; order usually fixed (on and off / off and on is flexible).
- **down and out** – going through a hard time, no luck/money – "I'm feeling down and out"
- **by leaps and bounds** – very quickly (progress) – "improve by leaps and bounds"
- **sink or swim** – no help; success/failure is up to you – "it's sink or swim now"
- **fair and square** – honestly, by the rules – "I won fair and square"
- **(leave someone) high and dry** – leave someone in a difficult position without help – "don't leave me high and dry"
- **out and about** – active, out doing normal things – "I'm out and about"
- **first and foremost** – most importantly – "I am first and foremost a teacher"
- **on and off / off and on** – happening some of the time, intermittently – "it rained off and on"
- **ins and outs** – detailed, complicated facts – "learn the ins and outs"
- **null and void** – no legal force/invalid – "the contract is null and void"
- **all or nothing** – completely or not at all – "it's all or nothing"
- **step by step** – gradually and carefully – "walk you through it step by step"

## 476. Phrases for Casual Conversation (improve your speaking fluency)

- **wine and dine (someone)** (v) – entertain/impress with good food and drink – "wine and dine clients"
- **be on the same page** – agree about what's happening/how things should be done – "we all need to be on the same page"
- **cut to the chase** – get to the point without wasting time (from film chase scenes)
- **face the music** – accept unpleasant consequences of what you did – "time to face the music"
- **a moot point** (n) /muːt/ – not worth discussing, irrelevant/debatable – "whether I'd be a good doctor is a moot point"
- **full of oneself** – thinks they're very important, annoying – "he's so full of himself"
- **social butterfly** – person who loves parties/social events
- **cover your tracks** – hide evidence of what you did – "I had to cover my tracks"
- **pay through the nose** – pay far too much – "paid through the nose for the ticket"

## 477. Phrases to Fine-Tune Your Speaking Fluency 🎶

Verb/noun/adjective + correct preposition:
- **take part in** – participate
- **sensitive to** – aware/responsive ("sensitive to the cold"); **sensitive about** – easily offended/worried
- **react to** something
- **in the background**
- **take care of** (someone/something) – collocation
- **up to you** – the choice is yours
- **influence over** someone – control; **influence on** someone – good/bad/positive/negative influence on; **under the influence of** – e.g. alcohol
- **at the national/state/local level**
- **captivated by**, amazed by, shocked by – passive + by
- **work for** (employer) vs **work on** (a project); "work in the city" = location

## 478. Phrases with IN, ON, and AT to Greatly Improve Your English Accuracy

IN:
- **interested in** (+ -ing) – "interested in joining"
- **experience in** – "experience in data analysis"
- **success in** – "success in business"
- **faith in** – confidence/trust in (also blind faith in, put your faith in, trust in, believe in)
- **change in** – "a change in the weather/schedule/diet"
- **expert in / expert on** – both OK; "in" stresses the person, "on" the subject

ON:
- **reliance on / rely on** – dependence
- **depend on** – "depends on teamwork"; "in a bind" = in a difficult situation
- **insistent on / insist on** (+ -ing)
- **decide on** – "decided on a new plan"

AT:
- **attempt at** (+ -ing) – "attempt at solving the puzzle" (verb: attempt to do)
- **skeptical at first** (fixed phrase); otherwise **skeptical of** – doubtful, not easily convinced
- **gaze at / look at / stare at**
- **surprised at** – "surprised at the price"
- **aim at** (+ -ing) – "aims at increasing market share"

## 479. Polite Ways to Express Sympathy in the Event of a Tragedy

- Small problems: "I hope things get better soon."
- Illness (only if recovery is likely): "I hope you feel better."
- Serious illness/loss/death (professional, safe): "I'm sorry for your loss."
- "You are in my thoughts." / "I am thinking of you." (add religious wording only if the person is religious)
- Death only: "Please accept my (heartfelt/sincere) condolences."
- Can combine several; match the expression to the severity and context.

- **cool** (adj) – impressive/likeable; **awesome** – very impressive
- **babe** (n) – loved one (partner); don't use with acquaintances
- **bae** (n) – "before anyone else"; romantic partner (younger slang)
- **dude** (n) – guy, young man
- **ripped** (adj) – very muscular; also intoxicated
- **wasted** (adj) – very drunk
- **lit** (adj) – awesome, exciting – "that movie is lit"

- **cram** (v) – study a lot in a short time – "cram for an exam"

## 481. Popular English Proverbs to Sound Like a Native Speaker

Proverb = well-known saying giving a general truth or advice.
- **When in Rome (do as the Romans do)** – follow local customs when visiting
- **A picture is worth a thousand words** – an image conveys more than words
- **Don't bite the hand that feeds you** – don't disrespect someone who helps you
- **Don't judge a book by its cover** – don't judge by appearance (binding = the spine holding pages)
- **The grass is always greener (on the other side of the fence)** – others' situations look better than yours
- **Two heads are better than one** – advice/help from another person is useful
- **Better safe than sorry** – be cautious rather than hasty and regret it
- **If at first you don't succeed, try, try again** – keep trying, don't give up

## 482. Popular Internet & Computer Symbols to Improve Your Vocabulary

- **.** – dot (not "period" in web/email)
- **@** – at
- **#** – hashtag
- **_** – underscore
- **/** – slash (forward slash / backslash \)
- **\*** – asterisk (notes/footnotes)
- **-** – hyphen (short, joins words, e.g. compound adjectives); **dash** – longer line for comments/emphasis
- **caps lock** – types all capitals
- **[ ]** – brackets; **( )** – parentheses

## 483. Practice & Improve your English! 👍

Channel promo only: Interactive English offers interactive lessons (participate, don't just watch). No teaching content.

## 484. Practice PREPOSITIONS in English | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Prepositions of time: **in** the morning / afternoon / evening; **in** + month (in November); **on** + day (on Thursday).
- **at** + place = the location (where are you? I'm at the gym). **in** = inside a place.
- **around** her neck (a scarf moves along the neck); **on** her neck is also acceptable.
- **on time** = at the scheduled time. **in time** = early enough, before a deadline.
- Many prepositions follow specific verbs, nouns or adjectives. Learn them by reading and listening more than by rules.

Expressions:
- **be fed up with** (phrasal verb) – annoyed or bored with something – "I'm fed up with it."
- **between a rock and a hard place** (expression) – stuck in a difficult situation with no good way out.
- **captivated by** (adj + prep) – holding your full attention – "The audience was captivated by the performance."
- **better to ask forgiveness than permission** (saying) – act first and apologize later, when a quick decision is needed.
- **dream of** – a hope or wish for the future (present tense); **dream about** – a dream while sleeping, or past.
- **think of** – imagine (a guessing game: "I'm thinking of a number"); **think about** – consider.

## 485. Present Perfect vs Present Perfect Continuous | When to use each so that you don't make mistakes

- Present perfect = have/has + past participle. Finished action, time unspecified. Focus: the **result**. "I have studied English."
- Present perfect continuous = have/has been + verb-ing. Unfinished action that started in the past and continues. Focus: the **action**. Signal words: recently, lately.
- Non-action (stative) verbs are not used in the continuous. Possession: "I've had an iphone for a long time" (not "I've been having").
- Permanent situation = present perfect: "I've worked for the company for 25 years."
- Temporary situation = present perfect continuous: "I've been working at home since the pandemic started."
- "I have thought about buying a new phone" = I decided not to. "I've been thinking about buying one" = I have not decided yet.
- With **for** (duration) or **since** (point in time), both forms mean the same thing: "I have studied English for three years" = "I've been studying English for three years."
- Work, play, live, teach and study are almost interchangeable in the two tenses. Only the permanent/temporary nuance may differ.

## 486. Present Simple vs Present Continuous | How to Improve Your Grammar

- Present simple: habits ("I watch TV at night"), general truths ("The earth is round"), repeated actions (every day, usually, always), unchanging situations ("He teaches English online"), and scheduled future ("The movie starts at 8:00 p.m.").
- Future in time clauses: use the future in the main clause and the present simple after before / after / when. "I'll turn off my phone before the movie starts."
- Present continuous = be + verb-ing: now or at the moment ("She is sleeping"), temporary situations ("I'm studying English this semester"), changing things ("Your English is improving"), and repeated situations with always ("They're always traveling").
- Future plans: be going to or present continuous ("I'm going to the dentist tomorrow"). Not for sudden decisions: "I'll get the door."
- Stative verbs (senses, opinions, emotions, mental states, measurement) are not used in the continuous. "I have a pencil" (no) but "I'm having a bad day" (yes). "He sees a spider" (no) but "He's seeing a therapist" (yes).
- "I'm loving it" is informal and not standard grammar. Avoid it in formal writing.
- Questions and negatives: present simple uses do/does + not ("She doesn't work"). Be needs no do: "Are you hungry?" / "I am not hungry."
- Contractions: "They aren't" = "They're not." Only subject pronouns contract with be (I'm, they're). "Wes isn't" works, but "Wes's not" does not.
- Pronunciation: drop the final "g" in -ing words ("talkin' way too loud"). Going to is often said as "gonna."

## 487. Professional Writing for Business, University, IELTS, TOEFL ✍️

- Know your purpose: narrative (tells a story), descriptive (captures an event), expository (gives facts), or persuasive (influences opinion).
- Know your audience. For formal writing, avoid idioms and slang. Idioms used: "the big picture" (the whole view of a situation) and "in a nutshell" (in the fewest possible words).
- Make a full outline with complete sentences. Cohesion = sentences in logical order. Unity = each paragraph centers on one idea.
- Find your voice (your own word and sentence choices). Keep one consistent tone (for example cheerful, sad, humorous). Do not switch tones.
- Paragraph structure: topic sentence, supporting sentences, conclusion.
- Use transition words: "in addition" (add), "however" (contrast), "first and foremost" (start).
- Vary sentence structures: simple, compound, complex, compound-complex. Varying them creates rhythm and flow.
- In a rough draft, keep it simple. Use vocabulary you already know, then refine it when you proofread.
- Proofread one error type per pass (punctuation, then spelling, then word choice). Use a spell or grammar checker.
- Get feedback from others, but you decide which changes to make. Read and write every day, for example in a journal.

## 488. Pronounce 25 Major US Cities Just Like an American 🇺🇸

- **Honolulu** – stress on 3rd syllable; schwa in 2nd.
- **Juneau** – stress on 1st syllable; final "-eau" = long o ("JOO-noh").
- **Seattle** – flap t (sounds like a soft d); final l not released.
- **Portland** – stop t; d is dropped.
- **San Francisco** – c sounds like s; stress on "SIS" (san fran-SIS-co).
- **Los Angeles** – g sounds like j; schwa in "-geles."
- **Boise** – s sounds like z ("BOY-zee") in standard US; locals may say s.
- **Phoenix** – ph = f; long e ("FEE-nix").
- **Tucson** – silent c ("TOO-son").
- **Albuquerque** – 4 syllables; q = k; stress on 1st; schwa in 2nd.
- **Sioux Falls** – pronounced "Soo"; i, o and x are silent.
- **Houston** – starts with an h + "yoo" sound ("HYOO-stun").
- **Des Moines** – stress on "MOIN"; first word has a schwa ("duh MOIN").
- **St. Louis** – "St." before a name = Saint.
- **New Orleans** – stress on "OR" (new OR-lee-uns).
- **Chicago** – ch sounds like sh; stress on 2nd syllable (shi-KAH-go).
- **Mobile** (Alabama) – stress on 2nd syllable, long e ("mo-BEEL"). Not the phone word.
- **Louisville** – 3 syllables; stress on 1st; 2nd and 3rd syllables use a schwa.
- **Cincinnati** – 4 syllables; stress on 3rd; both c's sound like s; final t is a flap.
- **Detroit** – stress on 2nd syllable; the first "e" sounds like "i."
- **Miami** – 3 syllables; stress on 2nd ("mee-AM-ee").
- **Pittsburgh** – stress on 1st; "gh" silent ("PITS-burg").
- **Raleigh** – "gh" silent; ends in long e ("RAW-lee").
- **Worcester** – 2 syllables; "rce" reduced to "ster."
- **New York** – no special point.

## 489. Pronounce the 50 US States Just Like an American 🇺🇸

(syllables; stressed syllable in CAPS)
- **Alabama** (4) al-uh-BAM-uh
- **Alaska** (3) uh-LAS-kuh
- **Arizona** (4) air-uh-ZOH-nuh
- **Arkansas** (3) AR-kun-saw – final syllable "saw", not "-sas"
- **California** (4) cal-uh-FOR-nyuh (informal: "Cali")
- **Colorado** (4) kah-luh-RAD-oh – "rad", not "rah"
- **Connecticut** (4) kuh-NET-ih-kut – spelling misleads
- **Delaware** (3) DEL-uh-wair
- **Florida** (3) FLOR-ih-duh
- **Georgia** (2) JOR-juh
- **Hawaii** (3) huh-WY-ee
- **Idaho** (3) EYE-duh-hoh
- **Illinois** (3) il-uh-NOY – final "s" silent
- **Indiana** (4) in-dee-AN-uh
- **Iowa** (3) EYE-uh-wuh – 2nd syllable schwa
- **Kansas** (2) KAN-zus
- **Kentucky** (3) kun-TUCK-ee
- **Louisiana** (5) loo-ee-zee-AN-uh – stress on 4th
- **Maine** (1) MAYN
- **Maryland** (3) MER-uh-lund – not "Mary-land"
- **Massachusetts** (4) mass-uh-CHOO-sits
- **Michigan** (3) MISH-ih-gun
- **Minnesota** (4) min-uh-SOH-tuh
- **Mississippi** (4) mis-ih-SIP-ee
- **Missouri** (3) mih-ZOOR-ee – "s" sounds like z
- **Montana** (3) mon-TAN-uh
- **Nebraska** (3) nuh-BRAS-kuh
- **Nevada** (3) nuh-VAD-uh
- **New Hampshire** (3) new HAMP-shur – "-shire" = "sher", not "shire"
- **New Jersey** (3) new JER-zee
- **New Mexico** (4) new MEK-sih-koh
- **New York** (2) new YORK
- **North Carolina** (5) north kair-uh-LY-nuh
- **North Dakota** (4) north duh-KOH-tuh
- **Ohio** (3) oh-HY-oh
- **Oklahoma** (4) oh-kluh-HOH-muh
- **Oregon** (3) OR-ih-gun – unstressed end sounds like "gun"
- **Pennsylvania** (4) pen-sul-VAYN-yuh
- **Rhode Island** (3) rohd EYE-lund – not an actual island
- **South Carolina** (5) south kair-uh-LY-nuh
- **South Dakota** (4) south duh-KOH-tuh
- **Tennessee** (3) ten-uh-SEE
- **Texas** (2) TEK-sus
- **Utah** (2) YOO-tah
- **Vermont** (2) vur-MONT
- **Virginia** (3) vur-JIN-yuh
- **Washington** (3) WASH-ing-tun (not the district, Washington DC)
- **West Virginia** (4) west vur-JIN-yuh – final "t" in "west" almost silent
- **Wisconsin** (3) wis-KON-sin
- **Wyoming** (3) wy-OH-ming

## 490. Pronounce these Famous Brands with the American Accent 🇺🇸

- **Levi's** /ˈliːvaɪz/ – both long vowels, not "levees"
- **Bose** /boʊz/ – final s = z
- **In-N-Out Burger** – linking: "i-nuh-nout burger"
- **Nike** /ˈnaɪki/ – 2 syllables
- **Costco** – elision: t dropped between consonants ("cos-co")
- **Old Navy** – elision: d dropped ("ol-navy")
- **Cinnamon Toast Crunch** – CIN-uh-mun (schwas); t in "toast" dropped
- **Ben & Jerry's** – "and" reduced to "n" and linked: "ben-n-jerry's"
- **Black & Decker** – "and" reduced to "n"
- **Reese's Pieces** – both end in "-iz" (tongue twister)
- **Doritos** – flap t (soft d): "doh-REE-doh-s"
- **Cheetos** – flap t
- **Tostitos** – 2nd t is a flap t
- **Gatorade** – flap t, 2nd syllable schwa: GAY-duh-rayd
- **Caterpillar** – flap t, 2nd syllable schwa
- **Tylenol** – y = long i: TY-luh-nol
- **Benadryl** – y = short i: BEN-uh-dril
- **Whirlpool** – two dark l's; tongue rises twice
- **Charmin** – "ch" sounds like "sh": SHAR-min
- **Listerine** – ends in long "-een", not "-rine"

## 491. Pronunciation English Lesson | Q&A w/ Interactive English

- **minimal pair** – two words differing by one sound; practice resource: English Club minimal pairs
- **adapt / adopt** – differ only in vowel: adapt /æ/ (jaw drops, like "hack"), adopt /ɑ/ (like "ah" at the doctor)
- **sew** /soʊ/ (verb, with needle) vs **sewage / sewer** /ˈsuː-/ ("sue") – waste / the underground pipe
- **regard** – ends in soft unaspirated d; verb "I regard X as…" (consider); "hold someone in high regard"; "no regard for"; "in regard to" (about)
- **flowers** – final s = z (voiced r before s)
- **affect / effect** – homonyms in speech (schwa start); affect = verb, effect = noun
- **flower / flour** – homonyms
- **thaw, law, saw, draw** – end in "aw"; the w is not heard
- **pessimism** – 4 syllables
- **career** – 2 syllables (kuh-REER) vs **courier** – 3 syllables
- **which / witch** – homonyms ("two witches watching two watches" tongue twister)
- **ear / year** – only the initial y differs
- **did you** – reduces to "didja"; "what'd you do yesterday?"
- **endure** (2 syl) vs **endeavor** (3 syl)
- **moon / soon** /uː/ vs **look / book** /ʊ/ – spelling "oo" is inconsistent
- **major / mayor** – j vs y sound
- **entrepreneurial** (adj) – likes starting businesses
- **veterinarian** – 6 syllables; flap t sounds like d (as in "water")
- **flabbergasted** – "-ed" adds an extra syllable (-id)
- **government** – the "n" is mostly not heard
- **Antarctica** – the first "c" is pronounced
- **culture** – "-ture" = /ɚ/ sound
- **almond** – 2 syllables, "-und" ending
- **complement** (go well together) / **compliment** (praise) – homonyms
- **you're / your** – homonyms
- **laboratory** – stress on 1st syllable; 2nd syllable schwa
- **paraphernalia** – schwa-heavy; items/equipment, often associated with drugs
- **trousers** – s = z
- **entourage**, **reservoir** – French borrowings; reservoir s = z
- **psychic** – silent p
- Tip: watch TV/movies with subtitles to see spelling vs pronunciation differences; use Cambridge online dictionary speaker button

## 492. Pronunciation Q&A Lesson | You ask and I'll tell you how to say it 🗣️

- **quarantine** (3) KWOR-un-teen – stress 1st, schwa 2nd
- **agnostic** (3) ag-NOS-tik – stress 2nd
- **unwittingly** (adv, 4) un-WIT-ing-lee – stress "wit"; flap t
- **plutocracy** (4) ploo-TOK-ruh-see – stress 2nd; government by the wealthy
- **aisle** (1) – silent s: "ayl"
- **candidacy** (4) KAN-dih-duh-see – stress 1st
- **mountain** – reduced: "moun'n" (t barely heard)
- **gnawed** – silent g; means chewed
- **castle** – silent t: "KAS-ul"
- **horizon** (3) huh-RY-zun – stress 2nd
- **comfortable** – reduced: "KUMF-ter-bul"
- **shouldn't have / wouldn't have** – "have" reduces to "of": "shouldn't-uv"
- **hoarding** – collecting too much (e.g., supplies)
- **hostel** (place to stay, "HOS-tl") vs **hostile** (aggressive; US often same as hostel; UK "HOS-tyl")
- **development** (4) dih-VEL-up-ment – stress 2nd
- **debt** (short e) vs **date** (long a)
- **disciplinary** (adj) DIS-uh-plin-air-ee – stress 1st
- **while** vs **a while** – add schwa before "while"
- **entertainment** – often reduced: "enter-tainment"
- **crew** (ooh sound; group) vs **crow** (long o; bird)
- **risk** (k) vs **wrist** (silent w, t at end)
- **uncle** (UNG-kul) vs **ankle** (ANG-kul)
- **ball** vs **bowl** (short a/aw vs long o)
- **word** vs **world** – tricky "rl"; tongue barely touches roof
- **feature** (long ee) vs **future** (yoo sound)
- **rural** (2) ROR-ul
- **party** – flap t sounds like d (US); UK keeps t
- **corona** – schwa on 1st and 3rd syllables: kuh-ROH-nuh
- **torture** (2) TOR-chur
- **unprecedented** (adj, 5) un-PRES-ih-den-tid – stress 2nd
- **pear** (strong puff of air) vs **bear** (softer b)
- **metal / medal** – homonyms in US (flap t)
- **manipulate** (verb, 4) muh-NIP-yuh-layt – stress 2nd
- Tips: dictionary.com, Cambridge dictionary, Google "how to pronounce X" and record yourself; shadow native speakers; count syllables by chin touching your hand; get more listening exposure

## 493. Q&A About Learning English, Life Updates, and More

Gist: Casual hangout Q&A after a short break (new baby); answers questions on learning English.
- **like + infinitive or gerund** – both allowed: "I like to play / I like playing"
- **prepositions** (to/for) – learn by reading a lot; "look forward to", "responsible for"
- **desert** (noun, /ˈdezərt/) vs **desert** (verb, abandon, /dɪˈzɜːrt/) vs **dessert** (sweet after a meal)
- **whose name is John** – adjective clause: "I know someone whose name is John"
- **play it by ear** (idiom) – decide as things happen, stay flexible
- **pretty much / pretty well** – used in both US and UK
- **flap t** – t between vowels sounds like d: "water", "better"; "button, curtain, mountain" – t barely heard
- **in a pickle** (idiom) – in a troubling situation
- **I'd love to** – polite acceptance; "No, thank you" – polite decline
- **turn up out of the blue** – appear unexpectedly
- **kick it off / get the show on the road** (idioms) – begin
- **oh my gosh / OMG** – interjection of surprise
- **how do you feel vs what do you feel like doing** – feeling/opinion vs wanting to do
- Speaking tip: don't fear mistakes; build vocabulary so you can say what you want
- Listening tip: use YouTube speed controls; practice at your level
- Why English is international: British Empire history + US influence (movies, music, TV)
- Learning other languages keeps the mind sharp

## 494. Q&A Live-Lesson with Interactive English | You ask, I'll answer...

- **even though** (true fact) vs **even if** (hypothetical) – "play even though it's raining" / "play even if it rains"
- **when** (certain) vs **if** (uncertain) – "We'll eat when she arrives" / "…if she arrives"
- **speak for yourself** – used at start of sentence to say "I don't agree; don't include me in your opinion"
- **chunking** – learn whole phrases ("What are you doing?", "I'm reading") instead of grammar rules; builds fluency
- **no longer** (between subject and verb: "I no longer play") vs **not…anymore** (end of sentence: "I don't play anymore")
- **promise** (general) vs **pledge** (more serious/formal, e.g., oath)
- Instruments: US often drops "the" ("I play guitar"); UK says "the guitar"
- **entire / whole** – interchangeable; "entire" adds emphasis
- **roll up** (phrasal) – fold up sleeves; or arrive ("we rolled up to the house")
- **come by / drop by** – visit; drop by = shorter visit
- **roller coaster** – amusement ride; also fast, up-and-down experience
- **wet your whistle** (idiom) – have a drink / get a taste of something
- **get through** – survive something difficult
- **tight** (slang) – awesome
- **tickle** – "it tickles me" = amuses/pleases me
- **bloody** (UK) – intensifier meaning "very" ("bloody difficult")
- **blasphemy** (noun) – disrespectful speech about something sacred or taboo
- **bear with me** – be patient, wait a moment
- **-ed vs -ing adjectives** – "I'm overwhelmed" (feeling) vs "the work is overwhelming" (cause)
- **wicked** – evil; New England slang for "great"
- **brick by brick** – prepositions learned one at a time, slowly
- Prepositions: best learned through reading/listening and by category (time, place)

## 495. QUANTIFIERS for Non-count Food Nouns 🍞🍕🍷| Grammar Quiz

Quantifier + "of" + non-count food noun; choose by context.
- **put on a pot of coffee** (start brewing); also **cup / mug of coffee**
- **wedge of cheese** (triangle; what you buy); **slice of cheese** (thin, for sandwiches); **wheel of cheese** (very large)
- **pack of sugar** (cafe packet); **bag / box of sugar** (grocery)
- **bag of flour** (stored in cabinet); **sack of flour** (large)
- **pitcher of lemonade**
- **carton of milk** (also eggs)
- **stick of butter**
- **grain of rice / salt / sand**
- **plate of cookies** (cookies are count nouns, but plate quantifies the whole)
- **pack of gum** (store); **piece / stick of gum** (individual)
- **spoonful of cereal / honey / medicine**
- **carafe of wine** – small glass jug, more than a glass, less than a bottle; (also **glass / bottle of wine**)
- **cut of steak** – type of steak by how/where it's cut (ribeye, New York strip, porterhouse, filet mignon); **piece of steak** also OK

## 496. READING + VOCABULARY LESSON | Immigration in the US 🇺🇸

- **immigrant** (n) – person who moves to a country to live permanently; **immigrate** (v) *to* a country; **immigration** (n)
- **emigrate** (v) – leave a country; emigrate *from*
- **burden** (n) – heavy load, something hard to carry
- **adopt** (v) – start to accept/use something new (a custom); **adapt** (v) – change to become comfortable in a new situation
- **migrant** (n) – person who moves temporarily, usually for work
- **substantial** (adj) – large in size, value, or importance; "a substantial amount"
- **paradigm** (n) – model for how something is done
- **humane** (adj) – showing kindness, care, sympathy
- **detention** (n) / **detain** (v) – keeping someone in one place (detention center)
- **deportation** (n) / **deport** (v) – forcing someone to leave a country
- **unprecedented** (adj) – never happened before
- **undocumented** (adj) – without legal papers; "undocumented immigrant"
- **higher learning** – college or university level
- **empower** (v) – give someone authority/freedom to do something
- Gist: Pew survey shows most major host countries view immigrants as a strength; article critiques US deportation-heavy enforcement; Freedom University (Georgia) offers free classes to undocumented students (fundraiser via Praxis Project).

## 497. READING QUIZ 📚 Learn Vocabulary & Answer Questions about the Cheetah

- **characterized** (adj/v) – distinguished by certain features or qualities
- **slender** (adj) – thin and fit
- **contrast** (n) – difference between things; "in contrast to"
- **uniformly** (adv) – the same throughout; adjective: uniform
- Cheetah facts: small rounded head, spotted tail and coat, deep chest, slender body, black streaks on face; taller than a leopard, much smaller than a lion; weighs 21-72 kg.
- Tip: numbers are often key details in reading questions.

## 498. READING YOUR POSITIVE COMMENTS | Share the Love 💖

Gist: Hosts read positive viewer comments and thank viewers; encourage participating in comments (practice) and keeping a positive tone.
- **a hell of a (video / job / party)** – informal intensifier meaning "a great/remarkable ___"
- **fella** – informal for "guy"
- **bear hug** – a big, tight hug
- **brighten up my day** – make my day happier
- **thanks a million / thanks a bunch** – informal "thank you very much"
- **stick up for** – defend

## 499. READ FASTER IN ENGLISH | Become a Speed Reader 📚

- Why: learners read slower than natives; faster reading means more reading, which improves grammar, vocabulary, writing; essential for tests (TOEFL/IELTS reading sections).
- Speed reading = read fast AND understand/remember; fast without comprehension is pointless.
- For: English language learners (ELLs), students, test takers, anyone who reads.
- Test yourself: freereadingtest.com (choose theme, level 1-13, story; gives speed in words per minute + comprehension score); retest later at new levels.
- Online practice: Zap Reader and Spreeder (paste any text, shows one word at a time at set speed); 10-15 min daily.
- Text sources by level: Simple Wikipedia (beginner) -> regular Wikipedia (intermediate) -> National Geographic, The Atlantic, The Economist (advanced).
- ESL-specific: Interlink language centers speed-reading exercises (sentence-by-sentence, spelling/grammar focus).
- App: Spreeder app – set words per minute, big text library, progress reports; any app with adjustable speed, texts, and progress tracking works.
- Paper method: move index finger under each word left to right, read only that word, time 1 minute, count words, log progress; don't go back.
- Tips: practice daily (10-20 min beats occasional marathons); time and test yourself often; start easy, then move up to harder texts; read topics you like; search "ESL speed reading exercises"; use Interactive English scrolling reading lessons with comprehension questions.

## 500. ROMANTIC PHRASAL VERBS FOR LOVE & RELATIONSHIPS 💖

Rules: transitive phrasal verbs (take an object) may be separable ("take the trash out / take out the trash"); intransitive ones are never separable ("wake up"); some transitive ones are inseparable ("get on the plane"). Learn phrasal verbs in context/sentences, by category or by verb; review after 1 day, 3 days, 1 week, 2 weeks, etc.
- **make up** (with someone) – reconcile after a fight – "After some time apart they decided to make up."
- **check out** (someone) (separable) – look at someone attractive – "Check out the cute soccer player." / "Check her out."
- **settle down** – start a quiet, stable life in one place, often with a partner – "One day I'll want to settle down and have a family."
- **move in (with)** – start living with someone – "They'll move in together in December."
- **move out** – stop living with someone/in a place
- **move on** (inseparable) – get over a breakup and accept new experiences – "He's found it difficult to move on since the break-up."
- **drift apart** (inseparable) – gradually become less close – "They started to drift apart when he moved to London."
- **fall for** (someone) (inseparable) – fall completely in love/infatuated – "He fell for her when he met her."
- **lead on** (someone) (separable) – mislead someone into thinking you're romantically interested – "It's not right to lead on your date."
- **flirt with** – behave playfully romantic toward someone – "She flirts with her colleagues."
- **ask out** (someone) – invite on a date – "He will ask her out on a date."

## 501. Reading + Vocabulary Lesson | EARTHQUAKES 😟

- **magnitude** (n) – measure of how strong/violent an earthquake is – "a magnitude 7.2 earthquake"
- **strike / struck** (v) – to hit suddenly and cause severe suffering – "The quake struck Haiti."
- **widespread** (adj) – existing or happening in many places/among many people – "widespread destruction"
- **death toll** (n phrase) – number of people killed by an event (war, accident, disaster) – "The death toll has risen."
- **search and rescue** (n phrase) – searching for and helping people in danger – "search and rescue teams"
- **collapse** (v) – fall suddenly from pressure or lack of support – "Buildings collapsed."
- **rubble** (n) – piles of broken stones/bricks left when a building falls – "survivors trapped in rubble"
- **quake** (n) – short form of earthquake
- **epicenter** (n) – point on the earth's surface directly above where an earthquake starts – "Where was the epicenter?"
- **cave in** (phrasal v) – roof/ceiling/structure breaks and falls inward – "The church caved in."
- **take matters into your own hands** (idiom) – deal with a problem yourself because those responsible failed to – "Locals took matters into their own hands."
- **tremor** (n) – a slight earthquake, often aftershock – "New tremors hit the city."
- **reeling** (adj) – very confused, shocked, unable to act (often of a community) – "still reeling from the quake"
- **devastating** (adj) – causing great damage/destruction – "a devastating earthquake"
- **devastated** (adj) – describes how someone feels (shocked/crushed) – "I was devastated."

## 502. Reading + Vocabulary Lesson | HELP FIGHT HUNGER

- **famine** (n) – situation where there is not enough food for a great number of people – "an impending famine"
- **starvation** (n) – state of having no food for a long period – "in danger of starvation"
- **impending** (adj) – about to happen, imminent – "impending famine"
- **shortage** (n) – not enough of something – "a food shortage"
- **on the brink of** (phrase) – at the point where something (bad) is about to begin – "on the brink of starvation"
- **loom** (v) – appear large and frightening/threatening – "Famine is looming."
- **crisis** (n) – a very bad situation – "the hunger crisis"
- **remote** (adj) – far from populated areas, isolated – "a remote camp"
- **gnaw (away at)** (v) – bite/chew repeatedly; figuratively, bother you constantly – "gnawing hunger"; "guilt gnawing away at me"
- **dire** (adj) – very serious/extreme – "dire warnings"
- **surge** (v) – increase suddenly and strongly – "surging malnutrition"
- **malnutrition** (n) – bad health/weakness from lack of food
- **humanitarian** (adj) – involved in improving lives/reducing suffering – "humanitarian office"
- **food insecurity** (n phrase) – not always being able to get enough food (some food now, unsure about later)
- **completely overwhelmed** (collocation) – too much to deal with – "food banks were completely overwhelmed"
- **influx** (n) – large amount arriving at the same time; "an influx of need"
- **keep up (with)** (phrasal v) – cope with something changing fast – "unable to keep up with demand"
- **devastating** (adj) – causing a lot of damage – "devastating impact"
- **fallout** (n) – unpleasant results of something – "the economic fallout"
- **grassroots** (adj) – at the local level, ordinary people – "grassroots initiatives"
- **nourish** (v) – provide people with food to keep them healthy (nurture = also care for/protect)

## 503. Reading + Vocabulary Lesson | Mental Health ❤️

- **keep a watchful eye on** (phrase) – pay close attention to someone – "keep a watchful eye on kids"
- **watchful** (adj) – closely watching/alert
- **prevent** (v) – stop something from happening – "prevent suicide"
- **suicide** (n) – intentionally killing oneself; verb pairing: "commit suicide"
- **suicidal** (adj) – having thoughts of suicide
- **ideation** (n) – forming ideas in the mind – "suicidal ideation"
- **isolation** (n) – being alone – "social isolation"
- **autism** (n) – brain condition affecting social/communication skills development
- **depression** (n) – state of being very unhappy and without hope; "suffer from depression"
- **anxiety** (n) – nervousness or worry; **anxious** (adj)
- **counselor** (n) – trained person who listens and gives advice; **counsel** (v)
- **treatment** (n) – use of drugs/exercise/therapy to improve an ill or injured person's condition
- **suffer** (v) – experience physical or mental pain – "suffer from mental health issues"
- **well-being** (n) – state of happiness/health
