- **stigma** (n) – strong disapproval most people in society have about something – "the stigma of mental illness"
- **therapy** (n) – treatment that helps someone feel better; verb: "get therapy"
- **therapist** (n) – professional who treats mental/physical illness
- **polarizing** (adj) – very divisive – "a polarizing election"
- **adolescent** (n) – young person/teenager
- **burnout** (n) – exhaustion from overwork, can't continue
- **symptom** (n) – sign that something is wrong (illness) – "symptoms of burnout"
- **patient** (n) – person receiving medical care
- **cope (with)** (v) – deal successfully with a situation – "cope with stress"
- **trauma** (n) – severe emotional shock caused by an upsetting experience

## 504. Reading + Vocabulary Lesson | Protecting the Environment 🌳

- **marine** (adj) – related to the sea – "a marine protected area"
- **sanctuary** (n) – safe protected place for wildlife – "marine sanctuary"
- **archipelago** (n) – group of small islands
- **remote** (adj) – far from any populated area
- **conservation** (n) – protection of plants, animals and natural areas, esp. from humans; **conserve** (v) = save/use sparingly (resources); **preserve** (v) = keep as it is
- **threatened** (adj) – at risk of harm or damage – "threatened species"
- **rebuild** (v) – build again (re- = again) – "rebuild habitats"
- **habitat** (n) – natural environment where an animal/plant lives
- **native** (adj) – growing/living naturally in one place – "native animals"
- **sequester** (v) – store a substance (e.g. carbon) safely – "sequester carbon"
- **Mother Nature** (n phrase) – nature
- **crisis** (n) – time of great suffering/difficulty – "climate crisis"
- **proliferate** (v) – increase a lot and suddenly – "tree-planting initiatives proliferate"
- **festoon** (v) – decorate (not environmental; "festooning the earth with trees")
- **deforestation** (n) – cutting down trees over a large area for other land use
- **destruction** (n) / **destroy** (v) – act of destroying; causing damage so it can't be used
- **raze** (v) – destroy completely (advanced)
- **wipe out** (phrasal v) – destroy completely – "wiped out 8% of the Amazon"
- **vital** (adj) – extremely important
- **curb** (v) – limit something unwanted – "curb climate change"

## 505. Reading + Vocabulary Lesson | Vaccines 💉

- **herd immunity** (n phrase) – disease can't spread because enough people are immune
- **eligible** (adj) – having the necessary qualities/meeting the conditions – "eligible for a vaccine"
- **administer** (v) – give (a shot) to someone; patient *receives*, nurse *administers*
- **pandemic** (n) – disease existing across almost a whole area – "global pandemic"
- **spread** (v) – affect more and more people – "the virus is spreading"
- **inoculate** (v) – give someone a weak form of a disease (usually by injection) to protect them
- **vulnerable** (adj) – easily physically/emotionally/mentally hurt – "vulnerable people"
- **vaccine** (n) / **vaccinate** (v) / **vaccination** (n)
- **dose** (n) – measured amount of medicine – "spare doses"
- **variant** (n) – something slightly different from similar things – "coronavirus variant"
- **anti-vaxxer** (n) – person opposed to vaccinating people (anti- = against)
- **rollout** (n) / **roll out** (phrasal v) – making something available for the first time
- **belatedly** (adv) – late (cf. "belated birthday")
- **shortage (of)** (n) – not enough of something
- **strained** (adj) – difficult/challenging because problems keep intensifying – "strained healthcare system"

## 506. Reading Mean and Nasty Comments... And Laughing at Them 😜

Gist: Wes and Ilana read funny hater comments as a teachable moment and ask viewers to post positive comments. Vocab:
- **troll** (n) – person who says mean things online just to provoke a reaction
- **prick** (n, slang) – jerk; always negative
- **condescending** (adj) – talking down to someone as if you're better than them
- **air quotes** – when someone uses quotes around a word, it implies the opposite
- **hater / hate on** – someone who is mean/negative for no reason
- **waffle** (v) – go back and forth without making a point
- **rubbish** (n, British) – trash; terrible
- **overact** (v) – act too dramatically
- **funny-looking** (adj) – odd-looking; not a compliment (say "funny-looking", not "looking funny")
- **a buck** (slang) – a dollar

## 507. Reported Speech vs Direct Speech | Advanced Grammar Lesson

- Direct speech = exact words in quotes; reported (indirect) speech = describing what someone said without exact words.
- Backshift of verbs: present simple -> past simple ("I go for a run" -> she said she went for a run).
- present continuous -> past continuous ("we're taking a vacation" -> they said they were taking a vacation).
- past simple -> past perfect ("I met my friend" -> he said he had met his friend).
- present perfect -> past perfect ("I have witnessed a crime" -> she explained she had witnessed a crime).
- will -> would; can -> could.
- "that" after the reporting verb is optional ("she said (that) she went"); usually omitted with common verbs like say/know.
- Statements: use said + (that) clause. Yes/no questions: use asked + if/whether ("Are you tired?" -> she asked if he was tired). Wh-questions: keep the wh-word ("Who is tired?" -> she asked who was tired).
- Commands: tell + object + to + infinitive ("Go to bed" -> she told him to go to bed). You *say* something; you *tell* someone.
- In casual speech people often keep the original tense (she said she goes for a run), but grammar books require backshift.
- **proclaim** (v) – state something in an excited/proud way (collocation: "proudly proclaimed")

## 508. Rhythm & Flow in English  | Thought Groups, Word Stress, & Connected Speech

- **Thought groups**: short pauses break long sentences into meaningful chunks (often at commas, but not always); each group usually ends with a stressed word.
- **English is stress-timed**: stressed syllables/words come at roughly regular intervals; words between them are shortened/reduced to fit the rhythm.
- **Linking** (connected speech): final consonant sound of one word blends into a following vowel sound – "when all" -> "whe-nall"; "not a" -> "nada" (flap T sounds like soft D); "not even a" -> "nada-ven-a"; "snug in" -> "snu-gin".
- Words in different thought groups are usually not linked, depending on speed.
- Practice: read poems/songs/speeches aloud; **shadowing** (copy a speaker exactly) lets your ears do the work.
- Vocab (poem "A Visit from St. Nicholas"): **'twas** (old contraction of "it was"; not for speech), **stirring** (moving slightly), **stocking** (large sock hung by the fireplace at Christmas), **chimney** (vertical pipe letting smoke escape), **St Nicholas** (Santa Claus), **nestled** (settled comfortably), **snug** (comfortable, warm, cozy), **sugar plum** (small round sugary candy).

## 509. Riddles in English | Improve Your Comprehension

Gist: riddle practice (puzzle question with a clever answer). Takeaways and vocab:
- **riddle** (n) – a question describing something in a difficult/confusing way with a clever or funny answer
- Answers: J, A (months' first letters); a cold ("catch a cold"; get a disease); not raining (umbrella); a promise/heart/silence ("break a promise / someone's heart / silence"); the match; short -> shorter; your age; a bottle (neck, no head); teapot; 30 cows and "28 (20-ate) chickens" -> 10 (homonyms eight/ate); St. Ives riddle -> one (only the narrator).
- **collocation** (n) – words frequently used together (catch a cold, break a promise)
- **bottleneck** (n) – problem that delays a process or stops it continuing
- **homonym** (n) – words with same pronunciation but different spelling/meaning
- Tip: the simplest answer is often correct; read literally.

## 510. SAY vs TELL | Confusing Words in English

- **say** – used when you don't need to name the listener; general info/opinion/instructions: "The weather report says it'll rain." "Instructions say..." Say something (to someone).
- **tell** – usually followed by a person (direct object): "She told me to sit." "I told you not to wake me up." Tell someone something.
- **tell exceptions (no person needed)**: tell a secret, a lie, the truth, a joke, a story.
- Quiz answers: tell a lie; say your answer; didn't say hello; what did you tell me; be careful what you say; tell the truth; say the first thing; don't say his name; what did you tell her; say something.

## 511. SENTENCES in English | Everything you need to know about TYPE & STRUCTURE

- **Sentence types (by purpose)**:
  - Declarative: statement giving information, ends with period ("I play basketball in the morning."); most common.
  - Interrogative: question, ends with ?, rising intonation ("Did everyone answer the question?").
  - Imperative: command/instruction/request/advice, ends with . or ! ("Preheat the oven." "Please hit the like button.").
  - Exclamatory: strong emotion, ends with ! ("That's great news!").
- Knowing types helps reading aloud/in your head, giving natural rhythm and intonation.
- **Independent clause** = complete thought, can stand alone. **Dependent clause** = incomplete, starts with a subordinating conjunction (after, before, while, when, because).
- **Sentence structures**:
  - Simple: one independent clause (subject + predicate) – "She watches the movie."
  - Compound: two independent clauses joined by a conjunction (for, and, but, or, so) – "He plays basketball but he doesn't play football."
  - Complex: one independent + at least one dependent clause – "Before I go to sleep, I like to read in bed."
  - Compound-complex: two independent + at least one dependent – "You left after ten minutes, but then you came back."
- Comma rule: dependent clause first -> comma ("Before I go to sleep, I read"); dependent clause second -> no comma ("I read before I go to sleep").
- Why it matters: types help reading/speaking; structures help writing – use a variety for engaging prose.
- Examples: "Go wash your hands or you can't eat" = imperative + compound; "What are you going to do after this lesson?" = interrogative + complex; "You did a great job today." = declarative + simple.

## 512. SEXUAL INNUENDOS 💋 How to understand these suggestive remarks....

Gist: an innuendo is a remark that suggests something sexual (or unpleasant) without saying it directly. Spot it by word choice, intonation, emphasis, and stock phrases; it is common in TV, movies (even kids' films), and casual talk.
- **innuendo** (n) – indirect suggestive remark – "The ad's innuendo was obvious."
- Suggestive words: hit, pound, smack (act); big, hard, wet (body); **naughty**, bad (behavior – calling an adult "naughty" can sound sexual).
- **if you know what I mean** – tag added after a suggestive remark.
- **Are you just happy to see me?** – joke about a man's arousal.
- **Do you think he's compensating for something?** – joke about someone's flashy car/castle = small genitals.
- **flash** (v) – briefly show body parts (e.g., breasts) – "She flashed him."
- **please / satisfied / exhausted** – everyday words that read as sexual in context.

## 513. SHOPPING PHRASAL VERBS TO IMPROVE FLUENCY 🛍

Rule: transitive phrasal verbs may be separable (take the trash out / take out the trash); intransitive ones are never separable (wake up). Learn them by listening.
- **stress out** (sep) – worry/feel anxious – "Crowded malls stress me out."
- **stand out** (inseparable) – be distinctive – "These red rain boots stand out."
- **wear in** (sep) – wear shoes until comfortable – "She'll wear in her heels before the wedding."
- **look for / look out for** (inseparable) – search for – "I'm looking out for a maternity swimsuit."
- **go with** (inseparable) – match/harmonize – "Do these shoes go with my dress?"
- **fork out / fork over** – pay a lot of money unwillingly – "I don't want to fork out $1,000 on a phone."
- **bring in** – generate money/profit – "Amazon brings in a lot of money."
- **pay for** – make a payment for – "We need cash to pay for our meal."
- **line up / queue up** – wait in line (line up = US, queue up = UK) – "People line up at 3 a.m. on Black Friday."
- **ring up** (sep) – record a sale on the register – "The cashier rang me up."

## 514. SHOULD HAVE | COULD HAVE  | WOULD HAVE - Modals of Lost Opportunity

- Form: modal + have + past participle (should have played / could have broken / would have bought).
- Pronunciation: reduces to "should of / coulda / woulda" (h dropped, schwa).
- **should have** – regret; something didn't happen but you wish it had – "I should have subscribed." Negative = regret about a wrong act – "I should not have yelled."
- **could have** – past possibility that didn't happen – "I could have stayed in bed." Negative = impossible – "I could not have stayed in bed."
- **would have** – imagined different result, often 3rd conditional – "The team would have won if their best player hadn't been injured." Could have = possibility; would have = certainty.
- **would not have** – "I would not have finished without your help."
- **shoulda, coulda, woulda** (idiom) – dismissing someone's regret/excuse; unsympathetic – "You can't change the past."

## 515. SLANG from All Over the United States 🇺🇸

- **bum** (v) – borrow – "Can I bum a cigarette?"
- **wicked** (adv) – very (Boston) – "He's wicked smart."
- **June gloom** – cool, overcast weather in California in early June (May gray in May).
- **bufflehead** (n) – idiot (Pennsylvania) – "That guy's a bufflehead."
- **the industry** – film/TV entertainment business (LA) – "Are you in the industry?"
- **snowbird** (n) – northerner who winters in warm places like Florida.
- **the bomb** – the best – "That movie's the bomb."
- **janky** (adj) – in poor condition – "That janky old car won't start."
- **heavy** (adj) – serious/sad (California) – "This is a heavy situation."
- **sketchy** (adj) – unsafe/untrustworthy – "a sketchy neighborhood."
- **bail** (v) – leave – "This party's janky, I'm gonna bail."
- **mad** (adv/det) – very/a lot of (NY/NJ) – "There were mad people at the party."
- **yadamean** – "you know what I mean" (Bay Area).
- **mom and 'em** – mom and the rest of the family (New Orleans) – "How's mom and 'em?"
- **swoop** (v) – pick someone up; or steal – "Swoop me up at 5."
- **cattywampus / caddywhompus** (adj) – crooked, messed up (Alabama) – "The picture is cattywampus."

## 516. SOME vs ANY | Commonly Confused Grammar

- Both are quantifiers (determiners of amount), used with plural and non-count nouns.
- **some** – positive statements: "I have some extra pizza."
- **any** – negative statements and questions: "I don't want any pizza." "Do you have any pasta?"
- Exception: **some** in offers/requests expecting yes/no: "Would you like some coffee?" "How about some rice?"
- Exception: **any** in positive sentences after if/whether: "Let me know if you have any questions."
- Question expecting more than yes/no uses any: "Do you have any other options?"
- Singular count noun takes a/an, not some/any: "a Caesar salad."
- **That's some pig** – some = impressive (some boat).
- **and then some** (idiom) – and even more – "They paid us $1,000 and then some."
- **by any means necessary** – whatever it takes, possibly unethical.
- **any one you like** – whichever, doesn't matter.
- **any old thing** – anything, unimportant – "I can't just wear any old thing."

## 517. SONGS TO IMPROVE YOUR LISTENING FLUENCY 🎵

Gist: pick songs at your level, read the lyrics, and vary styles/accents. Picks: Imagine (slow); Every Breath You Take (slow, speeds up); If I Were a Boy (2nd conditional, subjunctive "were"); Closer to Fine (simple past, fast); I Will Survive (disco, clear message); Word Crimes (grammar parody); I Am Moana (story); Devil Went Down to Georgia (story, Southern rock); Cat's in the Cradle (story, slow); Little Bitty (country, slow); Last Kiss (slow, clear).
- **mellifluous** (adj) – pleasant to hear – "Her voice is mellifluous."
- **little bitty** – very small.

## 518. SOUND LIKE YOU'RE FROM THE US! 🇺🇸 1-Hour Deep-Dive into American Accent, Pronunciation, & Culture

Standard American accent keys:
- Pronounce final **R** (father, car, water); New England/NY/Boston drop it.
- **Flap T** – T between vowels (or before dark L) sounds like soft D: water, metal, little, bottle, "get a".
- **Linking** – final consonant + next vowel: "bottle of water", "In a hole in the ground, there lived a hobbit." Practice: mark linking in any paragraph and read aloud.
- **Reductions** – gonna, wanna, hafta; unstressed, not emphasized.
- **for** → "fer"; **you** → "ya"; **to** → "ta" when unstressed.
- **Schwa** – unstressed vowel "uh" in any letter: official, pencil, address, syringe.
- Pause at thought groups: "Do you want to get a bottle of water for your father | before he goes to the airport?"
- Shadowing = say it with same speed, stress, intonation.

Regional accents:
- **New York** – drops final/pre-consonant R (wata, Pawk); "al" → aw (tawk, caw).
- **Boston** – drops R (caw keys, Stah Wahs); "or" → aw; "packie" = liquor store.
- **Southern drawl** – slow, drawn-out vowels, short vowels become diphthongs; pen → pin, get → git.
- **Southern twang** – normal speed, sharper, nasal.
- **Minnesota** – pure long O (boat, soap); "ag" → "eg" (bag → beg).
- **California valley** – over-stressed content words, rushed rest, filler "like".

US states (syllables, stress):
- **Alabama** – 4, al-uh-BAM-uh
- **Alaska** – 3, a-LAS-kuh
- **Arizona** – 4, ar-i-ZO-na
- **Arkansas** – 3, AR-kan-saw
- **California** – 4, cal-i-FOR-nya
- **Colorado** – 4, col-o-RA-do (not "rado" sound as spelled)
- **Connecticut** – 4, kuh-NET-i-kut
- **Delaware** – 3, DEL-a-ware
- **Florida** – 3, FLOR-i-da
- **Georgia** – 2, GEOR-ja
- **Hawaii** – 3, ha-WAI-ee
- **Idaho** – 3, I-da-ho
- **Illinois** – 3, il-i-NOY
- **Indiana** – 4, in-di-AN-a
- **Iowa** – 3, I-o-wa
- **Kansas** – 2, KAN-zus
- **Kentucky** – 3, ken-TUCK-ee
- **Louisiana** – 5, loo-ee-zee-AN-a
- **Maine** – 1
- **Maryland** – 3, MER-i-land
- **Massachusetts** – 4, mass-a-CHU-sets
- **Michigan** – 3, MISH-i-gan
- **Minnesota** – 4, min-uh-SO-ta
- **Mississippi** – 4, mis-i-SIP-ee
- **Missouri** – 3, mi-ZOOR-ee
- **Montana** – 3, mon-TAN-a
- **Nebraska** – 3, ne-BRAS-ka
- **Nevada** – 3, ne-VAD-a
- **New Hampshire** – HAMP-shur (not "shire")
- **New Jersey** – stress JER
- **New Mexico** – stress MEX
- **New York** – stress YORK
- **North Carolina** – ca-ro-LI-na (stress LI)
- **North Dakota** – da-KO-ta
- **Ohio** – 3, o-HI-o
- **Oklahoma** – 4, o-kla-HO-ma
- **Oregon** – 3, OR-e-gun
- **Pennsylvania** – 4, pen-sil-VAIN-ya
- **Rhode Island** – stress I-land
- **South Carolina** – ca-ro-LI-na
- **South Dakota** – da-KO-ta
- **Tennessee** – 3, ten-uh-SEE
- **Texas** – 2, TEX-us
- **Utah** – 2, U-tah
- **Vermont** – 2, ver-MONT
- **Virginia** – 3, vir-GIN-ya
- **Washington** – 3, WASH-ing-ton
- **West Virginia** – vir-GIN-ya (T in West nearly silent)
- **Wisconsin** – 3, wis-CON-sin
- **Wyoming** – 3, wy-O-ming

Movie quotes:
- **Show me the money!** (Jerry Maguire) – how much will I be paid?
- **You had me at hello.** – I'm convinced already; adaptable ("You had me at subscribe").
- **Here's looking at you, kid.** (Casablanca) – I'll never forget you.
- **Fasten your seat belts, it's going to be a bumpy night/ride.** – difficult times ahead.
- **Why so serious?** (The Dark Knight, Joker)
- **All right, all right, all right.** (Dazed and Confused)
- **Just keep swimming.** (Finding Nemo) – never give up.
- **Are you not entertained?** (Gladiator)
- **My precious.** / **You shall not pass.** (Lord of the Rings)
- **You ain't seen nothing yet.** – it will be even more impressive.
- **I'll have what she's having.** (When Harry Met Sally)
- **If you build it, he will come.** (Field of Dreams) – 1st conditional.

Brand pronunciations:
- **Levi's** – LEE-vyz (long vowels); **Bose** – BOHZ (z sound); **In-N-Out** – linked "innanout".
- **Nike** – NY-kee (2 syllables); **Costco** – T elided "Cosco"; **Old Navy** – D elided.
- **Cinnamon Toast Crunch** – schwa endings; T in toast elided.
- **Ben & Jerry's**, **Black & Decker** – "and" reduced to "n".
- **Reese's Pieces** – tongue twister; **Doritos, Cheetos, Tostitos, Gatorade, Caterpillar** – flap T.
- **Tylenol** – TY (long i) + schwa; **Benadryl** – short i, schwa.
- **Whirlpool** – two dark Ls; **Charmin** – SH-, not CH-; **Listerine** – ends -een.

## 519. SPEAKING ACTIVITY 💬  Practice using descriptive vocabulary

Gist: practice speaking alone by describing a picture in as much detail as possible, then compare vocabulary.
- **gate** (n) – airport area where passengers board/leave the plane.
- **tarmac** (n) – smooth concrete area where planes park.
- **gangway** (n) – elevated walkway connecting gate to plane; passengers **disembark** (get off).
- **pier** (n) – structure extending into the sea; **amusement park**, **Ferris wheel**, **solar panel**.
- **white caps** (n) – white foam on breaking waves.

## 520. SPEAK FAST (and clearly) like an American 🇺🇸

Dialogue: "Don't you want to get out of the pool?" "What's your hurry? I'm just taking it easy." "Let's go to the restaurant and get a bite to eat." "I'm going to do a few more laps and then I'm done."
- **Assimilation** – t + y → "ch" (don't you → "dohn-chu"); s + y → "sh" (what's your → "wut-shur").
- **want to → wanna**; linking + flap T: "get out of" → "gedaduv"; "out of" → "outta".
- **Elision** – T/D between consonants dropped: "just taking" → "jus' takin'".
- **G-dropping** – taking → takin', then link: "takin' it easy".
- **go to the** – "to" reduced to flap T + schwa ("go-da-the").
- **and** → "n": "restaurant 'n' get".
- **bite to eat** – coarticulation: one T flows into the reduced "to".
- **I'm going to** → "I'm gonna" → "Amina" (very reduced).
- **Intrusion** – slight W/Y inserted between vowels: "do a" → "do-wa".
- Practice by shadowing, taking turns as A and B.

## 521. SPEAK FAST (and easily) like an American 🇺🇸

Dialogue: "What's up with the bus? Should we keep waiting around?" "It's your call, but I think we may want to consider other options." "We're going to have to get an Uber if we want to make it on time." "I'm going to see if I can find one on the app."
- **What's up with (something)?** – informal; asks why something is happening/late.
- **It's your call.** – you make the decision.
- **Linking** – what's up; waiting around; consider other options; get an Uber; make it on time.
- **Co-articulation** – overlapping sounds (with the: voiceless th into voiced th).
- **G-dropping + glottal T** – waiting → "wai'-n" (T before N becomes glottal stop, like kitten, mitten, mountain).
- **Assimilation** – it's your → "it-chur".
- **Flap T** – "but I" → "budI".
- **Reductions** – want to → wanna; going to → gonna; "I'm going to" → "Amna".
- **Thought groups** – pause after "Uber".
- **Elision** – T/D after N dropped: find one → "fine one"; interactive.
- **the** – weak form (thuh) before consonants, strong "thee" before vowels (the app).
- **Uber** = app-based taxi.

## 522. SPEAK FAST LIKE AN AMERICAN 🇺🇸 #shorts #americanenglish

- "I'm going to get an Uber and go home" → "I'm gonna / I'm 'a / get-d-an Uber 'n go home".
- going to → gonna → even shorter; link "get an" with flap T; **and → 'n**.

## 523. SPRING IDIOMS & EXPRESSIONS TO BRIGHTEN UP YOUR DAY  🌸

- **not a cloud in the sky** – perfectly sunny, clear weather.
- **like watching the grass grow** (simile) – extremely boring – "Waiting for her to get ready is like watching the grass grow."
- **brighten up (someone's) day** – make you feel better – "These flowers brighten up my day."
- **spring into action** – start doing something quickly with energy.
- **spring to life** – suddenly start moving/waking – "She springs back to life after a nap."
- **spring something on someone** – give unexpected/unwelcome news at the last minute – "How can you spring this on me?"
- **no spring chicken** – not young anymore – "He's no spring chicken."
- **spring cleaning** – thorough cleaning of the whole home in spring.

## 524. START USING THESE REDUCTIONS - gonna, wanna, hafta, gimme, lemme

Reductions are for speech, not writing; they are unstressed, so keep the content words stressed.
- **gonna wanna** (going to want to) – "You're gonna wanna see this."
- **gonna have to** – "She's gonna have to bring it back."
- **gonna give me / gimme** – "Are you gonna give me a hand with this?"
- **gonna let me / lemme** – "Are you gonna let me drive?"
- **give someone a hand** – help; **give someone a hard time** – deliberately make things difficult.

## 525. STOP Making This Common Grammar Mistake!

- **recommend** (v) – advise someone to do something based on special knowledge.
- Best: **recommend (that) + subject + base verb** (subjunctive): "I recommend (that) she see a doctor." "that" is optional.
- Avoid "I recommend you to do..." – unnatural/uncommon in active voice.
- Passive is fine: "Passengers are recommended to arrive 2 hours early."
- **recommend + -ing**: "I recommend taking an umbrella."
- **recommend something to someone**: "She recommended the movie to her friend."
- Subjunctive is more common in American English.

## 526. STOP Mispronouncing These 5 Verbs! 🔥 Quick Pronunciation Fix

- **misinterpret** /mis-in-TER-prit/ – 4 syl, stress 3rd – get the wrong idea.
- **perpetuate** /per-PE-chu-ate/ – 4 syl, stress 2nd, "tu" = ch – keep going.
- **decipher** /di-SY-fer/ – 3 syl, stress 2nd, c = s – convert into normal language.
- **reciprocate** /ri-SIP-ro-kate/ – 4 syl, stress 2nd – respond in kind.
- **acknowledge** /ak-NOL-ij/ – 3 syl, stress 2nd – admit as true.

## 527. STOP SAYING BAD 🚫 Use these 46 words & phrases instead

- How's it going? – **not good**, **crappy**, **meh**, **I've been better**, **one day at a time**.
- Movie/TV – **terrible**, **atrocious** (stronger), **slow**, **disappointing**.
- Food – **disagreeable**, **gross**, **rotten**, **moldy**, **too spicy/sweet/sour**, **not spicy/sweet/sour enough**.
- Weather – **cold, hot, windy, rainy** (+ "too"), **yucky**, **lousy**, **nasty**.
- Art/work – **dreadful**, **hideous**, **garbage** (harsh), **unacceptable**, **wrong**.
- Behavior – **naughty** (children), **juvenile** (adult acting childish), **appalling**, **obnoxious**.
- Vacation – **could have been better**, **all right** (negative), **awful**, **god-awful**, **uneventful**.
- Performance – **a poor performance**, **a weak performance**, **a disaster**, **horrendous**.
- Event – **unpleasant**, **a downer**, **a bummer**, **depressing**.
- Class/meeting – **boring**, **lame**, **ordinary**.

## 528. STOP SAYING GOOD! Use these 77 words & phrases instead... ✅

- How's it going? – **great**, **fantastic**, **I'm doing well**, **never been better**, **not bad**, **okay**.
- Movie/TV – **spectacular**, **a must-see**, **entertaining**, **fascinating**, **captivating**, **riveting** (edge of seat), **hilarious**, **frightening**, **disturbing**, **powerful**, **thought-provoking**.
- Food – **fantastic**, **exceptional**, **tasty**, **savory**, **delicious**, **succulent** (juicy), **mouth-watering**, **fresh**; lukewarm: **all right**, **a little sweet/sour/spicy/bitter**.
- Weather – **gorgeous**, **a nice/pleasant day**, **crystal clear**, **sunny**, **fine**, **a bit windy/cool/chilly**.
- Work – **wonderful**, **excellent**, **I love what I do**, **challenging**, **demanding**, **busy**, **I'm learning a lot of new things**, **repetitive**, **monotonous**, **lonely**, **another day in paradise** (sarcastic), **coming along nicely**, **making progress**, **slow and steady wins the race**.
- Vacation – **adventurous**, **always on the go**, **non-stop**, **relaxing**, **restful**, **peaceful**, **perfect**, **just what the doctor ordered** (exactly what I needed).
- Party – **so much fun**, **off the hook** (slang, very fun), **lively**, **enjoyable**, **lovely**, **glamorous**, **elegant**, **tasteful**.
- Event – **amazing**, **exciting**, **energetic**, **thrilling**, **marvelous**, **non-stop action**.
- Class/meeting – **interesting**, **educational**, **cool**, **informative**, **helpful**, **inspiring**.

## 529. STOP SAYING UM, AH, ER, LIKE... | Avoid Using Fillers for Effective Speaking

- **disfluency** (n) – interruption in the flow of speech; fillers are one type.
- **filler / crutch word** – um, ah, uh, like, you know; signals you need thinking time.
- **crutch** (n) – support for walking; fillers become a crutch for fluency.
- Pause (good, lets listeners absorb) vs filler (sounds unsure, nervous, drains emotion).
- Fix: 1) record yourself and notice your fillers; 2) replace each filler with a silent pause; 3) build vocabulary; 4) practice presentations repeatedly.

## 530. STOP TRANSLATING from English to Your Native Language

Gist: Translating word-for-word hurts fluency. Translation is OK only for absolute beginners; aim to use it less.
- Dangers: (1) people don't understand you (language = syntax, context, idioms, culture, not just words); (2) sounds unnatural; (3) builds hard-to-break bad habits (wrong part of speech, wrong context); (4) makes learning harder (brain switches languages, more mistakes; like cheating on a test); (5) "easy come, easy go" quick fix – words looked up are forgotten fast, no skill built.
- Instead: think in English a little every day (e.g. on commute); surround yourself with current English (read/watch/listen); don't fear mistakes; if misunderstood, rephrase/paraphrase; if you don't know a word, describe it.
- **easy come, easy go** (idiom) – gained quickly, lost quickly.
- **paraphrase** (verb) – say the same thing in different words.

## 531. STOP USING THIS VOCABULARY 🚫 Use these words & phrases instead...

- **world wide web / the web** (outdated) – say **the internet**, "go online", "surf the internet".
- **groovy** (adj, 1970s) – say **cool, great, amazing, incredible, impressive**.
- **carbon copy** (noun) – in email just say **cc** ("Can you cc me on that email?").
- **baloney / phony baloney** (noun) – fake, untrue; say **fake news**, "not true", or **BS**.
- **Kodak moment** (phrase) – special moment; say "a special moment" / "a good memory".
- **roll up the window** – say **put up the window** / **put the window up** (separable phrasal verb).
- **pound sign** (#) – say **hashtag**.
- **like nails on a chalkboard** (idiom) – a terrible, uncomfortable sound (may fade as chalkboards disappear).

## 532. STOP Using VERY & Use These 54 AMAZING Alternatives!

Adverb + adjective collocations (replace "very"):
- **pretty** – pretty good/bad/easy/difficult/fast/slow/smart/dumb
- **extremely** – extremely important/dangerous/grateful/difficult
- **highly** – highly successful/unlikely/competitive/motivated
- **awfully** – awfully tired/nice (of you)/sorry/crowded
- **truly** – truly thankful/passionate/happy/content/remarkable
- **immensely** – immensely talented/popular/useful/profitable

Precise adjectives instead of "very + adj":
- very good: **incredible, amazing, spectacular, phenomenal, breathtaking**
- very bad: **terrible, horrible, lousy, dreadful, atrocious**
- very happy: **cheerful, delighted, elated, ecstatic, jubilant**
- very sad: **down** (informal), **heartbroken** (love involved), **dejected** (hopeless), **bitter** (sad + angry), **wistful** (sad thinking of the past)
- very beautiful: **lovely, marvelous, gorgeous, elegant** (formal/dressed up), **stunning**
- very tired: **exhausted, drained, beat** (informal), **exasperated** (annoyed + tired), **burnt out** (from work)

## 533. SUFFIXES to Help You Grow Your Vocabulary

Suffix = letters added to the end of a word to make a new word. Learn word families (check online dictionary "related words").
Noun suffixes:
- **-er** – person who does the action (teach→teacher, help→helper)
- **-ment** – action/result (retire→retirement, amaze→amazement)
- **-hood** – state/condition/character (likely→likelihood, child→childhood, parenthood, neighborhood, brotherhood)
- **-ness** – state/quality (happy→happiness, aware→awareness, madness)
- **-tion / -sion** (pronounced "shun") – action/state (transport→transportation, comprehend→comprehension)
- **-ism** – practice/condition/doctrine (socialism, plagiarize→plagiarism, racism, feminism, criticism)
Adjective suffixes:
- **-able / -ible** – capable of being (tolerate→tolerable, drink→drinkable, edible)
- **-ful** – full of (forget→forgetful, use→useful, hopeful, powerful)
- **-ish** – belonging to/like (Britain→British, child→childish, foolish; slang "-ish" = kind of)
- **-less** – without (care→careless, hope→hopeless, shameless)
- **-ous / -ious** – (fame→famous, caution→cautious)
- **-y** – made of/characterized by (cloud→cloudy, taste→tasty)
Verb suffixes:
- **-ed** (past regular), **-ing** (continuous)
- **-en** – make (soft→soften, strength→strengthen, fasten)
- **-ize / -ise** – cause/become (commercialize)
- **-ify** – make/cause to be (intense→intensify, rectify = make right, identify)
- **-ate** – do/become (dominate, irritate, concentrate)

## 534. SUPER-USEFUL ADVERBS | Take the quiz to become an adverb all-star! 📝

- Adverb = word/expression modifying a verb, adjective, other adverb, clause, or sentence; expresses manner, place, time, frequency, degree, certainty; answers how/when/where/to what extent.
- Frequency scale: **always** 100% – **usually/often** 75% – **sometimes** 50% – **rarely/seldom/hardly ever** 25% or less – **never** 0%.
- Sometimes (phrases): **every now and then, from time to time, once in a while, occasionally**.
- **once in a blue moon** (idiom) – very rarely (blue moon = 2nd full moon in a month).
- **Is it hot enough for you?** – joking question to someone suffering from heat (swap hot with spicy, heavy...); **enough** = adverb of degree, comes after the adjective.
- Degree collocations: **fully** aware/conscious/grown/understand/vaccinated; **deeply** sorry/regret/disappointed/involved; **utterly** insane/ridiculous/annoyed/confused.
- Manner: often -ly (**slowly, quickly, carefully, differently, happily**); "You speak English very **well**" (informal: "good"); **poorly** = badly.
- Sentence transformation: "Be careful when you take the test" → "Take the test **carefully**."
- **Are you working hard or hardly working?** – play on words: **hard** = with great effort; **hardly** = barely/very little.
- Time: **three days ago** (not "three days before") – use "ago" for past time from now.
- **by the time** (idiom/adverb clause) – "By the time I arrived, everyone had already eaten."

## 535. SUPER ADVANCED VOCABULARY | Descriptive words to be more precise

- **bamboozle** (verb) – trick/deceive, often by confusing – "She bamboozled me into eating ice cream secretly."
- **dwindle** (verb) – become smaller/fewer – "His chances of winning started to dwindle." (syn: abate, decline, diminish)
- **jeopardize** (verb) – put at risk of loss/harm/failure – "His injury could jeopardize the team's chances." (jeopardize safety/health/mission/career)
- **entertain** (verb) – give consideration to an idea (often negative) – "I won't even entertain such a low offer."
- **articulate** (verb, /ɑːrˈtɪkjuleɪt/) – express clearly and effectively; **articulate** (adj, /ɑːrˈtɪkjələt/) – able to express clearly – "He's very articulate."
- **sanctimonious** (adj) – self-righteous, acting morally superior – "a sanctimonious speech"
- **preposterous** (adj) – absurd, ridiculous – "That's preposterous!"
- **levelheaded** (adj) – calm, balanced, reasonable (compliment) – "She works well under pressure; she's very levelheaded."
- **daunting** (adj) – intimidating, discouraging – "the daunting prospect of three exams in one day"
- **egregious** (adj) – outrageously bad – "an egregious mistake"
- **air of superiority** (noun collocation) – arrogant manner; also technological/military/moral superiority.
- **custody** (noun) – legal right/duty to care for someone, esp. a child – award/get/share/grant/gain/lose custody.
- **novelty** (noun) – quality of being new and unusual – "The novelty started to wear off."
- **comeback** (noun) – return to popularity/success – make/mount/stage a comeback.
- **make a scene** (noun phrase) – publicly show anger/embarrassing behavior – "Don't make a scene."
- **blindly** (adv) – without thinking/questioning – blindly follow orders.
- **sparsely** (adv) – in small numbers – sparsely populated.
- **expeditiously** (adv) – with speed and efficiency – work expeditiously.
- **auspiciously** (adv) – in a way suggesting a good future – "The vacation didn't start auspiciously."
- **blithely** (adv) – cheerfully, without worry or care – blithely ignore evidence.
- (distractors explained: **lethargically** = sluggishly; **meticulously** = with great attention to detail; **tacitly** = unspoken; **profoundly** = deeply; **ardently** = passionately)

## 536. SUPER CONFUSING WORDS | 1-Hour Vocabulary Lesson

- **between vs among** – between = two (one-to-one): "between you and me"; among(st) = more than two/a group; "between a rock and a hard place" (idiom, difficult situation); "Talk amongst yourselves."
- **reluctant vs reticent** – reluctant = unwilling/hesitant to do something; reticent = shy/reserved in manner (esp. speaking).
- **achieve vs accomplish** – achieve = succeed in reaching (goal, fluency); accomplish = finish/complete (mission, task).
- **customer vs client** – customer buys product/service (mall, restaurant); client needs professional service (lawyer); companies can be either.
- **figuratively vs literally** – figuratively = non-realistic, imaginative; literally = really happened; people also use "literally" for emphasis in speech ("I literally haven't slept in weeks").
- **e.g. vs i.e.** – e.g. = for example (list of examples); i.e. = that is / specifically ("closed on Mondays" → i.e.).
- **consecutive vs concurrent** – consecutive = one after another without break; concurrent = at the same time.
- **burn down vs burn up** – both = destroyed by fire; burn down = structures (house, bridge), past events; burn up = smaller items consumed (photo albums), also calories, fever ("you're burning up"), intense present fire.
- **walk up vs walk down (the street)** – interchangeable; up = uphill, down = downhill; "walk down the street" more common; "walk up to someone" = approach confidently. Same for drive up/down the road.
- **take off vs lift off** – take off = leave the ground (planes, birds, insects, anything); lift off = rise straight up (rocket, helicopter, drone, hot-air balloon).
- **used to (+ base verb) vs be used to (+ noun/-ing)** – "I used to live in Korea" = past habit/state, no longer true; questions/negatives: "Did you use to...?", "I didn't use to..."; "I'm used to hot weather / used to taking the bus" = accustomed to; **get used to** = become accustomed.
- Homophones (same sound, different meaning):
  - **flower** (plant) / **flour** (baking powder)
  - **addition** (adding numbers) / **edition** (published version)
  - **click** (verb, mouse) / **clique** (/kliːk/, exclusive group)
  - **patients** (medical) / **patience** (ability to wait)
  - **moose** (animal) / **mousse** (foamy dessert/hair product)
  - **metal** (noun, gold/silver) / **meddle** (verb, interfere)
  - **hoarse** (rough voice) / **horse** (animal)
  - **principal** (school head; adj = main) / **principle** (rule, belief)
  - **gorilla** (ape) / **guerrilla** (unofficial fighter)
  - **core** (center) / **corps** (/kɔːr/, military unit; silent p, s)
  - **sweet** (sugary) / **suite** (/swiːt/, set of hotel rooms)
  - **seas** (oceans) / **sees** (verb see) / **seize** (grab suddenly)
  - **ate** / **eight** (riddle: 30 cows, 28 chickens → 10 didn't eat)
- **Prepositions of place** – **in**: enclosed/larger area (in the car, in town, in human resources, in the pool, in class, in the dictionary/book); **on**: surface (on the table, on the 7th floor, on the road/river, on the East Coast, on a bus/plane/bike, on the left/right, on Netflix/YouTube, on the first page/slide, on Main Street); **at**: specific point (at the front door, at 44 Main Street, at the mall, at the party, at school/work/home, at the beginning/end, at the front of the plane). "in/on/at the lake" = in the water / on a boat / at the location.
- **say vs tell** – say = express words (no listener required): "The report says it will rain"; tell = tell someone (object needed): "She told me to take out the garbage". Fixed with tell: **tell a secret/lie/the truth/a joke/a story**.

## 537. SUPER CONFUSING WORDS | Learn slight nuances to improve your vocabulary

- **persuade vs convince** – persuade = cause someone to DO something (by reasons); convince = cause someone to BELIEVE something (change mind). "She persuaded me to give her a ride." / "The lawyer convinced the jury of his innocence." (convince is more common)
- **fast vs quick** – fast = high speed (car, runner); quick = short time or sudden movement ("a quick snack", "that pig is quick").
- **amiable vs amicable** – both friendly; amiable = people (/ˈeɪmiəbl/); amicable = situations (/ˈæmɪkəbl/): amicable split/solution/settlement/end.
- **eavesdrop vs overhear** – eavesdrop (on) = listen intentionally to private talk (negative); overhear = hear unintentionally.
- **borrow vs lend** – borrow = take/receive to return; lend = give for a short time: "Could you lend me a piece of paper?"
- **revenge vs vengeance** – revenge = personal harm in return for harm (any side; more common): get/exact/plot/plan revenge; vengeance = righteous punishment: take/exact/swear vengeance; **with a vengeance** (idiom) = with great force/energy.
- **imply vs infer** – imply = suggest indirectly (speaker); infer = conclude from information (listener).
- **compliment vs complement** – compliment (i) = praise; complement (e) = something that completes/goes well with ("Peanut butter and jelly complement each other").
- **deny vs refuse** – deny = say not true / not allow (deny a request, application, allegation, rumor; opposite admit/allow); refuse = not accept (refuse an offer/help/treatment, refuse to pay/believe; opposite accept).
- **say vs tell** – "She told me not to say anything." say something; tell someone something.

## 538. SUPER IMPORTANT EXPRESSIONS | Clichés in Spoken English

Cliché = overused, unoriginal phrase; very common in speech, avoid in writing. Introduce with "You know what they say..."
- **better late than never** – excuse for lateness.
- **wake up on the wrong side of the bed** – be in a bad mood ("Someone must have woken up on the wrong side of the bed.")
- **all for one and one for all** – solidarity in a group (Three Musketeers).
- **happily ever after** – "...they lived happily ever after."
- **only time will tell** – result will be known only in the future.
- **work smarter, not harder** – be efficient, not just put in hours.
- **laughter is the best medicine** – laughing helps; cheer up.
- **time heals all wounds** – sadness fades with time (some say "cures").
- **keep your fingers crossed / fingers crossed** – hoping for good luck.
- **the writing is on the wall** – signs that something will fail/end (negative).
- **never say never** – you might change your mind.
- **in the blink of an eye** – very quickly.
- **time flies when you're having fun** – time passes fast when enjoying yourself.
- **ring a bell** – sound familiar.

## 539. SUPER USEFUL IDIOMS | Talk about Friends & Family

Family:
- **a spitting image (of)** – looks exactly like someone – "He's the spitting image of his father."
- **runs in the family** – trait inherited by relatives – "Red hair runs in the family."
- **helicopter parent** – overinvolved, hovering, controlling parent.
- **a chip off the old block** – child much like their parent.
- **like taking candy from a baby** – very easy.
- **like a kid in a candy store** – very excited, childlike.
- **sleep like a baby** – sleep deeply and well.
- **pop the question** – propose marriage.
- **breadwinner** – person who earns the money for the family.
- **black sheep** – family member who is different/disapproved of.
Friends:
- **like two peas in a pod** – very close, always together.
- **have friends in high places** – know powerful/influential people.
- **a shoulder to cry on** – someone who listens to your problems (have/need).
- **bury the hatchet** – end a conflict, make up.
- **hit it off** – become friends quickly, get along well.
- **build bridges** – create friendly relations between people/groups.
- **strike up a friendship** – start a friendship.
- **know someone/something inside out** – know very well.

## 540. SUPER USEFUL PHRASAL VERBS w/ FALL | Build Your Vocabulary

- **fall apart** – break into pieces / stop working well – "This old table is falling apart."
- **fall behind** – fail to keep pace / maintain a level – "I'm falling behind with my work."
- **fall through** – (plans) don't happen – "My weekend plans fell through."
- **fall back on** – rely on something (esp. savings) when all else fails – "I can fall back on my savings."
- **fall down** – drop to the ground/floor (person or thing) – "The painting fell down."
- **fall over** – topple onto its side from vertical – "He tripped and fell over."
- **fall off** – (1) detach – "Leaves fall off the tree"; (2) decline in rate/quality – "Sales are falling off."
- **fall out** – (1) drop out of something – "It fell out the window"; (2) hair/teeth come loose – "His hair is falling out."
- **fall for** – (1) develop romantic feelings – "I'm falling for her"; (2) be tricked – "I fell for the oldest trick in the book."
- **fall into** – (1) drop into – "fell into the pool"; (2) enter a state – "fell into a coma."
- **fall into place** (idiom) – things work out smoothly – "Everything finally fell into place."
- **fall into someone's lap** (idiom) – something good comes unexpectedly – "This job just fell into my lap."

## 541. SUPER USEFUL PHRASAL VERBS w/ SPRING | Build Your Vocabulary

- **spring for** – pay for something, often unexpectedly/generously (usually past tense) – "The boss sprang for everyone's lunch."
- **spring on (someone)** – suddenly tell/ask something unexpected – "Sorry to spring this on you, but can you work this weekend?"
- **spring up** – appear suddenly – "New cafes have sprung up all over town."
- **spring from** – (1) appear suddenly from – "The cat sprang from behind the bushes"; (2) result from – "His anxiety springs from insecurity."
- **spring forward / spring ahead** – set clocks one hour ahead (daylight saving) – "Clocks spring forward in March."
- **spring back** – return to original position after pressure is removed – "The diving board springs back."
- **spring to life** – become active and lively – "The crowd sprang to life when they scored."
- **spring into action** – suddenly start moving/working – "She sprang into action."
- **a spring in one's step** – walking energetically, happy/confident – "You've got quite a spring in your step."

## 542. SUPER USEFUL PHRASES | Binomial Pair Quiz

Binomial pairs: fixed informal two-word phrases joined by a conjunction (and/or), word order doesn't change.
- **loud and clear** – easy to understand – "I read you loud and clear."
- **first and foremost** – most importantly – "First and foremost, subscribe."
- **peace and quiet** – freedom from noise/activity – "I need some peace and quiet."
- **hustle and bustle** – lots of noisy activity – "the hustle and bustle of big cities."
- **give or take** – approximately (numbers) – "How old am I, give or take?"
- **pros and cons** – advantages and disadvantages – "Write down the pros and cons."
- **back and forth** – moving between two directions / changing your mind – "I kept going back and forth."
- **wine and dine** – entertain someone, usually with a nice meal – "They wined and dined the clients."
- **wear and tear** – damage from ordinary use – "The bike has minor wear and tear."
- **null and void** – no legal force – "The contract is null and void."
- **sick and tired (of)** – extremely annoyed, can't tolerate – "I'm sick and tired of seeing injustice."
- **sooner or later** – definitely will happen, time unknown – "Sooner or later love's gonna get you."
- **by leaps and bounds** – very fast growth – "grown by leaps and bounds."
- **rock and roll** – (informal) let's begin – "Let's rock and roll."
- **fair and square** – honestly, by the rules – "You won fair and square."

## 543. SUPER USEFUL PHRASES | Build Your Vocabulary | Ways to Say "Happening Soon"

- **right around the corner / just around the corner** – going to happen soon (often looked forward to) – "Christmas is right around the corner."
- **coming up** – will happen soon, often a specific date/event – "Your birthday is coming up."
- **approaching / fast approaching** – getting closer (fast = very soon) – "Graduation is fast approaching."
- **here before you know it** – very soon; also reassurance – "It'll be here before you know it."
- **upon us** (formal) – arrived/about to; often a season/big change – "Winter is upon us."
- **on the horizon** – will happen soon, exact time unknown – "Big things on the horizon."
- **brewing** – (negative) trouble about to start – "A storm is brewing."
- **on its way** – will arrive soon (physical thing) – "Your package is on its way."
- **in the cards** (informal idiom) – likely to happen – "A date? I think it's in the cards."
- **on the verge of** – about to (discovery/breakthrough) – "on the verge of a breakthrough."

## 544. SUPER USEFUL PHRASES | Expressing Ability vs Inability

Ability:
- **a knack for** – natural skill to do something easily – "She has a knack for negotiating." (also "the knack of")
- **proficient at/in/with** – skilled/experienced; at = specific activity, in = subject, with = tools – "proficient at coding / in two languages / with knives."
- **adept at** – naturally skilled at something requiring skill – "She is adept at problem solving."
- **a natural (at)** – inherent ability; "natural born + noun" – "He's a natural at chess"; "a natural born swimmer."
- **well versed in** – knowledgeable – "well versed in the art of deception."
Inability:
- **not my strong suit** – a skill you're weak at (used at end of statement) – "Grammar is not my strong suit."
- **not my cup of tea** – not something you like (likes/dislikes) – "Horror movies aren't my cup of tea."
- **Achilles' heel** – weakness – "Math has always been my Achilles' heel."
- **two left feet** – clumsy/uncoordinated (esp. dancing); also "two left hands" – "I have two left feet."
- **when it comes to + topic** – introduces the topic – "When it comes to dancing, I have two left feet."

## 545. SUPER USEFUL PHRASES | Ways to Express Ability vs Inability

Ability:
- **a knack for** – natural skill – "She has a knack for negotiating."
- **proficient at/in/with** – at = specific activity, in = subject, with = tools – "proficient at setting up a tent."
- **adept at** – natural skill (more common than proficient) – "adept at problem solving."
- **masterful at** – lots of skill; collocations: masterful job/performance/game/tone – "masterful at spotting new talent."
- **a natural at** / **natural born + noun** – "a natural at chess"; "natural born swimmer."
- **well-versed in** – knows a lot – "well-versed in the art of deception."
- **when it comes to** – introduces topic – "extremely proficient when it comes to coding."
Inability:
- **not my strong suit** – "Public speaking is not my strong suit."
- **not my cup of tea** – dislike/preference – "Early flights are not my cup of tea."
- **Achilles heel** – weakness – "Grammar is my Achilles heel."
- **rusty / a bit rusty** – worse than before from lack of practice – "I haven't bowled in a year, so I'm a bit rusty."
- **two left feet / two left hands** – awkward, uncoordinated – "two left feet when it comes to dancing."

## 546. Say this dialogue if you want to speak like a CHAMP 🏆

Shadowing practice (American accent) – connected speech points:
- **clear one's mind** – let go of distracting thoughts/worries – "Exercise helps me clear my mind."
- **first thing in the morning** – before anything else.
- **work-life balance** – balance between professional and personal life.
- Verb -ing: drop the g ("feelin'", "tryin'"); link final consonant to next vowel ("feeling a").
- Reductions: "and" -> "n"; "got to" -> "gotta"; "to" -> weak "tuh/da"; "for" -> "fer"; "rest of" -> "resta".
- Flap T (soft d) between vowels: "get up", "motivation", "in order"; T after R in unstressed syllable ("party") also flaps.
- Elision: T between consonants disappears ("first thing").

## 547. Silent Letters (K & W) | Learn English Pronunciation & Spelling

- Silent **K** before N (word starts with /n/): knee, know, knife, knock, knight.
- Silent **W** before R (word starts with /r/): write, wrong, wrap, wrist, wreck.
- Other silent W (not before r): e.g. answer, sword, two.
- Reason: spelling standardized after the printing press (15th c.) while pronunciation kept changing.

## 548. Similar Spelling, Wildly Different Sounds 😜 English Makes NO Sense!

"ear" spelling, four sounds:
- **fear** /ɪr/ – also clear, here, appear
- **pear** /er/ – also bear, wear, swear
- **learn** /ɜr/ – also earth, pearl, search
- **heart** /ɑr/ – (rare)

## 549. Slang, Informal, and Formal Vocabulary | English Class 👨‍🏫

Formal / informal / slang:
- exciting atmosphere: **lively** / **energetic** / **lit**
- strong admirer: **connoisseur** / **fan** / **stan**
- dislike something: **that was unpleasant** / **that was bad** / **that sucked**

## 550. Slang, Informal, and Formal Words in English | #Vocabulary Class #Shorts 👨‍🏫

Formal / informal / slang:
- greeting: **Pleased to make your acquaintance** / **Nice to meet you** / **What's up**
- lots of money: **wealthy** / **rich** / **bling bling**
- speak highly of oneself: **to boast** / **to brag** / **to flex**

## 551. Sound Fluent in English – Use These (C1) Phrases Like a Native!

- **all things considered** – in general, taking all aspects into account – "All things considered, the wedding was a great success."
- **bit by bit** – gradually (binomial; cf. "little by little") – "I'm growing this channel bit by bit."
- **before you know it** – very soon, quickly – "The baby will be here before you know it." (link "know it")
- **out of the blue** – completely unexpected (cf. "out of nowhere") – "She suggested we move in together out of the blue."
- **at a glance** – immediately/quickly (at first glance, at a quick glance) – "I could tell at a glance something was wrong."
- **have something against someone** – dislike for a reason – "I have nothing against him"; "What do you have against her?"
- **look on the bright side** – find good in a bad situation (give a reason after) – "Look on the bright side, now you can buy a new shirt."
- **take issue with** – strongly disagree – "I take issue with that."
- **keep a low profile** – avoid attracting attention – "I tried to keep a low profile at the party."
- **in accordance with** (formal) – following a rule/law – "in accordance with the new law." (-ance = /əns/)
- **back road(s)** – small road with little traffic – "Let's take the back roads to avoid rush hour."
- **bear left / bear right** – turn slightly in that direction – "Bear right at the intersection."

## 552. Sound Fluent with These Advanced English Phrases

- **put your thumb on the scale** – unfairly influence an outcome (negative, by someone with authority) – "He gave his nephew the interview questions in advance."
- **let me put it this way** – intro to a clearer/blunt/polite explanation – "He's not the hardest worker. Let me put it this way: I wouldn't hire him again."
- **come to terms with** – gradually accept a sad/difficult situation – "He's coming to terms with moving to a new city."
- **subject to change** – not final – "The flight is subject to change due to the storm."
- **pull out all the stops** – use every effort/resource (from organ stops) – "The hotel pulled out all the stops for the wedding."
- **weigh in on** – give opinion/advice (usually as expert) – "Ask the legal team to weigh in."
- **keep someone at arm's length** – avoid getting too close – "She keeps her colleagues at arm's length."
- **in the wake of** – after, as a result of something major/negative – "Many lost homes in the wake of the flood."
- **call into question** – cast doubt on – "The new evidence called his alibi into question."
- **fall by the wayside** – be forgotten/neglected/not completed – "New Year's resolutions fall by the wayside by February."
- **bridge the gap** – connect two things/reduce distance (positive) – "The mentor program bridges the gap between management and new hires."
- **for all intents and purposes** – in every practical sense (not "intensive purposes") – "The VP is, for all intents and purposes, the head of the company."

## 553. Sound Like a Native: Advanced Idioms You NEED to Know

- **cross that bridge when we come to it** – deal with a problem only if/when it arises – "What if the flight is cancelled? We'll cross that bridge when we come to it."
- **get the short end of the stick** – be treated unfairly/get the worst outcome (drawing straws) – "I got the short end of the stick."
- **throw someone under the bus** – blame/betray someone for selfish reasons – "My colleague threw me under the bus."
- **not my first rodeo** – I have experience – "Don't worry, it's not my first rodeo."
- **call a spade a spade** – speak honestly/directly about something unpleasant – "Let's call a spade a spade: this party stinks."
- **throw down the gauntlet** – issue a challenge – "I'm throwing down the gauntlet."
- **toe the line** (spoken "tow") – do what's expected without upsetting anyone – "She won't toe the line."
- **off the cuff** – without preparation (adverb; "off-the-cuff" adj) – "an off-the-cuff speech."
- **the elephant in the room** – obvious problem everyone ignores (talk about / address) – "We need to address the elephant in the room."
- **go down in flames** – fail spectacularly – "His presentation went down in flames."
- **up in the air** – uncertain/unresolved – "Our vacation plans are still up in the air."
- **move the goalposts** – unfairly change the rules mid-process – "She keeps moving the goalposts."
- **jump through hoops** – do a complicated/annoying series of things to achieve something – "I had to jump through hoops for a residency card."
- **shoot yourself in the foot** – do something that causes your own failure – "Arriving late shot me in the foot."

## 554. Sound More American FAST: Master Connected Speech in 45 Minutes!

Connected-speech points (American accent):
- **assimilation** – two sounds blend: "is your" -> "ishur" (z+y -> zh/j), "could you" -> "cudja", "what did you" -> "whaddja", "don't you" -> "dontcha".
- **dropping -g** in -ing verbs: "gettin'", "cookin'".
- **glottal T** before N: "getting" (t+n), kitten, written, button.
- **flap T** between vowels (soft d): better, get an, right away, got to.
- **linking** final consonant to next vowel: "get out of", "been in", "cooking up a".
- **reductions**: going to -> gonna/"I'mma"; want to -> wanna; got to -> gotta; and -> "n"; to -> weak "tuh/da"; you -> weak "ya"; for -> "fer".
- **elision**: drop H in he/him/her/his mid-sentence ("bother him", "out of his way"); T after N ("internet", "want me") and T between consonants ("last night").
- **intrusion**: add slight W between vowels ("do it" -> "do-w-it").
- **cooking up a storm** – doing something with lots of energy (and skill).
- **get a bite to eat** – have a quick meal.
- **be up for (doing) something** – be willing – "I'm up for watching it again."
- **call in sick** – phone to say you can't work.
- **get out of here right away** – leave immediately.

## 555. Sound Natural, Not Textbook! Advanced English Phrases Explained

- **in no uncertain terms** – in a very clear, direct way (before the forceful message) – "She told him in no uncertain terms to leave her alone."
- **a foregone conclusion** – result obvious before it happens – "Given the poor performance, the loss was a foregone conclusion."
- **nip it in the bud** – stop something before it gets worse – "We need to nip it in the bud."

## 556. Sound Professional with these Advanced Verbs | Build Your Vocabulary

- **spearhead** (v) – lead a big undertaking – "Who will spearhead the new program?"
- **forego** (v) – decide not to have/do something you want – "I had to forego the promotion."
- **procrastinate** (v) – delay doing what you must – "Don't procrastinate."
- **overhaul** (v/n) – examine thoroughly to repair/revise – "overhaul the curriculum"; "a major overhaul."
- **resolve** (v; s = /z/) – solve/end a problem – "resolve a dispute."
- **strategize** (v) – make a detailed plan for success – "We strategized for weeks."
- **implement** (v) – put a plan/policy into action.
- **execute** (v) – carry out/accomplish a task or plan.
- **improvise** (v) – do/invent without preparation.
- **champion** (v) – support/defend a cause – "champion workers' rights."
- **restructure** (v) – reorganize to operate more effectively.
- **troubleshoot** (v) – find and fix a (smaller) problem.
- **network** (v) – meet useful people, esp. for work.
- **attend** (v) – go to/be present at (more formal than go) – "attend a meeting."
- **assist** (v) – help (more professional).
- **submit** (v) – give for decision (vs. hand in) – "submit an application."
- **commute** (v) – travel regularly between home and work – "commute by train."

## 557. Sound Smarter Instantly with These B2 Words

- **nonsense** (n) – foolish/ridiculous, makes no sense (dismissing) – "That's absolute nonsense."
- **stopover** (n) – short stay mid-journey (in/at) – "a stopover in London."
- **tragedy** (n) – event causing great pain – "a personal/national tragedy."
- **dose** (n) – measured amount of medicine; unpleasant experience (dose of) – "a heavy dose of vaccine."
- **rehearsal** (n) – practice before performance – "dress rehearsal"; "in rehearsal."

## 558. Speak English Smoothly: Rhythm, Flow & Fast Speech Tricks 🇺🇸

- English is **stress-timed**: stressed words/syllables fall at roughly regular intervals; other words reduce to fit (e.g. "WHAT did you DO last NIGHT?").
- **mellifluous** (adj) – pleasant to hear, musical (describing language/sound).
- Function words (for, to, and, do, you) reduce: for -> "fer", to -> "tuh", and -> "n", do -> "duh".
- Assimilation: "brought you" -> "broughtchu"; flap T: "bottle"; elision: "want me" -> "wanme"; link "go ahead and open it".
- Drop -g: "havin' us over".
- Practice sentences: "Thanks for having us over for dinner. / We brought you a bottle of wine. / Where do you want me to put this? / Or should we go ahead and open it?"

## 559. Speak FAST (and naturally) like an American 🇺🇸

Practice dialogue: "Is your cold getting any better?" / "No, I'm gonna get an Uber and go home." / "But wait, you're gonna wanna see this." / "Sorry, I gotta get out of here right away."
- **Assimilation**: is + your -> "izh-yer" (z + y blend into a "zh" sound, as in "pleasure").
- **Flap T (US)**: T between two vowels sounds like a soft D (better, get an, get out of, right away).
- **Glottal T**: when "-ing" drops its g, T before "-n" becomes a throat stop: gettin' (like mountain, kitten).
- **Linking**: final consonant moves onto the next word's vowel: get an Uber, get out of, right away.
- **Reductions**: going to -> gonna; "I'm going to" -> "I'ma"/"Amina"; and -> 'n; want to -> wanna; got to -> gotta (schwa + flap T); "you're gonna wanna see this" = two reductions in a row.
- Yes/no questions have rising intonation. Reductions are for listening comprehension; you don't have to use them.

## 560. Speak FAST (and smooth) like an American 🇺🇸

Practice dialogue: "What do you want to do tonight?" / "I'm gonna get a bite to eat. Do you want to go?" / "Sure, could you wait till I finish work?" / "It's getting late, I don't want to wait around all night."
- **Elision**: T/D dropped in fast speech: "what do you" -> "wha-d'ya"; "want to" -> "wanna"; nt after n often loses T.
- **Weak forms**: unstressed do/you/to/for become schwa sounds (do -> "duh", you -> "ya", for -> "fer"); content words get stress (the 2nd "do" in "want to do").
- **Reductions**: going to -> gonna -> "I'ma"; don't want to -> don't wanna.
- **Linking + flap T**: get a -> "geta"; wait around all -> "waiter-round-all"; wait till I -> "wait-till-I" linked.
- **Assimilation**: could + you -> "couldja" (d + y -> "j").
- **Glottal T**: getting -> gettin' (throat stop), like mountain.
- Yes/no questions rise at the end ("Do you want to go?").

## 561. Speak Fluent Office English: Advanced Idioms for the Workplace

**go the extra mile** (idiom) – do more than expected – "I always go the extra mile." (fixed: no "to"; always "the")
**have your work cut out for you** (idiom) – have a very difficult task ahead – "You have your work cut out for you." (he has his / she has her / they have their ... for him/her/them)
**pull your weight** (idiom) – work as hard as others in a group – "He's not pulling his weight." (also "pull your own weight")
**pick up the slack** (idiom) – do work someone else hasn't done – "I had to pick up the slack when she called in sick." (slacker = lazy person)
**be on the same page** (idiom) – agree/understand each other – "Let's make sure we're on the same page."
**up to par** (idiom) – at the expected standard (often negative) – "The food wasn't up to par."
**learn the ropes** (idiom) – learn how to do a job/activity – "He's still learning the ropes." (learn the ropes of something)
**hit the ground running** (idiom) – start a new activity working hard and effectively at once – "We'll hit the ground running on Monday."
**burn the midnight oil** (idiom) – work late at night – "You're burning the midnight oil."
**up to speed** (idiom) – having the latest information – "Can someone get me up to speed?" (get/bring someone up to speed)
**cut corners** (idiom) – save time/money by skipping parts, lowering quality – "They cut corners to save money."
**have a lot on your plate** (idiom) – have a lot of important work to deal with – "She's got a lot on her plate."
- Pronunciation tips: "the" = "thee" before vowel sounds, "thuh" before consonants; flap T + linking (cut out, a lot on, not up); "ground" loses its D (elision); "oil" is one syllable (oy + dark L).

## 562. Speak Like a Leader: Advanced Phrases Every Professional Must Know

**hold down the fort** (idiom) – take charge while someone is away – "She had to hold down the fort when the manager was on leave."
**instill confidence (in someone)** (collocation) – create trust/self-assurance in others – "His calm approach served to instill confidence in the team." Also: build/boost/increase/maintain/restore confidence; shatter confidence (negative).
**The buck stops here.** (idiom, US) – I accept final responsibility; I won't pass blame.
**a tough/hard/difficult act to follow** (idiom) – predecessor was so good that succeeding them is hard – "Her style made her a tough act to follow." (similar: big shoes to fill)
**delegate authority** (collocation) – assign decision-making power to others – "Good managers delegate authority rather than micromanage." Also: respect/command/assume/demonstrate/exert authority; abuse authority (negative).
**steer the ship** (idiom) – guide/direct a project or organization – "The new director is steering the ship toward digital transformation."
**lead by example** (idiom) – model the behavior you expect – "A great manager leads by example, not by commands."
**walk the talk / walk the walk** (idiom) – back up boastful words with action – "Employees trust leaders who walk the talk." (talk the talk = talk confidently, sound good)
**keep your eye on the ball** (idiom) – stay focused on what matters – "Leaders must keep their eye on the ball during times of change."
**take the reins** (idiom) – take control/leadership – "The COO took the reins after the CEO retired." (spelling: reins, like on a horse)
**lead from the front** (idiom) – take an active role, setting the pace – "She leads from the front."
**move the needle** (idiom) – make a noticeable difference – "His contributions finally started to move the needle on revenue."

## 563. Speak Like a Native 💬 Compelling Vocabulary You MUST Know

**defer** (v) – put off to a later time – "He deferred his university application for a year." (defer a decision/payment/work/entry/responsibility; for + period, until + time)
**endorse** (v) – publicly state approval/support – "She refused to endorse a plan she didn't fully support." (endorse a candidate; skills on LinkedIn)
**assert** (v) – state confidently/forcefully – "He asserted his authority." (assert rights/innocence/control/dominance/independence)
**contend** (v) – assert as a position in an argument – "Some analysts contend that the market will recover."
**consensus** (n) – generally accepted opinion of a group – "build a consensus"; broad/general/overwhelming/clear/growing/scientific consensus.
**ambiguity** (n) – unclear, open to more than one interpretation – "The contract's ambiguity led to multiple interpretations." (adj: ambiguous; "I saw the man with the telescope")
**camaraderie** (n) – mutual trust and friendship in a group – "a strong sense of camaraderie among the musicians"
**resilience** (n) – ability to recover/be successful again after difficulty – "Learning a language requires patience and resilience." (adj: resilient)
**candid** (adj) – honest, truthful about something hard – "I appreciated her candid feedback."
**meticulous** (adj) – very careful, attentive to detail – "his meticulous preparation"
**voracious** (adj) – very eager (esp. for food) – "a voracious appetite for learning languages"; voracious reader/consumer/eater/predator.
**pragmatic** (adj) – reasonable, logical, realistic – "a pragmatic plan" (similar: practical, sensible)
**resoundingly** (adv) – completely, decisively – "The team won resoundingly." (resoundingly approve/reject; adj: resounding victory)
**unwaveringly** (adv) – steadily, determinedly, without hesitating – "He unwaveringly defended his position."
**deliberately** (adv) – intentionally, carefully planned – "She spoke deliberately." (deliberately ignore/avoid/refuse/withhold/deceive/mislead; deliberately vague)
**astutely** (adv) – accurately, perceptively – "The manager astutely negotiated better terms." (similar: shrewdly, skillfully)

## 564. Speak Like a Native 💬 Powerful Vocabulary You MUST Know

**allude (to)** (v) – make an indirect reference, hint – "The speaker alluded to the company's past mistakes."
**exacerbate** (v) – make a problem/negative feeling worse – "Delays exacerbate the team's frustration." (exacerbate a problem/situation/decline/poverty/shortage/symptom)
**reiterate** (v) – say again for emphasis or clarity – "Let me reiterate my main point."
**undermine** (v) – gradually weaken the power/effectiveness of – "False rumors undermine someone's credibility."
**ambivalent** (adj) – having mixed or contradictory feelings – "I feel ambivalent about the opera."
**impeccable** (adj) – perfect, highest standards – "impeccable taste/manners/style/service/record/credentials"
**lucrative** (adj) – producing a lot of profit – "a lucrative business opportunity" (beware of "easy money" scams)
**erratic** (adj) – irregular, unpredictable in movement/behavior – "erratic behavior/heartbeat/movement/weather; highly/increasingly erratic" (not "egregious" = shockingly bad)
**anomaly** (n) – something different/abnormal from the rest – "The test results were seen as an anomaly."
**dilemma** (n) – a difficult choice between undesirable options – "an agonizing dilemma"; face a dilemma.
**precedent** (n) – earlier act/decision/case used as a guide or justification – "There are precedents for promoting people without qualifications." (legal context)
**conundrum** (n) – confusing, difficult problem or question – "Child care over holidays is a real conundrum." (dilemma = a choice must be made; conundrum = puzzle/challenge to understand)
**seamlessly** (adv) – smoothly, without breaks – "Each song was seamlessly integrated." (fits/flows/blends/merges/connects seamlessly; spelled -seam-)
**inadvertently** (adv) – accidentally, unintentionally – "He inadvertently gave away their secret."
**explicitly** (adv) – clearly and in detail, leaving no doubt – "The professor explicitly stated the rules."
**presumably** (adv) – very likely but not certain – "It's 10:00, so presumably my roommates are still at the pub."

## 565. Speak Like a Native 💬 Powerful Vocabulary You Should Know

Verbs
**allude** (v) – hint indirectly – "alluded to the company's past mistakes"
**exacerbate** (v) – make worse – "exacerbate the team's frustration" (a problem/situation/decline/poverty/shortage/symptom)
**reiterate** (v) – say again for emphasis/clarity – "Let me reiterate my main point."
**undermine** (v) – gradually weaken – "undermine someone's credibility"
**concede** (v) – admit something is true after denying/resisting – "The manager conceded that mistakes were made."
**mitigate** (v) – make something bad less severe – "Breaks can help mitigate stress." (mitigate damage/a threat/the impact/consequences/a loss)
**compel** (v) – force or strongly persuade – "The evidence compelled the jury to reach a quick verdict."
**refrain (from)** (v) – stop yourself from doing – "Please refrain from using your phone." (+ gerund)
**defer** (v) – postpone – "defer his application"; defer a decision/payment/work/discussion/entry/responsibility (for / until)
**endorse** (v) – publicly approve/support – "refused to endorse a plan"
**assert** (v) – state confidently/forcefully – "asserted his authority" (rights/innocence/control/dominance/independence)
**contend** (v) – assert as a position in an argument – "analysts contend that the market will recover"
Adjectives
**ambivalent** (adj) – mixed/contradictory feelings – "ambivalent about the opera"
**resilient** (adj) – recovers quickly from setbacks – "Children are often remarkably resilient."
**impeccable** (adj) – perfect, highest standards – impeccable manners/taste/style/service/record/credentials
**lucrative** (adj) – very profitable – "a lucrative business opportunity"
**erratic** (adj) – irregular, unpredictable – erratic behavior/heartbeat/movement/weather
**tentative** (adj) – not fully worked out/final – "We set a tentative date for the meeting." (tentative schedule/plan/date/agreement/arrangements)
**formidable** (adj) – inspiring fear/respect by being large, powerful, capable – "a formidable challenge/opponent/ally"
**tedious** (adj) – too long, slow, dull – "Paperwork was a tedious process."
**ubiquitous** (adj) – found everywhere – "Smartphones have become ubiquitous."
**candid** (adj) – honest, truthful about hard things – "candid feedback"
**meticulous** (adj) – very careful and precise – "meticulous preparation"
**voracious** (adj) – very eager – "a voracious appetite for learning"; voracious reader/consumer/eater/predator
**pragmatic** (adj) – reasonable, logical, realistic – "a pragmatic plan"
Nouns
**consensus** (n) – generally accepted opinion – build a consensus; broad/general/overwhelming/clear/growing/scientific
**ambiguity** (n) – unclear/open to several interpretations (adj: ambiguous)
**camaraderie** (n) – mutual trust and friendship among people who spend time together
**resilience** (n) – ability to recover after difficulty – "requires patience and resilience"
**epiphany** (n) – sudden realization of something important – "She had an epiphany about how to solve the problem." (have an epiphany)
**anomaly** (n) – something abnormal/different from the rest
**disposition** (n) – a person's natural temperament – "Her cheerful disposition made her a natural team leader." (cheerful/friendly/sunny; nervous; aggressive/gloomy)
**precedent** (n) – past case/decision used as a guide or justification
**dilemma** (n) – difficult choice between undesirable options
**conundrum** (n) – confusing, difficult problem (dilemma demands a choice; conundrum is a puzzle)
**nuance** (n) – subtle difference in meaning/expression – "a nuance between confident and arrogant" (slight/subtle nuance; full of nuance; lacks nuance)
**repercussion** (n) – unintended, usually negative consequence – "serious repercussions for its employees"
**ramification** (n) – complex, often unintended result, positive or negative (more neutral than repercussion)
**catalyst** (n) – person/thing that precipitates an event – "The crisis served as a catalyst for innovation."
Adverbs
**seamlessly** (adv) – smoothly, without breaks – fits/flows/moves/blends/merges/connects seamlessly
**inadvertently** (adv) – accidentally – "inadvertently given away their secret"
**explicitly** (adv) – clearly and in detail – "explicitly stated the rules"
**presumably** (adv) – very likely but not certain
**unequivocally** (adv) – leaving no doubt – "The CEO stated unequivocally that there would be no job cuts." (state/demand unequivocally)
**incessantly** (adv) – constantly, without interruption (often negative) – talk/complain/worry/bark/chatter incessantly
**eloquently** (adv) – in a clear, strong, persuasive way – "eloquently explained the challenges"
**conspicuously** (adv) – in a clearly visible way – conspicuously late/absent/lacking/high/low/clean; (opposite: inconspicuously)
**resoundingly** (adv) – completely, decisively – resoundingly approve/reject/win
**unwaveringly** (adv) – steadily, without hesitating – "unwaveringly defended his position"
**deliberately** (adv) – intentionally, planned – deliberately ignore/avoid/refuse/withhold/deceive/mislead; deliberately vague
**astutely** (adv) – accurately, perceptively – "astutely negotiated better terms"

## 566. Speak Like a Pro 💼 Advanced Phrases ESL Learners Should Know

**hang by a thread** (idiom) – in a dangerous situation, barely surviving or succeeding – "Our chances are hanging by a thread."
**in the grand scheme of things** (phrase) – considering everything, the big picture – "In the grand scheme of things, Earth is a tiny planet."
**at the heart of the matter** (phrase) – the most fundamental part of an issue – "At the heart of the matter is a lack of trust."

## 567. Speak Like an American: Accent & Pronunciation Training 🇺🇸

Practice dialogue: "So, what are you going to work on next year?" / "I'm trying to get in shape, but I keep putting it off." / "Yeah, it's hard to stay consistent, especially when life gets in the way."
- English is **stress-timed**: stressed words/syllables fall at roughly equal intervals; words in between are reduced ("What did you do last night?" -> WHAT, DO, NIGHT).
- "what" is a weak schwa; final T before a vowel -> flap T + linking: what are -> "wadder"; get in; but I; put it off.
- going to -> gonna; got to -> gotta; to -> weak "tuh" (to stay, to be); "next year" -> T+Y blend to a "ch" sound (assimilation).
- G-dropping: trying -> tryin'; putting -> putting (flap T) or "pu'in'" (glottal T before -n, like kitten).
- "want" loses its T after n (wan' this year); also internet, center, interactive.
- Thought groups: short pause at a clause boundary (before "if"), so no linking across it.
- Intrusion: a slight Y/W glide connects vowels (be-y-a success).
- **especially** /ɪˈspeʃəli/ – stress on 2nd syllable, unstressed schwa at the end.
- **when life gets in the way** (idiom) – daily duties/unexpected events prevent you from doing things.
- **stick with it** (phrasal) – persevere – "I've got to stick with it."
- **put off** (phrasal verb) – postpone – "I keep putting it off."
- **get in shape** (phrase) – become physically fit.

## 568. Speak Naturally & Easily Using Contractions

- Contraction = two words squeezed together, apostrophe replaces letters. Used constantly in speech and informal writing; avoid in formal writing.
