- **Subject + be**: I'm, you're (sounds like "your"), he's, she's, it's, we're, they're – "It's a sunny day."
- **Subject + will**: I'll, you'll, he'll, she'll, it'll, we'll, they'll – "I'll pay." (US: flap T sounds like D in it'll)
- **Subject + have** (helping verb, present perfect only): I've, you've, we've, they've – "You've already lost."
- **he/she/it + has**: he's, she's, it's – same spelling/sound as "he is"; context tells which – "It's been a pleasure."
- **Subject + would**: I'd, you'd, he'd, she'd, we'd, they'd – "I'd love it."
- **going off the deep end** (idiom) – go a little crazy, lose control.
- Natural: "Thanks for watching, and I'll see you next time."

## 569. Speak Quickly & Naturally by LINKING These Common Words 💬

- Reductions are for speaking and listening only; do not write them in formal/professional writing.
- **gonna** /ˈɡənə/ (going to) – stress 1st syllable, schwa 2nd, no T; only before a base verb ("I'm gonna give this video a thumbs up"); not before a noun/place ("going to school" stays unreduced).
- **wanna** (want to) – before an infinitive ("What do you wanna do?"); not for he/she/it: "wants to" can't be reduced.
- **hafta** (have to) / **hasta** (has to) – obligation; the T is pronounced; has to for he/she/it – "I hafta go." / "He hasta study."
- **gotta** (got to / have got to / has got to) – flap T (like a light D); "have" can be dropped informally – "I gotta go." / "You've gotta practice."

## 570. Speak Quickly & Naturally 👉 "go to the" | Fast Speech in American English 🚀

- **go to the** -> "go-da-the": "to" becomes a weak schwa "tuh", and the T between vowels is a flap T (sounds like D) – "I always go to the gym / movies / bathroom."

## 571. St. Patrick's Day English Lesson | Pronunciation + Culture + Vocabulary 🍀

Culture quiz: St. Patrick's Day is March 17 (traditional death of Ireland's patron saint). Wear green (folklore: invisible to leprechauns, who pinch anyone they can see). Pot of gold is at the end of the rainbow.
- Tongue twister: "Irish wristwatch" – hard to go from /ʃ/ to /r/ (silent w in wristwatch)
- **shamrock** (n) – symbol of Ireland; from Irish "seamróg", young clover (a three-leaf clover; four-leaf clover is rare, brings good luck)
- **diminutive** (n) – shortened/smaller form of a word
- **limerick** (n) – short humorous 5-line poem, rhyme AABBA; "There was an old man from Peru…"
- **stanza** (n) – a "paragraph" of a poem
- **leprechaun** (n) – Irish folklore creature who guards a pot of gold
- **patron saint** (n) – saint who protects a place or group
- **folklore** (n) – traditional beliefs and stories

## 572. Start READING to Start Improve Your English | Reading Comprehension Quiz (La Tomatina)

Reading-comprehension exercise on La Tomatina (Spanish tomato-fight festival; held on the last Wednesday of August; tomatoes must be squashed by hand to avoid injury).
- **take place** (phr v) – to happen/occur (an event) – "When does it take place?"
- **food fight** (n) – a fight using food as ammunition
- **avoid** (v) – keep yourself from doing/staying away from something
- **spectacular** (adj) – great, amazing, incredible

## 573. Stop Confusing These Words | Advanced Vocabulary + Quiz

- **further** (adv/adj) – metaphorical/figurative distance – "His skills go much further than the basketball court." vs **farther** – physical distance – "It was farther than I expected." (further can also be used for physical distance)
- **elicit** (v) – provoke a response – "I elicit a smile from the baby." vs **illicit** (adj) – illegal/forbidden – "illicit activities" (homophones in US English)
- **averse** (adj) – strongly disliking/opposed (usually + to) – "I'm averse to eating broccoli." vs **adverse** (adj) – harmful, unfavorable – "adverse weather conditions"
- **sympathy** (n) – feeling sorry for someone's misfortune ("express sympathy") vs **empathy** (n) – understanding/sharing feelings because you've had the same experience; verbs: sympathize, empathize
- **imply** (v) – suggest (the speaker does it) vs **infer** (v) – draw a conclusion (the listener does it) – "He kept looking away, which implied he was lying; I inferred he wasn't telling the truth."
- **than** (prep/conj) – used in comparisons – "faster than a lion" vs **then** (adv) – at that time/next – "I was working more often then."
- **cereal** (n) – breakfast food vs **serial** (adj) – in a sequence/repeated – "serial number", "serial killer", "serial criminal"
- **cite** (v) – quote as evidence – "cite your sources" vs **site** (n) – a location (construction site) or webpage vs **sight** (n) – the ability to see

## 574. Stop Putting Adjectives in the Wrong Order 🚫

Rules/tips:
- Adjective order: quantity – opinion – size – physical quality – shape – condition – age – color – origin – material – purpose – NOUN.
- Examples: "a delicious big round wedding cake"; "two dirty old blue cotton shirts"; "a cute little turtle"; "a big square brown box" (color before shape is tolerable); "my shiny new car"; "a sleek thin mobile phone".
- Using 2-3 adjectives is natural; more than that sounds awkward.
- Best way to learn the order: lots of reading and listening; if unsure, go with what sounds right.
- Participle adjectives (e.g. broken) usually follow "be" ("my old black watch is broken").

## 575. Stop Saying Everything Is INTERESTING | Build Your Vocabulary with Advanced Synonyms

- **fascinating** (adj) – extremely interesting – "completely into it"
- **intriguing** (adj) – arouses curiosity, has some mystery – "an intriguing story"
- **thought-provoking** (adj) – makes you think – "a thought-provoking documentary"
- **captivating** (adj) – grabs and holds your attention – "captivating eyes"
- **enthralling** (adj) – holds your attention completely (tricky "th" sound) – "an enthralling novel"
- **compelling** (adj) – attention + admiration + conviction; makes you want to act – "a compelling argument"
- **stimulating** (adj) – inspires new ideas, refreshing – "a stimulating discussion"
- **appealing** (adj) – pleasing, attractive, interesting (also for appearance) – "an appealing idea"
- **riveting** (adj) – extremely interesting and exciting, engrossing – "a riveting thriller"
- **gripping** (adj) – holds your attention tightly – "a gripping film"

## 576. Stop Translating! Learn This B1 Vocabulary to Speak with Confidence

- **lighten up** (phr v) – relax, calm down – "Come on, lighten up. It was just a joke."
- **sense of humor** (n) – ability to find things funny – "Where's your sense of humor?" (US humor / UK humour)
- **above all** (phr) – most importantly – "cheap, attractive, and above all practical"
- **have a temperature / have a fever / run a temperature / run a fever** – body heat higher than normal (running = continuous tense; "have a fever" most common)
- **If I were you, I would…** – second conditional for advice; past simple + would + base verb; use the subjunctive "were" – "If I were you, I would hit the like button."
- **in the middle of (doing)** – busy with an activity (+ gerund, not infinitive) – "I was in the middle of eating dinner."
- **in general** – usually, in most situations – "You should review vocabulary every so often, in general."
- **burst into tears** (phr) – suddenly start crying – "She burst into tears when she got the news."
- **to be honest** (phr) – admitting something is true – "To be honest, this is my first B1 lesson."
- **by accident / by mistake** (phr) – unintentionally – "I deleted the picture by accident." (also accidentally, inadvertently, unintentionally)
- **on purpose** (phr) – intentionally – "He tripped me on purpose."
- **just about to (do)** – going to do something very soon – "The show is just about to start."
- **kick off** (phr v) – begin, start – "The event is about to kick off."
- **pay attention (to)** (phr) – watch/listen carefully; collocations: pay close/special/particular attention, pay no attention – "The students paid close attention to the teacher."
- **get rid of** (phr v) – throw away something unwanted – "I decided to get rid of my old couch."
- **keep calm / stay calm / remain calm** (phr) – stay quiet, peaceful, not worried in a frightening situation – "Please remain calm and walk to the nearest exit."
- **not believe your eyes** (idiom) – be very surprised – "I couldn't believe my eyes when I saw the garden."

## 577. Stop Translating in Your Head ❌  | Start Thinking in English ✅

Five habits to stop translating:
- Create an English bubble: double your daily exposure (music, articles, subtitles, classes all in English); you don't need to live abroad.
- Keep learning routines in English: grammar rules and lessons in English once you're intermediate or advanced.
- Change how you learn vocabulary: use an English dictionary (e.g. Cambridge) with simple definitions, example sentences and word families; put the translator away.
- Make word associations: link a new word to a person, picture or thing (e.g. frugal = me; "glass half full" = optimist, "glass half empty" = pessimist; devastating = natural disasters).
- Automate responses: prepare quick answers for common questions ("What do you do for a living?") and partial answers ("I'm gonna go…") to avoid pauses.
- Turn thinking into active learning: spend 5-10 minutes a day deliberately thinking only in English.

## 578. Stop Using Basic English! Speak Like a Pro with These Advanced Phrases

- **in no uncertain terms** – in a very clear, direct way – "She told him in no uncertain terms to leave her alone."
- **a foregone conclusion** – a result obvious before it happens – "Given the team's poor play, the loss was a foregone conclusion."
- **nip it in the bud** – stop something before it gets worse (not "butt") – "We need to nip these rumors in the bud."
- **go against the grain** – do something unusual/against the norm (fixed phrase) – "He's from a family of doctors but became an actor."
- **hang by a thread** – in a dangerous state, barely surviving – "His career is hanging by a thread."
- **a case in point** – a good example of what was just said (more formal than "for example") – "Many species struggle with climate change; a case in point is the polar bear."
- **in the grand scheme of things** – considering everything, big picture – "In the grand scheme of things, it's not a big deal."
- **strike a chord (with someone)** – resonate emotionally/intellectually – "His story struck a chord with many people."
- **the lion's share** – the largest portion – "She did the lion's share of the work."
- **cut to the chase** – get to the point – "Let's cut to the chase."
- **at the heart of the matter** – the most fundamental part of an issue – "Let's get to the heart of the matter."
- **be in over your head** – involved in something too difficult – "I was in over my head my first month teaching."

## 579. Summer Idioms to Help You Beat the Heat 😎

- **come rain or shine** – no matter what the weather/situation – "The match is on, rain or shine."
- **everything under the sun** – everything imaginable – "He's asking for everything under the sun."
- **Indian summer** – unexpected hot, dry spell in the fall
- **a ray of sunshine** – someone who brightens your day – "My daughter is my ray of sunshine."
- **take a shine to someone** – start to like someone – "Your father's taken a shine to him."
- **soak up the sun** – sit in the sun and enjoy it – "We'll soak up some sun at the beach."
- **summer fling** – short-term summer romance
- **not a cloud in the sky** – clear sky, everything fine
- **brighten up someone's day** – make someone's day better – "I bought you flowers to brighten up your day."
- **a scorcher** (n) – a very hot day – "It's going to be a scorcher today."
- **rise and shine** – time to get up – "Rise and shine, it's a beautiful morning!"
- **beat the heat** – do something to stay cool – "We went swimming to beat the heat."
- **take the heat** – accept criticism/blame – "I'll take the heat for this."
- **in a dead heat** – an exact tie/draw – "The polls show a dead heat."

## 580. Super Advanced Vocabulary that will BLOW YOUR MIND 🤯

- **vindicate** (v) – clear from blame or suspicion – "New evidence vindicated the accused."
- **circumspect** (adj) – carefully considering all circumstances before acting – "Always be circumspect when making big decisions."
- **vehement** (adj) – showing strong, forceful, passionate feeling (adv: vehemently) – "a vehement denial"
- **succinct** (adj) – brief and to the point – "a clear and succinct explanation"
- **perfunctory** (adj) – done without real interest, feeling or effort – "a perfunctory apology"
- **telling** (adj) – revealing something not explicitly stated – "Her silence was telling."
- **precarious** (adj) – exposed to danger, risky – "a precarious situation"
- **unflappable** (adj) – calm in a crisis – "The teacher remained unflappable."
- **cogent** (adj) – clear, logical and convincing – "a cogent argument"
- **ephemeral** (adj) – lasting a very short time – "Fame can be ephemeral." (syn: fleeting, short-lived)
- **ubiquitous** (adj) – found everywhere – "Smartphones are ubiquitous."
- **candor** (n) – honesty, frankness (adj: candid) – "He spoke with candor."

## 581. Super Confusing Words for English Learners | BUILD YOUR VOCABULARY

- **between** (prep) – two things/people, one-on-one – "between you and me" vs **among / amongst** – more than two, not distinct – "share it among your friends"; "talk amongst yourselves"
- **reluctant** (adj) – unwilling/hesitant to do something – "reluctant to prepare the presentation" vs **reticent** (adj) – shy, reserved in manner – "reticent to speak"
- **achieve** (v) – succeed in reaching a goal (ongoing) – "achieve English fluency" vs **accomplish** (v) – finish a task/mission – "accomplish the mission"
- **customer** (n) – buys products/services (shopping, restaurant) vs **client** (n) – needs professional services (lawyer, other companies)
- **figuratively** (adv) – imaginatively, not literally – "Figuratively speaking, people were kicking down the doors." vs **literally** (adv) – actually (often used loosely for emphasis in speech – "I literally haven't slept in weeks")
- **e.g.** – for example (gives examples) vs **i.e.** – that is to say/specifically
- **consecutive** (adj) – one after another without a break – "consecutive heat waves" vs **concurrent** (adj) – at the same time – "concurrent heat waves"

## 582. Super Confusing Words? | Useful HOMONYMS explained

- **flower / flour** – plant; fine baking powder
- **addition / edition** – adding numbers; a published version of a book or newspaper (stress on 2nd syllable)
- **click / clique** – to click a link; small exclusive group of friends
- **patients / patience** – people under medical care; ability to wait without being annoyed
- **moose / mousse** – large deer-family mammal; creamy dessert or hair-styling foam
- **metal / meddle** – hard element like gold (flap T sounds like /d/); interfere in others' business
- **horse / hoarse** – the animal; rough-sounding voice
- **principal / principle** – school leader or main/most important (adj); a rule or basis of conduct
- **gorilla / guerrilla** – giant ape; member of an unofficial military group
- **core / corps** – central part; military organization (corps is silent p and s, from French)
- **sweet / suite** – sugary flavor; set of rooms in a hotel
- **sea / see / seize** – the ocean; look; grab suddenly
- Riddle: "30 cows and 28 chickens, how many didn't (ate)?" – "eight / ate" – answer: ten

## 583. Super Descriptive Adjectives to Build Your Vocabulary

Sight:
- **uncanny** (adj) – strange/mysterious in a surprising way – "an uncanny resemblance"
- **dilapidated** (adj) – old and in poor condition – "a dilapidated house"
- **gruesome** (adj) – unpleasant, shocking, related to death/injury – "a gruesome death"
- **mesmerizing** (adj) – attractive, can't take your eyes off it – "mesmerizing eyes"
- **hazy** (adj) – not clear (weather or memory) – "my memory is hazy"
Hearing:
- **faint** (adj) – not strong or clear – "a faint noise"
- **piercing** (adj) – high, loud, unpleasant – "a piercing sound"
- **raspy** (adj) – unpleasantly rough (voice) – "a raspy voice"
- **thud** (n) – sound of something heavy hitting something – "a loud thud"
- **vociferous** (adj) – expressing opinions/complaints loudly and repeatedly – "vociferous demands"
Smell:
- **overpowering** (adj) – extremely strong – "the stench is overpowering"
- **heavenly** (adj) – very pleasing (smell/taste) – "a heavenly aroma"
- **putrid** (adj) – rotting, unpleasant smell – "a putrid odor"
- **toxic** (adj) – poisonous – "toxic fumes"
- **musty** (adj) – unpleasantly old and damp – "musty old books"
Touch:
- **slick** (adj) – smooth, shiny, slippery – "slick roads"
- **filthy** (adj) – extremely dirty
- **lukewarm** (adj) – slightly warm (liquid) – "a lukewarm shower"
- **mushy** (adj) – soft with no firm shape
- **cuddly** (adj) – you want to hug it – "a cuddly stuffed animal"
Taste:
- **subtle** (adj) – not obvious – "a subtle hint of cinnamon"
- **strong** (adj) – very noticeable and powerful – "strong coffee"
- **pungent** (adj) – very strong and sharp, often disagreeable – "a pungent taste/smell"
- **savory** (adj) – salty or spicy, not sweet – "savory snacks"
- **succulent** (adj) – pleasantly juicy – "a succulent steak"

## 584. Super USEFUL EXPRESSIONS for your next conversation (30-minute Lesson)

- **the tip of the iceberg** – a small visible part of a much bigger problem – "Allegations of corruption – this is just the tip of the iceberg."
- **give someone the benefit of the doubt** – choose to believe someone even though they might be lying – "I gave him the benefit of the doubt."
- **get the short end of the stick** – end up worse off than others, not your fault – "I always get the short end of the stick when we assign chores."
- **get your second wind** – regain energy to continue – "She was exhausted but got her second wind."
- **keep your eyes peeled (for)** – watch carefully for something (UK: keep your eyes skinned) – "Keep your eyes peeled for a gas station."
- **take something with a grain of salt** – don't completely believe it (UK: a pinch of salt) – "Take what you read on Facebook with a grain of salt."
- **put the cart before the horse** – do things in the wrong order – "Booking a honeymoon before you propose."
- **put your money where your mouth is** – show with actions, not just words, that you support/believe something – "I'll bet you $100 you can't do it."
- **put all your eggs in one basket** – depend on a single person/plan (risky) – "Don't apply to only one college."
- **the early bird catches the worm** – the person who arrives first gets what they want
- **put yourself in someone's shoes** (also place/position) – imagine how someone feels in a difficult situation – "Put yourself in my place."

## 585. Super Useful American Expressions | Build Your Vocabulary

- **err on the side of caution** – be extra careful to avoid mistakes (US: "air") – "I'll err on the side of caution."
- **play it by ear** – decide as things unfold, no advance plan – "Let's play it by ear."
- **I hear you** – expressing empathy/acknowledging someone – "Okay, I hear you."
- **close but no cigar** – nearly succeeded but no reward (from fairground games)
- **sooner rather than later** – in the near future, not later – "We need to fix the door handle sooner rather than later."
- **an accident waiting to happen** – a person/thing/situation likely to cause an accident – "That old bridge is an accident waiting to happen."
- **in and of itself** – on its own, by its nature – "Taking care of a child is a full-time job in and of itself."
- **throw a wrench in the works** (also monkey wrench) – prevent a plan from succeeding – "The bad weather threw a wrench into our plans."
- **not buying it** – not willing to believe it – "The boss called in sick again? I'm not buying it." (also "they bought it" = believed it)
- **jump on the bandwagon** – join a popular trend/activity or change your opinion because it's popular – "Everyone jumped on the bandwagon when the team started winning."

## 586. Super Useful Idioms & Expressions w/ SPEAK or TALK

- **speak one's mind** – say what you think openly and honestly – "Don't be afraid to speak your mind."
- **speaking from experience** – talking with credibility from prior experience – "Speaking from experience, exposure is the best way to learn a language."
- **talk one's ear off** – talk to someone too long/excessively – "He talked my ear off about a presentation."
- **actions speak louder than words** (proverb) – actions reveal true character more than promises
- **like talking to a brick wall** – trying to reason with someone who won't listen/react – "It's like talking to a brick wall."
- **(not) on speaking terms** – friendly enough to talk; usually negative = had a falling out – "We're not on speaking terms."
- **talk one's way into/out of something** – persuade someone to allow/not do something – "She talked her way out of a ticket."
- **as we speak** – at this exact moment, adds urgency – "The plane is boarding as we speak."
- **talk some sense into someone** – persuade an emotional/out-of-control person to see reason – "Please talk some sense into him."

## 587. Super Useful Idioms & Expressions | Learn American English 🇺🇸

- **a piece of cake** – very easy
- **run late / run behind** – be late (or behind schedule) – "I'm running a little late."
- **get the hang of (it)** – learn how to do something (usually ends with "it") – "You'll get the hang of it."
- **all set / good to go** – ready, everything in order – "We're all set."
- **sound like a broken record** – keep repeating the same thing – "I know I sound like a broken record."
- **pitch in** – help as part of a group – "Everyone needs to pitch in."
- **blow off some steam** – release pent-up energy/anger (e.g. run, yell) – "He needs to blow off some steam."
- **it's not rocket science** – it's not difficult
- **table something** – postpone a discussion until later – "Let's table this item."
- **jump on the bandwagon** – support/do something because it's trendy – "He only likes the team because they're winning."
- **sit in the nosebleeds / nosebleed section** – cheap seats very high up in a stadium
- **fall through the cracks** – be overlooked – "Don't let details fall through the cracks."

## 588. Super Useful Idioms & Phrases for the US Election 🇺🇸

- **political football** – issue parties argue over to gain advantage rather than solve – "Immigration is used as a political football."
- **backroom deal** – deal made secretly, out of public view (often negative) – "a backroom deal with Amazon's founder"
- **move the goalposts** – change the rules/conditions mid-process to make it harder – "They moved the goalposts on the Ukraine bill."
- **throw one's hat in the ring** – announce you're entering a contest/election – "Harris threw her hat in the ring." (not "throw in the towel" = quit)
- **grassroots movement** – movement driven by ordinary citizens, not leaders/politicians
- **elephant in the room** – obvious big problem everyone avoids discussing
- **vote of confidence** – (1) vote showing members still support the government/leader; (2) sign you're pleased with someone's work – "The CEO's visit was a vote of confidence."
- **pull strings** – secretly use influence/connections to get something – "I may be able to pull a few strings to get you an earlier flight."
- **red tape** – excessive bureaucracy/official rules that delay things – "There's a lot of red tape to get a visa."
- **cross the aisle** – cooperate/vote with the other party – "Democrats need Republicans to cross the aisle."
- **political capital** – influence/power a politician can spend to get things done – "He used a lot of political capital to pass the ACA."
- **political arena** – sphere of political activity and debate – "Women are entering the political arena."

## 589. Super Useful Phrasal Verbs for Everyday English

Travel/transport phrasal verbs:
- **get on / get off** – enter/leave bus, plane, train, boat, motorcycle, bike
- **hop on / hop off** – informal get on/off (same vehicles); "hop-on hop-off" tourist bus
- **get in / get out** – enter/leave car, truck, taxi, ambulance, helicopter, canoe
- **hop in / hop out** – informal get in/out
- **get around** – move from place to place – "How will you get around?"
- **hurry up** – go/move faster
- **take off** – (plane) leave the ground – "When does it take off?"
- **hold up** (sep.) – cause a delay – "I got held up in traffic."
- **check in / check out** – arrive and register at hotel/hospital/airport; leave a hotel/hospital after paying
- **buckle up** – fasten your seatbelt
- **cut (someone) off** (sep.) – block someone's way, esp. while driving – "That guy cut me off." (+ **road rage** = driver's anger)
- **speed up / slow down** – go faster / slower (not "speed down")
- **pick (someone) up** (sep.) – collect someone by vehicle – "I'll pick you up at 7:45."
- **drop (someone) off** (sep.) – leave someone at a place, staying in the car
- **connect through** – stop at an intermediate city on the way to final destination – "We connect through Houston."
- **see (someone) off** (sep.) – go with someone to airport/station to say goodbye

## 590. Super Useful Phrases for Advanced English Speakers

- **drag one's feet** – do something slowly / delay because you don't want to – "Stop dragging your feet."
- **go to bat for someone** – support/defend someone who needs help (from baseball) – pronounce "to" /tə/, flap T
- **run-of-the-mill** – ordinary, not special – "a run-of-the-mill presentation" (link: "run-of")
- **err on the side of caution** – be extra careful rather than risk a mistake ("err" = air/er)
- **pull strings** – secretly use influence/contacts to get something (usually "some/a few strings") – "I'll pull a few strings and get tickets."
- **chime in** – add a comment/opinion to a conversation (sometimes interrupting) – "Anyone want to chime in?"
- **cut someone some slack** – treat someone less harshly (some = affirmative, any = negative: "won't cut you any slack"; "cut" more common than "give")
- **one-up someone** – gain advantage by saying/doing something better or more extreme – "Climbing Everest? I met Michael Jordan."
- **at one's wits' end** – so worried/frustrated you don't know what to do – "I'm at my wits' end with these two." (link: "wits-end")
- **second-guess** – question/criticize a decision after it's made; also second-guess oneself – final D in "second" is dropped
- **heads up** – advance warning; "give someone a heads up"
- **foot the bill** – pay for something expensive (usually for others) – "My parents footed the bill for college."

## 591. Super Useful SPEAKING Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary 🚀

- **generally speaking** – in most cases, without exact detail – "Generally speaking, the weather in California is pleasant."
- **speak up for** – support/defend someone (or yourself: "speak up for yourself")
- **be on speaking terms** – friendly enough to talk; almost always negative – "My neighbors haven't been on speaking terms since the fence dispute."
- **shoot the breeze** – chat casually about unimportant things – "We spent the afternoon shooting the breeze."
- **speak highly of** – praise someone/something – "Everyone speaks highly of her leadership."
- **speak too soon** – say something that's quickly proven wrong – "I said traffic wouldn't be bad; I spoke too soon."
- **speak out against** – publicly protest/oppose (corruption, injustice, racism) – opposite of speak up for
- **talk tough** – use strong, aggressive language to show control (often **all talk** = no action)
- **speak out of turn** – say something at the wrong time or without authority – "I hope I'm not speaking out of turn, but..."
- **speak ill of** – say bad things about someone (also speak badly/poorly of) – "speak no ill of the dead"
- **talk is cheap** – saying is easier than doing; challenge to act (cf. actions speak louder than words)
- **be spoken for** – already claimed/reserved, or in a relationship – "That last piece of cake is spoken for."

## 593. Super Useful Weather Phrasal Verbs ☔️

- **let up** – (rain/snow) become less severe – "I think it's starting to let up."
- **rain out** – cancel an event because of rain – "The game got rained out."
- **blow over** – (storm) come to a quick end – "The storm will blow over."
- **clear up** – weather becomes pleasant – "It'll clear up by this afternoon."
- **break through** – (sun) start to appear through clouds
- **roll in** – appear in large amounts (clouds, fog) – "The fog started to roll in."
- **pick up** – (rain/snow/wind) get stronger
- **die down** – (rain/snow/wind) get weaker
- **hold off** – (rain/snow) not start yet – "I hope the rain holds off."
- **bundle up** – wear warmer clothes in cold weather
- **snowed in** – unable to leave because of too much snow
- **beat down** – (sun) shine intensely – "The sun is really beating down."

## 594. Surprising Things about Americans & Living in the USA 🇺🇸

Gist: Live cultural chat by a teacher who moved from Romania to California at 15. Surprises: huge ethnic diversity and food variety; free public bathrooms (stall doors don't reach the floor); friendly but cliquey; fast food/cake mixes/processed food; you need a car; bigger food portions, cars, houses; huge country with regional differences; fitness/outdoor culture but expensive to eat healthy; college sports are huge; drinking age 21 strictly enforced (ID checks); drive-throughs (even pharmacies, banks); pajamas in public; ice in every drink with free refills; peanut butter obsession; flags everywhere and the Pledge of Allegiance.
- **from scratch** – made from basic ingredients, not pre-mixed
- **drive-through** – service where you stay in your car at a window

## 595. TAKE THE IDIOM QUIZ 📝 | COMMON & USEFUL AMERICAN IDIOMS

- **under the weather** – feeling sick
- **a blessing in disguise** – something good that seemed bad at first – "Losing my job was a blessing in disguise."
- **call it a day** – stop working on something
- **hit the sack** – go to bed/sleep
- **ignorance is bliss** – better off not knowing
- **up in the air** – uncertain, undecided – "Our plans are up in the air."
- **sit tight** – wait patiently, take no action until told
- **rule of thumb** – general unwritten rule – "I before E except after C."
- **take a rain check** – postpone a plan (sometimes a polite refusal)
- **no pain, no gain** – achieving goals requires hard work/discomfort
- **over someone's head** – too difficult for them to understand – "The joke went over my head."

## 596. TAKE UP Has 6 Different Meanings 😲 Phrasal Verb Lesson

- **take up** (+ -ing) – start a new hobby/activity – "I took up guitar."
- **take up** (space/time) – fill/use an amount of space or time – "This movie takes up a lot of space."
- **take (someone) up on** – accept an offer – "I'll take you up on that."
- **take up** (clothing) – make shorter – "take the dress up two inches"
- **take up** (issue/cause) – discuss/act on a question, problem, or cause – "take it up with the police"
- **take up (a) position** – move into a place (military) – "The enemy has taken up position."
- Bonus: **take up arms** – prepare to fight; **take up residence** – start living somewhere (often temporarily)

## 597. THANKSGIVING PHRASES + COLLOCATIONS 🦃  | Build Your Holiday Vocabulary

- **(fall) is in the air** – the season/feeling is noticeable around you; "love is in the air"
- **get together** (phr. v.) – meet at an arranged time
- **autumn/fall harvest** – gathering of crops before winter
- **family gathering** – family coming together
- **have a feast** – a large meal – "It's going to be a huge feast."
- **carve the turkey** – cut up a big turkey
- **set the table** – lay out cloth, plates, silverware, glasses
- **give thanks** – express gratitude
- **thankful for / grateful for** (grateful = more formal)
- **count your blessings** – appreciate what you have
- **say grace** – pray before/after a meal
- **dig in** – start eating
- **I'm stuffed** – so full I can't eat more
- **room for seconds** – able to eat a second serving
- **leftovers** – food remaining after a meal – "have/eat leftovers"
- Fact: US Thanksgiving is the fourth Thursday of November; national holiday proclaimed by Lincoln.

## 598. THAT, WHICH, WHO? Important Relative Pronouns You Need to Know

- **that** = people/animals/things; **which** = animals/things; **who** = people.
- Relative clause = dependent clause (subject + verb) giving extra info about a noun, starts with a relative pronoun.
- Restrictive clause (essential info, no commas) -> use **that** (preferred, esp. US): "I don't like the dog that bit me."
- Non-restrictive clause (optional info, set off by commas/pauses) -> use **which**: "My flight, which is tomorrow, has been delayed."
- People: use **who** in both types; "that" for people is informal, avoid in writing.
- Subject of the clause -> use **who**: "my friend Tom, who got a promotion."
- Object of the clause: formal "the man to whom you were talking"; casual "the man who you were talking to"; informal "the man that you were talking to" (preposition goes at end).
- Non-restrictive with people: always **who** (not that): "My teacher, who taught me in high school, retired."

## 599. THE SHORTEST WORD IN ENGLISH  #shorts

- "a" is a monophthong (one vowel sound), shorter than "I" (diphthong); usually an unstressed schwa /ə/, the most common vowel sound in English; sixth most common English word.

## 600. TOP 10 REASONS TO LEARN ENGLISH 💬

Gist: Wes's 10 reasons: (1) don't feel left out (~1.35 billion L1+L2 speakers); (2) travel; (3) relationships; (4) work opportunities/promotion; (5) education (classes/universities/online); (6) entertainment (shows, movies, books without dubbing); (7) language of the internet (~25% of web content); (8) easy to get exposure; (9) learn about cultures; (10) learn about yourself.
- **L1 / L2** – first language / second language
- **dubbed** – foreign-language voice replaces the original in film/TV

## 601. TRAVEL VOCABULARY - Useful words & phrases for your next trip ✈️

- **staycation** (n, informal) – vacation spent at or near home
- **sense of adventure** – willingness to do unusual, sometimes risky things – "Where's your sense of adventure?"
- **book** (v) – secure something for a future time, usually by paying (flight, hotel)
- **reserve** (v) – keep something for you, may not need to pay (table, rental car); nouns: booking / reservation (reservation more common); meanings overlap online
- **peak / off-peak** – busiest/highest vs. quieter/cheaper (hours, season, prices)
- **pack light / travel light** – take very few things
- **connecting flight** – trip with two or more flights; "connect through" a city
- **direct flight** – one flight to the destination
- **jet lag** – tiredness/confusion after a long flight across time zones – "have/get/experience jet lag"
- **check in / check out** – check in at airport or hotel; check out only of a hotel (pay and return key)
- **exchange rate** – rate at which one currency is changed for another
- **kiosk** – small shop/stand, window booth, or public machine (e.g. money exchange, tickets)
- **When in Rome (do as the Romans do)** – follow local customs when visiting a place

## 602. TRICKY GRAMMAR QUIZ | MANY ENGLISH LEARNERS GET CONFUSED
- **-ing adjectives** describe things and situations ("confusing," "boring"). **-ed adjectives** describe feelings ("confused," "bored"). Say "I'm bored," not "I'm boring."
- Future time clauses (when, after, before, as soon as) use the present, not "will": "I will see him when he comes tomorrow."
- "On" for days and dates ("on Saturdays"). "At" for exact times ("at 3 p.m."). "In" for months, years, and long periods ("in January").
- Use "the" before bridges, museums, and some monuments: "the Golden Gate Bridge," "the Louvre," "the pyramids." No "the" before "Stonehenge" or "St. Peter's Basilica." Memorize these.
- After verbs like "recommend," use the base verb with no -s: "The doctor recommends she get some sleep."
- Present perfect continuous: "It's been raining a lot lately." Say "it's been," not "it's bin."
- "Stop + -ing" = quit: "She stopped smoking." "Stop + to + verb" = pause to do something: "She stopped to smoke."
- Adjective order: opinion, size, shape, age, color, origin, material, noun: "a big red plastic hat."
- "Bring" = movement toward you: "Bring the keys when you come over." "Take" = movement away: "Take them when you leave."
- "For" + duration, "since" + point in time: "for several years," "since 2004."

## 603. TRICKY VERB TENSES  😲 Advanced Grammar Quiz
- **ought to** + base verb (modal): "He ought to know better."
- Scheduled future uses present simple: "The plane arrives at 8 pm."
- Unspecified past experience uses present perfect: "I have been to China twice."
- An action at a point in the past uses past continuous: "At this time last year we were planning a trip to Romania."
- "Will" for a quick, sudden decision: "I'll get the door."
- "Before" + past simple clause: "Before my alarm went off, I heard a loud boom."
- Action that started in the past and continues: present perfect continuous + will: "I have been learning English for four years and I will continue to do so."
- Evidence that something is about to happen: "going to." "There's one minute left; our team is going to win."
- Subjunctive: "The doctor recommends that she get some rest." (Spoken English often says "gets.")
- "By the time" + simple past, main clause past perfect: "By the time I arrived, everyone had already eaten."

## 604. TRY LISTENING FOR KEY DETAILS | Advanced Practice Lesson
- **Details** = specific facts that support the main idea (hours, dates, names, numbers).
- **Content words** carry meaning: nouns, main verbs, adjectives, adverbs. In "The cat was sleeping on the bed," the content words are cat, sleeping, bed.
- Listen for content words: "a small red car" and "a big blue car" differ only in their content words.
- Detail questions appear in tests like IELTS and TOEFL.
- Practice: listen twice, then answer the detail questions.
- Tip: watch with English subtitles to match fast, contracted speech.
- Passage facts:
  - **Jane Austen**: born 16 Dec 1775 (captioned "7075"). English novelist of romantic fiction; comic stories; women depend on marriage for social standing.
  - **Florence Nightingale**: English nurse; helped create modern nursing; led nurses in the Crimean War; first woman to receive the Order of Merit; "the Lady with the Lamp."
  - **Marie Curie**: born 7 Nov 1867; Polish-French physicist and chemist; first woman professor at the University of Paris; first woman to win a Nobel Prize and first person to win two (physics and chemistry); discovered radium and polonium.

## 605. Take This Advanced Vocabulary Quiz | Learn New Words
- **trepidation** (n) – fear or anxiety about what may happen – "They entered with great trepidation." Synonym: apprehension.
- **brazen** (adj) – bold, without shame – "She brazenly told her boss he was incompetent."
- **frugal** (adj) – careful with money; thrifty – "He's so frugal he never buys anything." Synonyms: thrifty; cheap (harsher).
- **candid** (adj) – honest, truthful, straightforward – "The owner was candid about the car's problems."
- **pernicious** (adj) – harmful in a gradual or subtle way – "a pernicious effect." Synonym: insidious.
- **exonerated** (v/adj) – proven innocent; cleared of blame – "New evidence exonerated the suspect."
- **cynical** (adj) – believing people act only out of self-interest – "cynical about politics." Noun: cynic.
- **impeccable** (adj) – without fault or error – "an impeccable reputation."
- **flagrant** (adj) – obvious and disgraceful – "a flagrant foul."
- **replete with** (adj phrase) – full of – "replete with dialogue."
- **sesquipedalian** (adj) – characterized by long words (rare) – "sesquipedalian surnames."

## 606. Take Your SPEAKING FLUENCY to the NEXT LEVEL ⬆️
- Learn one new word or phrase every day.
- Keep reviewing old words. Repetition moves them into long-term memory.
- Talk about many topics (education, stereotypes, crime), not only work and home.
- Try role plays, presentations, and debates to use the language in new ways.
- Confidence comes from correct pronunciation, grammar, and vocabulary. Take every chance to speak. "Practice makes perfect."

## 607. Take the CONDITIONALS QUIZ | Advanced Grammar
- **Zero** (general truth): if + present simple, present simple. "If you touch fire, you get burned."
- **First** (real future): if + present simple, will + base verb. "If I go to the party, I'll call you."
- **Second** (imaginary): if + past simple, would + base verb. "If I had five dollars, I would buy a coffee."
- **Third** (unreal past): if + past perfect, would have + past participle. "If I had known it was going to rain, I would have brought my umbrella."
- **Mixed** (past condition, present result): if + past perfect, would + base verb. "If I had eaten breakfast, I wouldn't be so hungry now."
- The result can come first. Use a comma only when the if-clause comes first.
- Second conditional: "if I were you" is the formal form. "If I was" is common in speech.
- Rewrite: "I didn't take the bus, so I didn't arrive on time" = "If I had taken the bus, I would have arrived on time."
- Rewrite: "I'm shy, so I didn't go over and talk to her" = "If I weren't so shy, I would have gone over and talked to her."

## 608. Take vs Have  | How to Build Vocabulary with Useful Collocations
- Collocations are words that often go together. Learn the verb with its noun.
- "Have" for possession ("I have a phone") is not used in the continuous: not "I am having a phone." Collocations like "have breakfast" can be continuous: "I'm having breakfast."
- **take a walk** – go for a walk
- **take a bus / taxi / plane** – travel by transport
- **take a step** – move toward a goal
- **take action** – act
- **take control / take charge / take command** – lead a situation
- **take responsibility** – accept duty or blame
- **take advantage (of)** – use someone or something for benefit
- **take time** – need a period of time ("takes minutes, days, years")
- **take an eternity** – take a very long time
- **take notes / a photo / a nap / a break** – write, photograph, sleep briefly, rest
- **take advice** – follow advice
- **take a risk** – take a chance
- **have breakfast / lunch / dinner** – eat a meal
- **have a snack / a drink / a bite to eat** – eat or drink something
- **have a headache / an injury / the flu / a cold** – health problems
- **have problems / issues / trouble** – difficulties
- **have an accident** – break or damage something by accident
- **have a chat / a talk / a conversation** – talk with someone
- **have an argument** – disagree with someone
- **have a good time / fun** – enjoy yourself
- **have a relationship** – be in a relationship
- **have sympathy (for)** – feel for someone

## 609. The 3rd Conditional EASILY EXPLAINED | English Grammar Lesson
- Use: an unreal past event that did not happen. You imagine a different result.
- Form: if + past perfect (had + past participle), would have + past participle. "If I had studied for the test, I would have gotten a good grade."
- Comma: use one when the if-clause comes first. No comma when the result comes first: "I would have taken a road trip if I had bought a car."

## 610. The BEST BOOKS for Beginner, Intermediate, & Advanced Learners 📚
Auto-captions are unreadable (mixed Vietnamese and English). No reliable content can be recovered. Topic only: book recommendations by level.

## 611. The BEST BOOKS for learning English 📚
- **Compelling Conversations** – speaking; discussion questions for each topic (about 50 per topic); best for teachers.
- **Speak English Like an American** – US-focused idioms taught through dialogues; not for beginners.
- **501 English Verbs** – verb conjugations and example uses; good for memorizing vocabulary.
- **English Grammar in Use** – self-study grammar with explanations, exercises, and answers.
- **Understanding and Using English Grammar** (Betty Azar) – classroom grammar with speaking and reading parts. Blue is advanced, gray is intermediate, red is beginner.
- **What a World** – short readings on world topics, with comprehension and vocabulary questions.
- **Well Said** and **Clear Speech** – pronunciation: individual sounds, mouth position, intonation, stress, connected speech.
- **Effective Academic Writing (book 2)** – intermediate academic writing: paragraphs, essays, transition words.
- **Presenting in English** – business presentations: structure, transitions, hooks, Q&A.
- Tip: read articles about topics you like (fashion, cars, food). Reading alone is passive, so books with questions make it active.

## 612. The BEST Listening & Speaking Activity | Practice English on Your Own
- Method: listen to a story twice for details. Then pause and retell it aloud using the on-screen clues.
- Numbers and years are the hardest details. Note them while listening.
- Story 1 (Eiffel Tower): built in Paris 1887–1889 for the World's Fair; 324 m tall; made of iron; meant to stand 20 years, then kept as a landmark.
- Story 2 (Snow): frozen water that forms high in the cold sky; comes in many shapes and sizes; becomes water above 0 °C; used for skiing, sledding, snowmen, igloos; lowers driving visibility; salt or sand on roads helps tires grip.
- **ad-lib** (v) – say something unplanned – "I ad-libbed a little."
- **botch** (v) – spoil by doing badly – "I really botched that beginning."
- **iffy** (adj) – uncertain, shaky – "The beginning was a little iffy."

## 613. The BEST Ways to Greatly Improve Your Fluency in 2021 & Beyond
- **Make time, not excuses.** Set a fixed slot, such as 10 minutes daily or 30 minutes three days a week.
- **Get out of your comfort zone.** Find a speaking partner, a local or online group, or a class. Add this to what you already do.
- **Know your limits.** Do not overdo it. Take a break when you need one. You can restart easily if you kept your study time.
- **Use all four skills:** listening, speaking, reading, writing. They connect.

## 614. The Health Vocab Native Speakers Use Every Day (Advanced Words & Phrases)
- **push oneself to the limit** (phrase) – make maximum physical effort – "Athletes push themselves to the limit in training."
- **out of shape** (adj) – unfit; lacking stamina – "I'm so out of shape, I get out of breath climbing stairs."
- **in shape** (adj) – fit – "After months of training, she is finally in shape."
- **get in shape** (phrase) – become fit – "I want to get in shape."
- **back on your feet** (idiom) – fully recovered from illness or injury – "You'll be back on your feet in no time."
- **on the mend** (idiom) – recovering, not fully yet – "I'm on the mend."
- **recuperate** (v) – recover through rest – "It took her a month to fully recuperate from surgery."
- **congenital** (adj) – present from birth – "a congenital heart defect."
- **at my wit's end** (idiom) – very frustrated, don't know what to do – "I've tried everything to fix this laptop. I'm at my wit's end."
- **at the end of your rope** (idiom) – no patience left.
- **reach a breaking point** (idiom) – can no longer cope – "After 80-hour weeks, he reached a breaking point."
- **on edge** (adj) – nervous, irritable – "Lack of sleep leaves me feeling on edge."
- **burnout** (n) – exhaustion from long-term stress – "Health workers suffered burnout."
- **languishing** (adj) – not depressed, but not thriving; stuck.
- **bottle up** (phrasal v) – hide emotions instead of expressing them – "bottle up your anger."

## 615. The MOST DIFFICULT Tongue Twister 😜 #interactiveenglish
- **sells** /sɛlz/ vs **shells** /ʃɛlz/ – "s" vs "sh." Say the s sound, then the sh sound.
- **she** /ʃiː/ vs **sea** /siː/ – "sh" vs "s" (seashells = sea + shells).
- Say "She sells seashells by the seashore" slowly, then faster.

## 618. The SECRETS to Understanding American FAST SPEECH 🇺🇸
- **Linking** – final consonant joins next word's vowel: "how's it going" -> "how-zit goin'".
- **Contractions/reductions** – "how is" -> "how's"; "we are" -> "we're"; "going to" -> "gonna"; "have to" -> "hafta"; "to" -> weak /tə/.
- **Dropping -ing g** – "going" -> "goin'".
- **Assimilation** – two sounds blend into a new one: did + you -> "did-ja" ("where did ya get that"); face + your -> "face-sure" (s+y -> sh).
- **Silent/dropped t** – "postpone" (silent t), "get that" (t dropped or stop-t).
- **Flap t** – t between vowels sounds like soft d: "meeting".
- **Intrusion** – add a slight w/y glide to link vowels: "do it" -> "do-w-it".
- Linking is weak, so run words together: "we live in an apartment" -> "wee liv-in-an apartment".

## 619. The TRUTH about Becoming Fluent in English
- You will not become fluent in a few months; set realistic short- and long-term goals (vocabulary, listening, speaking practice).
- "Perfect is the enemy of good enough" – don't aim for perfect English; mistakes are fine.
- Practical test of "good enough": hold conversations on many topics, read a book without constant dictionary use, understand ~90% of a movie.
- Fluency is subjective ("in the eye of the beholder") – only you can judge it; comfort and confidence are the signs.

## 621. The Word ANTICIPATE | Build Useful English Vocabulary
**anticipate** (v, 4 syllables, stress 2nd: an-TIC-i-pate) – 1) expect/predict something will happen; 2) look forward to – "I anticipate a fun lesson."; "I anticipate winter."
**anticipated** (adj) – expected; "highly/most anticipated" – "the most anticipated movie"
**unanticipated** (adj) – unexpected, took you by surprise – "an unanticipated problem"

## 622. Things You Should NOT Say to Women (in English) 🚫
(Gist: Cultural lesson on comments seen as rude/inappropriate in Western (esp. US) culture, especially professionally; keep compliments general ("you look great").)
- Avoid: "You look tired / sick"; "You should smile"; "How much do you weigh?"; "How old are you?"; "You don't need makeup"; "Are you sure you want to eat that?"; "Women are emotional/crazy"; "You look great for your age"; "You've lost/gained weight"; "Are you pregnant?"
- Also avoid asking a teacher/coworker for their mobile number; money, age, weight, personal life are private topics in the US.
**taboo** (adj/n) – topic people avoid discussing
**backhanded compliment** (n) – praise that also insults – "You look great for your age."
**stereotyping** (n) – making assumptions about everyone in a group
**construed** (v) – interpreted – "may be construed as rude"
**no-no** (n, informal) – something not allowed/appropriate
**rule of thumb** (idiom) – general guideline

## 623. Think You're Fluent? This English Grammar Quiz Will Test You!
- **Predicate noun** – noun after a linking verb that renames the subject: "Mark became an **actor**."
- **Indefinite pronoun** stands alone ("He doesn't talk to anyone"; "There are several in the closet"); **indefinite adjective** modifies a noun ("He doesn't know many people").
- **like** as preposition = typical of: "It's just like Wes to show up late."
- A sentence can have two objects of prepositions: "after a few **minutes** ... in the **drawer**".
- Present simple for scheduled future: "The movie **starts** tomorrow at 8."
- Present perfect continuous: "I **have been waiting** for the bus for 20 minutes."
- **slink / slunk** (v) – walk away quietly unnoticed – "I slunk out of the room."
- **be going to** = prior plan ("Are you going to call them back?"); **will** = instant decision ("I'll answer it").
- Plurals: roof -> roofs/rooves; fungus -> fungi; emphasis -> emphases; elf -> elves.
- Figures of speech: **simile** (like/as: "tall as a tree"); **onomatopoeia** (boom, crack, splash); **personification** ("The wind howled all night"); **alliteration** ("Peter Piper picked...").
- Spelling: **nauseous**, **Wednesday**, **entrepreneur** (stress on last syllable), **narcissistic**.
- Transformations: "Do you **feel like watching** a movie?"; "I wish I **had spoken** to you sooner."; "It's only **a matter of time** before they learn the truth."; "My boss is **intent on not giving** me a raise." (intent on + -ing)

## 624. Think Your English Is Advanced? Prove It in This Grammar Challenge!
- **used to** (verb, past habit) + base verb: "I didn't **use to** wake up this early" (negative drops the d); **be used to** + -ing (adj, accustomed): "I'm used to running in hot weather."
- **to me** = opinion/perception ("sounds difficult to me"; "Can you read this to me?"); **for me** = how it affects/benefits you ("too difficult for me"). Tip: check Google Ngram Viewer for which is more common ("new to me" > "new for me").
- **from** = starting point ("from an early age", "from March to May"); **since** = point in time continuing to now ("has been here since 10").
- **in spite of = despite** (contrast, like although); both correct: "despite the rain".
- **been to** = visited and returned; **gone to** = went and not back yet ("She's gone to New York").
- Prepositions: **in** = enclosed space; **on** = surface/platform ("on YouTube"); **at** = specific point.
- Relative pronouns: **that** (people/animals/things, restrictive), **which** (animals/things, esp. non-restrictive; common in UK), **who** (people).
- **rummage through** (v) – search quickly and carelessly
- **e.g.** = for example (lists); **i.e.** = in other words.
- **must** = required/rule ("Applicants must submit..."); **have to** = necessity ("I have to make an appointment").
- **Split infinitive** ("to boldly go") – technically less preferred but acceptable, esp. in speech.
- **some** in affirmatives ("I have some pizza"); **any** in negatives/questions ("Did you catch any fish?").
- **who** = subject; **whom** = object ("Whom do you believe?" – test: "I believe him"); in speech, "who" is more common.

## 625. This is why your English teachers encourage you to make mistakes... 🚫
- Don't worry about mistakes; they're part of learning. Quotes: "A person who never makes mistakes never tried anything new" (Einstein); "The expert at anything was once a beginner."
- Laugh at mistakes and use them to learn: ask questions, understand the error.
- Music, reading, TV and movies expose you to correct English; you'll start self-correcting ("that doesn't sound right").
**kick the bucket** (idiom) – to die
**Sharing is caring.** (saying)

## 626. Try This! 5 Tricky English Adjectives Every Learner Should Master 🇺🇸
**unprecedented** /ʌnˈprɛsɪdɛntɪd/ – never happened before; stress 2nd syllable
**preposterous** /prɪˈpɑːstərəs/ – ridiculous; stress 2nd syllable
**simultaneous** /ˌsaɪməlˈteɪniəs/ – at the same time; 5 syllables, stress 3rd
**phenomenal** /fɪˈnɑːmɪnəl/ – ph = f; stress 2nd syllable
**sophisticated** /səˈfɪstɪkeɪtɪd/ – complex/advanced; first syllable is a schwa, ph = f

## 627. UNLOCK THE AMERICAN ACCENT 🇺🇸 | Useful Pronunciation Tips
1. **Elision of t** between consonants: exactly, mortgage, Christmas, last night, mashed potatoes.
2. **Elision of d** between consonants: sandwich, lands, used car, sand castle (t in castle also silent).
3. **Drop the g in -ing**: taking -> takin', playing, watching.
4. **Flap t** (soft d) between vowels: water, beautiful, "meet at the office".
5. **Pronounce the r's** (American vs British): father, whether, better.
6. **Use the schwa** in common words: banana; unstressed "you" -> "ya" ("Are ya gonna write to me?").
7. **Reductions**: going to -> gonna, want to -> wanna.
8. **Stress rule**: words ending -tion/-sion stress the syllable before: pronunciAtion, comprehEnsion.
9. **Stress rule**: words ending -ty, -cy, -phy, -gy, -al stress the third-to-last syllable: elecTRIcity, phoTOgraphy, biOlogy.
10. **Loanwords** keep foreign spelling/pronunciation: champagne (French), marijuana (Spanish), pajamas (Hindi), samurai (Japanese).

## 628. USEFUL HALLOWEEN VOCABULARY 👻 | Words & phrases you should know
**vampire** (n) – dead person who comes back and sucks blood
**jack-o'-lantern** (n) – carved pumpkin with a face and a candle inside
**petrified** (adj) – so scared you can't move (more advanced than "terrified")
**creepy** (adj) – strange, slightly frightening, uncomfortable – "a creepy place"
**spooky** (adj) – scary; the most common Halloween word
**eerie** (adj) – strange and frightening in a mysterious way – "an eerie feeling"
**paranormal** (adj) – can't be explained by science – "paranormal activity"
**séance** (n) – meeting to communicate with the dead
**lurk** (v) – be present in a threatening hidden way – "lurking in the shadows / over your shoulder"
**exorcism** (n) – forcing an evil spirit out by prayer or magic (exorcist = person)
**pitchfork** (n) – long farming fork, associated with angry mobs
**superstition** (n) – belief not based on science or proof – "a black cat crossing your path is bad luck"
**grim reaper** (n) – skeleton figure personifying death
**cackle** (n/v) – harsh laugh of a witch
**gory** (adj) – involving violence and blood – "gory movie/details"; **gore** (n) – blood and violence

## 629. USEFUL LISTENING SKILLS | Main Idea - Key Details - Make Inferences - Interpret Meaning
- **Main idea**: grasp the "big picture" first; in short talks it's often stated at the start. "Don't miss the forest for the trees" = don't get stuck on small details and lose the big picture.
- **Key details**: listen for adjectives, names, dates, numbers; picture them in your mind to recall later. Tests (IELTS/TOEFL) rephrase details ("quarter to 11" = 10:45).
- **Making inferences**: use clues to guess what isn't stated (soaking wet + pitter-patter on window = rain). "Read between the lines" = understand hidden meaning.
- **Interpret meaning**: intonation (rising/falling voice; rising = question: "I locked the door?") and emphasis (stressed word changes meaning: "She isn't DRIVING to the park" = she goes another way). "It's not what you say but how you say it."
- Tip: get lots of exposure (audio books, conversations, lessons).
**hands down** (idiom) – definitely, absolutely
**hit it off** (idiom) – instantly connect and get along well
**turbulent / turbulence** (adj/n) – bumpy, unsteady (flight or relationship)
**monotonous** (adj) – boring and repetitive
**PB&J** (n) – peanut butter and jelly sandwich
**quarter to eleven** – 10:45

## 630. USEFUL RESTAURANT VOCABULARY YOU NEED TO KNOW 🍽️
**waiter / waitress / server** (n) – brings food to tables (server = gender-neutral)
**chef** (n) – trained, most senior cook
**host / hostess** (n) – greets customers, takes reservations, seats guests, hands out menus
**party** (n) – the group you're with – "How many are in your party?"; "Smith, party of five"
**table / booth** (n) – seating options
**silverware / cutlery / utensils** (n) – spoons, forks, knives (silverware most common)
**beverage** (n) – drink; the word used on menus
**condiments** (n) – salt, pepper, ketchup, mustard, hot sauce on the table
**à la carte** (adj) – each dish ordered and priced separately
**appetizer / starter** (n) – small dish before the main meal
**entrée** (n) – main dish (US usage); "main course"
**side dish / sides** (n) – extra dish served with the entrée (vegetables, salad)

## 631. USE CONTEXT CLUES TO UNDERSTAND NEW VOCABULARY
- **Context clues** = hints in the same sentence or nearby sentences that reveal an unknown word's meaning. Method: 1) find the unknown word; 2) find clues; 3) guess the meaning. Great for reading comprehension and vocabulary. (Cambridge Dictionary for simple definitions.)
**grotesque** (adj) – strange, unpleasant – "a bloody, grotesque movie"
**perilous** (adj) – extremely dangerous – "a perilous journey"
**obfuscate** (v) – make something hard to understand – "obfuscate the truth"
**nonplussed** (adj) – surprised and confused – "replied, somewhat nonplussed"
**diligently** (adv) – carefully and with a lot of effort
**I'm beat** (adj, informal) – extremely tired
**call it a day** (idiom) – stop working for now
**tantrum** (n) – sudden period of uncontrolled anger – "throw/have a tantrum"
**at my wit's end** (idiom) – so worried/confused you don't know what to do next
**puerile** (adj) – childish, silly (esp. of adults/teens acting immaturely) – "puerile behavior"

## 632. US Election Aftermath (What happens now?) | Reading + Vocabulary
(Gist: after the Nov 2020 US election, states certify results (Dec 8 deadline for disputes), electors vote Dec 14, inauguration in January; Trump hadn't conceded and was contesting results.)
**president-elect** (n) – winner who hasn't taken office yet
**incumbent** (n) – person currently holding the position
**project** (v) – predict a future result from current data – "NBC projected Biden the winner"
**concede** (v) – admit unwillingly that something is true – "concede the election"
**inauguration** (n) – ceremony officially putting someone in office
**certify** (v) – officially state that something is true/accurate
**elector** (n) – person who casts a state's Electoral College vote
**cast a ballot/vote** (collocation) – vote; **on behalf of** – representing
**vital** (adj) – very important
**divisive** (adj) – causing great disagreement within a group
**peaceful transfer of power** (phrase) – outgoing leaders hand control to newly elected ones
**contest** (v/adj) – say something is unfair and try to change it – "a contested election"
**congenial** (adj) – friendly, pleasant
**albeit** (conj) – although – "observed, albeit less graciously"
**counter** (v) – respond with an opposing view – "counterargument"
**fraud** (n) – crime of deceiving people
**joint** (adj) – shared between two or more – "joint statement"
**allegation** (n) – claim made without proof; **unfounded** (adj) – not based on fact
**dismiss** (v) – reject as not worth considering – "lawsuit dismissed"
**bolster** (v) – support and make stronger – "bolster a claim"
**re-** (prefix) – again – "recount"

## 633. US Regional Vocabulary You Need to Know 🇺🇸  (+ Lesson Notes)

**soda** (n) – carbonated drink, most common across the US – "Would you like a soda?"
**pop** (n) – same drink; Midwest (e.g., Wisconsin, Minnesota) – "Would you like a pop?"
**Coke** (n) – any carbonated drink; South – "Would you like a Coke?"
**transplant** (n) – person who moved to a new place – "many transplants across the country"
**firefly** (n) – insect that lights up at night; most of US – "fireflies in the evening"
**lightning bug** (n) – same insect; South – "watch the lightning bugs"
**you guys** (pron) – a group of people, any gender; most of US – "you guys want to go?"
**y'all** (pron) – you all; South – "Y'all want a Coke?"
**garage sale / yard sale** (n) – informal sale of used items in front of your home; most of US – "a yard sale this weekend"
**tag sale** (n) – same as garage sale; Northeast (e.g., Connecticut)
**trash can / garbage can** (n) – bin for trash; garbage can preferred in Pacific Northwest
**dumpster** (n) – large outdoor trash container
**water fountain / drinking fountain** (n) – fountain for drinking; water fountain on East Coast, drinking fountain on West Coast
**bubbler** (n) – drinking fountain; some Northeast and Midwest areas
**lollipop / sucker** (n) – hard candy on a stick; "sucker" used in South and Midwest, also slang for a gullible person – "He's a sucker."
**freeway** (n) – road with controlled access (no cross traffic, no stops, enter by ramp); West Coast preference
**highway** (n) – larger road that may have cross traffic and stops; East Coast often uses it interchangeably with freeway
**interstate** (n) – freeway that crosses state lines; odd numbers run north-south, even numbers east-west
**grocery cart** (n) – cart at a grocery store; "carriage" (Northeast), "buggy" (South/Midwest)
**shopping cart** (n) – same cart when not in a grocery store
**teeter-totter** (n) – playground seesaw; most of US – "the kids on the teeter-totter"
**seesaw** (n) – same thing; South and British English

## 634. Understand EVERYTHING in English | Improve Your Listening

- Focus on content words (nouns, verbs, adjectives) that paint a picture in your mind; skim articles and prepositions.
- Be an active listener: answer questions, summarize, predict what comes next, or talk back, instead of passive background listening.
- Follow the signposts: transition words (first, also, in the beginning) show the order of ideas.
- Listen at your level: if you understand most content words, the level is right. Slower speech is good practice for fast speech later.
- Use listening material that requires a response, such as question-and-answer activities or test-style listening exercises.

## 635. Unlock Your English Fluency with These Speaking Hacks

- Shadowing: repeat right after the speaker, copying pronunciation, speed, intonation, and emphasis. Let your ears do the work; no rules needed.
- Connected speech to notice: "did you" blends into a "j" sound ("didja"); H drops in "him" mid-sentence; flap T in "meet him" (sounds close to "medium").
- English is stress-timed: stressed syllables come at roughly even intervals, and unstressed words get shortened.
- Read several articles on one topic to spot collocations, e.g., "rampant inflation."
- Vary opinion phrases: "in my view," "in my opinion," "I have no doubt that," "moreover," "furthermore," "in addition." They are not always interchangeable.
- Build confidence by practicing a little every day, stepping out of your comfort zone in a safe setting, and fixing mistakes without anxiety (say "take a vacation," not "make a vacation").

## 636. Unlock the American Accent: Speak Smooth, Natural English Now!

**ought to** – reduces to "oughta"; T is a flap T (sounds like a soft D)
**What do you** – sounds like "what-a-ya"; "do" reduced to a schwa
**see a** – add a slight "y" glide between: "see-ya-a movie"
**go to a** – add a slight "w" glide between: "go-to-wa concert"
**or** – reduced to "er" in "a movie or go"
**leaning** – drop the G: "leanin'" sounds like "lean in"
**towards a** – final consonant links to "a" (towards-a movie)
**but I** – flap T; links into "I"
**want to** – reduces to "wanna" ("I don't wanna...")
**make a decision** – collocation uses "make," not "take," with decision; "make a" links
**just let me** – T drops between consonants, sounds like "lemme"
**what you want** – "ch" sound from assimilation, sounds like "whatcha want"

## 637. Upper-Intermediate (B2) Expressions | Strengthen Your Vocabulary 💪

**the other way around** (phr) – in the opposite order or reverse – "In other countries it's the other way around."
**as far as I'm concerned** (phr) – in my opinion; polite and professional – "As far as I'm concerned, English is useful."
**at all costs** (phr) – whatever happens; must be done or avoided – "Protect the code at all costs."
**take someone's breath away** (phr) – be very beautiful or surprising – "Your art collection never fails to take my breath away."
**keep an open mind** (phr) – wait for all the facts before deciding; adj. "open-minded" – "Keep an open mind."
**lose track (of)** (phr) – no longer know what is happening or forget – "I lost track of time."
**a pain in the neck** (idiom) – very annoying person or thing; "pain in the butt" is stronger – "Commuting is such a pain in the neck."
**up to a point** (phr) – partly, to a limited degree – "Your method worked up to a point."
**cross someone's mind** (phr) – occur to someone as a thought; often in questions or negatives – "It never crossed my mind."
**break the ice** (idiom) – make people who don't know each other relax; noun "icebreaker" – "Let's break the ice."
**break someone's heart** (idiom) – make someone who loves you very sad; adj. "heartbroken" – "He broke her heart."
**can't be bothered** (phr) – too lazy or tired to do something; more common in British English – "I can't be bothered to cook tonight."

## 638. Upper-Intermediate (B2) Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary

**in that case** (phr) – because of the situation just mentioned – "No apple juice? In that case, orange juice."
**with respect to** (phr) – in connection with; formal, common in news – "...with respect to International guidelines."
**might as well / may as well** (phr) – suggest doing something since there's nothing better to do – "We may as well start eating."
**in a row** (phr) – one after another without a break – "The team has won three years in a row."
**take something into account** (phr) – consider something when judging a situation; also "take into consideration" – "I hope my boss takes into account the extra weekend."
**behind bars** (phr) – in prison – "He's going to spend four months behind bars."
**on the one hand ... on the other hand** (phr) – compare two opposing facts or views – "On the one hand I'd like to relax; on the other hand, Emmy wakes me early."
**make matters worse** (phr) – make a bad situation even more unpleasant; "to make matters worse" adds another problem – "The caterer canceled, and to make matters worse, the best man got sick."
**break someone's heart** (idiom) – repeat from 637 – "You broke my heart."
**on the contrary** (phr) – the opposite of what was just said; more formal – "On the contrary, people hit the like button all the time."
**more or less** (phr) – approximately; "give or take" is used for numbers – "She more or less started the business on her own."
**in the short term / medium term / long term** (phr) – in the immediate, middle, or distant future – "Volatile in the short term, but optimistic in the long term."

## 639. Upper-Intermediate (B2) Phrases to Supercharge Your Vocabulary 💪

**all along** (adv) – from the very beginning – "It's been there all along."
**keep in mind / bear in mind** (phr) – remember when making a decision; "keep" is more common – "Keep in mind that..."
**at your (earliest) convenience** (phr) – when it suits you (earliest = as soon as possible) – "Please call me back at your earliest convenience."
**keep someone company** (phr) – stay with someone so they're not alone – "I volunteer to keep him company."
**set an example / set a good example** (phr) – behave in a way others should copy – "Teachers should set a good example."
**have an eye for something** (phr) – be good at noticing a type of thing – "She has an eye for fashion."
**at ease** (adj/phr) – relaxed, free from worry; used as a military command too – "He looked at ease during the interview."
**put someone at ease / put someone's mind at ease** (phr) – make someone feel calm or reassured – "Her text really put me at ease."
**in due course / in due time** (phr) – at a suitable time in the future – "We will announce the opening in due time."
**fair enough** (phr) – reasonable or acceptable – "You changed your mind? Fair enough."
**get on someone's nerves** (idiom) – annoy someone a lot – "Wes got on my nerves at the party."
**in that case** (phr) – repeat from 638 – "In that case, I'm going to keep you company."
**all over again** (phr) – start again from the very beginning – "We had to do it all over again."

## 640. Upper-Intermediate B2 Vocabulary Every Fluent Speaker Knows

**nonsense** (n, uncountable) – foolish or untrue talk; "absolute/utter/pure nonsense" – "The new policy is absolute nonsense."
**stopover** (n) – short stay in the middle of a long journey; US: **layover** (usually shorter) – "a stopover in Singapore"
**tragedy** (n) – event causing great pain or suffering; "personal/national tragedy" – "It was a terrible tragedy."
**dose** (n) – measured amount of medicine; figuratively an unpleasant experience – "a dose of your own medicine"
**rehearsal** (n) – practice before a performance; "dress rehearsal" is the final one – "The band was disorganized during rehearsal."
**distinguish** (v) – recognize the difference; "distinguish A from B" or "between A and B" – "hard to distinguish the real brand from a knockoff"
**treat** (v) – behave toward someone; give medical care; buy something for someone – "treat someone like dirt," "treat someone for a disease," "treat someone to something"
**exploit** (v) – use something for advantage (neutral/positive), or treat people unfairly (negative) – "exploit the potential of social media"
**wander** (v) – move around without a specific purpose; "wander through" – "We wandered through the old town." (Don't confuse with "wonder.")
**mumble** (v) – speak quietly and unclearly; "mumble under one's breath" – "Stop mumbling and speak up."
**forthcoming** (adj) – happening soon; or willing to share information (opposite: "not forthcoming") – "the forthcoming school events"
**sharp** (adj) – intelligent, notices things quickly – "He has an incredibly sharp mind."
**worthwhile** (adj) – useful or good enough for the time or money spent; "it is worthwhile to do..." – "It is worthwhile to watch these lessons."
**last-minute** (adj, hyphen before noun) – at the latest possible time – "last-minute changes"; noun phrase is "at the last minute" (no hyphen)
**self-confident** (adj) – sure of your own ability; positive, unlike "arrogant" – "a more independent and self-confident person"
**simultaneously** (adv) – at exactly the same time; stress on the 3rd syllable – "stream to multiple devices simultaneously"
**preferably** (adv) – ideally, a soft preference, not a requirement – "an apartment with three bedrooms, preferably near a park"
**eagerly** (adv) – with excitement and enthusiasm – "eagerly awaiting the summer break"
**willingly** (adv) – by choice, not forced; opposite is **unwillingly** – "many parents willingly contribute their time"
**patiently** (adv) – waiting calmly without getting angry – "wait patiently"

## 641. Upper-Intermediate Phrases (B1 + B2) You Definitely Need to Know

**in advance** (phr) – before a particular time or event – "We should prepare the presentation in advance."
**take it easy** (phr) – rest and relax, or calm down (as a command) – "Take it easy, you need to calm down."
**in the end** (phr) – finally, after everything is considered; not the same as "at the end" – "In the end, we decided to get sushi."
**drag and drop** (v phr) – move something with the mouse from one place to another – "Drag and drop the files to upload them."
**make a face** (phr) – make an expression that shows you don't like something – "You may make a face when you eat something you don't like."
**wait a minute / wait a moment / wait a second** (phr) – to interrupt, get attention, or say you just thought of something – "Wait a second, just turn around."
**make matters worse** (phr) – make a bad situation even worse – "Schools are overcrowded, and to make matters worse..."
**run into** (phrasal v) – (1) drive into something by accident; (2) meet someone unexpectedly – "I ran into my ex today."
**out of nowhere** (adv phr) – very suddenly or unexpectedly – "The horse came out of nowhere to win the race."
**neither ... nor** (conj) – not one and not the other – "I have neither the time nor inclination to get involved."
**on top of that** (phr) – in addition, usually something unpleasant – "We missed our bus, and on top of that, the next one broke down."
**the latter** (n phr) – near the end of something; "the latter half/part" – "construction will begin in the latter part of the month"

## 642. Use CONTEXT CLUES to Build Your Vocabulary

**cognizant** (adj, formal) – aware of; often "cognizant of the fact that" – "be cognizant of the fact that..."
**veracity** (n) – truth or credibility – "doubts were cast on the veracity of her alibi"
**alibi** (n) – proof that you were somewhere else when a crime happened – "her alibi"
**truculent** (adj) – aggressive, unpleasant, antagonistic (negative) – "Trump angers UK with truculent tweet"
**affable** (adj) – friendly, positive (contrast with truculent) – "an affable host"
**call the shots** (idiom) – be in charge and make the decisions – "I'm in charge and I'm calling the shots."
**pontificate** (v) – express opinions in a pompous way, without real knowledge – "pontificate from prejudice"
**banal** (adj) – ordinary, dull, boring; synonyms: mundane, rudimentary – "banal activities like defrosting the freezer"
**janky** (adj, slang) – poor quality, often used for electronics – "my mic is kind of janky"
**mockish** (adj) – exaggerated, overly sentimental, false – "a mockish tour of smiles"
**exigent** (adj) – urgent, pressing – "exigent circumstances may require..."
**bamboozle** (v) – trick or deceive someone – "I got bamboozled into telling them my credit card number."
**cajole** (v) – persuade someone by pleasant talk or flattery – "My daughter cajoles me into buying her ice cream."
**toe the line** (idiom) – do what is expected without causing trouble – "learn how to toe the line"

## 643. Use PREFIXES to Build Your Vocabulary

- **un-** (not; remove; reverse) – unfinished, undo, unpack
- **dis-** (not; opposite of) – dissatisfied, disqualified, disagree
- **il- / im- / in- / ir-** (not; without) – illegal, improbable, insecure, irresponsible, inconvenient, irresistible, imbalance
- **out-** (beyond; outside) – outrun, outlast, outlaw
- **auto-** (self) – autobiography, autopilot, automobile
- **de-** (lessen; change; off) – devalue, deactivate, debug, depreciation
- **re-** (again) – rewrite, reapply
- **mis-** (incorrectly; badly) – misinterpret, misbehave, misunderstanding
- **omni-** (all; every) – omnipresent, omnidirectional
- **uber-** (very, beyond; informal slang) – "uber fancy," "uber cool"
- Tip: learn each prefix's meaning, then spot it while reading; reading lets you pause and see which words a prefix attaches to.
- Practice answers: impossible, defrost, unavailable, misinformed (vs. uninformed), indispensable.

## 644. Use These (B2) Phrases to Instantly Sound More Fluent!

**all of a sudden** (phr) – very quickly or without warning; can go at the beginning or end of a sentence – "All of a sudden, I got really tired."
**the last minute** (n phr) – the latest possible time; the final T in "last" is silent – "She submitted the report at the last minute."
**for the sake of someone / for someone's sake** (phr) – in order to help or benefit someone – "They stayed together for the sake of the children."
**by far** (adv) – by a large amount; often before a superlative – "English is by far the most commonly used language."
**as far as I know / so far as I know** (phr) – what I believe is true, though I don't have all the facts – "As far as I know, the staff wasn't hurt."
**can't take your eyes off someone/something** (phr) – can't stop looking at it – "I can't take my eyes off that dessert."
**take advantage of someone/something** (phr) – B1 positive: use an opportunity well; B2 negative: treat someone unfairly for gain – "He's taking advantage of her."
**as a result of** (phr) – because of; same as "due to" – "as a result of the city's budget deficit"
**in your 30s / 40s / 50s** (phr) – a 10-year age range, used to estimate someone's age – "He's in his 50s."
**one way or another** (phr) – somehow, by some means; confident expression – "We'll get that money one way or another."
**make a fool of someone / make a fool of yourself** (phr) – trick someone or make them look stupid – "She made a fool of him in front of his friends."
**one by one** (adv phr) – separately or one after another – "The children exited the classroom one by one."

## 645. Use These Advanced English Phrases to Sound Like a Native 🔥

**cross that bridge when we come to it / when we get to it** (idiom) – deal with a problem if and when it happens, instead of worrying now – "We'll cross that bridge when we get to it."
**get the short end of the stick** (idiom) – be treated unfairly or get the worst outcome (from drawing straws) – "He's getting the short end of the stick."
**throw someone under the bus** (idiom) – blame, betray, or abandon someone for selfish reasons – "She's quick to throw her brother under the bus."
