## 646. Used to vs Use to | Confusing Grammar You Should Know

- "used to" + base verb = a repeated past habit or a past situation that no longer applies – "I used to run around the lake twice a week."
- Form does not change for other subjects (no -s, no conjugation): "He used to live in Korea."
- Questions and negatives use did/didn't + "use to," spelled without the d – "Did you use to study English?" / "I didn't use to have hair like this."
- Pronunciation of "use to" and "used to" is the same (the D and T blend), so the difference only matters in writing.
- "be/get used to" + noun or -ing (adjective, spelled "used"): accustomed to – "I'm used to hot weather," "We had to get used to it."
- Dictionaries label it differently (modal verb, verb, or adjective); use the meaning to decide, not the label.

## 647. Useful (C1) #Collocations with BROADEN 🌅

**broaden your horizons** (idiom) – increase the range of your experiences or knowledge – "Travel can broaden your horizons."
**broaden your mind** (phr) – gain more knowledge or understanding – "Does travel broaden the mind?"
**broaden the scope** (phr) – widen the range of something – "broaden the scope of the project"
**broaden support / broaden talks** (phr) – widen backing or discussions – "Politicians may need to broaden their support."

## 648. Useful Adverb Collocations to Build Your Vocabulary

**strongly** + condemn, disagree (with), suggest, recommend, support – "I strongly recommend it."
**fully** + aware, awake, operational, upright – "I am fully aware of the risks."
**absolutely / utterly** + amazed, alone, appalled, beautiful, convinced, devastated, furious, miserable, ridiculous, stupid – "We were utterly devastated."
**deeply** + concerned, regret, divided, ashamed (mostly negative) – "people are deeply divided"
**highly** + unlikely, recommend, unusual, controversial, sophisticated, educated, intelligent, paid – "I highly recommend it."
**grossly** + exaggerated, negligent, inaccurate, mistreated, unfair, reckless, misled, underestimated (negative) – "a grossly exaggerated story"
**mildly** + upset, concerned, surprised – "I'm mildly surprised."
**widely** + available, known, accepted – "It is widely accepted."
**perfectly** + normal, acceptable, willing – "It's perfectly normal to make mistakes."

## 649. Useful Beach Vocabulary to Improve Your Fluency

**tide** (n) – rise and fall of sea level; high tide / low tide; happens about twice a day – "It's high tide."
**current** (n) – water moving in one direction – "There's a strong current."
**undertow** (n) – current below the surface moving another way; can pull swimmers out – "A strong undertow is dangerous."
**sandals** (n) – open shoes with a sole held by straps around the feet
**flip-flops** (n) – loose shoes held by a Y-shaped strap
**lifeguard** (n) – person who watches swimmers and keeps them safe – "She sits on the lifeguard stand."
**seagull** (n) – white bird found near the ocean
**sand dune** (n) – small mound of sand
**grain of sand** (n) – one piece of sand
**palm tree** (n) – tree with fan-shaped leaves at the top
**catamaran** (n) – boat with two parallel hulls joined by a deck
**longboard** (n) – long surfboard you stand on to ride waves
**boogie board** (n) – small foam board you lie on to ride waves
**skimboard** (n) – thin board you throw onto a thin layer of water on sand and glide across
**swimsuit** (n) – general word for swimwear
**bikini** (n) – two-piece swimsuit
**one-piece** (n) – one-piece swimsuit
**trunks** (n) – men's swim shorts
**speedo** (n) – tight, form-fitting swimsuit (from a brand name)

## 650. Useful Business Expressions to Sound More Professional

**ASAP** (adv) – as soon as possible; shows urgency – "Call me back ASAP."
**go the extra mile** (idiom) – do more than expected – "She always goes the extra mile."
**look at the big picture** (idiom) – see the whole situation, not just details – "Let's look at the big picture."
**24/7** (adv) – 24 hours a day, 7 days a week – "We're open 24/7."
**get the ball rolling** (idiom) – start an activity – "Let's get the ball rolling."
**pass the buck** (idiom) – shift responsibility to someone else – "Don't pass the buck."
**back to the drawing board** (idiom) – start over after an idea fails – "The plan failed. Back to the drawing board."
**back to square one** (idiom) – same meaning as above
**red tape** (n) – too many rules and too much bureaucracy – "We got stuck in red tape."
**I need it yesterday** (idiom) – I need it right away; it should already be done – "I need the report yesterday."
**I'm afraid I didn't get that** (phrase) – polite way to ask someone to repeat, esp. on the phone

## 651. Useful COLLOCATIONS in English (+ Lesson Notes)

**burst into tears** (verb + noun) – start crying suddenly – "Last night he burst into tears."
**pay attention** (verb + noun) – focus – "Are you paying attention?"
**have a baby** (verb + noun) – be giving birth / become a parent – "We're having a baby." (also: "expecting a baby")
**give a presentation** (verb + noun) – present to an audience – "He gave a great presentation."
**do damage / cause damage** (verb + noun) – harm something – "The storm caused damage to our home."
**the damage has been done** (phrase) – it's too late to change what happened
**make a decision** (verb + noun) – decide (US usage; British also says "take a decision")
**keep a secret** (verb + noun) – not tell a secret – "She's not good at keeping secrets."
**quick shower** (adj + noun) – short shower – "I'm going to take a quick shower."
**maiden voyage** (adj + noun) – a ship's first trip
**sound asleep / fast asleep** (adj + noun) – deeply asleep
**moral obligation** (adj + noun) – duty based on what is right – "We have a moral obligation to protect the planet."
**excruciating pain** (adj + noun) – very severe pain – "He was in excruciating pain."
**deep thought** (adj + noun) – thinking hard – "He's in deep thought."
**constructive criticism** (adj + noun) – helpful criticism meant to improve something
**wave frantically** (adverb + verb) – wave wildly, in panic – "She was waving frantically."
**vaguely remember** (adverb + verb) – remember a little, not clearly
**distinctly remember** (adverb + verb) – remember clearly
**reasonably priced** (adverb + adj) – fair price – "The meals were reasonably priced."
**completely forget** (adverb + verb) – forget totally – "He completely forgot her birthday."
**seriously doubt** (adverb + verb) – strongly doubt – "I seriously doubt it."

## 652. Useful Christmas Collocations🎄 #shorts #interactiveenglish

**Christmas break** – holiday from school or work
**Christmas vacation** – holiday trip or time off
**Christmas shopping** – buying gifts
**Christmas card** – card sent to friends and family
**Christmas tree** – decorated tree
**Christmas lights** – decorative lights
**Christmas decorations** – ornaments and displays
**Christmas stocking** – sock hung for gifts
**Christmas list** – list of gifts wanted or to buy
**Christmas present** – gift
**Christmas carol** – traditional Christmas song
**Christmas music** – holiday songs
**Christmas movie** – holiday film
**Christmas tradition** – custom followed each year
**Christmas celebration** – holiday event
**Christmas party** – holiday party
**Christmas spirit** – feeling of joy and goodwill
**Christmas miracle** – surprising good event
**Christmas Eve** – December 24
**Christmas Day** – December 25
**Merry Christmas** – holiday greeting
**Happy New Year** – New Year greeting

## 653. Useful Collocations to Greatly Improve Your English

**make a big mistake** (adj + noun) – "big" = large; "huge" adds more emphasis
**a wide range of** (adj + noun) – many choices – "a wide range of possibilities" (also "broad range")
**catch a flight** (verb + noun) – board/take a plane (also "take a flight")
**grave danger** (adj + noun) – serious danger (also "mortal danger")
**compelling argument** (adj + noun) – strong argument with good reasons (also "strong / convincing argument")
**deeply divided** (adverb + adj) – strongly disagree – "People are deeply divided along political lines."
**crystal clear** (adj) – very clear; also used for understanding – "Your instructions are crystal clear."
**draw your attention to** (verb phrase) – point something out for others to notice
**quite enough** (adverb + adj) – I've had enough – "I've heard quite enough."
**a waste of time** (noun phrase) – not worth the time spent
**excruciating pain** (adj + noun) – very severe pain
**highly recommend** (adverb + verb) – strongly suggest – "I highly recommend it."
**close the deal / seal the deal** (verb + noun) – finish a deal
**speak freely / speak frankly / speak openly** (verb + adverb) – say what you really think
**speedy recovery / full recovery** (adj + noun) – fast or complete healing – "He had a speedy and full recovery."

## 654. Useful English Expressions w/ BABY 👶 #learnenglish #shorts

**cry like a baby** (idiom) – cry a lot, often while complaining
**sleep like a baby** (idiom) – sleep very well – "I'm going to sleep like a baby."
**food baby** (idiom) – feeling bloated after eating too much (joke)

## 655. Useful English Expressions | Rain, Rain, Rain 🌧

**raining cats and dogs** (idiom) – raining very hard
**rain on someone's parade** (idiom) – spoil someone's plans or enjoyment – "Don't rain on my parade."
**when it rains it pours** (idiom) – one thing after another, usually bad (can also be good)
**save it for a rainy day** (idiom) – save money for a time of real need

## 656. Useful Expressions & Idioms All About HOME

**home sweet home** (idiom) – said when you get home and feel you belong
**there's no place like home** (idiom) – home is the best place
**home is where the heart is** (idiom) – home is with people or places you love
**homebody** (n) – person who likes to stay home – "She's a homebody."
**nothing to write home about** (idiom) – not special; just OK – "The movie was nothing to write home about."
**hit home** (idiom) – affect you personally, often negatively – "That comment really hit home."
**home away from home** (idiom) – a place that feels like home – "My office is my home away from home."
**home team** (n) – sports team from your hometown

## 657. Useful Expressions to Tell a Great Story 💭 | Time Order Words

**previously** (adv) – before now; recaps earlier events – "Previously, we identified the methods."
**not long ago** (adv phrase) – recent past (hours or days)
**a long time ago** (adv phrase) – far in the past
**two days ago / three weeks ago** – use "ago" when counting back from now (not "before")
**prior to** (prep) – before an event – "Prior to the meeting, we had a call."
**at the beginning (of)** – a specific point in time – "At the beginning of class, I sat in front."
**in the beginning** – a general time period – "In the beginning, the class was boring."
**at the onset (of)** – at the start of something – "At the onset of winter, it was cold."
**from this point on / from that point on** – from then onward – "From that point on, I've been honest."
**right after** (adv) – immediately after – "Right after I got to work, my boss yelled."
**as soon as** (conj) – immediately when – "As soon as I got to work..."
**soon after** (adv) – some time later, not long after
**shortly after** (adv) – same meaning as soon after
**consequently** (adv) – as a result – "It rained, consequently we canceled."
**from time to time / occasionally / every now and then** (adv) – sometimes
**finally / at last** (adv) – at the end; last
**in conclusion** (phrase) – formal; start of the final part of a talk
**eventually** (adv) – in the end, after time – "Eventually they got better."

## 658. Useful Food Idioms for Delicious English Fun 😋

**bread and butter** (n) – main source of income – "Teaching is our bread and butter."
**cool as a cucumber** (idiom) – calm and relaxed – "She stayed cool as a cucumber."
**not my cup of tea** (idiom) – not something you like or are interested in
**in a nutshell** (idiom) – briefly, in few words – "Tell me in a nutshell."
**put all your eggs in one basket** (idiom) – risk everything on one thing
**spice things up** (idiom) – make things more exciting – "They spiced things up with a trip."
**bring home the bacon** (idiom) – earn money; provide for your family
**a lemon** (n) – car or machine that does not work well – "He bought a lemon."
**spill the beans** (idiom) – reveal a secret, often by mistake
**cheesy** (adj) – cheap, low quality, or corny – "a cheesy pick-up line"

## 659. Useful Games to Help Practice & Improve Your English

**Scattergories** (n) – game: pick a letter, write a word for each category that starts with it (city, food, animal)
**Mad Libs** (n) – game: fill in blanks in a story with words (adjective, plural noun) and read the funny result
**Jeopardy** (n) – quiz game: pick a category and points; harder questions are worth more
**Pictionary** (n) – drawing game; players guess the word
**charades** (n) – acting game with gestures, no words
**Hangman** (n) – guess letters to find a hidden word
**Simon Says** (n) – follow commands only when "Simon says" is said
**2 truths 1 lie** (n) – guess which of three statements is false
**sick note** (n) – note explaining why you missed school because of illness
**sleet** (n) – rain mixed with ice; turns to ice when it hits the ground
**perspire** (v) – sweat
**break a leg** (idiom) – good luck (said before a performance)
**schwa** (n) – the most common vowel sound in English, /ə/ (upside-down e)

## 660. Useful Idioms You Absolutely NEED TO KNOW for 2025

**hit the ground running** (idiom) – start a new activity quickly and well – "We need to hit the ground running."
**game changer** (n) – something that greatly changes a situation – "Robotaxis are a game changer."
**throw shade** (idiom, informal) – criticize or disrespect someone subtly – "He threw shade at the Celtics."
**under the radar** (idiom) – not attracting attention – "Her music went under the radar."
**rain on someone's parade** (idiom) – spoil someone's good time
**break the internet** (idiom) – go viral; cause a huge online sensation
**FOMO** (acronym) – fear of missing out – "Some people buy Bitcoin because of FOMO."
**at the end of the day** (idiom) – when everything is considered; ultimately
**the new normal** (n phrase) – what is now accepted as usual
**clap back** (v, informal) – reply sharply to criticism
**under the weather** (idiom) – slightly ill – "I feel a little under the weather."
**in the driver's seat** (idiom) – in control of a situation

## 661. Useful Idioms in 60 minutes | Greatly Improve Your Vocabulary

Money:
**buck** (n, informal) – dollar – "Can I borrow a few bucks?"
**cost an arm and a leg** (idiom) – very expensive
**pony up** (v, informal) – pay money you owe – "Pony up the cash."
**put your money where your mouth is** (idiom) – prove your words with action
**make ends meet** (idiom) – earn just enough to live – "We're struggling to make ends meet."
**loaded** (adj, informal) – very rich
**look like a million bucks** (idiom) – look very good – "You look like a million bucks."
**shell out / fork over** (phrasal v, informal) – pay for something
**rags to riches** (n phrase) – going from poor to rich
**bread and butter** (n) – main source of income
**foot the bill** (idiom) – pay for something, often someone else pays – "Who's footing the bill?"
**break the bank** (idiom) – cost too much to afford – "It won't break the bank."
**penny pincher** (n) – person who hates spending money
**pick up the tab / pick up the check** (idiom) – pay the bill

Art (Paris):
**paint yourself into a corner** (idiom) – put yourself in a situation with few good options
**a picture is worth a thousand words** (proverb) – a picture explains more than words
**paint the town red** (idiom) – go out and party
**see the big picture** (idiom) – see the situation as a whole
**What's wrong with this picture?** (question) – sarcastic: something is absurd or wrong
**Do I have to paint you a picture?** (question) – I need to explain it more clearly
**like watching paint dry** (simile) – very boring
**paint a picture** (v) – describe a situation – "paint a bleak / rosy picture"

Soccer:
**keep your eye on the ball** (idiom) – stay focused
**be on the ball** (idiom) – alert and doing things right
**get the ball rolling** (idiom) – start something
**get a kick out of** (idiom) – enjoy something – "I get a kick out of watching matches."
**game plan** (n) – strategy to succeed
**blow the whistle on** (idiom) – report wrongdoing – "whistleblower" (n) = person who reports it
**we were robbed** (idiom) – treated unfairly
**take sides** (idiom) – support one side in a disagreement
**be on a roll** (idiom) – be successful for a while

Dessert:
**piece of cake** (idiom) – very easy
**have your cake and eat it too** (idiom) – have both things; not possible
**icing on the cake** (idiom) – extra good (or bad) thing on top of something
**sugarcoat** (v) – make something seem better than it is
**easy as apple pie** (idiom) – very easy
**flat as a pancake** (idiom) – very flat
**nutty as a fruitcake** (idiom) – a little crazy
**that's the way the cookie crumbles** (idiom) – that's life; accept a bad result
**sell like hotcakes** (idiom) – sell very quickly
**have a sweet tooth** (idiom) – love sweets

Hope and inspiration:
**keep your chin up** (idiom) – stay positive
**hang in there** (idiom) – keep trying; don't give up
**break a leg** (idiom) – good luck
**in the bag** (idiom) – certain to succeed
**sky's the limit** (idiom) – no limit to success
**the world is your oyster** (idiom) – many opportunities are open to you
**light at the end of the tunnel** (idiom) – sign that things will get better
**there's plenty of fish in the sea** (idiom) – you'll find someone else after a breakup
**look on the bright side** (idiom) – stay positive
**pull yourself together** (idiom) – control your emotions and act normal
**fingers crossed** (idiom) – hoping for something good
**blessing in disguise** (idiom) – bad thing that turns out good

Food idioms repeat the same 10 from lesson 658 (not repeated here).

## 662. Useful Idioms with the word TRAIN

**train wreck** (n) – something very bad – "My presentation was a train wreck."
**lose one's train of thought** (idiom) – forget what you were saying, often after an interruption
**step in front of a moving train (for someone)** (idiom) – make a big sacrifice to protect someone; usually negative – "I'm not stepping in front of that train."

## 663. Useful LOVE Idioms to Improve Your Fluency ❤️

**fancy someone** (v, British) – like someone and enjoy their company romantically
**fall for someone** (v) – develop romantic feelings over time
**head over heels (in love)** (idiom) – completely and deeply in love
**lovey-dovey** (adj) – very affectionate; can be too much
**be smitten** (adj) – strongly attracted to someone
**get hitched** (idiom) – get married
**tie the knot** (idiom) – get married
**go through a rough patch** (idiom) – have a period of problems
**double date** (n) – date with two couples together
**on the rocks** (idiom) – in trouble and likely to fail (can describe any relationship)

## 664. Useful Listening & Speaking Activity | Describe Words Around the Home

Method: listen to a description, guess the noun in 10 seconds, say it out loud.

**lamp** (n) – light you turn on at night
**table** (n) – where people eat; has four legs
**bed** (n) – where you sleep; has sheets, pillows, blankets
**door** (n) – open and close to move between rooms; can be locked
**bathroom** (n) – room with a sink and shower
**sofa / couch** (n) – furniture to sit on and watch TV
**chair** (n) – something you sit on; has four legs and a back
**refrigerator** (n) – appliance in the kitchen
**kitchen** (n) – room where food is made

## 665. Useful Listening Phrasal Verbs to Improve Your Fluency

**listen up** (phrasal v) – listen carefully (often said to a group) – "Listen up, this is important."
**listen in on** (phrasal v) – secretly listen to a conversation
**hear about** (phrasal v) – get information about something or someone
**hear from** (phrasal v) – get a call, message, or email from someone; or listen to someone speak – "You'll hear from my lawyer."
**hear someone out** (phrasal v) – listen to all of someone's words before reacting – "Just hear me out."
**hear of** (phrasal v) – learn of or know about – "I've never heard of him."
**pick up** (phrasal v) – hear a faint sound – "Can you pick up anything?"
**tune in (to)** (phrasal v) – watch or listen to a show, broadcast, or video
**tune out / tune someone out** (phrasal v) – stop paying attention – "I just tune her out."
**hang on someone's every word** (idiom) – listen very carefully, captivated

## 666. Useful Pet Idioms to Improve Your English Fluency

**catnap** (n) – very short nap during the day
**kill two birds with one stone** (idiom) – do two things at the same time
**pull a rabbit out of a hat** (idiom) – surprise everyone with something clever
**guinea pig** (n/idiom) – someone used as a test subject in an experiment – "He's using patients as guinea pigs."
**dog-eat-dog world** (idiom) – very competitive world where people do anything to win
**fish out of water** (idiom) – person who feels uncomfortable in an unfamiliar situation

## 667. Useful Phrasal Verbs to Easily Talk about Clothing

**put on** (phrasal v) – start wearing – "Put on a jacket."
**take off** (phrasal v) – remove clothing (not "put off") – "Take off your coat."
**break in** (phrasal v) – wear new shoes or clothes until they are comfortable
**try on** (phrasal v) – put on clothes to check fit and look
**throw on** (phrasal v) – put on quickly – "I threw on some clothes."
**button up** (phrasal v) – fasten with buttons – "Button up your shirt."
**dress up** (phrasal v) – wear more formal clothes, or a costume – "Dress up for a wedding."
**dress down** (phrasal v) – wear less formal clothes

## 668. Useful Phrasal Verbs with GET 👍  | Vocabulary Lesson

Phrasal verbs here are inseparable (object cannot go between the parts).

**get up** (phrasal v) – get out of bed; or climb to a higher place
**get away** (phrasal v) – move away from someone or something; leave or escape – "We need to get away for a few days."
**get away with** (phrasal v) – escape the consequences of something bad – "He got away with it."
**get over** (phrasal v) – recover from illness or injury; overcome a problem – "I can't get over the fact..."

Practice answers: 1 get up; 2 get over; 3 get away; 4 get up; 5 get away; 6 get over.

## 669. Useful Phrases to Express Your Opinion | Build Your Vocabulary

**I think** (phrase) – common; "that" is often dropped – "I think she's away."
**I believe** (phrase) – usually followed by "that" – "I believe that we are not alone."
**truly / firmly / strongly believe** (adverb + believe) – add emphasis
**honestly / really / definitely think** (adverb + think) – add emphasis
**in my opinion** (phrase) – common in formal and informal speech; pause after it
**in my honest opinion** (phrase) – signals you are being sincere
**in my humble opinion** (phrase) – softens your opinion; sounds less proud
**I'm of the opinion that** (phrase) – formal; followed by "that"
**in my view** (phrase) – same as in my opinion; a bit more formal
**from my point of view** (phrase) – from my perspective; use "from" with "point of view," "in" with "view"
**as far as I'm concerned** (phrase) – shows your opinion; often answers a question – "As far as I'm concerned, we should start."

## 670. Useful Phrases to Say "I'm tired." 😴 | Build Your Vocabulary

Lack of sleep:
**sleepy** (adj) – need sleep but can't stop yet – "I'm a little sleepy."
**beat** (adj, informal) – very tired, ready for bed – "I'm beat."
**exhausted** (adj) – very tired; the most common of these
**pooped** (adj, informal) – very tired (not about the bathroom)
**I can barely keep my eyes open** (phrase) – so tired you are fighting sleep
**knackered** (adj, British) – tired
Tip: don't tell someone they "look sleepy"; it can sound rude.

Overworked (physically or mentally):
**drained** (adj) – no energy left – "I feel drained."
**worn out** (adj) – tired; need a break
**spent** (adj) – used all your energy
**burnt out** (adj) – exhausted from too much work
**I'm done** (phrase) – can't continue anymore
**running on empty / running on fumes** (idiom) – almost no energy left

Getting sick:
**on my last legs** (idiom) – very weak or near the end of strength (also used for old pets or people)
**run down** (adj) – tired and not well; may get sick – "I'm feeling a bit run down."
**fatigued** (adj) – tired, used in a health context

Medicine and travel:
**drowsy** (adj) – tired because of medicine – "This medicine makes me drowsy."
**jet lagged** (adj) – tired after flying across time zones

## 671. Useful Proverbs to Share WISDOM | Advanced Expressions

**When in Rome, do as the Romans do** – act like locals when abroad – "When in Rome, try using chopsticks."
**Two wrongs don't make a right** – revenge only makes things worse – "Don't get revenge; two wrongs don't make a right."
**Absence makes the heart grow fonder** – we miss loved ones more when apart – "Absence makes the heart grow fonder."
**Money doesn't grow on trees** – money is hard to get, so spend carefully – "Money doesn't grow on trees."
**Patience is a virtue** – waiting calmly is a good quality – "Be patient; patience is a virtue."
**The early bird catches the worm** – whoever arrives first gets the result – "Get there early; the early bird catches the worm."
**Beauty is in the eye of the beholder** – people differ on what is beautiful – "We disagree, but beauty is in the eye of the beholder."
**The grass is always greener on the other side** – others' lives look better than they are – "The grass is always greener."
**A picture is worth a thousand words** – an image says more than words – "A picture is worth a thousand words."

## 672. Useful Proverbs to Share WISDOM | Build Your Vocabulary

**Beauty is only skin deep** – inner qualities matter more than looks – "Beauty is only skin deep."
**Every cloud has a silver lining** – bad situations have some good in them – "Things are sad, but every cloud has a silver lining."
**Every dog has its day** – everyone has success at some point – "Every dog has its day."
**One man's trash is another man's treasure** – what one person throws away, another values – "One man's trash is another man's treasure."
**Rome wasn't built in a day** – good work takes time – "Relax, Rome wasn't built in a day."
**Ignorance is bliss** – not knowing a problem means no stress – "Ignorance is bliss."
**You're never too old to learn** – you can keep learning at any age – "You're never too old to learn."
**You can't teach an old dog new tricks** – people set in their ways are hard to change – "You can't teach an old dog new tricks."

## 673. Useful SPEAKING Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary

**speak of the devil** (idiom) – said when the person you were talking about appears – "Speak of the devil, he just showed up!"
**so to speak** (phrase) – describes something figuratively – "We're on the same page, so to speak."
**speak up** (phrasal verb) – 1) speak louder; 2) share your opinion – "Please speak up, I can't hear." / "You should speak up at the meeting."
**speak for yourself** (phrase) – your opinion is different from theirs – "We think it's terrible." / "Speak for yourself."
**speaks for itself** (phrase) – is clear; no more information is needed – "My work speaks for itself."
**speak volumes** (phrase) – says a lot without words – "Your silence speaks volumes."
**technically speaking** (collocation) – adds precise detail, usually at the start – "Technically speaking, I get up at 5 a.m."

## 674. Useful Shopping Idioms So You Can Shop Till You Drop 🛍️

**window shopping** (idiom) – looking in shops without buying – "We've got a lot of window shopping to do."
**rip-off** (noun/verb) – something costs far more than it should – "That's a rip-off!"
**shop till you drop** (idiom) – shop until exhausted – "Everyone likes to shop till they drop."
**retail therapy / shopping therapy** (idiom) – shopping to feel better – "I needed some retail therapy after a bad day."
**bargain hunting** (noun/verb) – actively looking for good deals – "We are going bargain hunting."
**shoplift** (verb) – steal from a store – "Don't shoplift."
**fit like a glove** (idiom) – fits perfectly – "This uniform still fits me like a glove."
**like a bull in a China shop** (idiom) – acting clumsily or recklessly, causing damage – "You're coming in like a bull in a China shop."
**shopping spree** (noun) – short period of buying a lot – "She went on a shopping spree."
**pay through the nose** (idiom) – pay much more than the fair price – "They'll pay through the nose for it."

## 675. Useful Shopping Phrasal Verbs to Improve Your English

**sell out (of)** – no longer have something in stock – "They sold out of eggs."
**try out** – test something before buying – "Try the shoes out."
**pick up** – buy or grab something quickly – "I'll pick up some milk."
**pick out** – choose – "Pick out a dress."
**mark down** – reduce the price – "They marked the dress down 50%."
**look around** – browse without a plan – "We're just looking around."
**shop around** – compare prices in several stores – "Shop around before buying a TV."
**shell out** – pay a lot of money at once – "I had to shell out $500."
**blow money on (something)** – spend money wastefully – "Don't blow money on junk."
**take back** – return something to the store – "I'll take it back; it's defective."

## 676. Useful Signposts to Improve Your Listening Comprehension & Speaking Skills

Changing direction:
**however** – contrasts the previous statement – "I love restaurants; however, they're expensive."
**conversely** (formal) – reverses the previous statement – "The medicine didn't help; conversely, it made it worse."
**on the other hand** – contrasts two opposite ideas – "I want a vacation; on the other hand, I have work."
**another point to consider** – shifts focus to a new idea – "Another point to consider: summer crowds."

Emphasis:
**furthermore / moreover / in fact** – adds or stresses a point – "In fact, he eats pizza for breakfast."
**it is also important to highlight** – draws attention to a detail – "It's also important to highlight his love of spaghetti."

Similar points:
**in addition / also** – adds a related point – "I teach grammar; in addition, I teach vocabulary."
**similarly / likewise** – adds a similar point – "Smoking is harmful; similarly, drug use has effects."

Summarizing:
**for starters** – first point – "For starters, the offensive line is weak."
**finally** – last point – "Finally, we ate dinner."
**in conclusion** – ending – "In conclusion, oil prices will keep rising."
**to summarize / overall** – brief summary – "Overall, the chicken was bland."

Vocabulary:
**panacea** (noun) – a cure-all; a solution for everything – "The IPO was not a panacea."

## 677. Useful Speaking Phrasal Verbs for Better Communication

**talk to / talk with** (phrasal verb) – speak directly with someone; "with" implies a two-way conversation – "I talk to my friend."
**talk (someone) up** – praise someone enthusiastically – "Talk me up to your friends."
**talk back (to)** – reply rudely – "Kids shouldn't talk back to their parents."
**speak up** – raise your voice – "Could you speak up?"
**speak up for** – defend someone – "Speak up for yourself."
**speak for** – represent someone's opinion – "I speak for everyone when I say this."
**shut up** – 1) firmly tell someone to be quiet; 2) express disbelief – "No. Shut up!"

## 678. Useful Speaking Tips to Help You Prepare for the IELTS

- IELTS = International English Language Testing System; tests all four skills. Speaking test: 3 parts, 11–14 min.
- Topics are common (work, education, food, happiness, art), but can be anything.
- Prepare: research the format, timing and rules. Know what you can bring (for IELTS, no watch).
- Practice with topic cards: print topics, fold them, draw one, speak as in a real test.
- Use reputable sources (e.g., IELTS Liz; Cambridge books). Online advice is often bad.
- Time yourself. Aim for the full time (about 1.5 min per answer); do not stop at 45 seconds.
- Structure: state your main idea first, then give examples. Stay on topic.
- Stay current: read and watch the news for ideas, examples and vocabulary.
- Be formal enough: avoid "hey buddy" style, but do not overuse idioms (not one per sentence).
- Vary sentences: mix simple and complex sentences and transition words (eventually, finally, moreover).
- Use synonyms instead of "good" or "bad" (e.g., beneficial, harmful).
- Be yourself, speak naturally, and be confident. Think fast; if you have no example, make one up.

## 679. Useful Speaking Tips to Improve Your Fluency 💬

- Focus on one specific topic so you can concentrate on what you want to say.
- Speak when you learn something new (grammar tenses, vocabulary, idioms). Writing alone is not enough.
- Practice speaking clearly: pronunciation, intonation, emphasis, reductions and contractions. Accent is fine.
- Build confidence in a relaxed setting where mistakes are okay. Practice, practice, practice.

## 680. Useful Sport Idioms to Improve Your English

**the ball is in your court** (idiom, tennis) – it is up to you to act or decide – "I made an offer; the ball's in your court."
**get off the hook** (idiom, fishing) – escape punishment or responsibility – "You're not going to get off the hook that easy."
**go to bat for (someone)** (idiom, baseball) – defend or help someone – "Thanks for going to bat for me."
**give it your best shot** (idiom, hunting) – try your hardest – "Give it your best shot."
**the home stretch** (idiom, horse racing) – the final part, near the end – "We're in the home stretch."
**not up to par** (idiom, golf) – not good enough – "This work is not up to par."
**throw in the towel** (idiom, boxing) – give up – "Don't throw in the towel just yet."
**blind-sided** (idiom, football) – surprised by something unexpected – "I was blind-sided by the news."
**slam dunk** (idiom, basketball) – a sure thing, easy – "This decision is a slam dunk."
**call the shots** (idiom, billiards) – make the decisions – "The boss calls the shots."
(Bonus quiz: "hit below the belt". Its meaning is not given in the transcript.)

## 681. Useful Study Phrasal Verbs to Improve Fluency

**think through** (phrasal verb) – consider carefully before deciding – "Think it through first."
**pick up** – learn a language, skill or activity – "I'm trying to pick up Romanian."
**cheat on** – act unfairly on a test or quiz – "Don't cheat on a test."
**read up on** – learn about a topic by reading – "Read up on history before the midterm."
**go through** – examine carefully – "Go through your paper for mistakes."
**hand in / turn in** – submit work to a teacher – "Hand in your homework."
**fall behind** – not keep up with work or classmates – "Don't fall behind."
**drop out (of)** – leave school or a program without finishing – "He dropped out of school."
**hand out** – distribute – "The teacher will hand out a quiz."
**catch on** – understand how to do something – "I catch on quickly."

## 682. Useful TRAVEL PHRASES That You Need to Know

**hit the road** (phrase) – leave, start a journey – "Let's hit the road!"
**make a pit stop** (phrase) – short stop during a trip – "Can we make a pit stop?"
**in the middle of nowhere** (phrase) – very remote, far from towns – "We got lost in the middle of nowhere."
**travel light / pack light** (phrase) – bring few things – "I'll just travel light this weekend."
**lost in translation** (phrase) – meaning is lost when translated – "The sign was lost in translation."
**get a move on** (phrase) – hurry – "We need to get a move on."
**take a shortcut** (collocation) – a more direct or quicker route – "We'll get there faster if we take a shortcut."
**layover / stopover / connecting flight** (noun) – a temporary stop before continuing – "I have a layover in Denver."
**travel off the beaten path / off the beaten track** (phrase) – go to places that are not popular – "I like to travel off the beaten path."
**off the grid** (phrase) – remote, away from everything – "They went off the grid for a week."
**take a detour** (collocation) – take a less direct route to avoid a problem or visit a place – "We took a slight detour."
**make good time** (collocation) – travel quickly, ahead of schedule – "We made great time."
**take the scenic route** (collocation) – a beautiful route, not the fastest – "Let's take the scenic route."

## 683. Useful Technology Phrasal Verbs for Surfing the Internet

**log in / sign in** (phrasal verb) – access a computer or site with a name and password – "Log in to your account."
**click on** – select a link or app with the mouse – "Click on the link."
**scroll up / scroll down / scroll through** (phrasal verb) – move up or down a page; look through it – "Scroll down for more."
**back up** (phrasal verb) – keep an extra copy of data – "Back up your hard drive."
**hack into** (phrasal verb) – illegally access a computer or network – "They hacked into our system."
**run out of** (phrasal verb) – use up a supply – "I'm running out of space."
**free up** (phrasal verb) – make space or time available – "Free up some space."
**pop up** (phrasal verb) – suddenly appear – "An ad pops up."
**opt in / opt out** (phrasal verb) – choose to receive / stop receiving messages – "Opt out of emails."
**filter out** (phrasal verb) – remove unwanted emails – "Google filters out my emails."
**turn off / shut down / power off** (phrasal verb) – end a computer session (all mean the same) – "Turn off the computer."
**plug in** (phrasal verb) – connect a power cable to a socket – "Plug in your computer."
**print out** (phrasal verb) – make a paper copy – "I printed out the document."
**set up** (phrasal verb) – install a program or prepare a computer – "Set up the new program."
**keep up with** (phrasal verb) – stay current with changes – "It's hard to keep up with updates."

## 684. Useful Time Expressions to Help Improve Your Fluency

- Lessons use the 12-hour clock (a.m./p.m.), which is common in English-speaking countries.
- Basic format: say the hour, then the minutes. 10:29 = "ten twenty-nine" (+ "in the morning" if needed).
- On the hour: "four o'clock."
- Minutes 1–9: say "oh." 7:08 = "seven oh eight."
- **noon / midday** = 12 p.m.; **midnight** = 12 a.m.
- **dusk / twilight** = light after sunset; **dawn** = light before sunrise.
- **half past** = 30 minutes past: 3:30 = "half past three." British speakers may say "half eight" for 8:30.
- **quarter past / quarter after** = 15 minutes past: 2:15 = "quarter past two." (US: "quarter after")
- **quarter to / quarter till / quarter of** = 15 minutes before the hour: 4:45 = "quarter to five."
- **past / after** = for minutes 1–30 (e.g., "20 past 2" or "25 after 2").
- **to / till** = for minutes 31–59 (e.g., "20 to 7" or "10 till 11").
- **about** = estimate: "about half past ten."
- **12 to noon** = 11:48 (also "12 to midday" or "12 to midnight").

## 685. Useful Tips on How to Build the Perfect Resume or CV

- Resume: short summary, usually 1 page (max 2). Common in the US and Canada.
- CV: more detailed, usually 2–4 pages, more achievements. Common in many other countries.
- Check the job posting: it says whether to submit a resume or a CV.
- Know your audience: the country and industry decide the format. Architecture CVs differ from coding CVs.
- Do research: search Google Images for samples. German CVs often include a photo; US resumes usually do not.
- Use action verbs: develop, create, manage, implement, structure, utilize, facilitate, conduct.
- Use active sentences, not passive voice. Show that you do the action.
- Check for grammar and spelling mistakes. Ask someone else to proofread.
- Network: share your CV with friends and colleagues. Keep your LinkedIn updated.
- Keep your CV current and sell yourself: highlight education, experience and accomplishments.
- Chronological resume = most recent job first. Functional resume = highlights your achievements.

## 686. Useful Tongue Twisters to Help You Practice Pronunciation 😜

**red lorry, yellow lorry** – r vs. l sounds; r: tongue pulled back; l: tip behind top front teeth.
**stupid superstition** – st / sp / su clusters.
**Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers** – p sound (voiceless p, not b).
**The sixth sick sheik's sixth sheep's sick** – tricky "sixth" ending and s/sh sounds; say it slowly first.
**Pad kid poured curd pulled cod** – p / d / k sounds; reported by MIT as the hardest tongue twister.
- Tip: find a sound you struggle with and practice a tongue twister with it. Say it three times quickly.
- Tip: you don't need to practice every day. Revisit once a month.

## 687. Useful Verbs to Talk about the SPRING 🌻

**blossom** (verb) – flowers open or appear on trees – "The trees begin to blossom."
**sniff** (verb) – smell something with a short breath – "Sniff these flowers."
**sprout** (verb) – a seed starts to grow – "The seed is sprouting."
**teem with** (phrasal verb) – be full of something – "The garden is teeming with flowers."
**flourish** (verb) – grow successfully and healthily – "The business is flourishing."
**rejuvenate** (verb) – look better, fresher, healthier – "The park is rejuvenated."
**breathe life into** (expression) – bring something back to life – "Breathe life into the old garden."
**emerge** (verb) – become visible again – "A bear emerges from its cave."
**chirp** (verb) – birds and crickets make short sounds – "Birds chirp."
**buzz** (verb) – insects make a humming sound – "Bees buzz."
**croak** (verb) – frogs make a deep sound – "Frogs croak."
**crack** (verb) – an egg breaks open as a chick hatches – "The eggs crack."
**spring chicken** (idiom) – a young person – "I'm no spring chicken."
**mow** (verb) – cut grass – "Mow the lawn."
**barbecue** (verb) – cook food outdoors – "Let's barbecue this weekend."
**get** collocations (spring): get healthy, get outside, get a whiff of (smell), get allergies.
**melt** (verb) – snow turns to water – "The snow will melt."
**shine** (verb) – the sun gives light – "The sun is shining."
**warm up** (phrasal verb) – weather becomes warmer – "The temperatures are warming up."
**drizzle / sprinkle** (verb) – light rain – "It is drizzling outside."

## 688. Useful Vocabulary for Job Performance (Lesson Notes Included)
- **personable** (adj) – pleasant, easy to get along with – "I'm hardworking and personable."
- **meticulous** (adj) – very careful, pays close attention to detail – "He always does a meticulous review of his work."
- **detail-oriented** (adj) – focuses on small details – "She is detail-oriented."
- **result-oriented** (adj) – focuses on the outcome – "He is result-oriented."
- **proactive** (adj) – acts before being told – "She is very proactive when it comes to problem solving."
- **innovative** (adj) – new, different ideas – "She often has many innovative ideas."
- **inattentive** (adj) – not paying attention – "He is sometimes inattentive during meetings."
- **impetuous** (adj) – acts suddenly without thinking of results; more negative than impulsive – "an impetuous decision"
- **inept** (adj) – not skilled or effective; synonym: incompetent – "the inept handling of the situation"
- **standoffish** (adj) – cold, unfriendly, too formal; opposite of personable – "She is difficult to work with; she's standoffish."
- **disingenuous** (adj) – not fully honest; synonym: insincere – "He was disingenuous about his past experience."
- **take the initiative** (phrase) – start doing things without being asked – "She always takes the initiative."
- **go the extra mile** (idiom) – do more than expected – "My colleague always goes the extra mile."
- **pay close attention to detail** (collocation) – be very careful – "She pays close attention to detail."
- **take pride in** + -ing (phrase) – feel proud of your work – "She takes pride in completing projects on time."
- **take one for the team** (idiom) – make a personal sacrifice for the group – "She will take one for the team."
- **pull your weight** (idiom) – do your share of the work – "He's not pulling his weight."
- **lack of commitment** (noun phrase) – little desire to succeed – "She has a lack of commitment to her work."
- **no-show** (noun) – person who is expected but does not show up – "At weekly meetings, he is often a no-show."
- **run behind schedule** (phrase) – happen later than planned – "We are running behind schedule."
- **can't handle pressure** (phrase) – cannot manage stress – "He just can't handle pressure situations."

## 689. Useful Vocabulary for Social Media (Facebook, Instagram, & Twitter)
- **engagement** (n) – any interaction with a post (like, comment, share) – "I always love to get some engagement."
- **meme** (n) – humorous image or video shared widely – "cat memes"
- **follower** (n) – person who follows an account – "She has many followers."
- **follow / unfollow** (v) – start / stop following someone; there is no noun "unfollower" – "I unfollowed him."
- **feed** (n) – page of regularly updated posts – "Something popped up on my feed."
- **hashtag** (n) – # plus a topic word, no spaces – "#vocabulary"
- **phishing** (n) – scam to steal passwords or personal info using fake messages or links – "a phishing email"
- **cyberbully** (n/v) – use the internet to send hateful or harmful messages – "a cyberbully"
- **handle** (n) – the name someone goes by online – "We chose a different handle."
- **campaign** (n) – planned activity to reach a goal, such as raising money or awareness – "a campaign to raise money"
- **DM** (n/v) – direct message, a private message – "Send me a DM."

## 690. Useful Vocabulary to Talk About Sounds | ONOMATOPOEIA 🔊
- **splash** (n/v) – sound of water hitting something – "Did you hear a splash?"
- **boom** (n) – loud sound – "We heard a loud boom." (also "a booming voice" = very loud voice)
- **beep** (n/v) – short electronic sound (alarm clock, car horn) – "beep beep beep"
- **crack** (n/v) – sound of something breaking – "the crack of an egg"
- **pop** (n/v) – sudden loud sound (popcorn, fireworks, gunfire) – "the pop of popcorn"
- **yoink** (interj/v) – sound of grabbing something, playful – "Yoink, I have the glasses."
- **click** (n/v) – sound of pressing a button – "Click the link."
- **zoom** (v) – move fast, with a sound – "The cars zoomed by."
- **gasp** (n/v) – short breath showing shock or surprise – "She gasped."
- **slap** (n/v) – sound of two things hitting – "a slap in the face"

## 691. Useful Vocabulary to Understand the US Election | Trump vs Biden 2020 🇺🇸
- **apolitical** (adj) – not connected to a political party – "I'm trying to keep this lesson apolitical."
- **two-party system** (n) – politics dominated by two main parties (Republican, Democrat)
- **independent** (n) – voter not registered with a party – "I am an independent."
- **moderate** (adj) – views that are not extreme – "moderate voters"
- **primary** (n) – election where party voters choose the party's candidate
- **candidate** (n) – person running for office
- **popular vote** (n) – total votes a candidate receives
- **electoral college** (n) – 538 electors; a candidate needs 270 to win the presidency
- **winner-take-all** (adj) – state gives all its electoral votes to the popular-vote winner
- **partisan** (adj) – strongly supports one side, not objective – "a partisan decision"
- **amend** (v) – change a law or process – "amend the electoral college"
- **blue state / red state / purple state** (n) – state mostly Democrat / Republican / mixed
- **swing state** or **battleground state** (n) – state that could vote either way
- **fundraise** (v) – raise money for a campaign – "The candidate will fundraise in Texas."
- **PAC / super PAC** (n) – political action committee; organization that raises money to influence elections
- **civic duty** (n) – a citizen's responsibility – "It is our civic duty to vote."
- **civil discourse** (n) – polite conversation about public issues
- **go to the polls** (phrase) – go to vote – "People will head to the polls."
- **cast your ballot** (phrase) – vote – "Cast your ballot in November."
- **absentee ballot** (n) – ballot requested and mailed by a voter who cannot vote in person
- **mail-in voting** (n) – voting by mailed ballot sent to all registered voters
- **rigged** (adj) – dishonestly arranged to get a desired result – "claims the election will be rigged"
- **voter suppression** (n) – preventing people from voting
- **concede** (v) – admit you lost – "Someone will concede the election."
- **contested** (adj) – result challenged by the losing candidate – "a contested election"

## 692. Useful Water Phrases to Build Your Vocabulary 💦
- **get your feet wet** / **dip your toes in the water** (idiom) – try something for the first time – "Let's dip our toes in the water."
- **dive in head first** (phrase) – start immediately and fully committed – "Let's dive in head first."
- **keep your head above water** (idiom) – survive a dangerous or hard situation – "Keep your head above water until the project ends."
(Transcript ends early.)

## 693. Useful Weather Idioms to Build Your Vocabulary
- **under the weather** (idiom) – feeling a little sick – "I can't go to work; I'm under the weather."
- **cloud your judgment** (v) – impair clear thinking – "Emotions can cloud your judgment."
- **get wind of something** (idiom) – hear secret or private information – "The employees got wind of the news."
- **snowball effect** (n) – something growing bigger and faster over time – "The rumor had a snowball effect."
- **weather the storm** (v) – get through a difficult period – "The small business weathered the storm."
- **when it rains, it pours** (idiom) – bad things happen one after another (UK: "it never rains but it pours")
- **the calm before the storm** (n) – quiet period before trouble – "Right now is the calm before the storm."
- **take a rain check** (idiom) – decline now, maybe later – "Let's take a rain check on dinner."
- **break the ice** (v) – make people relaxed; noun: icebreaker – "Let's break the ice with a game."
- **on thin ice** (idiom) – in a risky or dangerous position – "One more mistake and you're on thin ice."
- **steal someone's thunder** (v) – take credit or attention meant for someone else – "She stole my thunder in the meeting."
- **raining cats and dogs** (idiom) – raining very heavily
- **on cloud nine** (idiom) – very happy
- **have your head in the clouds** (idiom) – unrealistic or not paying attention
- **rain on someone's parade** (idiom) – spoil someone's enjoyment – "He rained on my parade."
- **throw caution to the wind** (v) – do something risky, ignoring danger – "Sometimes you've got to throw caution to the wind."

## 694. Useful Winter Expressions to Build Your Vocabulary ❄️
- **leave someone out in the cold** (idiom) – exclude someone from a group or activity – "They left me out in the cold."
- **cold streak** (n) – period of little success; opposite: hot streak – "We're on a cold streak."
- **in cold blood** (phrase) – intentionally, without feeling – "He was killed in cold blood."
- **give someone the cold shoulder** (v) – act unfriendly on purpose – "She gave him the cold shoulder."
- **out cold** (adj) – unconscious – "He got knocked out cold."
- **pour cold water on something** (v) – discourage an idea – "Don't pour cold water on my idea."
- **the tip of the iceberg** (n) – only a small visible part of a bigger problem – "This is just the tip of the iceberg."
- **break the ice** (v) – relax a group; noun: icebreaker – "An icebreaker helps students relax."
- **put a freeze on something** (v) – temporarily stop a policy – "The company put a freeze on hiring."
- **freeze up** (phrasal verb) – become too anxious to move or speak – "He froze up during the presentation."
- **when hell freezes over** (idiom) – never – "We'll win when hell freezes over."
- **freeze someone out** (v) – exclude someone socially in an unfriendly way – "They froze him out."
- Difference: "left out in the cold" = not allowed in the group; "frozen out" = in the group but ignored.

## 695. Useful Words & Synonyms on How to Say You're SAD 🙁
- **blue** (adj) – a little sad, melancholy – "I feel blue today."
- **heartbroken** (adj) – extremely sad, often after a loss or breakup – "She was heartbroken."
- **down** (adj) – unhappy, less intense than depressed – "He's feeling down."
- **bitter** (adj) – sad plus anger or resentment, from unfair treatment – "He feels bitter about the decision."
- **dejected** (adj) – sad and hopeless, about to give up – "She felt dejected after the rejection."
- **gloomy** (adj) – dark and depressing (place or mood) – "a gloomy room"
- **disheartened** (adj) – lost confidence and determination – "The team was disheartened."
- **depressed** (adj) – extremely unhappy; use carefully – "He is depressed."
- **miserable** (adj) – extremely unhappy or uncomfortable – "I'm miserable in this heat."
- **dark** (adj) – unhappy, pessimistic, unpleasant to be around – "He's in a dark mood."

## 696. Useful Words to Say Things Are Cheap and Expensive
Cheap:
- **inexpensive** / **low-priced** (adj) – direct synonyms of cheap – "an inexpensive meal"
- **affordable** (adj) – not too costly for something usually expensive (car, house) – "an affordable house"
- **cost-effective** (adj) – good value; does the job without high cost – "a cost-effective solution"
- **budget** (adj) – inexpensive (not the noun) – "budget hotel"
- **reasonable** (adj) – fair price, neither cheap nor expensive – "a reasonable price"
- **steal** (n) – very cheap, a great deal – "This jacket is a steal!"
- **bargain** (n) – good deal, bought on sale for much less than expected – "a bargain at the store"
Expensive:
- **costly** (adj) – costs a lot (the t is almost silent) – "a costly repair"
- **pricey** (adj) – expensive – "a pricey restaurant"
- **overpriced** (adj) – costs more than it should – "The coffee is overpriced."
- **valuable** (adj) – worth a lot of money, or important to you – "a valuable ring"
- **extravagant** (adj) – costs more than you can afford; too much luxury – "an extravagant party"
- **lavish** (adj) – elaborate, luxurious, lots of money spent – "a lavish wedding"
- **exorbitant** (adj) – unreasonably high price – "an exorbitant fee"
- **an arm and a leg** (idiom) – very expensive – "That car cost an arm and a leg."

## 697. Useful World Cup Idioms for Your Vocabulary Goals
- **keep one's eye on the ball** (idiom) – stay focused and pay attention – "Keep your eye on the ball during the project."
- **on the ball** (idiom) – alert, aware, sharp – "I'm really on the ball today."
- **get the ball rolling** (v) – start something – "Let's get the ball rolling."
- **get a kick out of** (v) – enjoy something – "I get a kick out of watching World Cup matches."
- **game changer** (n) – new idea or event that shifts the situation – "This new system is a game changer."
- **game plan** (n) – strategy for success – "You need a game plan for negotiations."
- **blow the whistle on** (v) – report wrongdoing; noun: whistleblower (the person who reports it)
- **we were robbed** (phrase) – treated unfairly (bad call, near miss) – "That penalty was wrong. We were robbed."
- **take sides** (v) – support one side in a disagreement – "Who do I agree with? Whose side am I taking?"
- **on a roll** (idiom) – having a streak of success – "Lately I've really been on a roll."
- Quiz at end: "score an own goal." The answer is not in the transcript.

## 698. Using Pictures to Improve Your English
- Visual learners remember colorful pictures better; pictures link words to meaning.
- Use Google Images to learn a word's meaning – search "drawbridge" or "groundhog."
- Compare similar words – search "borrow versus lend" (borrow = take, lend = give).
- Learn grammar visually – search a tense name (e.g., "present perfect") for charts and example sentences.
- Learn pronunciation – search "silent letters" to see highlighted silent letters (knight, knee, wrist).
- Save useful images in a vocabulary folder to review later.

## 699. Using Subtitles to Enhance Your English Skills
- Subtitles help understanding and complement listening, reading, and grammar.
- Subtitles alone are not enough; combine them with class, books, and practice.
- Reading subtitles builds reading speed to keep up with fast speech.
- Subtitles show phrasal verbs and idioms with their particles – "put on" is not "put out."
- Shows on a topic (e.g., a hospital show) teach subject-specific vocabulary.
- Subtitles help with strong accents (e.g., Cockney) and poor audio or video.
- Beginners and low-intermediate learners benefit most; native-language subtitles are fine at first.
- Idioms from the lesson: **two birds in one stone** (do two things at once); **stand someone up** (not show up for plans – "She stood me up."); **walk before you can run** (learn in steps).

## 700. Using the Past Tense | Learn the Simple Past, Past Continuous, & Present Perfect
- **Simple past**: finished action at a specific time – "I read a book last night."
- Simple past: repeated finished actions – "Every Monday I went to soccer practice."
- Simple past: finished state – "We lived in the Bay Area for 3 years."
- Signal words: yesterday, last week, ago, last year; frequency adverbs (always, usually, sometimes, never).
- **Past continuous** (was/were + verb-ing): action in progress around a time – "I was reading all night."
- Change/growth verbs (change, grow, develop) often use past continuous – "The city was changing rapidly."
- **while / when**: long action in past continuous + short action in simple past – "She was watching the baby while I cooked dinner." / "I was cooking dinner when someone knocked."
- **Present perfect** (have/has + past participle): started in the past, continues to now – "I have played guitar for 10 years." (for = period) / "We've lived in Budapest since 2017." (since = point in time)
- Unspecified time (experience): "I have been to China." "We have taken the bus twice this week."
- Ever / never / already with present perfect – "Have you ever wished upon a star?" "I've never visited Italy."
- Specific time (last year) → simple past: "I went to China last year."

## 701. VERB TENSE QUIZ | Grammar Lesson for Intermediate to Advanced Learners
- **since / for** → present perfect progressive – "I have been teaching English since 2005."
- **from time to time** = sometimes → present simple (habit) – "We eat out for dinner from time to time."
- **by the time** + **already** → past perfect – "By the time I arrived, everyone had already started eating."
- **won't** (refusal, failure) → future simple – "I tried but the door just won't open."
- **lately** → present perfect progressive – "Lately we've been watching reruns of The Office."
- **by the time** + future → future perfect – "By the time this lesson ends, you will have hit the like button."
- **used to** (past habit, no longer true) – "Back in 2010 I used to live in Namibia."
- Past perfect for the earlier past event – "When her parents knocked on the door, she had already left for work."
- Past continuous for an action in progress when another happened – "When he got to work, everyone was meeting in the conference room."
- Past continuous for background action – "People were talking in the chat when I began this lesson."
- Future continuous – "At this time next week, I'll be teaching another live lesson."
- Present continuous for now – "What are you doing right now?" / "The phone is ringing."
- Present perfect for unspecified past (been to a place) – "I have been to Guatemala."
- Present perfect progressive for a recent ongoing habit – "This past month I have been exercising every other day."
- Past simple question – "Why didn't you give me a heads up last night?"
- Present simple question for habit/enjoyment – "Do you enjoy learning English with us?"
- Future perfect question – "When this quiz is over, how many questions will you have gotten correct?"
- Future perfect progressive – "By 2067 we will have been teaching on YouTube for 50 years."
- Past perfect progressive – "We had been trying to open the door for several minutes when she found her key."

## 702. VERB TENSE QUIZ 📝  | Advanced Grammar Lesson
- **ought to** + base verb (modal) – criticism or advice – "He ought to know better."
- Scheduled future uses present simple – "The plane arrives at 8 pm." / "The movie starts at nine."
- Present perfect for unspecified time – "I have been to China twice."
- Simple past for specific past time – "I went to China last year."
- Past continuous for an action at a specific past time – "Last year at this time we were planning a trip to Romania."
- **will** for quick, sudden decisions – "The door? I'll get it."
- Before-clause with simple past + main clause simple past – "Before my alarm went off, I heard a loud boom."
- Present perfect continuous for an action that started in the past and continues – "I have been learning English for four years and I will continue to do so."
- **going to** for evidence or signs – "Only one minute left; our team is going to win." / "Dark clouds; I think it's going to rain."
- Subjunctive after recommend/suggest (base verb) – "The doctor recommends that she get some rest." (speech often uses "gets")
- Past perfect with already – "By the time I arrived at the restaurant, everyone had already eaten."
- Question in past simple (did), answer in past continuous – "How did you break your leg?" / "I was skiing in Colorado."
- Question in present perfect, answer in simple past – "Have you ever taken a road trip?" / "We took one last year."

## 704. VOCABULARY TO HELP YOU UNDERSTAND THE PANDEMIC 😷

**outbreak** (noun) – sudden rise of a disease
**epidemic** (noun) – outbreak that spreads beyond its origin
**pandemic** (noun) – epidemic that becomes worldwide
**WHO** (acronym) – World Health Organization
**CDC** (acronym) – Centers for Disease Control (US)
**quarantine** (verb) – isolate a person so a disease does not spread – "They quarantined the patient."
**self-quarantine** (verb/noun) – isolate yourself by your own choice – "I'm self-quarantining."
**symptom** (noun) – sign that someone has a disease – "fever is a symptom"
**symptomatic** (adj) – showing symptoms
**asymptomatic** (adj) – not showing symptoms, but can still spread the disease
**spread** (verb) – pass from one person to another – "The virus is spreading."
**epicenter** (noun) – center of an area with the highest level – "New York is the epicenter."
**hotspot** (noun) – informal word for epicenter
**shelter-in-place / stay-at-home order** (noun) – government order to stay home
**contact tracing** (noun) – finding who an infected person was in close contact with
**flatten the curve** (idiom) – slow the spread so fewer cases happen at once
**ventilator** (noun) – machine that helps a person breathe
**respirator** (noun) – mask that filters harmful air
**vaccine** (noun) – substance that helps the body fight a disease
**furlough** (verb/noun) – temporary leave without pay; the worker returns to the job
**lay off** (phrasal verb) – let an employee go; the person is now unemployed
**respiratory** (adj) – relating to breathing and the lungs
**sanitize** (verb) – clean and disinfect – "hand sanitizer"
**tip of the iceberg** (idiom) – only a small part of a larger problem is visible
**amid** (preposition) – in the middle of, surrounded by – "amid the pandemic"
**lockdown** (noun) – everything is closed and no one can move around
**bumper-to-bumper** (adj) – very heavy traffic
**crammed like sardines** (idiom) – packed tightly together
**teeming with** (phrasal verb) – full of many people or things – "The mall is teeming with customers."

## 706. Vocabulary - however, nevertheless, nonetheless, in addition, moreover, furthermore

**however** (adv) – contrast; most common, conversational – "I love spring. However, I get allergies."
**nevertheless** (adv) – contrast; more formal, stronger emphasis – "There's a pandemic. Nevertheless, some refuse masks."
**nonetheless** (adv) – contrast; most formal, least common; often used with amounts – "It was risky. Nonetheless, we invested $1,000."
**in addition** (adv) – add information; most common, least assertive
**moreover** (adv) – add information; formal, builds on an argument – "He lacks skills. Moreover, he is untrustworthy."
**furthermore** (adv) – add information; formal, adds emphasis
- Start of sentence gives more emphasis. Middle or end is less emphasis.
- Use commas around the adverb when it is in the middle of a sentence.

## 711. WHERE TO PLACE ADVERBS? | ADVANCED ENGLISH GRAMMAR

- Adverbs can go in three positions: start (initial), middle (between subject and verb), or end (final).
- Start position gives more emphasis: "Repeatedly, I called you, but you didn't answer."
- Conjunctive adverbs (first, next, finally, therefore, moreover, however) link to the sentence before. They usually start the sentence.
- Viewpoint adverbs (obviously, apparently, personally, fortunately, interestingly) show opinion. They usually start the sentence.
- Frequency adverbs (always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never) go in the middle: "I sometimes pick my nose."
- Degree adverbs (really, very, so, quite, extremely, barely) go in the middle. "A lot" and "a bit" go at the end.
- Manner adverbs (carefully, slowly, quickly, sadly, openly) usually go at the end. Middle is also possible: "They openly talked."
- Time adverbs (today, tomorrow, yesterday, now, at night) usually go at the end. Start position adds emphasis: "In the morning, we breakfast."
- Adverb placement is flexible. Learn it through reading and listening, not by memorizing one rule.

## 712. WILL vs GOING TO | Learn to correctly talk about the future

- Use **will** for a quick, immediate decision, made at the moment of speaking – "I'll go get some."
- Use **going to** for a decision or plan made before – "We're going to the park this weekend."
- Use **will** for promises, offers, threats, refusals, and facts – "I promise I will respond." "I won't help you." "The sun will rise."
- Use **going to** for predictions with signs or evidence – "I'm going to be sick." "It's going to rain" (weather forecast).
- Use **will** for predictions that are only an opinion or a guess – "I think it'll rain tomorrow."
- With no prior plan, will and going to can often be used interchangeably in predictions.
- Spoken forms: will = 'll; going to = gonna.
- Wrong choice sounds awkward but is usually still understood.

## 714. Walk the Path to Achieving English Fluency 💬

Learning English is about the journey, not only the destination. Enjoy the people, friends, and experiences along the way. Delays are normal. Keep going, even slowly, and you will reach your goal.

## 715. Want to Sound Fluent? Advanced Words Native Speakers Actually Use!

**concede** (verb) – admit something is true after first resisting it – "The manager conceded that mistakes were made."
**mitigate** (verb) – make something bad less severe – "mitigate stress," "mitigate damage," "mitigate the impact"
**compel** (verb) – force or strongly persuade someone to do something – "The evidence compelled the jury to reach a quick verdict."
**refrain (from)** (verb) – stop yourself from doing something – "Please refrain from using your phone."
**epiphany** (noun) – sudden moment of understanding – "have an epiphany"
**nuance** (noun) – subtle difference in meaning, expression, or sound – "a nuance between confident and arrogant"
**cocky** (adj) – informal; confident in an arrogant way
**repercussion** (noun) – unintended, usually unwelcome consequence – "The cuts had serious repercussions for employees."
**ramification** (noun) – similar to repercussion; a consequence, often complex
**catalyst** (noun) – something that starts an event or change – "The crisis served as a catalyst for innovation."
**tentative** (adj) – not fully worked out – "a tentative date," "a tentative plan"
**formidable** (adj) – impressively large, powerful, or capable; inspires fear or respect – "a formidable opponent"
**tedious** (adj) – boring, too long, tiresome – "Filling out paperwork is tedious."
**ubiquitous** (adj) – found everywhere – "Smartphones are ubiquitous."
**unequivocally** (adv) – in a way that leaves no doubt – "The CEO stated unequivocally that no jobs would be cut."
**incessantly** (adv) – without stopping – "She talked incessantly about her project."
**conspicuously** (adv) – in a clearly visible way – "He arrived conspicuously late."
**eloquently** (adv) – in a clear, persuasive, pleasing way – "The speaker explained the problem eloquently."

## 716. Weird, Strange, and Fascinating Expressions | English Tautologies
Tautology = saying the same thing twice in different words (redundant).
- **it is what it is** (expr) – that's just the situation, can't change it – "My boss says work Saturday. It is what it is."
- **enough is enough** (expr) – stop, I've had too much – "Enough is enough!"
- **a short summary** (tautology) – "short" is already in "summary"; common in spoken English
- **6:00 a.m. in the morning** (tautology) – "a.m." already means morning; say "6:00 a.m."
- **a necessary requirement** (tautology) – requirements are already necessary
- **take turns one after the other** (tautology) – teachers repeat for clarity
- **ATM machine** (tautology) – "automated teller machine" already includes "machine"
- **VIN number** (tautology) – "vehicle identification number" already includes "number"
- **PIN number** (tautology) – "personal identification number" already includes "number"
- **RSVP please** (tautology) – RSVP already means "please respond"
- **new innovation** (tautology) – innovations are already new
- **prepay in advance** (tautology) – prepay already means pay in advance
- **added bonus** (tautology) – bonuses are already extra
- **giving away free tickets** (tautology) – "give away" already means free
- **personally made … with my own two hands** (tautology) – "personally" already implies "my own"
- **I just want to live while I'm alive** (tautology, Bon Jovi) – common song lyric; fine in speech, odd in writing
- **whatever will be, will be** (tautology, Doris Day song) – old famous song
- **shout it out loud** (tautology, Kiss song) – "shout" already means loud
- **to be or not to be, that is the question** (tautology, Shakespeare) – famous literary line
- **in my opinion, I think** (awkward) – only OK with a pause while you think

## 722. What to Say & What NOT to Say When a Woman is Pregnant 🙅
Say (safe):
- "Congratulations" / "Congrats" – safest choice when unsure.
- "You look amazing / great / wonderful" – always a safe compliment.
- "When is the baby due?" – harmless.
- "You're going to be a great parent." – supportive.
- "How are you feeling?" – only after she has told you she is pregnant.
- Wait until she tells you before saying "congratulations" if you are not sure.
Do NOT say:
- Anything about planning or sex ("Was it planned?", "Were you trying?") – very personal.
- Anything about weight ("How much weight have you gained?") – never ask.
- Anything about size: "big belly = big baby," "you're so small," "you're about to pop," "you look like a whale."
- "Are you sure there aren't two in there?" – joke that can hurt.
- "Who's the father?" – too personal; she may be alone.
- "You look tired / sick / miserable" or "Sleep now, you'll never sleep again."
- Touching the belly of an acquaintance without permission (family and close friends are different).
- Rule of thumb: if you are not sure, say "Congratulations" and keep quiet.

## 724. Why English is SO hard to learn? | + Lesson  Notes
Rule: a heteronym is a word with the same spelling but a different meaning and pronunciation. Two-syllable verbs usually stress the second syllable (re-FUSE); two-syllable nouns usually stress the first (REF-use).
- **wound** (noun) /wuːnd/ – injury – "The bandage was wound around his wound." (wound as past of *wind*: /waʊnd/)
- **produce** – PRO-duce (noun) = food grown; pro-DUCE (verb) = make – "The farm was cultivated to produce produce."
- **refuse** – re-FUSE (verb) = say no; REF-use (noun) = trash – "The workers had to refuse refuse."
- **polish** – POL-ish (verb) = shine; "Polish" (adj) = from Poland – "We must polish the Polish furniture."
- **lead** – /liːd/ (verb) = guide; /lɛd/ (noun) = metal – "He could lead if he could get the lead out."
- **get the lead out** (idiom) – hurry up – "We need to get the lead out."
- **desert** – de-SERT (verb) = abandon; DEZ-ert (noun) = sandy land – "The soldier decided to desert…"
- **dessert** – dih-ZERT (noun) = sweet course – "…his tasty dessert in the desert."
- **present** – PREZ-ent (noun) = gift or now; pre-ZENT (verb) = show or give formally – "time to present the present."
- **bass** – /bæs/ = fish; /beɪs/ = low notes or bass guitar – "A bass was painted on the head of the bass drum."
- **dove** – /dʌv/ = bird; /doʊv/ = past of *dive* – "The dove dove into the bushes."
- **object** – OB-ject (noun) = thing; ob-JECT (verb) = protest – "I did not object to the object…"
- **invalid** – IN-va-lid (noun) = sick person; in-VAL-id (adj) = not legally valid – "The insurance was invalid for the invalid."
- **row** – /raʊ/ (noun) = noisy argument, rhymes with "cow"; /roʊ/ (verb) = use oars, rhymes with "go" – "a row among the oarsmen about who would row."
- **close** – /kloʊz/ (verb) = shut; /kloʊs/ (adj) = near – "They were too close to the door to close it."
- **doe** – /doʊ/ = female deer; **does** – /dʌz/ = verb "do" – "The buck does many things when the does are present."
- **sewer** – /ˈsoʊər/ = one who sews; /ˈsuːər/ = drain pipe – "A seamstress and a sewer fell down into a sewer line."
- **sow** – /saʊ/ = adult female pig; /soʊ/ = plant seeds – "The farmer taught his sow to sow."
- **tear** – /tɛər/ = rip (hole); /tɪər/ = liquid from eyes – "She shed a tear."
- **subject** – SUB-ject (noun) = topic or person; sub-JECT (verb) = force – "I had to subject the subject to tests."
- **intimate** – IN-ti-mit (adj) = close; IN-ti-mayt (verb) = hint – "How can I intimate this to my most intimate friend?"
- **wind** – /wɪnd/ = air; /waɪnd/ = turn – "The wind was too strong to wind the sail."

## 725. Why It's Difficult to Understand Movies, TV, or Music | Improve Your Listening
Why it's hard:
- Speech is colloquial: slang, idioms, and expressions you didn't learn in class.
- Words link together (going to → "gonna", have to → "hafta"), so it sounds like one long word.
- Speakers may talk faster, especially in music or on camera when nervous.
- Listening to TV or music is an advanced skill; it's fine to find it hard.
Why keep doing it:
- Do it because you want to, not because you feel you must.
- Choose content you like (sports, cooking, etc.); you get the most value from it.
- Any exposure is good; surround yourself with English.
- You control what you learn from; pick what keeps you engaged.
- English media is easy to access through TV, streaming, and the internet.

## 726. Why You Should Learn Other Languages | Natural Conversation + Vocabulary

- **learn through osmosis** (idiom) – learn gradually without trying hard – "I learned Spanish through osmosis."

## 730. Write Smarter, Not Harder 💡 | 7 Tips to Instantly Improve Your Academic Writing
- Be concise: use as few words as possible. Use action verbs. "The report provided an explanation" → "The report explained."
- Use vocabulary you already know. Avoid hard words you don't understand.
- Outline first: spend 5 minutes listing your main points in order.
- Avoid informal language: contractions ("could've" not "could of"), idioms ("piece of cake" → "easy"), and phrasal verbs ("put up with" → "endure"; "check out" → "view").
- Vary sentence structure: mix simple, compound, complex, and compound-complex sentences to avoid choppy text.
- Use the active voice most of the time. "Archaeologists conducted a study" (active, 5 words) beats "A study was conducted… by archaeologists."
- Avoid repetition: vary transition words ("also" → "in addition," "furthermore," "moreover"). Avoid "very."
- Proofread twice: read forward for flow and logic; read backward (last sentence first) to catch spelling and grammar errors.

## 731. You're definitely fluent if you can change these. | Advanced (C1 + C2) Grammar
- **had** – I regret not hitting the like button → I wish I had hit the like button.
- **ever** – I almost always go out on Saturday night → I hardly ever stay at home on Saturday night.
- **else** – I asked her if she needed more information → I asked her if there was anything else she needed to know.
- **difficulty** – It's easy for my colleague to speak in public → My colleague has no difficulty speaking in public.
- **lose** – Losing weight will take longer than you think → You won't be able to lose weight as fast as you think.
- **unless** – We need to run or the bus will leave before we get there → Unless we run, the bus will have left by the time we get there.
- **rare** – You rarely get less than 80% on these quiz lessons → It is rare for you to get less than 80% on these quiz lessons.
- **for** – Thanks to my colleague we won the competition → Had it not been for my colleague, we would have lost the competition.
- **resemblance** – He looks a lot like my teacher Wes → He bears an uncanny resemblance to my teacher Wes.
- **doubled** – That child is twice as tall as she was two years ago → That child's height has doubled in the last two years.
- **truth** – He promised never to lie again → He swore to tell the truth from now on.
- **foot** – I don't think I made a good impression on my new boss today → I think my boss and I might have gotten off on the wrong foot today.
- Collocations: uncanny / striking / remarkable resemblance; "had it not been for" = "if it had not been for" (third conditional).
- **get off on the wrong foot** (idiom) – start a relationship or task badly.

## 732. YouTube Vocabulary that You Need to Know ▶️
- **thumbnail** (noun) – small preview image for a video – "a catchy thumbnail"
- **clickbait** (noun) – misleading title or thumbnail meant to get clicks – "That title is clickbait."
- **go viral** (verb phrase) – spread quickly and become very popular – "The video went viral."
- **troll** (noun) – person who starts arguments or insults online – "Don't be a troll."
- **hater** (noun) – person who always criticizes, without personal attacks – "a hater in the comments"
- **stream** (verb/noun) – broadcast live – "I'll stream a lesson on Saturday."
- **chat** (noun) – live messages during a stream (not the same as comments) – "Write in the chat."
- **vlogger / vlog** (noun/verb) – person who films and shares their daily life – "She vlogs every week."
- **trending** (adj) – very popular and widely discussed right now – "What's trending?"
- **influencer** (noun) – person with strong influence over an audience, often in marketing – "a fashion influencer"
- **algorithm** (noun) – the system that decides which videos are recommended – "the YouTube algorithm"
- **copyright** (noun) – legal ownership of content someone creates – "copyright strike"
- **infringe (on)** (verb) – use someone's content without permission – "You infringe on their copyright."
- **fair use** (noun) – legal use of short clips for criticism, news, or education – "a gray area"
- **gray area** (noun) – unclear situation with no clear rules – "Fair use is a gray area."
- **notification** (noun) – alert about new content – "Hit the bell for notifications."
