## 735. You are obviously fluent in English if you can understand these

**punch line** (noun) – last part of a joke that makes it funny
**wordplay** (noun) – using a word's multiple meanings for humor
**pun** (noun) – joke using a word with two meanings or a similar sound
**one-liner** (noun) – short joke in one sentence
**double entendre** (noun) – phrase with an innocent and a rude meaning
**satire** (noun) – humor using irony or sarcasm to ridicule people or institutions
**cheesy** (adj) – corny, low-quality (of a joke)

**hard vs. hardly** (adverbs) – "hard" = with great effort ("work hard"); "hardly" = barely ("hardly work")

## 736. You have certainly achieved fluency if you can change these. | Advanced Grammar (B2 - C2)

**supposed to** (phr.) – expected to; should have – "This train was supposed to arrive five minutes ago."
**if only** (phr.) – expresses regret – "If only I had studied more for the exam."
**cut back on** (phr. verb) – reduce – "We need to cut back on our spending."
**give your resignation notice** (collocation) – formally announce you are leaving a job – "I gave my boss my resignation notice."
**think highly of** (phr.) – have a good opinion of – "The company is highly thought of in the community."
**in addition to + -ing** (phr.) – as well as – "In addition to being a student, she is a waitress."
**see the point of + -ing** (collocation) – understand the purpose of – "I don't see the point of learning this." ("in" also correct, less common)
**averse to** (adj) – unwilling or reluctant – "I am not averse to driving." (= willing)
**aversion to** (noun) – strong dislike – "I have an aversion to raw eggs."
**be bound by** (phr.) – required to follow (a contract) – "You are bound by the agreement."

Grammar:
- Reported speech shifts back a tense: "I went for a walk" -> "He said he had gone for a walk."
- Years use cardinal numbers, not ordinal: "seven years," not "seventh years."
- Future perfect for an ongoing state until a future point: "I will have been married for seven years."
- Passive for emphasis on the action: "Once this contract is signed, you are bound by the agreement."

## 737. You know YOU'RE FLUENT when ... | English Fluency Test

Signs of fluency (no official test exists; only you decide):
- Feel comfortable surrounded by English without getting burnt out.
- Dream and think in English; you no longer translate from your language.
- Can "survive" in English: bank, post office, doctor, emails.
- Discuss big topics (climate change, globalization, equality) with confidence.
- Speak with few pauses; fillers (um, hmm) are fine but not overused.
- Tell captivating stories using intonation and emphasis.
- Understand about 85% of a TV show or movie without subtitles.
- Infer the meaning of a new word from context; understand why jokes are funny.
- Eavesdrop (overhear English conversations) and follow them.
- Read newspaper articles without constant dictionary use; average adult reads 200-300 words per minute.
- Self-correct your writing; adapt tone to audience (formal vs. informal); write poetry (rhyme, similes, metaphors).

**eavesdrop** (verb) – listen secretly to others' conversation – "I could eavesdrop on their talk."
**burnt out** (adj) – exhausted from constant pressure – "I was burnt out after the exams."
**captivating** (adj) – very interesting; holds attention – "a captivating story"

## 738. Your English grammar is EXCELLENT if you can answer these

- Unreal "as if / as though" + past subjunctive "were": "She speaks English as though she were a native." ("was" common in speech.)
- Future continuous with a time point: "They will be living in their new home by this time next year."
- Inversion for emphasis after negatives or "little did": "Little did they know what was about to happen." / "Never have I seen such a beautiful sunset."
- Third conditional, including inverted form (no "if"): "If he had studied, he would not have failed." = "Had I known, I would have done it differently."
- suggest / recommend that + base verb: "He suggested that I go." (not "suggested me going")
- "prefer X to Y" (not usually "over"): "I prefer tea to coffee." "look forward to" + -ing: "I look forward to meeting you." Same -ing pattern: committed to, used to, object to, confess to, admit to, opposed to.
- "Neither of the options is good." Formal: singular verb. Informal speech: "are" is common.
- Fewer + countable (people, items); less + uncountable: "The fewer people there are, the better." "10 items or fewer."
- Ambiguous sentence: "The man saw the woman with the telescope." Who has the telescope depends on context.
- Reported speech shifts back a tense: "I am tired." -> "She said she was tired."
- Passive: "They are repairing the car." -> "The car is being repaired." Passive gerund: "I hate when people interrupt me." -> "I hate being interrupted."
- "any intention of + -ing": "We had no idea he was planning to resign." -> "We had no idea he had any intention of resigning."

## 739. Your Path to Speaking Confidently, Clearly, & Effectively

Confidence comes from frequent practice and varied conversations (rehearsing a talk 10-15 times builds confidence). "Clearly" means mutual intelligibility, not accent. "Effectively" means using emphasis, pauses, precise language, and storytelling. Course promo omitted.

**mutual intelligibility** (noun) – speakers of different English varieties can understand each other

## 740. Your listening skills are EXCELLENT if you understand AMERICAN FAST SPEECH

- **Assimilation** – two sounds blend: "did you" -> "dija" (J sound): "What did you do?"
- **Dropped T / overlap** – tongue stops then releases into next sound: final T of "what" is barely heard in "what did you."
- **Reduction** – "going to" -> "gonna"; "have to" -> "hafta": "I'm gonna have to call you back."
- **Linking** – final consonant joins next vowel: "was up all night" sounds like "wuz-up-all-night."
- **G-dropping** – "-ing" -> "-in'": "thinking about" -> "thinkin' about."
- **Flap T** – American T between vowels sounds like a soft D: "thinking about it" -> "thinkin' aboud it."
- **Gemination** – double consonant merged into one longer sound: "promised to" (final T + T).
- **Elision** – sound dropped: "with him" (H muted), "next Saturday" (T dropped).
- **Weak "to"** – unstressed "to" reduces to a schwa-like sound: "go to the movies" -> "go-tuh-the."

## 741. ☑️ ONLINE ENGLISH LESSON | Q&A with Interactive English

**take a raincheck** (idiom) – postpone an invitation – "Let's take a raincheck on dinner."
**straight out of** (phr.) – from (a place) – "I'm coming straight out of Budapest."
**binge-watch** (verb) – watch many episodes in a row – "We binge-watched the whole season."
**kill two birds with one stone** (idiom) – achieve two things at once
**rule of thumb** (idiom) – a general guideline, not a strict rule
**conflate** (verb) – mix up two things as if they were the same
**chinwag** (noun, British) – a casual chat
**have a crush on** (collocation) – be attracted to – "I have a crush on my neighbor."

- someone / somebody: positive sentences ("Someone copied my homework"). anyone / anybody: questions and negatives ("Does anybody study English?" "I can't hear anyone.")
- however: starts a sentence, followed by comma ("However, ..."). but: joins two clauses ("I was tired, but I went.")
- "I do want" = emphatic "want" (informal).
- who vs. whom: "whom" for the object ("Whom do you like?"); "who" is common in speech.
- "analysis" (singular) / "analyses" (plural). "data" is often used as both in the US.
- Essay: thesis is usually the last sentence of the intro; each paragraph has a topic sentence, support, and a conclusion.
- Learning tips: search images for grammar examples; read at your level; use spaced repetition (e.g., Anki) for vocabulary; keep the list small (a few words a day).

## 742. ⚡ LIVE ENGLISH LESSON ⚡ Q&A with Interactive English

**nod off** (phr. verb) – start to fall asleep – "The baby was nodding off."
**every other day** (phr.) – every two days – "Classes every other day: Mon, Wed, Fri."
**bamboozle** (verb) – trick someone, often to take something from them – "He bamboozled me out of my money."
**grim** (adj) – bad, with a negative outlook – "The situation looks grim."
**blaze of glory** (idiom) – going down fighting boldly, even if you lose – "He went down in a blaze of glory."
**off the top of my head** (idiom) – said without research or preparation
**shadowing** (noun) – repeat a speaker right after them to copy pronunciation and intonation
**schwa** (noun) – unstressed "uh" sound: "banana" /bəˈnænə/

- too + adjective (adverb of degree): "too hot," "too tired." also: transition word for adding information. in addition: more formal than "also."
- "months" /mʌnθs/: the "th" is often nearly silent when speaking fast.
- Note-taking: focus on content words (nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs) that carry the meaning.
- Active listening: listen with a task (answer questions, take notes); passive listening is just listening.
- Speaking fast: do not worry about small mistakes; writing lets you self-correct, speaking does not.
- Writing: read a lot; reading builds vocabulary and shows correct grammar structures.

## 743. ✍️ 35 Creative Ways to Write SAID | Make Your Writing POP

**pleaded** (verb) – make an urgent or emotional request – "Don't leave," he pleaded.
**begged** (verb) – make a stronger urgent request – "I want a balloon," the child begged.
**entreated** (verb) – try hard to persuade – "Help me with the presentation," he entreated.
**implored** (verb) – ask sincerely and emotionally – "Please don't give me a ticket," she implored.
**besieged** (verb) – ask insistently because you really need it – "I need this raise," he besieged his boss.
**cajoled** (verb) – persuade by pleasant talk – "Please, pretty please," she cajoled.
**insisted** (verb) – say firmly, despite disagreement – "I want to see my client," the lawyer insisted.
**demanded** (verb) – ask forcefully, expecting no refusal – "I want another candy," the child demanded.
**gushed** (verb) – praise excessively, perhaps insincerely – "You look amazing," he gushed.
**cheered** (verb) – shout approval or encouragement – "We won!" she cheered.
**touted** (verb) – praise or advertise repeatedly – "You won't find another car like this," the salesman touted.
**laughed** (verb) – laugh because something is funny – "I knew it was you," she laughed.
**chortled** (verb) – laugh with satisfaction – "Did you hear the news?" she chortled.
**giggled** (verb) – laugh repeatedly and uncontrollably – "Where did you get that hat?" he giggled.
**proclaimed** (verb) – announce officially, usually something positive – "This is a new beginning," he proclaimed.
**announced** (verb) – make known officially – "We got engaged!" she announced.
**declared** (verb) – announce clearly, firmly, publicly – "We made it to the finals," the coach declared.
**pouted** (verb) – push out the lower lip to show annoyance – "I want some ice cream," the child pouted.
**complained** (verb) – say something is wrong or unsatisfactory – "We don't want to work weekends," the employees complained.
**sobbed** (verb) – cry noisily with deep breaths – "It's all over," she sobbed.
**cried** (verb) – produce tears from strong emotion – "It's over," he cried.
**lamented** (verb) – express sadness or regret – "I should never have accepted the offer," he lamented.
**sneered** (verb) – speak unkindly, showing no respect – "Is that the best you can do?" she sneered.
**whined** (verb) – complain in a high, sad voice – "My ice cream melted," the boy whined.
**bemoaned** (verb) – complain or express sadness about something – "We must lay people off," the manager bemoaned.
**shouted** (verb) – speak loudly – "We won!" they shouted.
**exclaimed** (verb) – shout suddenly from surprise, fear, or pleasure – "That's not fair!" she exclaimed.
**bellowed** (verb) – shout in a deep, loud voice – "Who's there?" he bellowed.
**yelled** (verb) – shout angrily or in pain – "Get out!" she yelled.
**screamed** (verb) – shout very loudly, e.g., for help – "Help us!" they screamed.
**hollered** (verb) – shout loudly to get attention – "Pass me the ball!" he hollered.
**murmured** (verb) – say something very quietly – "I love you," the boy murmured.
**muttered** (verb) – speak quietly and low, hard to hear, often complaining – "You're the lazy one," the student muttered.
**mumbled** (verb) – speak quietly and unclearly – "I can't believe he remembered," he mumbled.
**whispered** (verb) – speak very softly so only nearby people hear – "Don't tell anyone," she whispered.
**under one's breath** (phr.) – quietly, so others cannot hear – "She muttered under her breath."

## 744. 🔥 FLUENCY TEST | What is your level of English?

**be built by** (passive) – "The house was built by my grandfather." (active: "My grandfather built the house.")
**beg to differ** (phr.) – a polite way to disagree – "I beg to differ."
**look forward to + -ing** (collocation) – "I look forward to going on vacation."
**have a knack for + -ing** (collocation) – be naturally good at – "My colleague has a knack for getting on my nerves." ("knack of" is informal.)
**get on (someone's) nerves** (idiom) – annoy someone – "My colleague sometimes gets on my nerves."
**it is rare for someone to + verb** (phr.) – "It is rare for her to get less than 70% on her exam."
**feel like + -ing** (phr.) – want to do – "Do you feel like watching Netflix?"
**on her exam / in her exam** (prepositions) – both used; "on" is more common in US English.
**as far as (someone) is concerned** (phr.) – in (someone's) opinion – "As far as he was concerned, the music was too loud."
**in light of** (phr.) – considering or because of new information – "The suspect was released in light of new evidence."
**be bound by** (phr.) – legally required to follow – "You are bound by the agreement."
**keep someone in the dark** (idiom) – not tell someone information – "My friend kept me in the dark about the surprise party."
**it slipped my mind** (idiom) – I forgot – "I'm sorry, the meeting slipped my mind."
**no sign of** (phr.) – nothing shows someone is present – "After an hour, there is still no sign of him."
**the school is behind the office buildings** -> "The office buildings are in front of the school." (singular "is" vs plural "are" must match the subject)

Informal score scale (14 items): 0-1 = A1; 2-4 = A2; 5-7 = B1; 8-9 = B2; 10-12 = C1; 13-14 = C2.

## 745. 🚨 CRIME VOCABULARY | Words & phrases you need to know

Crime levels (least to most severe):
**infraction** (n) – minor offense – "speeding ticket"
**misdemeanor** (n) – more serious minor crime – "stealing clothes from a store"
**felony** (n) – most severe crime – "murder"

Crimes:
**arson** (n) – setting fire to damage or destroy something – "charged with arson"
**bribery** (n) – giving money or favors to influence decisions – "took a bribe"
**homicide** (n) – killing someone on purpose; also the police unit that solves murders – "works in homicide"
**shoplifting** (n) – stealing from a store – "caught shoplifting"
**embezzlement** (n) – stealing money you are responsible for, often over time; verb **embezzle** – "embezzled funds"
**perjury** (n) – lying in court while under oath; verb **commit perjury** – "committed perjury"

People:
**witness** (n) – person who saw the crime – "a witness described the thief"
**suspect** (n) – person police think committed the crime – "the suspect"
**accomplice** (n) – person who helps commit a crime – "his accomplice"
**alibi** (n) – proof you were somewhere else; the person who confirms it – "my alibi"

Law enforcement:
**pick up** (phr v) – arrest or take in for questions – "police picked up a suspect"
**apprehend** (v) – arrest, capture – "police apprehended him"
**search warrant** (n phr) – court order needed to enter and search a premises – "police need a search warrant"
**Miranda rights** (n phr, US) – rights read at arrest: right to remain silent, right to an attorney – "read his Miranda rights"
**take the law into your own hands** (idiom) – act without police – "he took the law into his own hands"
**vigilante** (n) – person who takes the law into their own hands – "vigilante justice"
**DUI** (abbr) – driving under the influence, usually alcohol – "got a DUI"

Courtroom:
**subpoena** (n) – legal order to appear in court – "received a subpoena"
**prosecutor** (n) – lawyer against the accused; the other side is the defense – "the prosecutor"
**plead the fifth** (v phr, US) – refuse to answer to avoid self-incrimination – "he pleaded the fifth"
**hearsay** (n) – unverified information or rumor – "that's just hearsay"

## 746. 🚨 LIVE ENGLISH LESSON | Q&A with Interactive English

Pronunciation:
**luxurious** – the "x" is soft, close to a "g" sound in fast speech
**either / neither** – said two ways; depends on where you grew up
**pious** – PIE-us
**prestigious** – the middle has a long "ee" sound
**plough** – pronounced "plow" (to till land)
**rendezvous** – French; RON-day-voo, no "z" sound
**fortnight** – two weeks; more common in British English
**ban / van, bass / vase** – minimal pairs; B: lips close, puff of air; V: teeth on lower lip, voice buzzes

Vocabulary:
**as such** (phr) – in the strict sense; used with negatives – "I'm not a learner as such."
**catch-22** (n) – problem with no solution because the solution is part of the problem – "I need a visa to get a job, and a job to get a visa."

Grammar:
- Transitive verb takes a direct object: "I play soccer." Intransitive does not: "I jumped on the bus" (prepositional phrase follows).
- Gerund = -ing noun: "Smoking is bad." "I enjoy dancing."
- "Could" = past ability of "can": "I could touch my toes."
- "manage to" + verb: "I managed to finish."
- -ed vs -ing adjectives: "I am bored" (feeling) vs "I am boring" (causes the feeling).
- "Walking along the road" = next to it; "walking on the road" = on the surface.
- Contractions are reduced in speech: "shouldn't have" sounds like "shouldn't of."
- Past "-ed" endings do not always sound the same. Listen and practice.

Learning tips:
- Get lots of listening and reading exposure.
- Find a speaking partner through a shared interest (e.g., a book club).
- Practice alone: talk to a mirror, record yourself, think in English while walking.

## 747. 🚨 LIVE ENGLISH Q&A LESSON | Interactive English

Pronunciation:
**Chicago** – "ch" sounds like "sh"

Vocabulary and phrases:
**far** (adj/adv) – long distance – "It's far."
**so far** (phr) – up until now – "So far, it's okay."
**so far so good** (idiom) – everything is going well up to now – "So far so good."
**as of now** (phr) – up until this point – "As of now, my internet is down."
**ought to** (modal) – should, advice – "You ought to rest."
**achieve / accomplish** (v) – succeed at a goal; similar meaning – "accomplish a project"
**help yourself** (phr) – take food or drink yourself – "Help yourself to cake."
**I helped myself** (phr) – I took it myself – "I helped myself to cake."
**hold it / hold up** (phr) – stop what you're doing (command) – "Hold it! Don't move."
**go above and beyond** (idiom) – do more than expected – "She went above and beyond."
**shilly-shally** (v, informal) – hesitate, fail to act decisively – "Stop shilly-shallying."
**dilly-dally** (v, informal) – waste time, delay – "Stop dilly-dallying."
**dither** (v) – hesitate – "Don't dither."
**hitherto** (adv, mostly writing) – until now – "Hitherto, we've had no problems."
**due to** (prep, formal) – because of – "Due to the rain, we stayed in."
**pile on** (phr v) – criticize or tease someone along with others – "Don't pile on."
**bear with (someone)** (phr v) – be patient with – "Thanks for bearing with me."
**fair game** (idiom) – acceptable to ask or do – "Any question is fair game."

Grammar:
- Question inversion: "It is an iPhone." → "Is it an iPhone?"
- Active voice is preferred. Use passive when the doer is unknown: "Mistakes were made."
- "Go" + -ing for many sports: "go swimming," "go boxing" (not "do boxing").
- "Due to" = formal; "because of" = casual.
- "Ought to" = "should" (same advice meaning).

Learning tips:
- Read at your own speed. It builds grammar, vocabulary, and comprehension.

## 748. 🚨 LIVE English Lesson | Q&A Session

Vocabulary and phrases:
**in cahoots** (idiom) – working together, often secretly – "They're in cahoots."
**shrewd** (adj) – clever, looks out for yourself – "a shrewd businessman"
**give / get a ride** – give = drive someone; get = receive a ride – "Can you give me a ride?"
**give / get a lift** – same as "ride" – "Can I get a lift?"
**take me to / drop me off** – ask for a ride to a place – "Can you drop me off at school?"
**out of my way** (phr) – not on your route – "It's out of my way."
**bum / catch / hitch a ride** (informal) – get a ride, often free – "Can I bum a ride?"
**ain't** (contr., informal, esp. US South) – am not / is not / are not – "I ain't gonna do it."
**carry out** (phr v) – take food to go; carry someone out – "I want it for carryout."
**dude** (n, informal US slang) – friendly term; avoid with professors
**fella** (n, informal) – can sound awkward or rude
**in person** (phr) – physically present, not online – "The interview is in person."
**bloke / chap** (n, British) – informal word for a man
**what are you thinking of / about** – prepositions at end of a question are common in speech

Pronunciation:
**subtle** – "b" is silent; American "t" sounds like a soft "d"
**though** – "gh" is silent; the vowel is unstressed (schwa)
**bed / bad** – short "e" (bed) vs short "a" (bad); the mouth is wider for "a"
**shrewd** – one syllable

Grammar:
- Causatives: make / let + person + base verb: "They made us do push-ups."
- have + person + base verb: "I'll have her call you."
- have + thing + past participle: "I'm having the house painted."
- get + person + to + infinitive: "I got him to help me."
- help + base verb or to + base verb: "She helped me wash the dishes."
- "I have my students do homework" (they do it) vs "I've got my students to do homework" (I convinced them).
- Subjunctive: "I suggest that you be more careful." Simpler: "You should be more careful."
- Third conditional (unreal, impossible): "If I had gone, I would have gotten the job." The past can't change.
- Second conditional (hypothetical): "If I had wings, I would fly."

## 749. 🚫 DON'T MAKE THESE GRAMMAR MISTAKES THAT EVEN NATIVE SPEAKERS MAKE

- Double negative: "I didn't do nothing" → "I didn't do anything" or "I did nothing." Use "anything" after negatives.
- "Fewer" + count nouns; "less" + non-count nouns: "10 items or fewer," not "10 items or less."
- "Irregardless" is nonstandard. Use "regardless": "Regardless of the polls..."
- "Gonna" = "going to." Fine in speech, avoid in writing.
- "Bigly" is a joke word. Avoid it as a learner.
- Incomplete comparison: add "than." "I ate more than my cousin." (Ads sometimes leave it out.)
- Subject pronouns (I, we, he) vs object pronouns (me, us, him): "My neighbor and I" (subject); "She told my wife and me" (object). Put I or me last.
- Comparatives: 1–2 syllable adjectives and -y words take -er: "spicier," not "more spicy." Longer words take "more": "more beautiful." Exceptions: "more fun," "more likely."
- "e.g." = for example (list examples). "i.e." = in other words (clarify). Mainly for writing.
